Chapter 1 of 39

The Physics of Bayesian Inference

Statistical mechanics, field theory and simulation-based inference, in thirty-eight chapters, a summary and a historical appendix. It explains how to learn parameters from data when you can simulate an experiment but cannot write down its likelihood, and it does so in the language of statistical physics: Boltzmann weights, partition functions and free energies. Every chapter has a worked example with an exact answer to check against.

A short note from the author. I was trained as a physicist, and have spent most of my working life on the other side of that subject — developing, testing and deploying statistical methods for cosmology and large surveys. These notes are my attempt to bring the two threads back together, and to trace the link between theoretical physics and the methods now used for inference, chapter by chapter. Readers do not need both backgrounds to follow along: each chapter states the physics equation with its variables defined and puts the statistics equation beside it.

The claim of this course

Modern statistical estimators, including the neural ones, are not a new subject laid beside physics. They are statistical physics and the Euclidean path integral, restated. The posterior is a Boltzmann weight, the evidence is a partition function, a likelihood is a sum over hidden histories, and each estimator is one of the strategies physicists developed for evaluating such sums: saddle points, variational bounds, Monte Carlo, changes of variables, stochastic dynamics. Every chapter states its piece of that claim, derives it, and works an example with an exact answer. Sections marked construction go to the level at which the two subjects are the same mathematics.

The problem in one paragraph

You measure data x and want a parameter θ. Bayes' rule needs the likelihood p(x | θ), the probability of your exact data given θ. For a detector, a galaxy survey or a turbulent flow nobody can write that function down. But a computer can simulate data for any θ you choose. Simulation-based inference (SBI) is the family of methods that recover the posterior from simulations alone.

p(θ | x)  =  p(x | θ) · p(θ)  /  p(x) posterior = likelihood × prior / evidence

What is in each chapter

Every chapter has the same plan. It opens with a box stating the connection to physics. A background section says where the idea came from. The main text works an example with an exact answer. Under every worked example a button marked Show the code opens a short Python program that reproduces its numbers, followed by the output of running it. A section of questions people ask discusses the subtleties and the limits of the analogy, and a section marked construction, where there is one, derives the correspondence. The chapters can be read in order or singly. The main line runs from the dictionary through ABC, the neural methods and the curve-fitting example to the checks. The other chapters each take one topic further.

What you need to know first

  • The Boltzmann distribution and the partition function, at the level of a first thermal physics course.
  • The Gaussian (normal) distribution and what a mean and a standard deviation are.
  • No machine learning. Neural networks are treated as flexible functions that can be fitted to examples.

Notation: θ is the parameter, x the data, N(μ, σ²) a Gaussian with mean μ and variance σ². Energies are in units where kT = 1 unless stated.

2. The dictionary →

Chapter 2 of 39

The dictionary: inference as statistical physics

Statistical physics met the central difficulty of SBI seventy years ago and found the way round it.

physics

The connection. This chapter is the dictionary itself. Everything later is one of its entries worked out.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Posterior p(θ | data)Boltzmann distribution exp(−E/kBT)/Z
−ln(likelihood × prior)Energy E
EvidencePartition function Z
−ln(evidence)Free energy F

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: why the two subjects meet

Statistical mechanics was built between 1870 and 1902 by Boltzmann and Gibbs to explain heat in terms of atoms. Its central object, the partition function, is a sum over every arrangement the atoms could take. For anything more complicated than an ideal gas that sum cannot be done, and a large part of twentieth-century theoretical physics consists of ways to approximate it: mean-field theory, perturbation expansions, the renormalisation group, and from 1953 computer simulation.

Bayesian statistics has the same object under another name. The evidence is a sum over every value the parameters could take, and it also cannot be done once there are more than a few parameters. Statisticians met this wall later, in the 1980s, when computers made large models thinkable. They found that the tools were already on the shelf. Markov chain Monte Carlo, the variational method and annealing were all taken from physics, sometimes by people who had trained as physicists.

In 1957 Jaynes turned the borrowing into a principle. He argued that statistical mechanics is not a branch of mechanics at all. It is inference: the best guess about a system's microscopic state given the few things that are known about it. On that reading the dictionary in this chapter is no coincidence. The two subjects are one subject applied to different questions.

The same difficulty

A system at temperature T is found in microstate s with the Boltzmann probability

p(s)  =  e−E(s)/kT  /  Z,     Z  =  Σs e−E(s)/kT Z is the partition function

The energy of one state is easy. The sum over all states is not: 100 spins have 2100 states. In 1953 Metropolis and co-workers showed how to draw states with the right probabilities without ever computing Z, using only energy differences. Every sampler in Bayesian statistics descends from that paper.

physics

Easy: energy of one state. Hard: the sum over all states.

Fix: simulate states in proportion to their weight and never compute Z.

inference

Easy: simulate one data set. Hard: the probability of your data.

Fix: simulate many data sets and learn from them, never writing the likelihood.

The correspondence is exact

Define an energy for each parameter value as minus the log of likelihood times prior. Bayes' rule then is the Boltzmann distribution at kT = 1:

E(θ)  =  −ln[ p(x | θ) p(θ) ]   ⇒   p(θ | x)  =  e−E(θ) / Z,    Z = p(x)
Bayesian inferenceStatistical physicsIn plain words
Parameter θMicrostate sThe thing that could take many values
Posterior p(θ | x)Boltzmann distributionHow probable each value is
−ln(likelihood × prior)Energy EGood fits are low-energy states
Best-fit valueGround stateThe single most probable value
Evidence p(x)Partition function ZThe normalising sum that is hard to compute
−ln(evidence)Free energy FOne number for comparing models, as for comparing phases
Noise level in the dataTemperatureMore noise flattens the distribution, like heating
Error bar on θThermal fluctuationHow far the system wanders from its ground state
Markov-chain Monte CarloMetropolis simulationVisit states in proportion to their weight
Marginalising a nuisance parameterIntegrating out degrees of freedomSum over what you do not care about
Summary statisticsMacroscopic variables (M, U, P)A few numbers that carry what matters

Worked example: one measurement of a Gaussian mean

Prior θ ~ N(0, τ²). One measurement x with noise σ. The energy is

E(θ) = (x − θ)² / 2σ² + θ² / 2τ² + const

This is a quadratic well, a harmonic oscillator. Completing the square gives its minimum and its stiffness:

θ0 = x · τ² / (τ² + σ²),     κ = 1/σ² + 1/τ²
Result. The posterior is N(θ0, 1/κ). The error bar is the thermal width of the well, √(kT/κ) at kT = 1. A stiffer well (less noise, tighter prior) gives a smaller error bar, by equipartition.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
x, sigma, tau = 1.2, 0.5, 2.0                    # the measurement, its noise, the prior width
theta = np.linspace(-6, 6, 200001)
E = (x - theta)**2 / (2 * sigma**2) + theta**2 / (2 * tau**2)
p = np.exp(-E); p /= np.trapezoid(p, theta)      # Boltzmann weight of the energy
mean = np.trapezoid(theta * p, theta); sd = np.sqrt(np.trapezoid((theta - mean)**2 * p, theta))
kappa = 1 / sigma**2 + 1 / tau**2
print("numerical: mean %.4f, sd %.4f" % (mean, sd))
print("formula:   mean %.4f, sd %.4f" % (x * tau**2 / (tau**2 + sigma**2), kappa**-0.5))

Output

numerical: mean 1.1294, sd 0.4851
formula:   mean 1.1294, sd 0.4851

Why a likelihood is a partition function

A simulator makes many random choices on the way from θ to data: thermal kicks, collisions, detector noise. Call these hidden variables z. The probability of your data sums over every hidden history that could have produced it:

p(x | θ)  =  Σz p(x, z | θ) a sum over all hidden histories

That is a partition function, one for each θ, and it is intractable for the same reason. Sampling one history is cheap even when summing over all of them is impossible. That asymmetry is what SBI exploits.

Example: the Ising model

You photograph one spin pattern of a magnet and ask what the temperature was. The probability of that pattern is e−E/kT/Z(T), and Z(T) sums 2N terms. You cannot evaluate it for a large lattice, but you can simulate patterns at any T in seconds. Simulate at many temperatures, see which produce patterns like your photograph, and you have done SBI.

More worked examples

Worked example: free energy = energy − entropy, for two hypotheses

Two hypotheses are equally likely beforehand. The data have probability 0.02 under the first and 0.005 under the second. Give each an energy E = −ln(likelihood × prior):

E1 = −ln(0.02 × 0.5) = 4.605,    E2 = −ln(0.005 × 0.5) = 5.991

The partition function is Z = e−4.605 + e−5.991 = 0.0125, so the posterior probabilities are 0.8 and 0.2, and the free energy is F = −ln Z = 4.382. Now compute it the thermodynamic way. The average energy is 0.8 × 4.605 + 0.2 × 5.991 = 4.882. The entropy of the posterior is −0.8 ln 0.8 − 0.2 ln 0.2 = 0.500.

Result. ⟨E⟩ − S = 4.882 − 0.500 = 4.382 = F. The identity F = E − TS of thermodynamics holds for a posterior, with T = 1. The evidence rewards a low energy (a good fit) and a high entropy (many acceptable explanations).
Show the code 
import numpy as np
like  = np.array([0.02, 0.005])          # probability of the data under each hypothesis
prior = np.array([0.5, 0.5])
E = -np.log(like * prior)                # energies
Z = np.exp(-E).sum()                     # partition function = evidence
p = np.exp(-E) / Z                       # posterior
F = -np.log(Z)
meanE = (p * E).sum()
S = -(p * np.log(p)).sum()
print("energies", E.round(3), " Z = %.4f" % Z, " posterior", p.round(2))
print("F = %.3f   <E> = %.3f   S = %.3f   <E> - S = %.3f" % (F, meanE, S, meanE - S))

Output

energies [4.605 5.991]  Z = 0.0125  posterior [0.8 0.2]
F = 4.382   <E> = 4.882   S = 0.500   <E> - S = 4.382

Worked example: counting photons

A detector records 7 photons in one second. The unknown rate is λ per second, with an exponential prior of mean 10. The Poisson likelihood and the prior give the energy

E(λ) = λ − 7 ln λ + λ/10 + constant

The first term pushes the rate down, because a high rate would have produced more counts. The logarithm pushes it up, like a repulsive barrier at zero. Setting the derivative to zero gives the minimum at λ = 7/1.1 = 6.36. The Boltzmann weight e−E is a gamma distribution, with mean 8/1.1 = 7.27 and standard deviation √8/1.1 = 2.57.

Result. The rate is 7.3 ± 2.6 per second. The energy is not a parabola, so the mean (7.27) and the minimum (6.36) differ: the well is steeper on the low side. That asymmetry is what a Gaussian approximation would miss.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
k, prior_mean = 7, 10.0                  # counts seen; mean of the exponential prior
rate = 1 + 1/prior_mean                  # posterior is Gamma(shape k+1, rate 1.1)
post = stats.gamma(k + 1, scale=1/rate)
print("minimum of the energy: %.2f" % (k / rate))
print("posterior mean %.2f, sd %.2f" % (post.mean(), post.std()))

Output

minimum of the energy: 6.36
posterior mean 7.27, sd 2.57

Questions people ask

Is this an analogy or an identity? It is an identity at the level of mathematics. Write the posterior as exp(−E)/Z and every theorem about Boltzmann distributions applies to it, with no approximation. What differs is the interpretation. In physics the randomness is thermal and the energy is a property of matter. In inference the randomness is our ignorance and the energy is a property of a model and a data set.

What plays the temperature? Nothing needs to: the natural temperature of a posterior is 1. But a temperature can be introduced on purpose, by raising the likelihood to a power. That is done to help samplers cross barriers, and to down-weight data that are not fully trusted. The noise variance of a Gaussian likelihood acts in the same way, because it divides the misfit just as temperature divides the energy.

If the mathematics is the same, why did the two fields develop separately? Because the questions differ. A physicist usually knows the energy and wants average properties. A statistician knows some observed properties and wants the parameters inside the energy. The second is the inverse of the first, and it is harder: every step of an inference contains a forward problem of the physicist's kind.

Does the dictionary help in practice or is it only a way of talking? It helps in two ways. It tells you which approximation to try, because each physical approximation has a known range of validity. And it tells you how a method fails. A sampler stuck in one mode is a system out of equilibrium, and a variational fit with error bars that are too small is mean-field theory ignoring correlations.

constructionThe same dictionary in field-theory language

Quantum field theory in imaginary time is statistical mechanics: the weight of a field configuration is e−S, with S the Euclidean action, and every observable is an average over configurations. Replace "field configuration" by "parameter value" and the formulae of Bayesian inference appear unchanged. This is the frame the rest of the course builds on.

Field theoryInferenceRemark
Action S[φ]−ln(likelihood × prior)The energy of the earlier table
Path integral Z = ∫ Dφ e−SEvidenceSum over everything not observed
Classical field (stationary action)Best fit (MAP)δS = 0
PropagatorPosterior covarianceInverse of the second derivative of S
Correlation functionsPosterior momentsMean, covariance, skewness, …
Free (quadratic) theoryGaussian posteriorSolved exactly; Wick's theorem gives all moments from the covariance
InteractionsNon-Gaussian terms in ln pHandled by perturbation theory or by sampling
Loop expansion parameter ħNoise level, or 1/(number of data)Small parameter: fluctuations about the best fit are small
Source JThe dataMade precise in the next chapter
Integrating out heavy fieldsMarginalising nuisance parametersLeaves an effective action for what remains

When the unknown is itself a function, such as a sky brightness or a density field, the correspondence is literal and not a relabelling. A Gaussian-process prior is a free field theory, its kernel is the propagator, and the optimal linear reconstruction (the Wiener filter) is the classical field in the presence of the data as a source. This programme is known as information field theory.

← 1. Start3. Path integrals →

Chapter 3 of 39

Path integrals: the likelihood as a sum over histories

The previous chapter said that a likelihood is a sum over hidden variables. Here that sum is written down and carried out for the simplest stochastic process, and the tools that field theory built for such sums are matched, one by one, to the estimators in the rest of the course.

physics

The connection. The likelihood of a simulator is a Euclidean path integral: a sum over every hidden history that could have produced the data.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Hidden variables of the simulatorPaths, or field configurations
−ln(probability of one history)Euclidean action S
Best-fit history; Laplace approximationClassical path; saddle-point approximation
Corrections to the Gaussian approximationLoop corrections

Background: sums over histories

The idea of adding up contributions from every possible path was introduced twice. Wiener, in the 1920s, built a rigorous probability measure on the paths of a Brownian particle. Feynman, in 1948, reformulated quantum mechanics as a sum over paths weighted by a complex phase. Kac noticed that the two are related by replacing time with imaginary time, which turns Feynman's oscillating phase into Wiener's decaying weight. Field theory done in imaginary time is called Euclidean, and it is the same mathematics as the statistical mechanics of a fluctuating field.

A simulator is a machine for drawing histories. Each run makes a long list of random choices: where each photon lands, when each neuron fires, how each galaxy forms. The data you see are the end of that history. The probability of the data is the total probability of all histories that end there, and that total is a path integral in exactly Wiener's sense. This is why a simulator has no usable likelihood: the likelihood exists, but it is an integral over millions of hidden variables.

Seen like this, simulation-based inference is not a new kind of problem. It is the problem field theorists have had since the 1940s, and the remainder of the course is a tour of their solutions.

A random walk has an action

A particle starts at the origin and takes N independent steps, each Gaussian with variance 2D δt. The probability of one complete history x1, x2, …, xN is the product of the step probabilities:

p(path) ∝ exp[ −Σi (xi+1 − xi)² / 4D δt ]  →  exp[ −∫ ẋ² / 4D dt ] as δt → 0

The exponent is an action, the time integral of a kinetic energy. It is called the Onsager–Machlup action, and it is the Euclidean action of a free particle of mass 1/2D. Smooth, slow paths have low action and are probable. Jagged ones are suppressed. Add a force f(x) and the integrand becomes (ẋ − f)²/4D: the path is penalised for departing from the drift.

Now suppose you observe only where the particle ends, and want to infer D. The likelihood of the end point is the probability of every history that arrives there, added up. That sum is a path integral, with the intermediate positions as the hidden variables z of the previous chapter.

Worked example: doing the path integral

Take two steps of durations t1 and t2 and integrate over the unobserved midpoint x1:

∫ N(x1; 0, 2Dt1) · N(x2 − x1; 0, 2Dt2) dx1 = N(x2; 0, 2D(t1 + t2))

A Gaussian integrated against a Gaussian is a Gaussian whose variance is the sum. Repeating the step N − 1 times removes every intermediate position:

p(xend | D) = N(xend; 0, 2Dt)
Result. The sum over all histories of a free random walk is a Gaussian of variance 2Dt. This is one of the few path integrals that can be done exactly, and it can be done because the action is quadratic: a free theory. The likelihood for D follows at once, and it is also the solution of the diffusion equation, which is the subject of the scores chapter.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats, integrate
D, t1, t2, x2 = 0.5, 1.0, 2.0, 1.3
integrand = lambda x1: stats.norm(0, np.sqrt(2*D*t1)).pdf(x1) * stats.norm(0, np.sqrt(2*D*t2)).pdf(x2 - x1)
print("integral over the midpoint: %.6f" % integrate.quad(integrand, -20, 20)[0])
print("N(x2; 0, 2D(t1 + t2)):      %.6f" % stats.norm(0, np.sqrt(2*D*(t1 + t2))).pdf(x2))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)                   # the same thing by simulating many walks of 300 steps
steps = rng.normal(0, np.sqrt(2 * D * 0.01), (20000, 300))
print("variance of the end point: %.2f (2Dt = %.2f)" % (steps.sum(1).var(), 2 * D * 3.0))

Output

integral over the midpoint: 0.173789
N(x2; 0, 2D(t1 + t2)):      0.173789
variance of the end point: 2.97 (2Dt = 3.00)

Put in a force that is not linear, or let the diffusion constant depend on position, or observe the particle through a detector with its own randomness, and the integral can no longer be done. The histories can still be simulated one at a time. That is the whole situation of simulation-based inference, in its smallest example.

What field theory does with such an integral

There are only a few things one can do with a path integral that has no closed form, and each has a name in statistics.

1. Expand about the most probable history

Find the configuration of least action, then include Gaussian fluctuations about it, then corrections order by order. The first two terms are the Laplace approximation, the Fisher forecast is the leading fluctuation term, and the expansion is worked through for a concrete integral in the free-energy chapter.

2. Solve the Gaussian part exactly and perturb

For a quadratic action every average follows from the covariance C. This is Wick's theorem: an average of a product of variables is the sum over all ways of pairing them, with a factor of the covariance for each pair.

Worked example: Wick's theorem, and the first correction to an error bar

For one zero-mean Gaussian variable with variance C, four factors can be paired in three ways and six factors in fifteen:

⟨θ²⟩ = C,    ⟨θ⁴⟩ = 3C²,    ⟨θ⁶⟩ = 15C³

Now make the posterior slightly non-Gaussian by adding a quartic term to the energy, E = θ²/2C + λθ⁴/24. To first order in λ, expand e−λθ⁴/24 and use the Gaussian averages:

⟨θ²⟩ = [ C − (λ/24)·15C³ ] / [ 1 − (λ/24)·3C² ] ≈ C − λC³/2

In diagram language this is the one-loop correction to the propagator. Compare with the exact integral, for C = 1:

Coupling λFirst-order ⟨θ²⟩Exact ⟨θ²⟩
0.20.9000.919
10.5000.751
5−1.50.497
Result. For a weakly non-Gaussian posterior, perturbation theory gives the change in the error bar on paper. For a strongly non-Gaussian one it gives nonsense, here a negative variance. The same split exists in physics between what diagrams can compute and what needs a lattice simulation. Sampling and neural estimators are the non-perturbative methods of inference.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import integrate, stats
print("Gaussian moments <x^2>, <x^4>, <x^6>:", [round(stats.norm.moment(n)) for n in (2, 4, 6)])
C = 1.0
for lam in (0.2, 1.0, 5.0):
    w = lambda t, n: t**n * np.exp(-t**2 / (2 * C) - lam * t**4 / 24)
    exact = integrate.quad(w, -10, 10, args=(2,))[0] / integrate.quad(w, -10, 10, args=(0,))[0]
    print("lambda = %.1f: first order %.3f, exact %.3f" % (lam, C - lam * C**3 / 2, exact))

Output

Gaussian moments <x^2>, <x^4>, <x^6>: [1, 3, 15]
lambda = 0.2: first order 0.900, exact 0.919
lambda = 1.0: first order 0.500, exact 0.751
lambda = 5.0: first order -1.500, exact 0.497

3. Use a trial distribution

Bound the free energy from above with a tractable family and optimise. This is the variational method, also in the free-energy chapter.

4. Evaluate it by Monte Carlo

Generate configurations with probability e−S and average. This is lattice field theory, and it is every sampler in the samplers chapter.

5. Change variables until it is trivial

Find a map under which the action becomes quadratic. This is a normalising flow, in the flows chapter.

6. Treat it as the equilibrium of a stochastic process

Invent a dynamics whose stationary state is e−S, and run it, forwards or backwards. This is stochastic quantisation, and it is the diffusion model of the scores chapter.

Why "imaginary time"

Quantum mechanics weights each history by a phase, eiS/ħ, and histories interfere. Replace time t by −iτ (a Wick rotation) and the phase becomes a real, positive weight, e−SE/ħ, which can be read as a probability. Under the same substitution the Schrödinger equation of a free particle becomes the diffusion equation. So a quantum particle in imaginary time is a Brownian particle, the path integral of quantum mechanics is the sum over random-walk histories done above, and ħ takes the place of the noise strength 2D. Statistical inference lives entirely on the rotated side, where weights are probabilities, which is why Monte Carlo methods work for it without the sign problem that afflicts real-time quantum dynamics.

The estimators, as ways of evaluating one integral

EstimatorWhat it does to the path integralChapter
Fisher forecast, Laplace approximationSaddle point plus Gaussian fluctuations (one loop)Free energy
Variational inferenceVariational bound on the free energy with a trial actionFree energy
Metropolis, Hamiltonian Monte CarloMonte Carlo evaluation by a fictitious dynamics with the right equilibriumSamplers
Nested samplingDensity of states, then a one-dimensional integralSamplers
ABCMonte Carlo over histories, with the constraint "data = observed" regulated by a toleranceABC
NLELearns the effective action for the data after the hidden variables are integrated outNeural methods
NREFree-energy perturbation between two ensembles, by a classifierNeural methods
NPE with a flowA learned change of variables that makes the posterior a free theory, for every data set at onceFlows, Amortisation
Diffusion modelsTime-reversal of a stochastic process that relaxes to a free theoryScores and diffusion

Read this way the estimators are not a list of unrelated algorithms. They are the known strategies for one problem, an integral over more variables than can be enumerated, and each neural method is a strategy from physics with a learned function in the place where physics used a formula.

More worked examples

Worked example: a sum over two histories

A detector records an event with measured energy 1.2. It may have come from a signal process, which produces energies with a Gaussian spread of 0.2 about 1.0, or from a flat background between 0 and 2. A fraction θ = 0.3 of events are signal. The simulator makes a hidden choice, signal or background, and then draws the energy. The likelihood sums over that hidden choice:

p(1.2 | θ) = 0.3 × 1.210 + 0.7 × 0.5 = 0.363 + 0.350 = 0.713

The two terms are the weights of the two histories. The probability that this event was signal is the first term over the sum.

Result. 0.363 / 0.713 = 51 per cent. At a measured energy of 1.0 it would be 63 per cent, and at 1.6 only 2 per cent. With a million events and a hidden choice in each, the likelihood is a sum over 21,000,000 histories. It factorises here because the events are independent. In a real simulator the hidden choices are coupled, and the sum does not factorise. That is the path integral.
Show the code 
from scipy import stats
theta = 0.3                              # fraction of events that are signal
signal = stats.norm(1.0, 0.2)            # signal energies
background = 0.5                         # flat density between 0 and 2
for x in (1.2, 1.0, 1.6):
    a, b = theta * signal.pdf(x), (1 - theta) * background
    print("energy %.1f: likelihood %.3f = %.3f + %.3f,  P(signal) = %.2f" % (x, a + b, a, b, a / (a + b)))

Output

energy 1.2: likelihood 0.713 = 0.363 + 0.350,  P(signal) = 0.51
energy 1.0: likelihood 0.948 = 0.598 + 0.350,  P(signal) = 0.63
energy 1.6: likelihood 0.357 = 0.007 + 0.350,  P(signal) = 0.02

Questions people ask

Is the path integral here the quantum one? No. It is the Euclidean one, in which every history has a positive weight exp(−S). There is no interference between histories. Quantum mechanics becomes this after rotating time to imaginary values, which is why methods carry across, but nothing quantum is being claimed about data analysis.

What is the action of a simulator? Minus the log of the joint probability of one complete run: every random draw made inside the simulator together with the data that came out. For a random walk it is the sum of squared steps. For a real simulator nobody writes it down, but it exists, and the simulator samples from exp(−S) every time it is run.

If the integral cannot be done, what do the methods do instead? They avoid it in different ways. ABC replaces it by counting how often simulated data land near the real data. Neural likelihood estimation fits a function to the result of the integral without ever doing it. Saddle-point methods keep only the most probable history and the fluctuations round it. Monte Carlo methods sample histories in proportion to their weight.

Where do Feynman diagrams come in? They are the bookkeeping for corrections to the Gaussian approximation. If the action is quadratic, the integral is exact and the posterior is Gaussian. Cubic and quartic terms give corrections, organised by diagrams with one, two or more loops. In statistics the same terms are the skewness and kurtosis corrections to a Laplace approximation.

← 2. The dictionary4. Error bars →

Chapter 4 of 39

Error bars are thermal fluctuations

A measured value with its uncertainty and a particle jiggling in a potential well are one and the same calculation. This chapter sets the two side by side.

physics

The connection. An error bar is the size of a thermal fluctuation in the energy well that the data make.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Fisher matrixStiffness: the curvature of the energy at its minimum
Covariance matrixThermal fluctuations ⟨δq δq⟩ = kBT K⁻¹
The rule Δχ² = 1Equipartition: ½ kBT per degree of freedom
Effect of one data point on the fitFluctuation-response theorem

Background: from Brownian motion to the Cramér-Rao bound

In 1905 Einstein explained the jiggling of pollen grains in water and showed that the size of the jiggling is tied to the friction the grain feels when it is pushed. Nyquist found the same relation for electrical noise in a resistor in 1928, and Callen and Welton proved the general statement in 1951. It is called the fluctuation-dissipation theorem: how much a system fluctuates when left alone fixes how it responds when disturbed.

Statistics reached the same structure by a different road. In the 1920s Fisher defined the information that a data set carries about a parameter as the curvature of the log-likelihood. In the 1940s Rao and Cramér proved that the inverse of this information is the smallest variance any unbiased estimate can have. Neither was thinking about springs, but the curvature of a log-likelihood is the stiffness of an energy well, and the bound is the statement that a particle in that well cannot fluctuate less than its temperature requires.

The link is worth learning early, because the Fisher matrix is the workhorse of forecasting in physics and astronomy. Every time a survey is designed by computing a Fisher matrix, the calculation being done is the harmonic approximation of solid-state physics.

The simplest measurement

Measure one quantity θ a total of n times. Each reading has Gaussian noise of standard deviation σ. Everyone knows the answer: the best estimate is the average of the readings, ȳ, and its error bar is σ/√n.

Now write the same thing as physics. Minus the log of the likelihood is

E(θ) = n (θ − ȳ)² / 2σ² + constant

This is the energy of a mass on a spring. The spring is centred on the average of the data, and its stiffness is k = n/σ². More data or less noise means a stiffer spring. The posterior is the Boltzmann distribution of that spring at temperature 1.

physics

A particle on a spring in a heat bath

Energy. E(q) = ½ k q²

q: displacement from the rest position. k: stiffness of the spring.

Boltzmann distribution. p(q) ∝ exp( −E(q) / kBT )
kBT: temperature in energy units.

Size of the jiggling. ⟨q²⟩ = kBT / k

Equipartition. ⟨E⟩ = ½ kBT for each direction of motion, whatever the stiffness.

inference

The mean of n noisy readings

Energy. E(θ) = ½ (n/σ²) (θ − ȳ)²

θ − ȳ: distance from the best fit. n/σ²: the Fisher information.

Posterior. p(θ | data) ∝ exp( −E(θ) )
The temperature is 1.

Error bar squared. Var(θ) = σ² / n = 1 / stiffness.

The rule Δχ² = 1. On average the energy is ½ above its minimum, so χ² = 2E is 1 above its minimum for each parameter. This is equipartition.

See it

The red dot is a particle in the energy well of the measurement, kicked about by a heat bath. On the right is the record of where it has been. Change the number of readings and the noise.

The energy well, and the particle now
Where the particle has been, against the posterior
Particle now, and the exact posteriorRecent and recorded positionsEnergy ½ (dashed)
–stiffness n/σ²
–error bar σ/√n
–measured spread of the particle
–its average energy (equipartition says 0.50)

Stiffening the spring narrows the histogram, and the spread always settles on σ/√n. The average energy stays near ½ whatever you choose. A sampler such as MCMC is this particle: it finds the error bar by jiggling.

Several parameters

With more than one parameter the stiffness becomes a matrix, and the dictionary extends term by term.

Data analysisStatistical mechanics
Best-fit parametersMinimum of the energy
Fisher matrix FStiffness matrix K: the curvature of the energy at its minimum
Covariance matrix C = F⁻¹Thermal fluctuations ⟨δq δqᵀ⟩ = kBT K⁻¹
Uncorrelated combinations of parametersNormal modes
A degeneracy between parametersA soft mode: a direction of low stiffness that fluctuates widely
Noise variance σ²Temperature: hotter means larger fluctuations
Error bar falling as 1/√nRelative fluctuations falling as 1/√N in a large system
Marginalising over a nuisance parameterIntegrating out a degree of freedom to get an effective energy

Fluctuation and response

There is a deeper statement than "the covariance is the inverse stiffness". Push the particle with a small constant force f. Its average position shifts. The fluctuation-response theorem says that the size of the shift is set by how much the particle was already fluctuating.

physics

Fluctuation-response theorem

∂⟨q⟩ / ∂f = ⟨δq δqᵀ⟩ / kBT

f: a small applied force. Left side: how far the average position moves per unit force, the susceptibility. Right side: the spontaneous fluctuations with no force applied.

A system that jiggles a lot is easy to push.

inference

How far a data point moves the fit

∂⟨θ⟩ / ∂y = C Xᵀ / σ²

y: the data. X: the table of derivatives of the model with respect to the parameters. Changing a data point by δy applies a force Xᵀδy/σ² to the parameters, and they respond in proportion to their covariance C.

A parameter with a large error bar is easily moved by one data point.

Worked example: nudging one data point

Take the straight-line fit of the curve-fitting chapter: 20 points, noise 0.3, best fit intercept 0.773 ± 0.067 and slope 0.724 ± 0.110. Raise the last data point, at x = 1, by one noise standard deviation, 0.3.

The force on (intercept, slope) is (1, x) × 0.3 / 0.3² = (3.33, 3.33). The two parameters are uncorrelated here, so the predicted shifts are the variances times the force:

intercept: 0.067² × 3.33 = 0.0150     slope: 0.110² × 3.33 = 0.0406

Refitting with the moved point gives shifts of 0.0150 and 0.0406.

Result. The response to a nudge equals the fluctuation, to all the digits shown. The slope moves by 0.37 of its error bar and the intercept by 0.22: the less well determined parameter is the easier one to push.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
x = np.linspace(-1, 1, 20)
y = np.array([-0.0578, -0.4708, 0.2324, 0.0531, 0.1977, 0.7224, 0.7013, 0.6845, 1.3723, 0.2087,
              0.9230, 0.9663, 1.6518, 1.0614, 1.3489, 1.0267, 1.7997, 1.3545, 0.4366, 1.2630])
sigma, tau = 0.3, 2.0            # noise per point, prior width of each coefficient
X = np.vander(x, 2, increasing=True)             # columns 1, x
K = X.T @ X / sigma**2 + np.eye(2) / tau**2      # stiffness = Fisher + prior
C = np.linalg.inv(K)                             # covariance
fit = lambda yy: C @ X.T @ yy / sigma**2
y2 = y.copy(); y2[-1] += 0.3                     # raise the last point by one sigma
print("best fit", fit(y).round(3), " error bars", np.sqrt(np.diag(C)).round(3))
print("shift from refitting       ", (fit(y2) - fit(y)).round(4))
print("covariance times the force ", (C @ X[-1] * 0.3 / sigma**2).round(4))

Output

best fit [0.773 0.724]  error bars [0.067 0.11 ]
shift from refitting        [0.015  0.0406]
covariance times the force  [0.015  0.0406]

This is why error propagation works, and why an outlier does the most damage to the worst-constrained parameter.

The best any measurement can do

The Cramér-Rao bound says that no unbiased estimator can have a variance smaller than the inverse of the Fisher information:

Var(θ) ≥ 1 / F F: the Fisher information, the average curvature of minus the log-likelihood

In the spring picture this says that a particle in a well cannot fluctuate less than the stiffness and the temperature allow. Physics has the same statement under the name of thermodynamic uncertainty relations, and a close relative in quantum mechanics: the bound on how precisely a parameter can be estimated from a quantum state is set by the fluctuations of the operator that generates changes in that parameter.

When the dictionary stops being simple

Everything above assumed a quadratic energy. That is true exactly for linear models with Gaussian noise, and approximately whenever the data are informative enough for the posterior to sit in the bottom of its well. When the signal is weak the well is not a parabola. It can be lopsided, or have two minima. The Fisher matrix still gives the curvature at the bottom, but the fluctuations are no longer described by it, and the particle has to be followed with a sampler. Physics calls this the anharmonic regime, and the corrections to the quadratic answer are the loop corrections of the path-integral chapter.

More worked examples

Worked example: two parameters and a soft mode

Two parameters have the stiffness (Fisher) matrix with 10 on the diagonal and 8 off it. Its normal modes are the sum and the difference of the parameters, with stiffness 18 and 2.

stiff mode: sd = 1/√18 = 0.236      soft mode: sd = 1/√2 = 0.707

Inverting the matrix gives each parameter a variance of 0.278, so an error bar of 0.527, with correlation −0.8. If the other parameter were known exactly, the error bar would be 1/√10 = 0.316.

Result. The error bar on each parameter alone, 0.527, is dominated by the soft mode. Fixing the other parameter removes the soft direction and gives 0.316. The gap between the two numbers, marginal against conditional, is the cost of the degeneracy. The data determine the sum three times better than the difference.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
K = np.array([[10., 8.], [8., 10.]])             # stiffness (Fisher) matrix
k, modes = np.linalg.eigh(K)
C = np.linalg.inv(K)
print("mode stiffnesses", k, " mode sds", (1/np.sqrt(k)).round(3))
print("marginal sd %.3f, correlation %.1f, conditional sd %.3f" % (np.sqrt(C[0,0]), C[0,1]/C[0,0], 1/np.sqrt(K[0,0])))

Output

mode stiffnesses [ 2. 18.]  mode sds [0.707 0.236]
marginal sd 0.527, correlation -0.8, conditional sd 0.316

Worked example: shot noise

A radioactive source is observed for T = 100 seconds and 400 decays are counted. The estimated rate is 4.0 per second. For a Poisson process the Fisher information about the rate λ is T/λ = 25.

Result. The error bar is 1/√25 = 0.2 per second, which is √400/100. The relative error is 1/√N = 5 per cent. This is the 1/√N law of statistical mechanics in its plainest form: a count of N independent events fluctuates by √N.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
N, T = 400, 100.0
rate = N / T
fisher = T / rate
print("rate %.1f per s, Fisher information %.0f, error %.2f, relative error %.0f%%" % (rate, fisher, fisher**-0.5, 100/np.sqrt(N)))

Output

rate 4.0 per s, Fisher information 25, error 0.20, relative error 5%

Questions people ask

Is the error bar a property of the data or of my knowledge? In the Bayesian reading used here it is a property of your knowledge: the width of the distribution of parameter values compatible with the data. The spring picture describes that distribution. The particle does not exist; its jiggling stands for the range of values you cannot rule out.

Why is the temperature 1? Because the noise level is already inside the likelihood. A Gaussian likelihood divides the squared misfit by σ², so a noisier experiment is a softer spring at fixed temperature. One could equally say the spring is fixed and the temperature is σ². Both descriptions give the same distribution.

When does the Fisher matrix give the wrong error bar? When the well is not a parabola over the range the particle explores. That happens with weak signals, with parameters near a boundary, with strong degeneracies that bend, and with several separate minima. The Fisher matrix describes only the bottom of one well.

Does the Cramér-Rao bound limit Bayesian posteriors? Not directly. It is a statement about unbiased estimators over repeated experiments. A posterior with an informative prior can be narrower than the bound, because the prior adds stiffness. With a flat prior and plenty of data the posterior width and the bound agree.

What to remember

  • An error bar is the size of the thermal fluctuation of the parameters in the energy well made by the data. The Fisher matrix is the stiffness and the covariance is its inverse.
  • The rule Δχ² = 1 for a one-sigma error is equipartition: half a unit of energy per parameter.
  • How far a data point moves the fit equals how much the parameter fluctuates. Poorly determined parameters are the easiest to push.
  • The Cramér-Rao bound is the statement that fluctuations cannot be smaller than the stiffness allows.
← 3. Path integrals5. Cumulants and correlators →

Chapter 5 of 39

Cumulants and correlation functions: describing what is not Gaussian

A Gaussian is fixed by its mean and its variance. Everything else about a distribution is a statement of how it differs from a Gaussian, and statistics and field theory keep those differences in the same ledger.

physics

The connection. Cumulants are connected correlation functions. Both come from differentiating the logarithm of a partition function, and both vanish beyond second order for a Gaussian, which is a free theory.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Cumulant generating function K(t) = ln⟨etx⟩Generating functional W[J] = ln Z[J]
CumulantsConnected correlation functions
Gaussian distributionFree field theory (Wick's theorem)
Skewness, kurtosis; bispectrumThree- and four-point vertices
Edgeworth expansionPerturbation theory in the interaction

Background: two sets of books

Thiele introduced cumulants in 1889 as the natural quantities for describing a distribution: the first is the mean, the second the variance, the third measures lopsidedness and the fourth the weight of the tails. Their defining property is that the cumulants of a sum of independent variables add. Edgeworth, in the early 1900s, used them to write corrections to the Gaussian approximation.

Field theory developed the same quantities for a different reason. The correlation functions of a field, the averages of products of the field at several points, contain pieces that are merely products of lower-order ones. Removing those leaves the connected correlation functions, which measure real interactions. Wick's theorem of 1950 states that for a free field all connected functions beyond the second vanish. Connected correlation functions are cumulants, and a free field is a Gaussian.

In cosmology the two vocabularies are used together. The three-point function of the galaxy distribution, or its Fourier transform the bispectrum, is a third cumulant of a field. It is zero for Gaussian initial conditions evolving linearly, and its measured value is a probe of gravity and of the physics of inflation.

The generating function

Both subjects define the quantities through a generating function. Take the logarithm of the average of an exponential and differentiate.

physics

Connected correlation functions

Generating functional. W[J] = ln Z[J],   Z[J] = ∫ 𝒟φ exp(−S[φ] + ∫ Jφ)

J(x): a source coupled to the field. S: the action.

Connected functions. ⟨φ(x1) … φ(xn)⟩c = δnW / δJ(x1) … δJ(xn) at J = 0.

Free theory. W is quadratic in J, so only the two-point function, the propagator, survives. Interactions generate the higher ones, and each is a sum of connected diagrams.

inference

Cumulants

Generating function. K(t) = ln ⟨etx⟩

t: a dummy variable in the role of the source. The average is over the distribution of x.

Cumulants. κn = dnK / dtn at t = 0. κ1 is the mean, κ2 the variance, κ3/κ23/2 the skewness, κ4/κ2² the excess kurtosis.

Gaussian. K is quadratic in t, so every cumulant beyond the second is zero. Non-zero higher cumulants are the measure of non-Gaussianity.

The posterior has the same structure once more. With the data in the role of a source, ln Z generates the cumulants of the posterior: its first derivative with respect to a linear shift of the data is the posterior mean and its second the posterior covariance. The amortisation chapter uses this.

Worked example: counting statistics

For a Poisson count with mean λ the generating function is K(t) = λ(et − 1). Every derivative at zero equals λ, so all cumulants are equal to the mean. For λ = 4:

mean 4    variance 4    skewness 4/43/2 = 0.5    excess kurtosis 4/4² = 0.25
Result. A count of 4 is noticeably non-Gaussian. The skewness falls as 1/√λ and the kurtosis as 1/λ, so at λ = 100 they are 0.1 and 0.01. This is the central limit theorem seen through cumulants: adding independent events adds every cumulant, and the higher ones become negligible relative to the right power of the variance.
Show the code 
from scipy import stats
for lam in (4, 100):
    m, v, s, k = stats.poisson(lam).stats(moments="mvsk")
    print("mean %g: variance %g, skewness %.2f, excess kurtosis %.2f" % (m, v, s, k))

Output

mean 4: variance 4, skewness 0.50, excess kurtosis 0.25
mean 100: variance 100, skewness 0.10, excess kurtosis 0.01

Worked example: an exponential waiting time

For an exponential distribution with mean 1, ⟨etx⟩ = 1/(1 − t), so K(t) = −ln(1 − t) = t + t²/2 + t³/3 + … The nth cumulant is (n − 1)!.

Result. The cumulants are 1, 1, 2, 6: skewness 2 and excess kurtosis 6. An exponential is far from Gaussian. The sum of ten such waiting times has skewness 2/√10 = 0.63, still enough to matter in the tails, as the next example shows.
Show the code 
import numpy as np, math
from scipy import stats
print("cumulants (n-1)!:", [math.factorial(n - 1) for n in (1, 2, 3, 4)])
m, v, s, k = stats.expon().stats(moments="mvsk")
print("skewness %.0f, excess kurtosis %.0f; sum of ten: skewness %.2f" % (s, k, s / np.sqrt(10)))

Output

cumulants (n-1)!: [1, 1, 2, 6]
skewness 2, excess kurtosis 6; sum of ten: skewness 0.63

Worked example: correcting a tail probability

Ten independent waiting times, each with mean 1, are added. What is the chance that the total exceeds 18? The total has mean 10 and standard deviation √10, so 18 is 2.53 standard deviations out. A Gaussian gives 0.0057. Edgeworth's first correction adds a term proportional to the skewness: the Gaussian density at 2.53, times 0.63/6, times (2.53² − 1).

Result. The corrected estimate is 0.0150 and the exact answer, from the gamma distribution, is 0.0154. The Gaussian was wrong by a factor of 2.7, and one term of the cumulant expansion removed nearly all of the error. In field-theory language the Gaussian is the free theory and the skewness term is the first diagram with a three-point vertex.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
n, x = 10, 18.0
z = (x - n) / np.sqrt(n)
skew = 2 / np.sqrt(n)
gauss = stats.norm.sf(z)
edgeworth = gauss + stats.norm.pdf(z) * skew / 6 * (z**2 - 1)
exact = stats.gamma(n).sf(x)
print("z = %.2f   Gaussian %.4f   Edgeworth %.4f   exact %.4f   exact/Gaussian %.1f" % (z, gauss, edgeworth, exact, exact / gauss))

Output

z = 2.53   Gaussian 0.0057   Edgeworth 0.0150   exact 0.0154   exact/Gaussian 2.7

Worked example: a weak quartic interaction

Take the distribution proportional to exp(−x²/2 − g x⁴). At g = 0 it is a unit Gaussian. To first order in g, perturbation theory gives a variance of 1 − 12g and a connected four-point function of −24g. The table compares with the exact integrals.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import integrate
for g in (0.001, 0.01):
    mom = lambda n: integrate.quad(lambda x: x**n * np.exp(-x**2/2 - g*x**4), -14, 14)[0]
    var = mom(2) / mom(0)
    k4 = mom(4) / mom(0) - 3 * var**2            # connected four-point function
    print("g = %g: variance %.4f (first order %.4f), fourth cumulant %.4f (first order %.4f)" % (g, var, 1 - 12*g, k4, -24*g))

Output

g = 0.001: variance 0.9884 (first order 0.9880), fourth cumulant -0.0221 (first order -0.0240)
g = 0.01: variance 0.9065 (first order 0.8800), fourth cumulant -0.1288 (first order -0.2400)
Coupling gVariance: first orderexactFourth cumulant: first orderexact
0.0010.98800.9884−0.0240−0.0221
0.010.8800.907−0.240−0.129
Result. At g = 0.001 first-order theory is good to a few per cent. At g = 0.01 it overestimates the fourth cumulant by almost a factor of two. The quartic term narrows the distribution and makes its tails lighter, and the perturbation series, which is only asymptotic, stops being useful sooner than the smallness of g suggests. The same is true of corrections to a Laplace approximation of a posterior.

Questions people ask

Why cumulants and not moments? Moments mix information. The fourth moment of a Gaussian is three times the square of its variance, which says nothing new. The fourth cumulant subtracts that part and is zero. Cumulants isolate what each order adds, as connected diagrams isolate what each interaction adds.

Is a distribution fixed by its cumulants? Usually, but not always, and a finite number of them never fixes it. A distribution with small third and fourth cumulants can still be far from Gaussian in ways those two numbers miss.

Why does the power spectrum not capture everything in a map? The power spectrum is the two-point function. It is a complete description only of a Gaussian field. The cosmic web has the same kind of power spectrum as a Gaussian field with randomised phases and looks nothing like it. The difference is in the higher connected functions, and extracting it is one of the main uses of SBI in cosmology.

Where do neural summaries fit in? A network trained to compress a map can learn combinations of all orders at once, without anyone choosing to measure a bispectrum or a trispectrum. It is an automatic, and less interpretable, version of the expansion in this chapter.

What to remember

  • Cumulants are connected correlation functions. Both are generated by the logarithm of a partition function.
  • A Gaussian distribution is a free theory: nothing beyond the second cumulant. Higher cumulants are the signature of interactions.
  • The Edgeworth expansion corrects a Gaussian approximation term by term, as perturbation theory corrects a free theory, and has the same limits.
  • Information beyond the power spectrum lives in the higher connected functions.
← 4. Error bars6. Maximum entropy →

Chapter 6 of 39

Maximum entropy: where distributions and priors come from

Every chapter so far has started from a prior and a noise model as if they were given. Where do they come from? One principle supplies both, and it is the same principle that gives statistical mechanics its Boltzmann factor.

physics

The connection. Jaynes showed that statistical mechanics is maximum-entropy inference. The Boltzmann distribution is what you get from knowing only the average energy.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Least committed distribution given what is knownEquilibrium state: maximum entropy at fixed constraints
A constraint on an averageFixed mean energy, or mean particle number
Lagrange multiplierInverse temperature β, or chemical potential
Exponential familyGibbs distribution exp(−βE)/Z

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: entropy as a measure of ignorance

Boltzmann, in 1877, connected the entropy of a gas to the number of microscopic arrangements compatible with what is seen macroscopically. Gibbs generalised it to a formula in terms of probabilities. In 1948 Shannon, working on telephone signals, asked for a measure of the uncertainty in a message source and arrived at the same formula. The story goes that von Neumann advised him to call it entropy, since the formula already had that name.

Jaynes joined the two in 1957. Suppose you know only the average energy of a system. Which probability distribution over its states should you use? He proposed the one with the largest Shannon entropy among all those with the right average, on the grounds that any other choice claims knowledge you do not have. The answer is the Boltzmann distribution. Temperature appears as the multiplier that enforces the constraint. The whole of equilibrium statistical mechanics follows from that one rule.

For data analysis the principle answers a practical question that is otherwise awkward: where do priors and noise models come from? A Gaussian noise model is not a claim that nature is Gaussian. It is the least committed distribution for an error of which only the typical size is known.

The idea in plain words

Suppose all you know about a die is that its average roll, over many throws, is 4.5 instead of the 3.5 of a fair die. What probabilities should you assign to the six faces? Many assignments have that average. The principle of maximum entropy says: choose the one that assumes nothing else. Among all distributions that agree with what you know, pick the most spread-out one, as measured by the entropy

S = −Σi pi ln pi

Entropy is largest when the probabilities are equal and zero when one outcome is certain. Maximising it subject to your constraints gives the least committed answer.

Worked example: the loaded die

Maximise S subject to Σpi = 1 and Σ i pi = 4.5. Introduce a Lagrange multiplier λ for the average. Setting the derivative with respect to each pi to zero gives

pi = eλ i / Z,    Z = Σi eλ i

The multiplier is fixed by requiring the average to come out right, which gives λ = 0.371:

Face123456
Probability0.0540.0790.1140.1650.2400.347
Result. The probabilities rise by the same factor, e0.371 = 1.45, from each face to the next. That is the Boltzmann distribution, with the face value as the energy and −λ as the inverse temperature. Nothing about dice was used: an exponential in the constrained quantity is what "know the average and nothing else" always produces.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import brentq
faces = np.arange(1, 7)
mean_of = lambda lam: (faces * np.exp(lam * faces)).sum() / np.exp(lam * faces).sum()
lam = brentq(lambda l: mean_of(l) - 4.5, 0, 2)   # fix the multiplier by the constraint on the mean
p = np.exp(lam * faces); p /= p.sum()
print("lambda = %.3f, ratio between faces %.2f" % (lam, np.exp(lam)))
print("probabilities", p.round(3), " mean %.2f" % (p @ faces))

Output

lambda = 0.371, ratio between faces 1.45
probabilities [0.054 0.079 0.114 0.165 0.24  0.347]  mean 4.50

The familiar distributions, each from one constraint

What you knowMaximum-entropy distributionPhysics counterpart
Only the rangeUniformMicrocanonical ensemble: all allowed states equally likely
The mean of a positive quantityExponentialBoltzmann factor for the energy
The mean and the varianceGaussianMaxwell distribution of one velocity component
The mean of several quantitiesexp(Σ λk sk)/ZGrand canonical ensemble; each multiplier is a conjugate variable

This is why Gaussian noise is the default assumption. If all you trust about an instrument's noise is its size, the Gaussian is the model that adds no further claim. It is also why the Boltzmann-form models of the summaries chapter have sufficient statistics: the constrained quantities are the sufficient statistics.

Priors that do not depend on your units

A "flat" prior sounds neutral, but flat in what? A prior flat in a length L is not flat in the area L². A rule is needed that gives the same state of knowledge whatever variable you use.

Worked example: the prior for a noise level

You want a prior for the width σ of a Gaussian, knowing nothing of its scale. Jeffreys' rule takes the prior proportional to the square root of the Fisher information. For σ the Fisher information is 2/σ², so

p(σ) ∝ 1/σ    (flat in ln σ)

Change variable to the variance v = σ². Then dσ/σ = dv/2v, so the prior is ∝ 1/v: the same form.

Result. The prior is unchanged by a change of variable, and by a change of units. For a location parameter the same rule gives a flat prior. These are the two priors used in the hyperparameters chapter.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
for sigma in (0.5, 2.0):
    x = rng.normal(0, sigma, 2_000_000)
    score = -1 / sigma + x**2 / sigma**3         # derivative of ln N(x; 0, sigma) with respect to sigma
    print("sigma = %.1f: Fisher information %.3f, 2/sigma^2 = %.3f" % (sigma, (score**2).mean(), 2 / sigma**2))
# Jeffreys prior = sqrt(Fisher) = sqrt(2)/sigma. In terms of v = sigma^2 it is (1/sigma) dsigma/dv = 1/(2v): the same form.

Output

sigma = 0.5: Fisher information 8.007, 2/sigma^2 = 8.000
sigma = 2.0: Fisher information 0.501, 2/sigma^2 = 0.500

More worked examples

Worked example: a lifetime with a known mean

All that is known about a decay time is that it is positive and that its mean is 2 microseconds. Maximising the entropy subject to that mean gives the exponential distribution p(t) = e−t/2/2. Its entropy is 1 + ln 2 = 1.693. Compare a rival with the same mean: a uniform distribution between 0 and 4, with entropy ln 4 = 1.386.

Result. The exponential has the higher entropy, as it must. Choosing the uniform distribution would be claiming to know that no decay takes longer than 4 microseconds, which was not given. The exponential law of radioactive decay is what you get from knowing only the mean.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
print("entropy of the exponential with mean 2: %.3f" % stats.expon(scale=2).entropy())
print("entropy of the uniform on [0, 4]:       %.3f" % stats.uniform(0, 4).entropy())

Output

entropy of the exponential with mean 2: 1.693
entropy of the uniform on [0, 4]:       1.386

Worked example: temperature from a mean energy

An atom has two levels, with energies 0 and ε. Its mean energy is measured to be 0.25 ε, so the upper level is occupied a quarter of the time. The maximum-entropy distribution with that mean is the Boltzmann distribution, and the ratio of the two occupations fixes the multiplier:

e−βε = 0.25 / 0.75 = 1/3   ⇒   βε = ln 3 = 1.099
Result. The temperature is kBT = ε/ln 3 = 0.91 ε. Temperature was not put in. It came out as the Lagrange multiplier of the constraint on the mean energy. This is Jaynes's point in one line.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
p_upper = 0.25                                   # mean energy / level spacing
beta_eps = np.log((1 - p_upper) / p_upper)
print("beta * eps = %.3f,  kT = %.2f eps" % (beta_eps, 1 / beta_eps))

Output

beta * eps = 1.099,  kT = 0.91 eps

Questions people ask

Is maximum entropy a law of nature or a rule of reasoning? Jaynes said a rule of reasoning. A system does not know about entropy. But if the constraints you imposed are the only things that matter, the overwhelming majority of microscopic states compatible with them look alike, so predictions made from the maximum-entropy distribution come out right. When they come out wrong, it means a relevant constraint was missed, and that is how new physics is found.

Does it give a unique prior? Only once you say what is known and what you are ignorant with respect to. A constraint on the mean gives an exponential, and a constraint on the variance gives a Gaussian. Entropy for a continuous variable also needs a reference measure, which is where arguments about invariance under changes of units come in.

How does this relate to SBI? In two places. The noise and prior choices inside a simulator are usually maximum-entropy choices, made knowingly or not. And the best summary statistics of a model are the quantities whose averages define it as a maximum-entropy distribution, a point the summaries chapter takes up.

constructionStatistical mechanics as inference, and the prior as a volume element

In 1957 Jaynes proposed reading statistical mechanics backwards. The usual account derives the Boltzmann distribution from dynamics and ergodicity. Jaynes derived it as above: it is the least-biased inference about microstates given only the macroscopic averages. On this reading temperature is a Lagrange multiplier, the partition function is the normalisation that the multipliers must satisfy, and thermodynamic entropy is the maximised information entropy. Statistical mechanics is then not merely like inference. It is inference, applied to systems with very many degrees of freedom.

The Legendre structure of thermodynamics follows at once. Since ln Z is a function of the multipliers and S a function of the constrained averages,

S = ln Z − Σk λk ⟨sk⟩,    ∂ ln Z/∂λk = ⟨sk⟩,    ∂S/∂⟨sk⟩ = −λk

the same pair of relations as between free energy and entropy, with the same convexity. Every maximum-entropy model carries a small thermodynamics.

The Jeffreys prior is a volume element. The Fisher information is a metric on the space of distributions (summaries chapter). In a curved space, "uniform" means uniform with respect to the volume element √det g, which is what makes an integral independent of the coordinates chosen. Jeffreys' rule, prior ∝ √det(Fisher matrix), is that volume element. Reparametrisation invariance of a prior is general covariance, and the prior is to parameter space what √g d4x is to spacetime.

← 5. Cumulants and correlators7. Information →

Chapter 7 of 39

Information: how much a measurement tells you

Before running an experiment it is fair to ask how much it could teach you. Entropy turns that question into a number, and the number is the same entropy that appears in thermodynamics.

physics

The connection. Shannon's entropy and the entropy of Gibbs are one formula. Information gained is entropy removed.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Entropy of a distribution, −Σ p ln pGibbs entropy S/kB
Information gained from data (relative entropy)Free-energy difference from equilibrium, in units of kBT
Mutual information between parameters and dataCorrelation entropy between two coupled subsystems
Erasing one bitLandauer's bound: heat kBT ln 2

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: information as a physical quantity

Shannon's 1948 paper defined information as the reduction of uncertainty and measured it in bits. Kullback and Leibler, in 1951, defined the relative entropy between two distributions, which measures how much one must be changed to become the other. In 1956 Lindley used these ideas to answer a statistician's question: before doing an experiment, how much can it be expected to teach us? His answer, the expected gain in information from prior to posterior, is still the basis of Bayesian experimental design.

Physics had been circling the same idea since 1867, when Maxwell imagined a demon that sorts fast molecules from slow ones and so appears to violate the second law. Szilard, in 1929, argued that the demon must pay for the information it gathers. Landauer, in 1961, located the cost precisely: erasing one bit of memory must release at least kBT ln 2 of heat. Experiments on single colloidal particles confirmed the bound in 2012. Information is therefore not only a way of counting. It is a quantity with thermodynamic consequences.

Counting what you do not know

The entropy of a distribution measures how spread out it is. For a Gaussian of width σ:

S = ½ ln( 2π e σ² )

Halve the width and the entropy falls by ln 2, one bit. A measurement reduces your uncertainty from the prior's entropy to the posterior's, and the reduction is the information gained. It is measured in nats (natural logarithm) or bits (logarithm to base 2), with 1 nat = 1.44 bits.

Worked example: one measurement, then many

Prior N(0, τ²), one measurement with noise s. The posterior variance is τ²s²/(τ² + s²), whatever value is observed, so the information gained is

I = Sprior − Sposterior = ½ ln( 1 + τ²/s² )

With τ = 2 and s = 0.5 this is ½ ln 17 = 1.42 nats, or 2.0 bits: the measurement locates the parameter within a quarter of its prior range. For n measurements of noise σ = 1 each:

Measurements nInformation (nats)Information (bits)
10.801.2
41.422.0
162.093.0
642.774.0
Result. Four times the data buys one more bit. Information grows as the logarithm of the amount of data, because error bars shrink only as 1/√n. The first measurements are worth far more than the last.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
tau = 2.0
print("tau = 2, s = 0.5: %.2f nats = %.1f bits" % (0.5 * np.log(1 + tau**2 / 0.5**2), 0.5 * np.log2(1 + tau**2 / 0.5**2)))
for n in (1, 4, 16, 64):                         # n measurements of noise 1: variance of the mean is 1/n
    nats = 0.5 * np.log(1 + tau**2 * n)
    print("n = %2d: %.2f nats, %.1f bits" % (n, nats, nats / np.log(2)))

Output

tau = 2, s = 0.5: 1.42 nats = 2.0 bits
n =  1: 0.80 nats, 1.2 bits
n =  4: 1.42 nats, 2.0 bits
n = 16: 2.09 nats, 3.0 bits
n = 64: 2.77 nats, 4.0 bits

The number 1.42 has appeared before. It is the Occam penalty of the model comparison chapter, ½ ln(1 + τ²/s²). That is no accident: the penalty a model pays for a free parameter is the information the data had to supply to pin that parameter down.

Choosing the experiment

If different experiments are possible, do the one expected to teach the most. For a straight-line model y = θ x + noise, a measurement at position x gains

I(x) = ½ ln( 1 + τ² x² / σ² )

which grows with |x|. To learn a slope, measure far from the origin, where the slope has the most leverage. The general rule is the same: measure where the predictions of the plausible parameter values differ most. This is Bayesian experimental design, and the quantity being maximised is the mutual information between the parameter and the data.

In SBI

For a simulator there is no formula for the information, but there is a route to it. The log-ratio learned by NRE (neural methods) is ln[p(x | θ)/p(x)], and its average over simulated pairs is exactly the mutual information. So a ratio estimator trained for one proposed experiment reports how informative that experiment would be, before any real data are taken.

More worked examples

Worked example: how many bits is a measurement worth?

A quantity has a Gaussian prior of standard deviation 10. It is measured once with noise of standard deviation 1. The expected information gained is the mutual information between the quantity and the reading, ½ ln(1 + 10²/1²).

Result. ½ ln 101 = 2.31 nats, or 3.33 bits. The measurement narrows the range by a factor of about ten, and log2 10 = 3.32. One bit halves the uncertainty.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
prior_sd, noise_sd = 10.0, 1.0
nats = 0.5 * np.log(1 + prior_sd**2 / noise_sd**2)
print("%.2f nats = %.2f bits;  log2(10) = %.2f" % (nats, nats / np.log(2), np.log2(10)))

Output

2.31 nats = 3.33 bits;  log2(10) = 3.32

Worked example: where to put the next data point

A straight line through the origin has an unknown slope with prior standard deviation 2. One measurement can be made, with noise 0.3, at a position x of your choice between 0 and 1. The expected information about the slope is ½ ln(1 + x² × 2² / 0.3²).

x = 0:  0 nats     x = 0.5:  1.25 nats     x = 1:  1.91 nats
Result. Measure at the far end. A point at the origin says nothing about the slope, however precise it is. This is Bayesian experimental design: choose the experiment whose outcome is expected to remove the most entropy.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
prior_sd, noise_sd = 2.0, 0.3
for x in (0.0, 0.5, 1.0):
    print("x = %.1f: expected information %.2f nats" % (x, 0.5 * np.log(1 + (x * prior_sd / noise_sd)**2)))

Output

x = 0.0: expected information 0.00 nats
x = 0.5: expected information 1.25 nats
x = 1.0: expected information 1.91 nats

Worked example: the heat of forgetting

Landauer's bound says that erasing one bit releases at least kBT ln 2 of heat. At room temperature, 300 K, that is 1.38 × 10−23 × 300 × 0.693.

Result. 2.9 × 10−21 joules per bit. Present-day computers dissipate many orders of magnitude more than this for each operation, so the bound is far from being reached in practice. It matters as a principle: information has a minimum thermodynamic price.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
kB, T = 1.380649e-23, 300.0
print("kT ln 2 = %.2e joules per bit" % (kB * T * np.log(2)))

Output

kT ln 2 = 2.87e-21 joules per bit

Questions people ask

Is Shannon entropy the same thing as thermodynamic entropy? It is the same formula, and for a system in equilibrium the same number once multiplied by Boltzmann's constant. The difference is in what the probabilities describe. Thermodynamic entropy uses the probabilities of microstates given the macroscopic variables. Shannon entropy can be computed for any distribution, including a posterior over parameters.

What does the relative entropy between prior and posterior mean? It is the information the data provided, in nats or bits. If the posterior is ten times narrower than the prior in one parameter, the gain is about ln 10 nats. In physical terms it is the free energy, in units of kBT, that could be extracted from a system prepared in the posterior state when it relaxes to the prior.

Why does mutual information appear in the training of neural estimators? Because the losses used for neural posterior and ratio estimation are bounds on the mutual information between parameters and data. A network that minimises them is extracting as much information about the parameters as its capacity allows, which is also the definition of a good summary statistic.

constructionInformation is physical

The entropy of this chapter and the entropy of thermodynamics are the same quantity, up to Boltzmann's constant, and the identification has measurable consequences.

Landauer's bound. Erasing one bit of information in a system at temperature T must release at least kT ln 2 of heat. At room temperature that is 2.9 × 10−21 joules. It has been confirmed experimentally with single colloidal particles.

Maxwell's demon and the Szilard engine. A demon that knows which half of a box a molecule is in can extract kT ln 2 of work by letting it expand. One bit of knowledge is worth kT ln 2 of free energy. The second law survives because the demon must eventually erase its record, paying Landauer's price.

Inference as a thermodynamic process. A measurement that supplies I nats lowers the entropy of your description of the system by I. Written in the language of the free-energy chapter, the expected information gain is the expected Kullback–Leibler divergence from prior to posterior:

I(θ; x) = ⟨ KL( posterior ‖ prior ) ⟩x = ⟨ Fprior − Fposterior ⟩

the average drop in free energy on conditioning on the data. Designing an experiment to maximise information is designing it to extract the most free energy from the measurement.

Mutual information is a correlation function. For two Gaussian variables with correlation coefficient ρ, the mutual information is −½ ln(1 − ρ²). It counts all the dependence between two parts of a system, linear or not, which is why it is used to locate phase transitions and to measure entanglement, and why it is the natural measure of what data say about parameters.

← 6. Maximum entropy8. Information geometry →

Chapter 8 of 39

Information geometry: the Fisher matrix is a metric

How different are two probability distributions? The Fisher matrix answers by turning the space of models into a curved surface with distances on it. Thermodynamics has the same surface.

physics

The connection. The Fisher matrix is a metric on the space of models. Thermodynamics has the same metric on its space of states, built from second derivatives of the entropy.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Fisher information metricRuppeiner and Weinhold metrics, −∂²S/∂xi∂xj
Distance: the number of error bars between two modelsThe number of fluctuations between two states
Jeffreys prior, √det gVolume element of the space of states
Singular Fisher matrixDiverging curvature at a critical point
Natural gradientSteepest descent in the thermodynamic metric

Background: distance between distributions

In 1945 Rao observed that the Fisher information matrix has the properties of a metric tensor: it defines a distance between neighbouring probability distributions that does not depend on how they are parametrised. Jeffreys used the same matrix in 1946 to construct a prior that is invariant under changes of parameters. Amari developed the subject into information geometry from the 1980s, and his natural gradient, proposed in 1998, is used in training neural networks.

In thermodynamics, Weinhold in 1975 and Ruppeiner in 1979 put a metric on the space of equilibrium states, built from second derivatives of the energy or the entropy. Distance in this geometry measures how many fluctuations separate two states. The curvature of the space is zero for an ideal gas, grows with the strength of interactions, and diverges at a critical point. The two constructions are the same: the entropy of statistical mechanics is a log-probability, and its second derivatives are a Fisher matrix.

The metric

Take two nearby members of a family of distributions, with parameters θ and θ + dθ. The relative entropy between them is, to lowest order, a quadratic form in dθ, and the matrix of that form is the Fisher matrix.

physics

Thermodynamic geometry

Metric. gij = −∂²S / ∂xi∂xj (in units of kB)

S: the entropy. xi: the extensive variables, such as energy and volume.

Meaning. The probability of a fluctuation is P ∝ exp(−½ gij δxi δxj). Distance counts fluctuations: two states one unit apart are just distinguishable.

Curvature. Zero for an ideal gas. It diverges at a critical point, where fluctuations become correlated over all scales.

inference

Information geometry

Metric. gij = ⟨ ∂i ln p · ∂j ln p ⟩

p(x | θ): the distribution of the data. The average is over data. This is the Fisher matrix.

Meaning. KL(θ ‖ θ + dθ) = ½ gij dθi dθj. Distance counts error bars: two models one unit apart can just be told apart by the data.

Volume. √det g is the density of distinguishable models. It is the Jeffreys prior.

Worked example: the geometry of the Gaussian family

For a Gaussian with mean μ and standard deviation σ, and one data point, the Fisher metric is

ds² = (dμ² + 2 dσ²) / σ²

This is the metric of the hyperbolic plane. Distances have a closed form. Compare three moves, each starting from the standard Gaussian: shift the mean by 1, double the width, and shift the mean by 1 for a Gaussian of width 0.1.

Result. Shifting the mean of a unit Gaussian by one standard deviation is a distance of 0.98. Doubling its width is also 0.98. Shifting the mean by 1 when the width is 0.1, which is ten standard deviations, is a distance of 5.6 and not 10, because the shortest path goes through wider Gaussians, where a shift in the mean costs less. The space of Gaussians is curved, and straight lines in the parameters are not the shortest routes.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
def distance(mu1, s1, mu2, s2):                  # Fisher-Rao distance between two Gaussians
    return np.sqrt(2) * np.arccosh(1 + ((mu1 - mu2)**2 / 2 + (s1 - s2)**2) / (2 * s1 * s2))
print("shift the mean by 1, width 1:   %.2f" % distance(0, 1, 1, 1))
print("double the width:               %.2f" % distance(0, 1, 0, 2))
print("shift the mean by 1, width 0.1: %.1f" % distance(0, 0.1, 1, 0.1))

Output

shift the mean by 1, width 1:   0.98
double the width:               0.98
shift the mean by 1, width 0.1: 5.6

Worked example: a prior that does not depend on the parametrisation

A coin has probability p of heads. The Fisher information for one toss is 1/[p(1 − p)], so the Jeffreys prior is proportional to 1/√[p(1 − p)]. It is not flat. It puts more weight near 0 and 1, where a small change in p is easier to detect. After 7 heads in 10 tosses the posterior is a beta distribution with parameters 7.5 and 3.5.

Result. The posterior mean is 0.682 ± 0.134. A flat prior gives 0.667 ± 0.131. The difference is small here and vanishes with more data. What the Jeffreys prior buys is consistency: someone who describes the coin by its odds, or by the log of its odds, and follows the same rule, reaches the same posterior. A prior that is flat in p is not flat in the log-odds.
Show the code 
from scipy import stats
heads, tails = 7, 3
jeffreys = stats.beta(heads + 0.5, tails + 0.5)
flat = stats.beta(heads + 1, tails + 1)
print("Jeffreys prior: %.3f +/- %.3f" % (jeffreys.mean(), jeffreys.std()))
print("flat prior:     %.3f +/- %.3f" % (flat.mean(), flat.std()))

Output

Jeffreys prior: 0.682 +/- 0.134
flat prior:     0.667 +/- 0.131

Worked example: how many coins can be told apart?

With n tosses the metric is n/[p(1 − p)]. The total length of the family of coins, from p = 0 to p = 1, is the integral of √n/√[p(1 − p)], which is π√n.

Result. For 100 tosses the length is 31. About 31 coins can be distinguished from one another at the level of one standard deviation, and for 10,000 tosses, 314. The number of distinguishable models grows as √n for each parameter. Its logarithm, ½ ln n, is the Occam penalty per parameter in the BIC. The penalty for a parameter is the log of the number of distinct values the data could have resolved.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import integrate
length = integrate.quad(lambda p: 1 / np.sqrt(p * (1 - p)), 0, 1)[0]     # equals pi
for n in (100, 10000):
    print("%d tosses: %.0f distinguishable coins" % (n, length * np.sqrt(n)))

Output

100 tosses: 31 distinguishable coins
10000 tosses: 314 distinguishable coins

Why this matters for training networks

Ordinary gradient descent takes a step in the parameters. The same step can be a tiny change of the distribution in one region of parameter space and a huge one in another, as the Gaussian example shows. The natural gradient corrects for this by multiplying the gradient by the inverse Fisher matrix, so that each step is a fixed distance in the space of distributions and not of parameters. In the spring picture of the error-bars chapter, it rescales each direction by its stiffness. Second-order optimisers and several methods for training normalising flows are approximations to it.

Questions people ask

Is the Fisher metric a choice or is it forced? It is essentially forced. Chentsov proved that it is the only metric on a family of distributions, up to a constant, that does not change when the data are replaced by a sufficient statistic.

What is the practical use of the distance? It is the number of standard deviations by which the data can separate two models. A forecast of whether an experiment can distinguish two theories is a calculation of this distance.

What does curvature mean for inference? Where the space is flat, the posterior is Gaussian in suitable coordinates and the Fisher forecast is reliable. Strong curvature means that no choice of parameters makes the posterior Gaussian, and it is the geometric sign of the non-Gaussian regime.

Is there a link to phase transitions? In thermodynamics the curvature diverges at a critical point. In inference the Fisher matrix becomes singular at the degeneracies discussed in the phase-transitions chapter, and the geometry breaks down in the corresponding way.

What to remember
