  • The Fisher matrix is a metric on the space of models. Distance counts how many error bars separate two of them.
  • Thermodynamics has the same metric, from second derivatives of the entropy, and there distance counts fluctuations.
  • The volume element of the metric is the Jeffreys prior, and the number of distinguishable models gives the Occam penalty.
  • The natural gradient takes steps of equal size in the space of distributions, not in the parameters.
← 7. Information9. ABC →

Chapter 9 of 39

ABC: approximate Bayesian computation

The oldest and simplest form of SBI. It needs no neural network and no formula, only a simulator and patience.

physics

The connection. Rejection ABC is a microcanonical ensemble: it keeps the states inside a thin shell around the observed data and weights them equally.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Keeping the simulations that match the dataMicrocanonical ensemble: the states on one energy shell
Tolerance εThickness of the energy shell
Fraction of simulations acceptedPhase-space volume of the shell, eS
Accepting with a smooth kernel in place of a hard cutCanonical ensemble at a finite temperature
ε → 0Recovering the exact ensemble

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: where ABC came from

Approximate Bayesian computation was invented by population geneticists in the late 1990s. They had good simulators of how genes are inherited through a family tree and no hope of writing down the probability of an observed set of DNA sequences, which would require summing over every possible tree. Tavaré and colleagues in 1997, and Pritchard and colleagues in 1999, proposed simply simulating data sets and keeping the parameter values whose simulations resembled the real data. Beaumont and colleagues gave the method its name in 2002 and added a correction that adjusts the accepted values by a regression.

The idea is older than its name. In 1984 Rubin described the same procedure as a thought experiment, to explain what a posterior distribution means: imagine drawing parameters from the prior, generating data, and keeping only the cases that reproduce what you observed. ABC turns that definition into an algorithm by relaxing "reproduce" to "come close to".

The method spread to ecology, epidemiology and cosmology during the 2000s. Its weakness, the collapse of the acceptance rate as the number of parameters or data dimensions grows, was clear from the start and is what the neural methods of later chapters were built to overcome. ABC remains the method to try first: it has almost no moving parts, so when it disagrees with something more elaborate, it is usually the elaborate method that has gone wrong.

The algorithm

  1. Draw a parameter θ from the prior.
  2. Run the simulator at that θ to get fake data x′.
  3. Keep θ if x′ is within a tolerance ε of the real data x. Otherwise discard it.
  4. Repeat. The kept values are samples from the posterior.

Why it works: the chance that a given θ is kept is the chance that it produces your data, which is the likelihood. Drawing from the prior and keeping with probability proportional to the likelihood is Bayes' rule carried out by hand. The method came from population genetics in the late 1990s, where family-tree simulators are easy to write and likelihoods are hopeless.

What the tolerance does, exactly

With ε = 0 the kept samples follow the exact posterior, but for continuous data an exact match never happens. With ε > 0 you sample from a slightly different distribution. For a Gaussian problem that difference can be worked out in full.

Worked example: ABC for a Gaussian mean

Prior θ ~ N(0, τ²). The data are summarised by the mean x̄ of n measurements, each with noise σ, so x̄ has variance s² = σ²/n. Accept a simulated mean x̄′ with probability exp[−(x̄′ − x̄)²/2ε²], a soft version of "within ε".

The simulated mean is θ plus Gaussian noise of variance s². The acceptance rule multiplies by another Gaussian of variance ε². A Gaussian convolved with a Gaussian adds variances, so:

Result 1. ABC with tolerance ε gives the exact posterior of a noisier experiment, one whose measurement variance is s² + ε² in place of s².
mean = x̄ · τ² / (τ² + s² + ε²),     variance = [ 1/τ² + 1/(s² + ε²) ]−1

The fraction of simulations kept follows from the same integral. Before seeing data, x̄′ is distributed as N(0, τ² + s²). Averaging the acceptance probability over that:

kept fraction = √[ ε² / (ε² + τ² + s²) ] · exp[ −x̄² / 2(ε² + τ² + s²) ]
Result 2. For small ε the kept fraction is proportional to ε. With d summary numbers in place of one it is proportional to εd. Halving the tolerance on ten summaries costs a factor of about a thousand in simulations.

Numbers, for τ = 2, σ = 1, n = 4 (so s² = 0.25) and x̄ = 1.2:

Tolerance εPosterior meanPosterior s.d.Kept fraction
0 (exact)1.1290.4850
0.11.1270.4944.1%
0.51.0670.66720.1%
1.00.9140.97638.1%
Show the code 
import numpy as np
tau, s2, xbar = 2.0, 0.25, 1.2                   # prior width, variance of the mean (sigma^2/n), observed mean
for eps in (0.0, 0.1, 0.5, 1.0):
    v = s2 + eps**2                              # ABC = exact posterior of a noisier experiment
    mean, sd = xbar * tau**2 / (tau**2 + v), (1 / tau**2 + 1 / v)**-0.5
    V = eps**2 + tau**2 + s2
    kept = np.sqrt(eps**2 / V) * np.exp(-xbar**2 / (2 * V))
    print("eps = %.1f: mean %.3f, sd %.3f, kept %.1f%%" % (eps, mean, sd, 100 * kept))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)                   # check eps = 0.5 by doing it
theta = rng.normal(0, tau, 2_000_000)
sim = theta + rng.normal(0, np.sqrt(s2), theta.size)
keep = rng.random(theta.size) < np.exp(-(sim - xbar)**2 / (2 * 0.5**2))
print("simulated, eps = 0.5: mean %.3f, sd %.3f, kept %.1f%%" % (theta[keep].mean(), theta[keep].std(), 100 * keep.mean()))

Output

eps = 0.0: mean 1.129, sd 0.485, kept 0.0%
eps = 0.1: mean 1.127, sd 0.494, kept 4.1%
eps = 0.5: mean 1.067, sd 0.667, kept 20.1%
eps = 1.0: mean 0.914, sd 0.976, kept 38.1%
simulated, eps = 0.5: mean 1.069, sd 0.667, kept 20.1%

Move the slider to see the trade. The physics reading: the tolerance is extra noise, and extra noise is extra temperature. A loose tolerance heats the posterior.

Exact posteriorABC posterior at this tolerancePrior
–exact: mean ± s.d.
–ABC: mean ± s.d.
–ABC error bar too wide by
–simulations kept

A physics experiment: temperature from two-level atoms

Fifty atoms each have two levels separated by an energy gap εgap. At temperature T (in units of εgap/k) each is excited with the Boltzmann probability p = 1/(1 + e1/T). You count the excited atoms and want T. The prior is flat between 0.2 and 5. Here the data are whole numbers, so an exact match is possible and a tolerance of zero works.

Kept guesses from simulationExact posterior from the binomial formula
0simulations run
0kept (0%)
–T from simulation
–T from exact formula

Things to try

  • Tolerance 0, more simulations. The histogram settles onto the exact curve. No likelihood formula was used to make it.
  • Raise the tolerance. More guesses are kept and the histogram becomes wider than the truth, as Result 1 predicts.
  • Set the count to 22. Nearly half the atoms are excited, which needs a high temperature. The posterior piles up against the top of the prior: the data give only a lower limit.
  • Watch the kept fraction. About 2.5% at zero tolerance. With a million pixels in place of one count, nothing would ever match. That waste is why the methods below exist.

Better versions of ABC

MethodIdeaPhysics counterpart
ABC-MCMCPropose a small step from the last kept θ instead of a fresh draw from the prior, so proposals stay where matches are likely.Metropolis sampling: local moves in a region of low energy.
ABC-SMCRun a population of parameter values through a sequence of shrinking tolerances, resampling the survivors at each stage.Simulated annealing: cool slowly so the system follows the distribution down.
Regression adjustmentFit a straight line of θ against the mismatch x′ − x among kept samples and slide each sample to zero mismatch.Linear response: correct a measurement for a known small offset.
Synthetic likelihoodAt each θ, simulate several data sets, fit a Gaussian to their summaries and use it as the likelihood.Gaussian fluctuations about the mean, as the central limit theorem gives for a large system.

The next step in that sequence is to replace the fitted Gaussian with a neural network that can take any shape. That is the neural methods chapter. Before that come three shorter chapters: why matching fails in many dimensions, what to compare, and how to sample.

More worked examples

Worked example: ABC with zero tolerance, done exactly

A coin is tossed 10 times and gives 7 heads. The prior on the probability of heads is flat. ABC with an exact match draws a probability, simulates 10 tosses, and keeps the draw if it gives exactly 7 heads. Averaged over a flat prior, every count from 0 to 10 is equally likely, so the chance of a match is 1/11.

Result. 9.1 per cent of simulations are kept, and the kept values follow the exact posterior, a beta distribution with mean 0.667 and standard deviation 0.131. With discrete data and a zero tolerance ABC is not approximate at all. It is the microcanonical ensemble: only states with the observed count are allowed.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
p = rng.random(1_000_000)                        # flat prior on the probability of heads
heads = rng.binomial(10, p)                      # simulate 10 tosses for each draw
kept = p[heads == 7]                             # exact match
exact = stats.beta(8, 4)
print("kept %.1f%% (1/11 = %.1f%%)" % (100 * kept.size / p.size, 100 / 11))
print("ABC: %.3f +/- %.3f    exact: %.3f +/- %.3f" % (kept.mean(), kept.std(), exact.mean(), exact.std()))

Output

kept 9.1% (1/11 = 9.1%)
ABC: 0.666 +/- 0.131    exact: 0.667 +/- 0.131

Worked example: the price of a tolerance

Now accept any simulation with 6, 7 or 8 heads. Three counts out of eleven are accepted, so the rate triples to 27 per cent. The kept values are an equal mixture of the three posteriors for 6, 7 and 8 heads. The mean is unchanged at 0.667. The variance is the average of the three variances plus the variance of their means.

Result. The standard deviation becomes 0.146 in place of 0.131, which is 12 per cent too wide. Three times as many samples were bought with a 12 per cent error in the width. That exchange, efficiency for accuracy, is the whole of ABC.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
p = rng.random(1_000_000)
heads = rng.binomial(10, p)
kept = p[np.abs(heads - 7) <= 1]                 # accept 6, 7 or 8 heads
exact_sd = stats.beta(8, 4).std()
print("kept %.1f%%, mean %.3f, sd %.3f, sd / exact = %.2f" % (100 * kept.size / p.size, kept.mean(), kept.std(), kept.std() / exact_sd))

Output

kept 27.2%, mean 0.667, sd 0.146, sd / exact = 1.12

Questions people ask

Is ABC really Bayesian? Yes, for a slightly different problem. It gives the exact posterior for data that have been blurred by the tolerance and reduced to the chosen summaries. With a zero tolerance and sufficient summaries it gives the exact posterior of the original problem. The approximation is entirely in those two choices, and both are visible.

How small should the tolerance be? Small enough that making it smaller does not change the answer, which has to be checked. In practice people keep a fixed fraction of simulations, such as the closest one per cent, and then test whether keeping the closest tenth of a per cent gives the same posterior.

Why not use the full data as the summary? Because distance in a high-dimensional space is dominated by noise. Two data sets generated with identical parameters differ in every one of their thousands of noise values, so almost nothing is ever close. The dimensionality chapter makes this quantitative.

What is the physics of the tolerance? A hard tolerance selects a shell, which is the microcanonical ensemble. Accepting with a smooth weight that falls off with distance is the canonical ensemble, and the width of the weight is the temperature. Shrinking the tolerance is cooling.

constructionHard and soft matching are two ensembles

Rejection ABC with a hard rule, "keep if the simulated data lie within ε of the real data", selects a thin shell of data space. That is the microcanonical ensemble: all states in a narrow shell of the conserved quantity, equally weighted. The soft Gaussian rule used in the worked example weights every simulation by e−d²/2ε². That is the canonical ensemble, with the mismatch d²/2 as the energy and ε² as the temperature.

Statistical mechanics proves that the two ensembles agree when the shell is thin and the system is large. The same equivalence is why the choice of ABC kernel does not matter as ε → 0. The exact statement of what ABC samples is

pε(θ | x) ∝ p(θ) ∫ Kε(x′ − x) p(x′ | θ) dx′

which is the true likelihood smeared by the kernel. In field-theory terms the kernel is a regulator: a delta function, which cannot be sampled, replaced by a narrow smooth function, with the answer defined as the limit in which the regulator is removed.

Worked example: removing the regulator by extrapolation

Lattice field theory computes at several lattice spacings a and extrapolates to a = 0, using the fact that the leading error is proportional to a². The ABC bias is proportional to ε², so the same procedure applies. From the formula earlier in this chapter, the posterior mean is 1.0667 at ε = 0.5 and 1.1130 at ε = 0.25. A straight line in ε² through those two points has intercept

1.1130 + (1.1130 − 1.0667) × 0.0625 / (0.25 − 0.0625) = 1.1285
Result. The exact value is 1.1294. Extrapolating two cheap, loose runs gives an error of 0.001, against 0.016 for the tighter run alone, with no simulation at small tolerance. This is the continuum extrapolation of lattice gauge theory applied to ABC.
Show the code 
tau, s2, xbar = 2.0, 0.25, 1.2
mean = lambda eps: xbar * tau**2 / (tau**2 + s2 + eps**2)
m1, m2 = mean(0.5), mean(0.25)
extrap = m2 + (m2 - m1) * 0.25**2 / (0.5**2 - 0.25**2)       # straight line in eps^2, read off at eps = 0
print("eps = 0.5: %.4f   eps = 0.25: %.4f   extrapolated: %.4f   exact: %.4f" % (m1, m2, extrap, mean(0)))
print("errors: tighter run alone %.3f, extrapolation %.3f" % (mean(0) - m2, mean(0) - extrap))

Output

eps = 0.5: 1.0667   eps = 0.25: 1.1130   extrapolated: 1.1285   exact: 1.1294
errors: tighter run alone 0.016, extrapolation 0.001
← 8. Information geometry10. Dimensionality →

Chapter 10 of 39

The curse of dimensionality

Rejection ABC works for one number and fails for a hundred. The reason is geometry, and it is the same geometry that makes entropy so large.

physics

The connection. The acceptance rate of ABC is a ratio of phase-space volumes, and volume is the exponential of entropy. Entropy grows with the number of dimensions, so the rate falls exponentially.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Fraction of parameter space compatible with the dataRatio of phase-space volumes
ln(acceptance rate)Entropy difference, proportional to the number of degrees of freedom
Samples concentrate in a thin shell (the typical set)Energy of a large system is sharply defined

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: the geometry of many dimensions

The phrase "curse of dimensionality" is due to Bellman, who met the problem in 1957 while studying optimal control: the number of grid points needed to cover a space grows exponentially with its dimension. The geometry behind it is older and stranger than the phrase suggests. In a space of many dimensions almost all of the volume of a ball lies in a thin skin at its surface. Almost all of the volume of a cube is in its corners. Two points chosen at random are almost always nearly the same distance apart.

Physicists live with this every day without calling it a curse. A gas of 1023 molecules has a phase space of that many dimensions, and the reason thermodynamics works is exactly the concentration that defeats ABC. Because nearly all states compatible with a given energy look alike, a few macroscopic numbers describe the system. The same fact that makes a random search hopeless makes averages predictable.

The lesson for inference is that brute force fails for a reason of principle, and that every successful method is a way of using structure: smoothness, low-dimensional summaries, or gradients.

Matching gets exponentially harder

In the Gaussian example of the ABC chapter, a tolerance of 0.5 kept about one simulation in four. Suppose the data are summarised by d independent numbers instead of one, and each must match to the same tolerance. A simulation is kept only if every one matches, so the kept fractions multiply.

Worked example: kept fraction against number of summaries

For one summary with prior-predictive variance V = τ² + s² = 4.25 and tolerance ε = 0.5, the kept fraction at the centre is √[ε²/(ε² + V)] = 0.236. For d summaries it is that number to the power d:

Summaries dKept fractionSimulations per kept sample
10.244
20.05618
57.3 × 10−41,400
105.3 × 10−71.9 million
202.8 × 10−133.6 trillion
Result. The cost of rejection grows as (√V/ε)d. Each extra summary multiplies the cost by the same factor, here about four.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
V, eps = 4.25, 0.5
f = np.sqrt(eps**2 / (eps**2 + V))
print("one summary: %.3f" % f)
for d in (1, 2, 5, 10, 20):
    print("d = %2d: kept fraction %.2g, simulations per kept sample %.3g" % (d, f**d, f**-d))

Output

one summary: 0.236
d =  1: kept fraction 0.24, simulations per kept sample 4.24
d =  2: kept fraction 0.056, simulations per kept sample 18
d =  5: kept fraction 0.00073, simulations per kept sample 1.37e+03
d = 10: kept fraction 5.3e-07, simulations per kept sample 1.89e+06
d = 20: kept fraction 2.8e-13, simulations per kept sample 3.57e+12

Where the volume goes

The underlying fact is that in many dimensions almost no volume is near any given point. Two standard results make it concrete.

  • A ball inside a box. The largest ball that fits in a unit cube fills 79% of it in two dimensions, 16% in five, 0.25% in ten and 2.5 × 10−8 in twenty. "Within a fixed distance of the data" is a ball, and the prior is the box.
  • Everything is at the surface. The fraction of a ball's volume lying within 90% of its radius is 0.9d: 35% for d = 10, and 0.003% for d = 100. A high-dimensional ball is nearly all skin.

Statistical mechanics is built on this. The number of microstates grows exponentially with the number of particles, which is why entropy is extensive, and nearly all of them sit in a thin shell of energy, which is why the microcanonical and canonical ensembles agree. The same concentration that makes thermodynamics sharp makes blind matching hopeless.

The ways out

RemedyHow it helpsWhere to read on
Compress the dataReduce d to the number of parameters. A sufficient summary loses nothing.Summaries and Fisher
Propose locallyStop drawing from the whole prior; move in small steps from a point that already matches.Samplers
Interpolate with a networkUse every simulation, near or far, to learn a smooth function instead of discarding the misses.Neural methods
Simulate where it mattersConcentrate later rounds of simulation near the observed data.Amortisation

More worked examples

Worked example: where the volume of a ball is

What fraction of a ball lies in the outer tenth of its radius? The inner ball of radius 0.9 has a fraction 0.9d of the volume in d dimensions.

d = 2:  19%     d = 10:  65%     d = 100:  99.997%
Result. In a hundred dimensions essentially all of the ball is skin. A tolerance region for ABC, a proposal for a sampler, or a prior: all have their volume at the edge, far from the centre you had in mind.
Show the code 
for d in (2, 10, 100):
    print("d = %3d: outer tenth of the radius holds %.3f%% of the volume" % (d, 100 * (1 - 0.9**d)))

Output

d =   2: outer tenth of the radius holds 19.000% of the volume
d =  10: outer tenth of the radius holds 65.132% of the volume
d = 100: outer tenth of the radius holds 99.997% of the volume

Worked example: a Gaussian in 100 dimensions is a shell

Draw a point from a standard Gaussian in 100 dimensions. Its squared distance from the origin is a sum of 100 squared unit Gaussians, with mean 100 and standard deviation √200. So the distance is 10, give or take 0.71.

Result. 84 per cent of draws lie between 9 and 11 from the centre. The chance of being within 5 of the centre, where the density is highest, is 10−15. The most probable point is not a typical point. In physics this is why a gas is never found in its lowest-energy state: there are too few such states.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
r2 = stats.chi2(100)                             # squared distance from the centre
print("distance: mean %.1f, sd %.2f" % (np.sqrt(100), np.sqrt(0.5)))
print("P(9 < r < 11) = %.2f,  P(r < 5) = %.1e" % (r2.cdf(121) - r2.cdf(81), r2.cdf(25)))

Output

distance: mean 10.0, sd 0.71
P(9 < r < 11) = 0.84,  P(r < 5) = 1.1e-15

Questions people ask

Is the curse about the number of parameters or the number of data values? Both, in different ways. For ABC it is the dimension of what is being matched, the summaries, that sets the acceptance rate. For samplers it is the dimension of the parameter space that sets how hard the posterior is to explore.

Why does volume behave so oddly? Because volume scales as a length to the power of the dimension. Shrinking a ball's radius by ten per cent removes 10 per cent of a line, 19 per cent of a disc, and all but a fraction 0.9d of a ball in d dimensions. For d = 100 the inner ball holds three parts in a hundred thousand of the volume.

Is there a version of the curse that cannot be escaped? Yes. If the posterior truly has no structure, with many separated modes of similar weight scattered through a large space, no method finds them all without a number of evaluations that grows exponentially. Physics has the same class of problem in glasses, which is why some systems never reach equilibrium in a simulation.

What is the typical set? The region where the probability mass actually is. For a Gaussian in many dimensions it is not near the peak. It is a thin shell at a radius of about √d standard deviations, because there is so much more volume out there. Samplers must find and move around this shell, and optimisers, which head for the peak, do not.

constructionConcentration of measure is the thermodynamic limit

Draw a point from a standard Gaussian in d dimensions. Its squared distance from the origin is a sum of d independent terms each of mean 1, so by equipartition ⟨r²⟩ = d, while the spread of r stays of order 1 whatever the dimension. Almost every sample lies in a thin shell of radius √d, far from the peak of the density at the origin.

This is the microcanonical shell of statistical mechanics. A gas of 1023 particles is essentially never found at its most probable microstate. It is found in the shell where the density of states, rising steeply, balances the Boltzmann factor, falling steeply. The balance point is the minimum of the free energy F = E − TS, not of the energy.

For inference the consequence is the same and is often overlooked: in many dimensions the best-fit point is not a typical posterior sample, and the region that matters is set by a competition between probability density (energy) and volume (entropy). Rejection sampling fails because it looks for volume near a point. Every later chapter is a way of handling the entropy term properly.

← 9. ABC11. Summaries and Fisher →

Chapter 11 of 39

Summaries, sufficiency and the Fisher limit

Raw data are too large to match. Which few numbers should you keep, and how well can any method then do? Thermodynamics answers both.

physics

The connection. Choosing summary statistics is coarse-graining: keeping the few macroscopic variables that matter and discarding microscopic detail.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Summary statisticsMacroscopic variables, order parameters
Sufficient statisticsVariables that fix the macrostate completely
Compression that keeps the Fisher informationA renormalisation step that keeps the relevant couplings
Fisher informationSusceptibility: the response of observables to a parameter

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: keeping what matters

Fisher introduced the idea of a sufficient statistic in 1922: a function of the data that contains everything the data have to say about a parameter. The sample mean is sufficient for the centre of a Gaussian, so a million measurements can be replaced by one number with no loss. In the 1930s Pitman, Koopman and Darmois showed that such finite sufficient statistics exist only for a special family of distributions, the exponential family. That family is the set of Boltzmann distributions.

Physics arrived at coarse-graining from the other end. Thermodynamics describes a gas by pressure, volume and temperature, discarding 1023 coordinates. In 1966 Kadanoff proposed grouping the spins of a magnet into blocks and describing each block by its average. Wilson turned this into the renormalisation group in 1971: repeat the grouping, and track which details survive. The quantities that survive are the relevant variables, and they are few.

Modern cosmology uses both lines. A galaxy survey of a billion pixels is compressed to a power spectrum, and methods developed from 1997 onwards, by Tegmark, Taylor and Heavens and later by Alsing and Wandelt, compress further to one number per parameter while keeping the Fisher information. Neural networks trained to do this compression are now a standard first stage of SBI.

Summary statistics are macroscopic variables

ABC cannot compare a million pixels, so the data are first reduced to a few summary numbers. A summary is sufficient if it loses no information about θ: once you know the summary, the rest of the data tell you nothing more. Thermodynamics is built on the same idea. You describe a gas by its energy, volume and particle number and ignore 1023 coordinates, because those few numbers fix everything that can be predicted.

Worked example: for two-level atoms the count is sufficient

Suppose you record which of the N atoms are excited, not only how many. The probability of one particular pattern with k atoms excited is

p(pattern | T) = pk (1 − p)N−k,    p = 1 / (1 + e1/T)

The pattern enters only through k. Two patterns with the same count are equally probable at every temperature, so which atoms are excited says nothing about T.

Result. The count k, which is the total energy in units of the gap, is a sufficient statistic for the temperature. Matching on k in ABC loses nothing.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
N = 50
pattern_a = np.zeros(N, int); pattern_a[:12] = 1               # the first 12 atoms excited
pattern_b = np.zeros(N, int); pattern_b[4::4] = 1               # 12 different atoms excited
print("excited atoms in each pattern:", pattern_a.sum(), pattern_b.sum())
for T in (0.5, 0.87, 2.0):
    p = 1 / (1 + np.exp(1 / T))
    like = lambda pat: np.prod(np.where(pat == 1, p, 1 - p))
    print("T = %.2f: likelihood ratio of the two patterns = %.6f" % (T, like(pattern_a) / like(pattern_b)))

Output

excited atoms in each pattern: 12 12
T = 0.50: likelihood ratio of the two patterns = 1.000000
T = 0.87: likelihood ratio of the two patterns = 1.000000
T = 2.00: likelihood ratio of the two patterns = 1.000000

The general rule: the Boltzmann form

Whenever the probability of the data can be written in Boltzmann form,

p(x | θ) = exp[ θ · s(x) ] / Z(θ) statisticians call this an exponential family

the quantity s(x) multiplying the parameter is a sufficient statistic. In physics the parameter is −1/kT and s is the energy, or the parameter is the field and s is the magnetisation. The partition function then generates the statistics of s by differentiation, exactly as in a thermodynamics course:

∂ ln Z / ∂θ = ⟨s⟩,     ∂² ln Z / ∂θ² = Var(s) mean and fluctuation of the conjugate variable

The second derivative has a name in statistics, the Fisher information, and a name in physics, a response function (heat capacity, susceptibility). They are the same object.

How well can you possibly do?

The Fisher information I(θ) sets a floor on the error of any unbiased estimate, the Cramér–Rao bound: variance ≥ 1/I.

Worked example: the best possible thermometer

For N two-level atoms the count is binomial, so its information about p is N/[p(1 − p)]. Change variable to temperature using dp/dT = p(1 − p)/T²:

I(T) = (dp/dT)² · N / [p(1 − p)] = N p (1 − p) / T⁴

Now compute the heat capacity. The energy is U = N p (in units of the gap), so C = dU/dT = N p(1 − p)/T². Comparing the two:

Result. I(T) = C/T², so the smallest possible variance of a temperature estimate is T²/C, or kT²/C with units restored. This is the textbook formula for the temperature fluctuation of a small system. A good thermometer is a system with a large heat capacity.

Numbers for the experiment in the ABC chapter (12 of 50 excited): the best-fit temperature is T = 1/ln(38/12) = 0.87, the heat capacity is 12.1 k, and the bound gives an error of 0.25.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
N, k = 50, 12
p = k / N
T = 1 / np.log((N - k) / k)                      # from p = 1 / (1 + exp(1/T))
C = N * p * (1 - p) / T**2                       # heat capacity dU/dT, in units of k_B
fisher = N * p * (1 - p) / T**4
print("T = %.2f, heat capacity %.1f, Fisher information %.1f = C/T^2 = %.1f, error %.2f" % (T, C, fisher, C / T**2, fisher**-0.5))

Output

T = 0.87, heat capacity 12.1, Fisher information 16.1 = C/T^2 = 16.1, error 0.25

Where the bound misleads

The simulation in the ABC chapter gave T = 1.28 ± 0.70 for the same data, an error bar nearly three times the Fisher value and a centre well above the best fit. Nothing is wrong with either number. The Fisher calculation describes the curvature at the peak, as if the posterior were a Gaussian. The true posterior has a long tail to high temperature, because once most of the thermal energy is above the gap the count barely changes with T.

The lesson carries to real problems: a Fisher forecast is a statement about a quadratic well. When the energy landscape is not quadratic (few data, low signal-to-noise, parameters near a boundary) only the full posterior tells the truth, and SBI or a sampler is how you get it.

Choosing summaries in practice

  • By physics. Conserved quantities and order parameters first: total energy, magnetisation, power spectrum.
  • By the score. The derivative of the log-likelihood with respect to θ, evaluated at a reference point, is the best linear compression. It needs a likelihood, even an approximate one.
  • By a network. Train a network to predict θ from simulated data and use its output as the summary. Modern SBI learns the summary and the posterior together.

A summary that is not sufficient does not make the answer wrong. It makes the posterior wider than it needed to be, because information was thrown away.

More worked examples

Worked example: five pixels, one number

Five pixels of a detector look at the same uniform source and record 3, 5, 2, 6 and 4 photons. The Poisson likelihood for the rate λ per pixel is proportional to λ20 e−5λ. It depends on the data only through the total, 20.

Result. The total is a sufficient statistic. With a flat prior the rate is 4.2 ± 0.92 per pixel, and any other five counts adding to 20 would give the same answer. The individual values carry no further information about the rate. They are the microstate, and the total is the macrostate.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
counts = np.array([3, 5, 2, 6, 4])
post = stats.gamma(counts.sum() + 1, scale=1 / counts.size)       # flat prior on the rate
print("total %d: rate %.1f +/- %.2f per pixel" % (counts.sum(), post.mean(), post.std()))

Output

total 20: rate 4.2 +/- 0.92 per pixel

Worked example: a summary that loses information

One hundred readings have Gaussian noise of standard deviation 1. Estimating the centre by the mean gives an error of 1/√100 = 0.100. Estimating it by the median gives an error larger by the factor √(π/2) = 1.253.

Result. The median gives 0.125, which is 25 per cent worse. It keeps 2/π = 64 per cent of the Fisher information. A posterior built on the median would be wider than necessary but still correct. Losing information through a summary is a safe failure. Losing it without knowing is the thing to avoid.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
data = rng.standard_normal((200_000, 100))       # 200,000 experiments of 100 readings
sd_mean, sd_median = data.mean(1).std(), np.median(data, 1).std()
print("error of the mean %.3f, of the median %.3f, ratio %.3f (sqrt(pi/2) = %.3f)" % (sd_mean, sd_median, sd_median / sd_mean, np.sqrt(np.pi / 2)))

Output

error of the mean 0.100, of the median 0.125, ratio 1.246 (sqrt(pi/2) = 1.253)

Questions people ask

How do I know my summaries are good enough? Compare the Fisher information they carry with that of the full data, where a forecast is possible. Otherwise add more summaries and see whether the posterior narrows. Summaries that lose information give a posterior that is wider than necessary but still correct. That is a safe kind of failure.

Can a neural network find the summaries for me? Yes, and this is common practice. A network trained to predict the parameters from simulated data learns an approximation to the posterior mean, which is a good summary. The risk is that it learns features that exist in the simulator and not in reality.

In what sense is this renormalisation? Both procedures replace many variables by a few, chosen so that the quantities of interest are unchanged. In renormalisation the quantities of interest are long-distance correlations. In inference they are the likelihood's dependence on the parameters. Irrelevant operators correspond to features of the data that carry no information about the parameters.

Do sufficient statistics exist for my simulator? Almost never exactly. Real simulators are not in the exponential family. What exists is a trade: more summaries keep more information and make every later step harder.

constructionFluctuation, response and the renormalisation group

Fluctuation equals response. The identity ∂⟨s⟩/∂θ = Var(s) in this chapter is the static fluctuation–dissipation theorem. The left side is a response: how far the mean of an observable moves when its conjugate parameter is nudged. The right side is a fluctuation, measured with nothing nudged. The Fisher information is therefore a susceptibility, and information geometry, which uses the Fisher matrix as a metric on parameter space, is thermodynamic geometry under another name.

The Cramér–Rao bound is an uncertainty relation. For the thermometer, the bound Var(T) ≥ T²/C combines with the energy fluctuation Var(U) = T² C to give

ΔU · Δβ ≥ 1,    β = 1/T

the thermodynamic uncertainty relation between energy and inverse temperature. A system in contact with a bath has a sharp temperature and a fluctuating energy; an isolated one has a sharp energy and no well-defined temperature. You cannot know both conjugate variables.

Compression is coarse-graining. The renormalisation group replaces microscopic variables by block averages and asks which combinations still matter at large scales. Those that do are called relevant; the rest are irrelevant and flow away. A sufficient statistic is a set of relevant variables: the combinations of the data that the parameters still depend on after everything else has been averaged out. The rule that processing data can never add information about θ is the statement that the renormalisation flow only loses information, and a learned summary network is a coarse-graining found by optimisation instead of by hand.

← 10. Dimensionality12. Eigenmodes →

Chapter 12 of 39

Eigenmodes: Karhunen-Loève, PCA and signal-to-noise

A data set with a thousand correlated numbers is hard to think about. In the right basis it is a thousand independent numbers, most of which do not matter. Finding that basis is the oldest trick in physics.

physics

The connection. The patterns that diagonalise a covariance matrix are the normal modes of physics. For a stationary field they are Fourier modes, and their variances are the power spectrum.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Karhunen-Loève modes; principal componentsNormal modes of coupled oscillators
Eigenvalue: the variance of a modeThermal fluctuation kBT/k of a mode of stiffness k
Power spectrum of stationary dataPropagator in Fourier space, 1/(k² + m²)
Keeping the modes of high signal-to-noiseKeeping the relevant operators under renormalisation

Background: one idea, many names

Pearson introduced principal components in 1901 and Hotelling developed them in the 1930s. Karhunen and Loève, in the 1940s, proved the corresponding theorem for random functions: any random field can be written as a sum of fixed patterns with uncorrelated random amplitudes, and the patterns are the eigenfunctions of the covariance. Meteorologists call the patterns empirical orthogonal functions. In cosmology, Bond in 1995 and Tegmark, Taylor and Heavens in 1997 used signal-to-noise eigenmodes to compress maps of the microwave sky and galaxy surveys.

Physicists had been doing this since the eighteenth century under the name of normal modes. A set of masses joined by springs moves in a complicated way, but there are special patterns of motion, the normal modes, each of which oscillates independently of the others. Finding them is diagonalising the stiffness matrix. A free field theory is solved in the same way: the Fourier modes of the field are its normal modes, and each is an independent oscillator.

The idea

Let the data be a vector x of n numbers with mean zero and covariance matrix C. Find the eigenvectors of C. Each is a pattern across the data, and its eigenvalue is the variance of the amount of that pattern present. Writing the data as a sum of patterns turns correlated numbers into uncorrelated ones.

physics

Normal modes of coupled oscillators

Modes. K ei = ki ei

K: the stiffness matrix. ei: the pattern of mode i. ki: its stiffness.

Energy. E = ½ Σi ki ai²
ai: the amplitude of mode i. The modes do not interact.

Fluctuations. ⟨ai aj⟩ = (kBT / ki) δij
Soft modes fluctuate most.

Free field. For the action S = ½ ∫ [(∇φ)² + m²φ²] the modes are plane waves, with ⟨|φk|²⟩ = 1/(k² + m²).

inference

Karhunen-Loève modes of a data set

Modes. C ei = λi ei

C: the covariance matrix of the data, which is the inverse of a stiffness. ei: pattern i. λi: its variance.

Expansion. x = Σi ai ei, with ai = ei · x.

Statistics. ⟨ai aj⟩ = λi δij
The amplitudes are uncorrelated. The largest eigenvalues are the softest modes.

Stationary data. If the correlations depend only on separation, the modes are Fourier modes and the eigenvalues are the power spectrum.

Worked example: two correlated measurements

Two quantities have variances 2 and 1 and covariance 1.2. The eigenvalues of the covariance matrix are 2.8 and 0.2, with patterns (0.83, 0.55) and (0.55, −0.83).

Result. The first mode carries 2.8/3.0 = 93 per cent of the total variance. The two numbers are, to a good approximation, one number: the amount of the pattern (0.83, 0.55). The second mode, with a standard deviation of 0.45 against 1.67, is where to look for something unexpected, because the model says little should be happening there.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
C = np.array([[2.0, 1.2], [1.2, 1.0]])
lam, vec = np.linalg.eigh(C)
print("eigenvalues", lam.round(2), " largest carries %.0f%%" % (100 * lam[-1] / lam.sum()))
print("patterns", np.abs(vec[:, 1]).round(2), np.abs(vec[:, 0]).round(2), " sds", np.sqrt(lam[::-1]).round(2))

Output

eigenvalues [0.2 2.8]  largest carries 93%
patterns [0.83 0.55] [0.55 0.83]  sds [1.67 0.45]

Worked example: a ring of four points

Four points sit on a ring. Each has variance 1. Neighbours have correlation 0.5 and opposite points 0.2. Because the correlations depend only on separation, the eigenvectors are the four Fourier patterns on the ring, and each eigenvalue is the Fourier sum of the correlations.

constant: 1 + 0.5 + 0.2 + 0.5 = 2.2     one wave (two patterns): 1 − 0.2 = 0.8     alternating: 1 − 0.5 + 0.2 − 0.5 = 0.2
Result. The eigenvalues 2.2, 0.8, 0.8 and 0.2 are the power spectrum of the ring. They add up to 4, the total variance. Smooth patterns have large variance and rapidly varying ones small, as expected for positively correlated neighbours. For any translation-invariant field the Karhunen-Loève modes are Fourier modes, which is why power spectra are the natural summary of the microwave background and of noise in a time series.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy.linalg import circulant
C = circulant([1.0, 0.5, 0.2, 0.5])              # correlation depends only on separation
print("eigenvalues of the covariance:", np.sort(np.linalg.eigvalsh(C))[::-1].round(2))
print("Fourier transform of the row: ", np.sort(np.fft.fft(C[0]).real)[::-1].round(2))

Output

eigenvalues of the covariance: [2.2 0.8 0.8 0.2]
Fourier transform of the row:  [2.2 0.8 0.8 0.2]

Signal-to-noise eigenmodes

Usually the data are signal plus noise, with covariances S and N. The useful modes are then not those of largest variance but those of largest signal relative to noise. They solve the generalised problem S e = λ N e, and λ is the signal-to-noise ratio of the mode. Modes with λ much less than 1 can be thrown away with almost no loss.

Worked example: which combination of two pixels to keep

Two pixels see a signal with unit variance and correlation 0.9. The first pixel has noise variance 0.1 and the second 1.0. The two signal-to-noise eigenvalues are 10.8 and 0.18. If the unknown is the overall amplitude of the signal, the Fisher information carried by a mode is ½ [λ/(1 + λ)]².

mode 1: ½ (10.8/11.8)² = 0.419      mode 2: ½ (0.18/1.18)² = 0.011
Result. The first mode carries 97 per cent of the information (0.419 out of 0.430), so two pixels compress to one number at a cost of 3 per cent. With equal noise in the two pixels the modes would be the sum and the difference, with signal-to-noise 3.8 and 0.2. This is the compression used on real surveys, where a million pixels reduce to a few thousand modes.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy.linalg import eigh
S = np.array([[1.0, 0.9], [0.9, 1.0]])           # signal covariance
N = np.diag([0.1, 1.0])                          # noise covariance
lam = eigh(S, N, eigvals_only=True)[::-1]        # signal-to-noise eigenvalues
info = 0.5 * (lam / (1 + lam))**2                # Fisher information on the amplitude, per mode
print("signal-to-noise", lam.round(2), " information", info.round(3), " share of the first: %.0f%%" % (100 * info[0] / info.sum()))
print("equal noise 0.5:", (np.linalg.eigvalsh(S)[::-1] / 0.5).round(1))

Output

signal-to-noise [10.82  0.18]  information [0.419 0.011]  share of the first: 97%
equal noise 0.5: [3.8 0.2]

Why this is renormalisation

Ordering modes by signal-to-noise and keeping the top few is a decision about which degrees of freedom are relevant. The renormalisation group makes the same decision for a field theory: it orders fluctuations by length scale, integrates out the short ones, and keeps the few combinations of couplings that still matter at long distances. The stiff and soft directions of the Fisher matrix in the symmetry chapter are the same structure seen in parameter space: a few well-measured combinations and many that the data barely touch.

Questions people ask

Is PCA the same as Karhunen-Loève? Yes, for a finite set of numbers. Karhunen-Loève is the name used for functions and fields, and principal components the name used for tables of data. Both diagonalise a covariance.

Do the modes mean anything physically? Sometimes. If the covariance comes from a physical theory, as for a stationary field, the modes are Fourier modes and have a clear meaning. If it is estimated from the data, the modes are whatever patterns happen to be uncorrelated in that sample, and reading meaning into the fourth or fifth is risky.

What is lost by keeping only the top modes? Any information carried by the discarded ones. For Gaussian data and a fixed question, that loss can be computed exactly, as in the worked example. For non-Gaussian data the discarded modes may hide structure that variances do not show.

How does this help SBI? It is the cheapest form of summary statistic. Projecting simulated and observed data onto a few hundred modes before training a network reduces the dimension by orders of magnitude and removes directions that are pure noise.

What to remember

  • The eigenvectors of a covariance matrix are patterns whose amplitudes are uncorrelated. In physics they are normal modes.
  • For stationary data the modes are Fourier modes and the eigenvalues are the power spectrum.
  • Signal-to-noise eigenmodes say which combinations of the data carry information. Keeping the top few is a controlled compression.
  • Keeping relevant modes and discarding the others is what the renormalisation group does to a field theory.
← 11. Summaries and Fisher13. Large deviations →

Chapter 13 of 39

Large deviations: the probability of the unlikely

The Gaussian bell curve describes small, everyday fluctuations well. It is unreliable for rare ones, which is where detection thresholds and false-alarm rates live. The correct description is exponential in the amount of data, and its exponent is an entropy.

physics

The connection. Large-deviation theory is the mathematics underneath thermodynamics. The rate function is an entropy, and the two are related by the Legendre transform that links entropy to free energy.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
P(rare average) ≈ exp(−n I)Einstein's fluctuation formula P ∝ exp(ΔS/kB)
Rate function IEntropy per particle (with a minus sign)
Cumulant generating functionFree energy per particle
Cramér's theoremLegendre transform between free energy and entropy

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: the mathematics underneath thermodynamics

The central limit theorem says that averages of many random numbers are Gaussian near their mean. It says nothing reliable about the far tails, and the far tails are where rare events live. In 1938 Cramér, working on the risk of ruin for insurance companies, found the correct law: the probability that an average of n terms deviates by a fixed amount falls exponentially in n, at a rate given by a function that is generally not quadratic. Sanov extended the result to whole distributions in 1957, and Varadhan built the general theory in the 1960s, for which he received the Abel Prize.

Physicists had been using the result since Boltzmann. His formula for the entropy, and Einstein's 1910 formula for the probability of a fluctuation, P ∝ exp(ΔS/kB), are large-deviation statements. The modern view, set out by Ellis in the 1980s, is that the entropy is a rate function and the free energy is its Legendre transform. Thermodynamics is large-deviation theory for systems with very many particles.

In data analysis the theory matters wherever a small probability has to be quoted: a false-alarm rate for a detection, the chance that a rare fluctuation mimics a signal, or the rate at which the posterior weight of a wrong model decays as data accumulate.

A question about coins

Toss a fair coin 100 times. Getting about 50 heads is typical. How unlikely is 70 or more? The central limit theorem says the count is roughly Gaussian with mean 50 and standard deviation 5, so 70 is a four-sigma event. That estimate is not bad here. For 90 heads it is wrong by a factor of a hundred. The reason is that the Gaussian comes from expanding about the typical value and keeping only the quadratic term, and far from the typical value that is not enough.

The exact exponent

For n tosses of a coin with heads probability p, the chance that the fraction of heads is near x behaves as

P( fraction ≈ x ) ≈ e−n I(x),    I(x) = x ln(x/p) + (1 − x) ln[ (1 − x)/(1 − p) ]

The function I(x) is called the rate function. It is zero at the typical value x = p and positive elsewhere. It comes from counting: the number of sequences with a fraction x of heads is a binomial coefficient, and Stirling's formula (free-energy chapter) turns its logarithm into an entropy.

Worked example: 100 tosses

Near x = ½ the rate function is approximately quadratic, I ≈ 2(x − ½)², which reproduces the Gaussian. Compare the two exponents, and the resulting tail probabilities with the exact binomial answer:

HeadsExact I(x)Quadratic approximationExact probability of at least this manyGaussian estimate
600.02010.02000.0280.029
700.08230.08003.9 × 10−54.8 × 10−5
900.3680.3201.5 × 10−171.4 × 10−15
Result. At two sigma the Gaussian is accurate to a few per cent. At four sigma it is off by 20%. At eight sigma it overstates the probability by a factor of ninety. The error is in the exponent and is multiplied by n, so it grows without limit.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
n = 100
for heads in (60, 70, 90):
    x = heads / n
    I = x * np.log(2 * x) + (1 - x) * np.log(2 * (1 - x))        # exact rate function for a fair coin
    exact = stats.binom(n, 0.5).sf(heads - 1)
    gauss = stats.norm.sf((heads - 0.5 - 50) / 5)                # Gaussian with continuity correction
    print("%d heads: I = %.4f, quadratic %.4f, exact %.1e, Gaussian %.1e, ratio %.1f" % (heads, I, 2 * (x - 0.5)**2, exact, gauss, gauss / exact))

Output

60 heads: I = 0.0201, quadratic 0.0200, exact 2.8e-02, Gaussian 2.9e-02, ratio 1.0
70 heads: I = 0.0823, quadratic 0.0800, exact 3.9e-05, Gaussian 4.8e-05, ratio 1.2
90 heads: I = 0.3681, quadratic 0.3200, exact 1.5e-17, Gaussian 1.4e-15, ratio 91.0

Why this matters for inference

  • Thresholds and false alarms. A "five-sigma" claim is a statement about a far tail. If the test statistic is not exactly Gaussian, the true false-alarm probability can differ from the nominal one by orders of magnitude, and it should be found by simulation.
  • Posterior tails. A Fisher forecast is the quadratic approximation to the log-posterior. Like the Gaussian above it is good near the peak and unreliable in the tails, which is the failure seen for the thermometer in the summaries chapter.
  • Rare events in simulators. If the observation is a rare outcome of the simulator, blind simulation almost never produces it. The cost of rejection sampling in the dimensionality chapter is a large-deviation probability.

More worked examples

Worked example: the Gaussian case

Fifty readings have unit Gaussian noise about zero. What is the chance that their average exceeds 0.5? For Gaussian variables the rate function is I(a) = a²/2, so the large-deviation estimate is e−nI = e−50 × 0.125 = e−6.25.

Result. The estimate is 1.9 × 10−3 and the exact probability is 2.0 × 10−4. The exponent is right and the factor in front, which is of order 1/√n, is missing. Large-deviation theory gives the exponential rate and nothing more, exactly as thermodynamics gives the free energy to leading order in the size of the system.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
n, a = 50, 0.5
print("large-deviation estimate exp(-n a^2/2) = %.1e,  exact = %.1e" % (np.exp(-n * a**2 / 2), stats.norm.sf(a * np.sqrt(n))))

Output

large-deviation estimate exp(-n a^2/2) = 1.9e-03,  exact = 2.0e-04

Worked example: a rare count

A background produces 10 counts on average. How likely is it to produce 25? For a Poisson variable the cost of seeing k when λ was expected is I = k ln(k/λ) − k + λ = 25 ln 2.5 − 15 = 7.91. Including the prefactor from Stirling's formula, the probability is e−I/√(2π × 25).

Result. The estimate is 2.94 × 10−5 and the exact Poisson probability is 2.93 × 10−5. The exact chance of 25 or more is 4.7 × 10−5. A Gaussian approximation with standard deviation √10 would call it a 4.7 sigma event, 1.1 × 10−6, too small by a factor of about 40. Tails of counting distributions are heavier than the Gaussian suggests, which matters for every claim of a detection.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
lam, k = 10, 25
I = k * np.log(k / lam) - k + lam                # rate function
print("I = %.2f,  estimate %.2e,  exact P(25) = %.2e" % (I, np.exp(-I) / np.sqrt(2 * np.pi * k), stats.poisson(lam).pmf(k)))
z = (k - lam) / np.sqrt(lam)
print("P(25 or more): exact %.1e, Gaussian (%.1f sigma) %.1e" % (stats.poisson(lam).sf(k - 1), z, stats.norm.sf(z)))

Output

I = 7.91,  estimate 2.94e-05,  exact P(25) = 2.93e-05
P(25 or more): exact 4.7e-05, Gaussian (4.7 sigma) 1.1e-06

Questions people ask

When is the Gaussian approximation good enough? For deviations of a few standard deviations of the average. A five-sigma claim sits in the tail, and whether the Gaussian tail is right there depends on the underlying distribution. For sums of bounded or light-tailed variables it is usually close. For heavy tails it can be wrong by orders of magnitude.

What is the rate function, in words? It is the price, per data point, of seeing an average that differs from the expected one. Equivalently it is the relative entropy between the distribution you would need in order to make that average typical and the distribution you have.

Why is the posterior weight of a wrong model exponentially small? Because the log-likelihood is a sum over data points, so the ratio of likelihoods of two parameter values is the exponential of n times an average. The average converges to a relative entropy, which is the rate. This is the reason posteriors narrow, and also the reason a misspecified model becomes overconfident.

Is there a link to the phase-transitions chapter? Yes. A phase transition is a point where the rate function, or the free energy, is not smooth. Large-deviation theory is the language in which that is made precise.

constructionEntropy is a rate function

Large-deviation theory is the mathematical form of statistical mechanics, and each of its statements has been in the physics textbooks for a century under another name.

Boltzmann and Einstein. Boltzmann's S = k ln W counts the microstates compatible with a macrostate. Einstein inverted it to get the probability of a fluctuation: P ∝ eΔS/k, with ΔS the entropy lost by fluctuating away from equilibrium. The rate function is that entropy deficit per degree of freedom, and the number of data plays the part of the number of particles. For the coin, I(x) is the Kullback–Leibler divergence between a coin of bias x and the true one, which is the entropy cost of making the sample look like a different coin.

The Legendre transform. The rate function is the Legendre transform of the cumulant generating function, ln⟨eλxλ, the rate function is an entropy as a function of the conjugate observable, and each is recovered from the other by a Legendre transform. Convexity of one implies concavity of the other, which is thermodynamic stability.

Saddle points are large deviations. The statement "for large n the integral is dominated by its largest term" is the large-deviation principle. It is why thermodynamics has definite values instead of distributions, why the saddle point of a path integral is the classical solution, and why a posterior with many data collapses onto its best fit. The three are one theorem.

The central limit theorem is the harmonic approximation. Expanding the rate function to second order about its minimum gives Gaussian fluctuations, with variance set by the curvature. That curvature is a susceptibility, by the fluctuation–response relation. Everything beyond the quadratic term, the anharmonic part, is what Gaussian approximations miss.

← 12. Eigenmodes14. Samplers →

Chapter 14 of 39

Samplers: Metropolis, Hamiltonian and nested

Three of the methods in this course (NLE, NRE and any problem with a known likelihood) end with a sampling step. All three samplers below came from physics, and two still carry its names.

physics

The connection. The samplers of Bayesian statistics were invented by physicists to simulate matter, and were borrowed afterwards.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Metropolis MCMCMonte Carlo for a fluid of hard discs (1953)
Hamiltonian Monte CarloHybrid Monte Carlo for lattice QCD (1987): Hamilton's equations plus a heat bath
Nested samplingIntegrating over the density of states
TemperingSimulated annealing; replica exchange

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: algorithms borrowed from physics

In 1953 Metropolis, the Rosenbluths and the Tellers, working at Los Alamos on one of the first electronic computers, wanted the equation of state of a fluid of hard discs. They could not enumerate the arrangements of the discs, so they invented a way to wander through them at random that visits each arrangement in proportion to its Boltzmann weight. Hastings generalised the rule in 1970. Statisticians took little notice until 1984, when Geman and Geman used it for image restoration, and 1990, when Gelfand and Smith showed that it solved a wide class of Bayesian problems. Bayesian statistics as a practical subject dates from then.

Hamiltonian Monte Carlo has the same history, thirty years later. Duane, Kennedy, Pendleton and Roweth devised it in 1987 to simulate quarks on a lattice, where random-walk proposals are hopelessly slow. Neal brought it to statistics in the 1990s. Hoffman and Gelman's automatic version, NUTS, has been the default engine of the Stan and NumPyro packages since the 2010s.

Nested sampling was proposed in 2004 by Skilling, a physicist, to compute the evidence, and has close relatives in the density-of-states methods of computational physics. Parallel tempering was introduced by Swendsen and Wang in 1986 for spin glasses. Simulated annealing, by Kirkpatrick and colleagues in 1983, came from the same community. The pattern is consistent: a hard sampling problem in physics, a method, and adoption by statistics a decade or two later.

Metropolis: wander with a rule

Start anywhere. Propose a move to a new state. If the energy goes down, accept. If it goes up by ΔE, accept with probability e−ΔE. Repeat. The states visited are distributed as e−E/Z, and Z was never needed.

Worked example: detailed balance on three states

Take three states with energies 0, 1 and 2 (in units of kT). Their Boltzmann weights are 1, e−1 and e−2, summing to Z = 1.503, so the equilibrium probabilities are

p0 = 0.665,    p1 = 0.245,    p2 = 0.090

Use the rule: from any state, propose one of the other two with probability ½. Check the flow between states 0 and 1.

0 → 1:   p0 · ½ · e−1 = 0.665 × 0.5 × 0.368 = 0.1224      1 → 0:   p1 · ½ · 1 = 0.245 × 0.5 = 0.1224
Result. The flows are equal. The same holds for every pair, so the Boltzmann distribution is left unchanged by the rule. This condition is called detailed balance, and it is all a sampler needs to be correct. Only the ratio p1/p0 = e−1 entered: Z cancelled.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
E = np.array([0.0, 1.0, 2.0])
w = np.exp(-E); Z = w.sum(); p = w / Z
print("Z = %.3f, probabilities" % Z, p.round(3))
T = np.zeros((3, 3))                             # Metropolis: propose one of the other two with probability 1/2
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        if i != j: T[i, j] = 0.5 * min(1, np.exp(-(E[j] - E[i])))
    T[i, i] = 1 - T[i].sum()
print("flow 0->1 %.4f, flow 1->0 %.4f" % (p[0] * T[0, 1], p[1] * T[1, 0]))
print("distribution after one step:", (p @ T).round(3))

Output

Z = 1.503, probabilities [0.665 0.245 0.09 ]
flow 0->1 0.1224, flow 1->0 0.1224
distribution after one step: [0.665 0.245 0.09 ]

The weakness is speed. Small random steps explore by diffusion: to travel a distance L with steps of size δ takes about (L/δ)² steps. In many dimensions the steps must be small to be accepted, and the walk crawls.

Hamiltonian Monte Carlo: give the walker momentum

Treat the parameter θ as the position of a particle and the energy E(θ) as its potential. Add a fictitious momentum p and a Hamiltonian

H(θ, p) = E(θ) + p² / 2

Draw a random momentum, follow Hamilton's equations for a while, and take the end point as the proposal. Because energy is conserved along the path, the proposal is nearly always accepted even though it lies far away. This is literal classical mechanics, integrated with the leapfrog scheme used in molecular dynamics.

Worked example: one trajectory in a quadratic well

For E = κ θ²/2 the motion is simple harmonic with angular frequency ω = √κ:

θ(t) = θ0 cos ωt + (p0/ω) sin ωt

After a quarter period, ωt = π/2, the position is p0/ω. It depends only on the random momentum and has forgotten where it started.

Result. One trajectory of a quarter period gives a fully independent sample, with variance ⟨p0²⟩/κ = 1/κ, the correct posterior variance. Travel is ballistic: distance L costs about L/δ steps, not (L/δ)².
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
kappa, n = 4.0, 200_000
theta0 = rng.normal(3.0, 0.1, n)                 # start far from equilibrium
mom = rng.standard_normal(n)                     # fresh random momenta
steps = 100; dt = (np.pi / 2) / np.sqrt(kappa) / steps        # a quarter period
theta, pm = theta0.copy(), mom.copy()
for _ in range(steps):                           # leapfrog integration of Hamilton's equations
    pm -= 0.5 * dt * kappa * theta; theta += dt * pm; pm -= 0.5 * dt * kappa * theta
print("after a quarter period: mean %.3f, variance %.4f (1/kappa = %.4f)" % (theta.mean(), theta.var(), 1 / kappa))
print("correlation with the starting point: %.3f" % np.corrcoef(theta, theta0)[0, 1])

Output

after a quarter period: mean 0.000, variance 0.2508 (1/kappa = 0.2500)
correlation with the starting point: -0.003

The price is that the method needs the gradient of the energy, the force. That is available when the model is written in a framework that differentiates automatically. The NUTS algorithm, the default in most modern packages, is Hamiltonian Monte Carlo that chooses its own trajectory length.

Nested sampling: measure the density of states

Scatter n "live" points over the prior. Repeatedly discard the one with the lowest likelihood and replace it with a new point drawn from the prior above that likelihood. Each step shrinks the enclosed prior volume X by a factor of about e−1/n, so the method climbs steadily inward through shells of equal likelihood. The evidence is the sum of likelihood times shell volume:

Z = ∫01 L(X) dX compare Z = ∫ g(E) e−E dE in statistical mechanics

Worked example: how long a run takes

Suppose the posterior occupies one millionth of the prior volume. Reaching it needs the volume to shrink by 106, that is ln 106 = 13.8 factors of e, and each takes n steps.

Result. With n = 400 live points the run needs about 400 × 13.8 ≈ 5,500 iterations to reach the bulk of the posterior. Cost grows with the logarithm of the compression from prior to posterior, which is the information gained, and linearly with the number of live points.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
compression, live = 1e6, 400
print("ln(1e6) = %.1f, iterations to reach the posterior = %.0f" % (np.log(compression), live * np.log(compression)))

Output

ln(1e6) = 13.8, iterations to reach the posterior = 5526

Nested sampling gives the evidence as its main product and posterior samples as a by-product, and it copes with several separated peaks. Its accuracy depends on the number of live points, so results should be shown to be stable when that number is increased.

Side by side

MetropolisHamiltonian (HMC, NUTS)Nested sampling
Physics originEquation-of-state simulations, 1953Lattice field theory, 1987Density of states
NeedsEnergy differencesEnergy and its gradientLikelihood and a prior to draw from
MotionDiffusiveBallisticInward through likelihood shells
Gives the evidenceNoNoYes
Several separated peaksGets stuckGets stuckHandles them
Check before trustingSeveral chains from different starts must agreeStable when live points are increased

More worked examples

Worked example: the best step size for Metropolis

A random-walk Metropolis sampler explores a unit Gaussian in one dimension. The proposal is a Gaussian step of size s. Three choices were run for 200,000 steps each.

Step sizeAcceptedSteps per independent sample
0.391%54
2.444%4.3
1013%12
Result. A high acceptance rate is not the aim. Small steps are nearly always accepted and go nowhere, and large steps are nearly always rejected. The best step is about the width of the distribution, with somewhat under half the moves accepted. In physics the same tuning is done for the size of a trial move in a simulation of a liquid.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
for step in (0.3, 2.4, 10.0):
    x, chain, accepted = 0.0, [], 0
    for _ in range(200_000):
        y = x + step * rng.standard_normal()
        if np.log(rng.random()) < 0.5 * (x*x - y*y):     # Metropolis rule for a unit Gaussian
            x, accepted = y, accepted + 1
        chain.append(x)
    c = np.array(chain) - np.mean(chain)
    tau = 1.0                                            # integrated autocorrelation time
    for lag in range(1, 2000):
        rho = (c[:-lag] * c[lag:]).mean() / c.var()
        if rho < 0.02: break
        tau += 2 * rho
    print("step %4.1f: accepted %2.0f%%, %.1f steps per independent sample" % (step, 100 * accepted / len(chain), tau))

Output

step  0.3: accepted 91%, 54.3 steps per independent sample
step  2.4: accepted 44%, 4.3 steps per independent sample
step 10.0: accepted 13%, 12.0 steps per independent sample

Worked example: how long nested sampling takes

The likelihood is a unit Gaussian and the prior is flat between −5 and 5. The evidence is √(2π)/10 = 0.251. Nested sampling with 100 live points shrinks the enclosed prior volume by a factor of about e−1/100 at each step. It reaches the bulk of the posterior after the volume has shrunk by e−H, where H is the information gained from prior to posterior, here ln(10/√(2πe)) = 0.88 nats.

Result. About 100 × 0.88 = 88 steps to reach the posterior, and a few hundred to integrate over it. The cost of nested sampling is the number of live points times the information gained. A posterior a million times smaller than its prior costs ln 106 = 14 times the number of live points.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
Z = np.sqrt(2 * np.pi) / 10                      # unit Gaussian likelihood, flat prior of width 10
H = np.log(10 / np.sqrt(2 * np.pi * np.e))       # information gained, in nats
print("evidence %.3f,  H = %.2f nats,  steps with 100 live points = %.0f" % (Z, H, 100 * H))
print("a posterior 1e6 times smaller than the prior: %.0f x live points" % np.log(1e6))

Output

evidence 0.251,  H = 0.88 nats,  steps with 100 live points = 88
a posterior 1e6 times smaller than the prior: 14 x live points

Questions people ask

Why do samplers appear in a course about likelihood-free inference? Because two of the three neural methods end with one. NLE and NRE learn a likelihood or a ratio, and the posterior then has to be sampled. Only NPE produces samples directly.

What does it mean for a chain to have converged? That it has forgotten where it started and is visiting regions in proportion to their probability. In physics this is thermal equilibrium, and the time taken to reach it is the relaxation time. The standard test, running several chains from different starting points and checking that they agree, is the test a simulator of matter would use.

Why does Hamiltonian Monte Carlo work so much better in many dimensions? A random walk covers distance as the square root of the number of steps. A particle with momentum travels in a straight line until a force turns it, so it covers distance in proportion to time. Giving the walker inertia replaces diffusion by ballistic motion.

When should I use nested sampling? When you need the evidence for model comparison, or when the posterior has several separated modes. It is less efficient than Hamiltonian Monte Carlo for a single smooth peak in many dimensions.

Can a sampler be wrong without my knowing? Yes. A chain trapped in one mode looks perfectly converged. This is metastability, the same phenomenon as supercooled water staying liquid, and no diagnostic that looks only at the chain can rule it out.

constructionStochastic quantisation and why sampling slows down

Sampling as dynamics. In 1981 Parisi and Wu proposed computing a Euclidean path integral by inventing a fictitious time and letting the field evolve by a Langevin equation whose equilibrium distribution is e−S. Averages over the path integral become long-time averages of the dynamics. Every Markov-chain sampler is a version of this idea: replace an integral that cannot be done by a process whose stationary state is the integrand.

What makes Hamiltonian Monte Carlo exact. Two properties of Hamiltonian flow do the work. It is reversible, and by Liouville's theorem it preserves phase-space volume, so the proposal has unit Jacobian and no correction factor is needed. The leapfrog integrator keeps both properties exactly even though it conserves energy only approximately, and the Metropolis step corrects the energy error. The method was built for lattice QCD, where the "parameters" are millions of gauge-field variables.

Critical slowing down. Near a phase transition the correlation length grows and local updates take longer and longer to produce an independent configuration. Samplers of posteriors suffer the same disease for the same reason: a long, narrow degeneracy between parameters is a soft mode, and a posterior with separated peaks is a system at a first-order transition, where the time to cross grows exponentially with the barrier height. The cures are also shared: collective moves along the soft direction, tempering, and reparametrisation.

Nested sampling and the microcanonical view. Writing Z = ∫ g(E) e−E dE separates what depends on the problem's geometry, the density of states g, from the Boltzmann factor. Nested sampling measures g once, after which the partition function at any temperature follows by reweighting. Flat-histogram methods in computational physics, such as Wang–Landau sampling, are built on the same separation.
