Chapter 15 of 39

Importance sampling and the work of changing a distribution

Often you can draw from one distribution but want averages under another. Reweighting the samples does it, in principle. How well it works in practice is governed by a result from the thermodynamics of driven systems.

physics

The connection. An importance weight is a Boltzmann factor for the energy difference between two systems, and the average weight is a free-energy difference.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Importance weight p/qexp(−ΔE/kBT) between two Hamiltonians
Ratio of two evidencesFree-energy perturbation (Zwanzig's formula)
Annealed importance samplingJarzynski's equality: free energy from work done out of equilibrium
Effective sample sizeOverlap between two ensembles

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: reweighting, and its limits

Importance sampling was developed by Kahn and colleagues around 1950 for calculating how neutrons penetrate shielding. Almost no neutrons get through, so simulating them naively wastes nearly all the effort. The trick is to simulate a modified process in which penetration is common and to correct each outcome by a weight. In 1954 Zwanzig derived the same idea for chemistry: the free-energy difference between two systems is the logarithm of an average, taken in one system, of the Boltzmann factor of their energy difference.

Both run into the same wall. If the two distributions overlap poorly, a handful of samples carry almost all the weight and the estimate is useless. The cure, used in chemistry since the 1970s, is to move from one system to the other through a chain of intermediates. In 1997 Jarzynski proved a remarkable exact result about such chains: even if the system is driven quickly and never equilibrates, the average of exp(−work/kBT) over many repetitions equals exp(−ΔF/kBT). Neal's annealed importance sampling, published in 2001, is the same identity discovered independently for statistics.

In SBI, importance weights are what make a learned posterior exact in principle: samples from the network are reweighted by the ratio of the true target to the network's density, whenever that ratio can be computed.

Reweighting

Suppose you can sample from a distribution q but want an average under p. Give each sample a weight w = p/q: large where q under-represents p, small where it over-represents it. Weighted averages are then correct. The simplest use is to compute an evidence by drawing parameters from the prior and averaging the likelihood:

Z = ⟨ L(θ) ⟩prior

The danger is uneven weights. If a few samples carry nearly all the weight, the average is effectively based on those few. The effective sample size measures this: it is the number of equally weighted samples that would give the same precision.

Worked example: evidence from prior samples

Take the Gaussian problem used throughout: prior variance 4, measurement variance 0.25, observed value 1.2. The exact evidence is a Gaussian of variance 4.25 evaluated at 1.2:

Z = 0.1634,    ln Z = −1.812

Drawing from the prior and weighting by the likelihood, the effective sample size is a fraction Z²/⟨L²⟩ = 0.29 of the number drawn.

Result. In one dimension, with a posterior a quarter the width of the prior, about 29% of the samples are effective. That fraction is roughly the ratio of posterior to prior volume, so in ten dimensions it would be about 10−6. Direct reweighting from the prior fails for the same reason rejection does.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
tau2, s2, x = 4.0, 0.25, 1.2
Z = stats.norm(0, np.sqrt(tau2 + s2)).pdf(x)
theta = rng.normal(0, np.sqrt(tau2), 2_000_000)  # draw from the prior
L = stats.norm(theta, np.sqrt(s2)).pdf(x)        # weight by the likelihood
print("exact Z = %.4f, ln Z = %.3f;  from prior samples Z = %.4f" % (Z, np.log(Z), L.mean()))
print("effective fraction of the samples: %.2f" % (L.mean()**2 / (L**2).mean()))

Output

exact Z = 0.1634, ln Z = -1.812;  from prior samples Z = 0.1635
effective fraction of the samples: 0.29

Get there in stages

The cure is to move from prior to posterior gradually. Define a ladder of intermediate distributions by raising the likelihood to a power β that climbs from 0 to 1:

pβ(θ) ∝ prior(θ) · L(θ)β

Start with samples from the prior. At each rung, reweight by the small factor LΔβ, then let the samples move a little under the new distribution. Each step is a small change, so the weights stay even. The product of the weight factors along the ladder estimates the evidence. This is annealed importance sampling, and with resampling of the population at each rung it is sequential Monte Carlo, the scheme behind ABC-SMC (ABC chapter) and sequential neural methods (amortisation chapter).

Worked example: how many rungs?

Define the "work" done on a sample as minus the log of its accumulated weight. Its average over samples, for the Gaussian problem with equally spaced rungs, can be computed exactly:

Number of rungsAverage work ⟨W⟩Excess over −ln Z = 1.812
1 (direct reweighting)11.119.29
26.134.32
102.480.67
1001.870.05
10001.820.005
Result. The average work always exceeds −ln Z and approaches it as the ladder is made finer. The excess measures how uneven the weights are, and it falls roughly in proportion to one over the number of rungs.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
tau2, s2, x = 4.0, 0.25, 1.2
def mean_lnL(beta):                              # average log-likelihood at inverse temperature beta
    prec = beta / s2 + 1 / tau2
    mu, var = (beta * x / s2) / prec, 1 / prec
    return -0.5 * np.log(2 * np.pi * s2) - 0.5 * ((x - mu)**2 + var) / s2
lnZ = stats.norm(0, np.sqrt(tau2 + s2)).logpdf(x)
for K in (1, 2, 10, 100, 1000):
    betas = np.arange(K) / K                     # each rung is sampled, then its weight to the next is added
    work = -sum(mean_lnL(b) / K for b in betas)
    print("%4d rungs: average work %.2f, excess over -ln Z (%.3f) = %.3f" % (K, work, -lnZ, work + lnZ))

Output

   1 rungs: average work 11.11, excess over -ln Z (1.812) = 9.294
   2 rungs: average work 6.13, excess over -ln Z (1.812) = 4.316
  10 rungs: average work 2.48, excess over -ln Z (1.812) = 0.670
 100 rungs: average work 1.87, excess over -ln Z (1.812) = 0.054
1000 rungs: average work 1.82, excess over -ln Z (1.812) = 0.005

More worked examples

Worked example: a proposal that is too narrow is fatal

The target is a unit Gaussian. Samples are drawn from a Gaussian of width s and reweighted. The fraction of the samples that is effective is √(2s² − 1)/s².

s = 2:  66%     s = 1:  100%     s = 0.8:  83%     s = 0.72:  37%     s ≤ 0.707:  zero
Result. A proposal twice too wide loses a third of the samples. A proposal 30 per cent too narrow makes the variance of the weights infinite, and the estimate never settles however many samples are drawn. Always err on the wide side. For a neural posterior this is the argument for preferring conservative error bars: a posterior that is too broad can be repaired by reweighting, and one that is too narrow cannot.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
for s in (2.0, 1.0, 0.8, 0.72):
    x = s * rng.standard_normal(2_000_000)               # proposal of width s
    w = np.exp(-x**2 / 2 + x**2 / (2 * s**2))            # target / proposal, up to a constant
    print("s = %.2f: effective fraction %.2f (formula %.2f)" % (s, w.sum()**2 / (w**2).sum() / w.size, np.sqrt(2*s*s - 1) / s**2))
# At s = 0.72 the sampled value disagrees with the formula and changes from run to run:
# the variance of the weights is almost infinite, which is the failure the example describes.

Output

s = 2.00: effective fraction 0.66 (formula 0.66)
s = 1.00: effective fraction 1.00 (formula 1.00)
s = 0.80: effective fraction 0.83 (formula 0.83)
s = 0.72: effective fraction 0.49 (formula 0.37)

Worked example: Jarzynski's equality for a shifted spring

A particle sits in a harmonic well of stiffness 1 at temperature 1. The well is suddenly moved a distance 2. The free energy does not change, because the well has the same shape. The work done on a particle at position x is the change in its energy, ½(x − 2)² − ½x² = 2 − 2x. Averaged over the starting positions, the work is 2.

Result. The average work, 2, exceeds the free-energy change, 0: the excess is dissipated. Yet the average of e−W is exactly 1 = e−ΔF. That average is carried by the 16 per cent of particles that started beyond x = 1 and so had work done by them. A free-energy estimate from fast switching always rests on a rare minority of trajectories, as an importance-sampling estimate rests on a few large weights.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x = rng.standard_normal(2_000_000)               # equilibrium positions, stiffness 1, temperature 1
W = 0.5 * (x - 2)**2 - 0.5 * x**2                # work done when the well jumps by 2
print("mean work %.2f,  <exp(-W)> = %.3f,  fraction with W < 0: %.1f%%" % (W.mean(), np.exp(-W).mean(), 100 * (W < 0).mean()))

Output

mean work 2.00,  <exp(-W)> = 1.002,  fraction with W < 0: 15.9%

Questions people ask

How do I know whether my weights can be trusted? Compute the effective sample size, which is the square of the sum of the weights divided by the sum of their squares. If ten thousand samples have an effective size of twenty, the estimate rests on twenty points and should not be believed.

Why does it fail in many dimensions? Because overlap between two distributions is a product over dimensions. If each dimension has an overlap of 0.9, a hundred dimensions have an overlap of 0.9100, about three in a hundred thousand. This is the curse of dimensionality once more.

What is 'work' in a statistical calculation? The accumulated change in log-density as the target is moved from the starting distribution to the final one while the samples are carried along. If the move is made slowly the work equals the free-energy difference. If it is made fast, the excess is dissipation, and it shows up as variance in the weights.

Does reweighting fix a bad neural posterior? Only if the network's posterior is broader than the truth everywhere that matters. Weights can remove probability from places where the network put too much. They cannot create samples where it put none.

constructionThe Jarzynski equality: free energies from irreversible processes

The table above is a thermodynamics experiment. Raising β is changing a control parameter of a system, as a piston compresses a gas. The quantity called work is the work done on the system. And −ln Z is the free-energy difference between the final state, the posterior, and the initial one, the prior.

The second law. For any way of changing the control parameter, the average work is at least the free-energy difference, ⟨W⟩ ≥ ΔF, with equality only for an infinitely slow, reversible change. The excess is dissipated. That is the right-hand column: 9.3 units dissipated by an instantaneous switch, almost none with a thousand small steps.

The equality behind the inequality. In 1997 Jarzynski showed that the second law is the shadow of an exact relation that holds however fast the change is made:

⟨ e−W ⟩ = e−ΔF

The average of the exponential of the work, over repetitions of the process, gives the equilibrium free-energy difference exactly, even though each repetition is far from equilibrium. The inequality ⟨W⟩ ≥ ΔF follows from it because the exponential is convex. Annealed importance sampling is this equality used as an algorithm: the importance weights are e−W, and their average is the evidence. With one rung it reduces to the free-energy perturbation formula of the neural methods chapter.

Why dissipation is the enemy. The average of e−W is dominated by rare repetitions with unusually small work. The more a process dissipates, the rarer those are and the more samples are needed, roughly edissipated work of them. With 9.3 units dissipated that is about ten thousand samples per effective one. Making the process slower, or steering it better, is the only remedy.

Learned steering. A normalising flow placed between the rungs can carry the samples most of the way from one distribution to the next, so that little reweighting is left to do. In thermodynamic terms it is a protocol with less dissipation. Stochastic normalising flows and the annealed flow samplers now used in lattice field theory are built on exactly this combination, with the Jarzynski weights correcting whatever the flow gets wrong.

Chapter 16 of 39

Inference in time: filtering and the Kalman filter

Many quantities change while you watch them: a position, a temperature, an instrument's gain. Measurements arrive one at a time, and you want the best current estimate without reprocessing the whole history. The recipe is two steps, repeated, and it is a path integral computed one slice at a time.

physics

The connection. A quantity that drifts while it is measured is a Brownian particle under observation. The probability of its whole history is a path integral.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
State-space modelLangevin equation plus a measuring device
Probability of a trajectoryOnsager-Machlup path integral
Kalman filterExact solution for a Brownian particle on a spring (Ornstein-Uhlenbeck)
Particle filterDiffusion Monte Carlo: walkers with weights and resampling

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: estimation while the system moves

Kalman published his filter in 1960. It solved a problem of the early space age: a spacecraft's position has to be estimated continuously from noisy radar and inertial measurements, while the craft moves under known dynamics. The filter alternates two steps, predicting forward with the dynamics and correcting with each new measurement, and for linear dynamics with Gaussian noise it is exactly optimal. It flew on the Apollo guidance computer and is now inside every satellite-navigation receiver.

The underlying mathematics was already in the physics of Brownian motion. A quantity that drifts randomly while being pulled back towards a centre is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, studied in 1930. The probability of a whole trajectory is a path integral of the kind Onsager and Machlup wrote down in 1953. The Kalman filter is the exact evaluation of that Gaussian path integral, done one time step at a time.

When the dynamics are not linear or the noise not Gaussian, the integral can no longer be done exactly. The particle filter, introduced by Gordon, Salmond and Smith in 1993, represents the distribution by a cloud of weighted samples that are moved, reweighted and resampled. Physicists had used the same scheme since the 1970s under the name diffusion Monte Carlo, to find the ground states of quantum systems.

Predict, then update

Keep a running belief about the current value: an estimate and its uncertainty. Each time step do two things.

  1. Predict. Time has passed and the quantity may have moved. Carry the estimate forward, and widen its uncertainty to allow for the movement.
  2. Update. A new measurement arrives. Combine it with the prediction, weighting each by how much you trust it. The uncertainty narrows.

When the motion and the measurement noise are Gaussian, every belief stays Gaussian and the two steps are simple formulas. That is the Kalman filter. Let the quantity take a random step of variance q each tick, and be measured with noise variance r. If the current belief has variance P:

predict:   P− = P + q      update:   K = P− / (P− + r),    x̂ ← x̂ + K (y − x̂),    P = (1 − K) P−

The number K, between 0 and 1, is the gain. It is the fraction of the way the estimate moves toward the new measurement. If the prediction is poor (P− large) the gain is near 1 and the measurement is believed. If the measurement is noisy (r large) the gain is small and the prediction is kept. The update is the Gaussian formula of the dictionary chapter, with yesterday's posterior serving as today's prior.

Worked example: four measurements of a wandering quantity

Take q = 1 and r = 4, and start knowing almost nothing: estimate 0, variance 10.

StepMeasurementGain KNew estimateIts error bar
12.10.731.541.71
22.90.502.211.41
32.40.432.291.31
43.30.402.701.27

The gain and the error bar settle down. Their limits come from requiring the variance to be the same before and after a full cycle, which gives a quadratic equation:

P² + qP − qr = 0   ⇒   P = ½ [ −q + √(q² + 4qr) ] = 1.56,    K = 0.39
Result. The error bar does not shrink to zero however long you watch, because the quantity keeps moving. It settles at √1.56 = 1.25, better than a single measurement (2.0) by a fixed factor. Each new measurement is given 39% weight, and the memory of older ones fades geometrically.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
q, r = 1.0, 4.0
est, P = 0.0, 10.0
for step, z in enumerate((2.1, 2.9, 2.4, 3.3), 1):
    P += q                                       # predict: the quantity has wandered since the last look
    K = P / (P + r)                              # gain
    est, P = est + K * (z - est), (1 - K) * P    # update
    print("step %d: gain %.2f, estimate %.2f, error bar %.2f" % (step, K, est, np.sqrt(P)))
P_inf = 0.5 * (-q + np.sqrt(q*q + 4*q*r))
print("limits: variance %.2f, error bar %.2f, gain %.2f" % (P_inf, np.sqrt(P_inf), P_inf / r))

Output

step 1: gain 0.73, estimate 1.54, error bar 1.71
step 2: gain 0.50, estimate 2.21, error bar 1.41
step 3: gain 0.43, estimate 2.29, error bar 1.31
step 4: gain 0.40, estimate 2.70, error bar 1.27
limits: variance 1.56, error bar 1.25, gain 0.39

When it is not Gaussian

  • Mildly nonlinear. Linearise the motion about the current estimate (the extended Kalman filter) or push a few chosen points through the nonlinear motion (the unscented filter).
  • Strongly nonlinear or many-peaked. Represent the belief by a cloud of samples. Move each one forward by simulating the motion, weight it by how well it explains the new measurement, and resample. This is the particle filter, and it is sequential Monte Carlo (importance sampling chapter) with time as the ladder.
  • Only a simulator. A particle filter needs only the ability to simulate one step forward, so it is already simulation-based inference. Neural versions learn the update step, giving an amortised filter.

The trade against the batch methods of earlier chapters is the usual one. A filter is fast and works in real time but commits to a summary of the past. A batch analysis of the whole record is slower and can revise its view of earlier times in the light of later data.

More worked examples

Worked example: how much to trust the newest measurement

A quantity wanders by a random step of variance 1 between measurements, and each measurement has noise variance 4. After many steps the filter settles down. The variance P of the prediction just before a measurement satisfies P² − P − 4 = 0, so P = (1 + √17)/2 = 2.56. The weight given to the new measurement is the gain, P/(P + 4).

Result. The gain is 0.39. Each new reading receives 39 per cent of the weight and the running estimate 61 per cent, and the uncertainty after the update is ±1.25. Older readings are forgotten geometrically, with a memory of about 1/0.39 = 2.6 steps. A filter is an average with a memory length set by how fast the quantity moves compared with how noisy the readings are.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
q, r = 1.0, 4.0                                  # process and measurement noise variances
P = q
for _ in range(100):                             # iterate the filter to its steady state
    P_pred = P + q
    K = P_pred / (P_pred + r)
    P = (1 - K) * P_pred
print("predicted variance %.2f, gain %.2f, uncertainty after update +/- %.2f, memory %.1f steps" % (P_pred, K, np.sqrt(P), 1 / K))

Output

predicted variance 2.56, gain 0.39, uncertainty after update +/- 1.25, memory 2.6 steps

Worked example: rain or sun? a hidden two-state chain

The weather is rain or sun and stays the same from one day to the next with probability 0.7. You cannot see it. You see only whether a colleague carries an umbrella: probability 0.9 on a rainy day and 0.2 on a sunny one. Before any observation rain and sun are equally likely. On day 1 there is an umbrella:

P(rain) = 0.5 × 0.9 / (0.5 × 0.9 + 0.5 × 0.2) = 0.818

Predict day 2 with the dynamics: 0.818 × 0.7 + 0.182 × 0.3 = 0.627. On day 2 there is an umbrella again:

P(rain) = 0.627 × 0.9 / (0.627 × 0.9 + 0.373 × 0.2) = 0.883
Result. 88 per cent. Each day is one multiplication by a two-by-two matrix, followed by a normalisation. In physics that matrix is the transfer matrix of a one-dimensional chain of spins, and the sum over all possible weather histories is its partition function.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
T = np.array([[0.7, 0.3], [0.3, 0.7]])           # transition matrix: rows = today (rain, sun)
umbrella = np.array([0.9, 0.2])                  # P(umbrella | rain), P(umbrella | sun)
belief = np.array([0.5, 0.5])
for day in (1, 2):
    if day > 1:
        belief = belief @ T                      # predict
        print("   predicted P(rain) = %.3f" % belief[0])
    belief = belief * umbrella                   # update on seeing an umbrella
    belief /= belief.sum()
    print("day %d: P(rain) = %.3f" % (day, belief[0]))

Output

day 1: P(rain) = 0.818
   predicted P(rain) = 0.627
day 2: P(rain) = 0.883

Questions people ask

Is filtering different from ordinary Bayesian inference? It is ordinary Bayesian inference organised in time. The posterior after each measurement becomes the prior for the next. Doing it in sequence avoids ever handling the whole data set at once.

What is the difference between filtering and smoothing? A filter estimates the state now from the data so far. A smoother estimates the state at an earlier time using data from before and after it, and is always at least as accurate. In the path-integral picture the smoother conditions on both ends of the path.

Why do particle filters degenerate? Because after a few updates most of the weight sits on a few particles, exactly as in importance sampling. Resampling duplicates the heavy particles and drops the light ones, which helps, at the price of losing diversity. In high dimensions the degeneracy returns quickly.

Where does this meet SBI? Many simulators are state-space models with hidden dynamics. Their likelihood is a path integral over the hidden trajectory. A particle filter estimates that likelihood, and neural methods learn it. Tracking a satellite's orbit from a sequence of observations is a problem of exactly this type.

constructionTransfer matrices and the path integral in slices

The belief is a partially summed path integral. The history of the quantity is a path. Its probability is the product of step probabilities, the action of the path-integral chapter, times a factor for each measurement. The belief at the current time is that weight integrated over all earlier positions. The filter never forms the whole integral. It integrates out one time slice per step:

belieft(xt) ∝ p(yt | xt) ∫ p(xt | xt−1) belieft−1(xt−1) dxt−1

This is the transfer-matrix method. The one-dimensional Ising model is solved the same way, by summing over one spin at a time and carrying forward a vector that summarises everything to the left. The kernel p(xt | xt−1) is the transfer matrix, the measurement factor is a site-dependent field, and the normalisation accumulated along the chain is the partition function, here the evidence for the whole record.

Feynman–Kac. In continuous time the predict step is the diffusion equation and the measurements act as a potential, so the belief obeys a Schrödinger equation in imaginary time. That statement is the Feynman–Kac formula, and it is the reason quantum-mechanical language (propagators, evolution operators) fits filtering so well.

The Kalman filter is the free theory. With linear motion and Gaussian noise the action is quadratic, every integral is Gaussian, and the mean and covariance carry everything. The steady-state equation for P is a fixed-point condition, the same kind of self-consistency that determines a propagator in a free field theory.

Smoothing is the classical path. To estimate the whole past trajectory given all the data, sweep forwards and then backwards. For the linear-Gaussian case the result is the path of least action with the measurements acting as sources, and it satisfies the corresponding Euler–Lagrange equation. Filtering uses the past only; smoothing uses past and future, as a classical trajectory is fixed by conditions at both ends.

The steady-state error bar is a balance of two rates. Diffusion adds uncertainty at rate q per step and measurement removes it at a rate set by 1/r. Their balance, P ≈ √(qr) when q is small, is the geometric mean of the two variances, the same structure as the width of a Brownian particle held by a spring.

← 15. Importance sampling17. Neural methods →

Chapter 17 of 39

Neural methods: NPE, NLE and NRE

ABC discards most simulations. Neural SBI keeps every one and fits a flexible function to them. There are three choices of what to fit.

physics

The connection. Each neural method fits an approximate distribution by minimising a relative entropy. That is the variational principle of statistical mechanics, with a network as the trial function.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
NPE: minimise the relative entropy to the posteriorGibbs-Bogoliubov variational bound on the free energy
NLE: learn p(data | θ)Learning the energy function from samples
NRE: learn a likelihood ratio by classificationMeasuring a free-energy difference between two ensembles
The networkA flexible trial distribution

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: how the neural methods arrived

By about 2015 the limits of ABC were well understood, and neural networks had become good at fitting probability distributions. Papamakarios and Murray proposed in 2016 to train a network, on simulated pairs of parameters and data, to output the posterior directly. Lueckmann and colleagues in 2017 and Greenberg and colleagues in 2019 refined the idea so that simulations could be concentrated near the observed data. This family is neural posterior estimation.

Two alternatives followed quickly. In 2019 Papamakarios, Sterratt and Murray trained a network on the same pairs to model the data given the parameters, which is the likelihood, and then sampled the posterior by MCMC. In 2020 Hermans, Begy and Louppe, and Durkan, Murray and Papamakarios, trained a classifier to tell matched pairs from mismatched ones. The classifier's output gives the ratio of likelihood to evidence, which is all that MCMC needs. The same year Cranmer, Brehmer and Louppe published a review, "The frontier of simulation-based inference", that gave the field its name and its map.

From the point of view of physics none of the three is new in kind. Fitting a flexible distribution by minimising a relative entropy is the variational method. Learning an energy from samples is the inverse problem of statistical mechanics. Estimating a ratio of normalising constants by telling two ensembles apart is how free-energy differences are measured.

MethodThe network learnsPhysics counterpart
NPE
neural posterior estimation		The posterior p(θ | x) directly. Give it data, it returns the distribution of θ.Learning the Boltzmann distribution itself, so equilibrium states can be drawn at once with no Metropolis run.
NLE
neural likelihood estimation		The likelihood p(x | θ), a fast stand-in for the simulator. You then sample θ with ordinary Monte Carlo.Learning the energy function from examples, then running Metropolis on the learned energy.
NRE
neural ratio estimation		Only the ratio p(x | θ)/p(x), by training a classifier.Working with energy differences only. Metropolis needs e−ΔE and never Z.

All three start the same way: draw many θ from the prior, simulate data for each, and keep the pairs (θ, x) as a training set. To see what each then does, take the Gaussian problem of the ABC chapter, where every answer can be written down: prior θ ~ N(0, τ²) and data x ~ N(θ, s²).

NPE: fit the posterior

The network outputs a distribution q(θ | x) and is trained to give high probability to the true θ of each simulated pair, by minimising the average of −ln q(θ | x).

Worked example: NPE with the simplest possible network

Let q be a Gaussian whose mean is a straight line in the data, a x, with a constant variance v. The loss to minimise over a and v is

L(a, v) = ⟨ (θ − a x)² ⟩ / 2v + ½ ln v

The average is over the training pairs. Setting ∂L/∂a = 0 gives a least-squares slope, a = ⟨θ x⟩/⟨x²⟩. Under the prior and the simulator, ⟨θ x⟩ = τ² and ⟨x²⟩ = τ² + s². Setting ∂L/∂v = 0 gives v equal to the leftover scatter.

a = τ² / (τ² + s²),     v = τ² s² / (τ² + s²)
Result. These are exactly the mean and variance of the true posterior from the dictionary chapter. A network that only ever saw simulated pairs, and was never told Bayes' rule, lands on the Bayesian answer. With τ = 2 and s² = 0.25: a = 0.941, √v = 0.485.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
tau, s2, N = 2.0, 0.25, 1_000_000
theta = rng.normal(0, tau, N)                    # parameters from the prior
x = theta + rng.normal(0, np.sqrt(s2), N)        # simulated data
a = (theta * x).mean() / (x * x).mean()          # least-squares slope: the loss minimised over a
v = ((theta - a * x)**2).mean()                  # leftover scatter: the loss minimised over v
print("learned from simulations: a = %.3f, sqrt(v) = %.3f" % (a, np.sqrt(v)))
print("exact posterior:          a = %.3f, sqrt(v) = %.3f" % (tau**2 / (tau**2 + s2), np.sqrt(tau**2 * s2 / (tau**2 + s2))))

Output

learned from simulations: a = 0.941, sqrt(v) = 0.485
exact posterior:          a = 0.941, sqrt(v) = 0.485

Real posteriors are not Gaussian, so NPE uses a normalising flow: a smooth, invertible change of variables that bends a standard bell curve into any shape. If θ = f(u) with u standard normal, then

q(θ) = N(u; 0, 1) · | du / dθ | the Jacobian keeps total probability equal to one

This is the rule for changing variables in an integral, the same one that tracks phase-space volume under a change of coordinates in mechanics. It is derived, with a lognormal as the worked case and an experiment to run, in the flows chapter. The worked example is a flow with the simplest map, a shift and a stretch: θ = a x + √v · u.

NPE is amortised: once trained it gives the posterior for any new data set in milliseconds. What that buys and what it costs is the subject of the amortisation chapter.

NLE: fit the likelihood

Here the network models the data given the parameter, q(x | θ), again by minimising −ln q over the training pairs. For the Gaussian problem the same algebra gives a mean of θ and a variance of s²: the network has learned the simulator's noise model. You then multiply by the prior and sample θ with Metropolis, exactly as if a formula had been given.

NLE costs a sampling run for each data set, but it has two advantages. The prior can be changed afterwards without retraining, and several independent measurements combine by adding their learned log-likelihoods, as energies add for independent subsystems.

NRE: fit a ratio with a classifier

Make two piles of pairs. In pile A each x was simulated from its θ. In pile B the x values are shuffled so each is paired with an unrelated θ. Train a classifier to output the probability d(θ, x) that a pair came from pile A.

Worked example: what the best classifier knows

Pile A has density p(x | θ) p(θ). Pile B has density p(x) p(θ). With equal pile sizes, the probability that a pair is from A is, by Bayes' rule for two classes,

d = p(x | θ) p(θ) / [ p(x | θ) p(θ) + p(x) p(θ) ]   ⇒   d / (1 − d) = p(x | θ) / p(x)
Result. The odds reported by a perfect classifier equal the likelihood-to-evidence ratio. Multiply by the prior and you have the posterior. No density was ever modelled.

For the Gaussian problem the ratio can be written out, and its logarithm is a quadratic in θ and x:

ln r(θ, x) = −(x − θ)² / 2s² + x² / 2(τ² + s²) + ½ ln[ (τ² + s²) / s² ]

So for this problem a classifier with quadratic features is already exact.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
tau2, s2 = 4.0, 0.25
theta, x = 0.7, 1.2
direct = stats.norm(theta, np.sqrt(s2)).logpdf(x) - stats.norm(0, np.sqrt(tau2 + s2)).logpdf(x)
formula = -(x - theta)**2 / (2 * s2) + x**2 / (2 * (tau2 + s2)) + 0.5 * np.log((tau2 + s2) / s2)
d = 1 / (1 + np.exp(-direct))                    # what a perfect classifier would output
print("ln r: from the densities %.4f, from the quadratic formula %.4f" % (direct, formula))
print("classifier output d = %.3f, odds d/(1-d) = %.3f = r" % (d, d / (1 - d)))

Output

ln r: from the densities 1.0860, from the quadratic formula 1.0860
classifier output d = 0.748, odds d/(1-d) = 2.962 = r

The physics reading: the log-ratio is minus an energy difference between two ensembles. As in Metropolis sampling, differences are all you need, and the partition function cancels.

Which to use

NPENLENRE
Speed for a new data setInstantNeeds a sampling runNeeds a sampling run
Change the prior laterRetrainFreeFree
High-dimensional data (images)Good, with a summary networkHard: must model every pixelGood
Combine independent measurementsAwkwardAdd log-likelihoodsAdd log-ratios
Typical failureError bars too narrowPoor fit in the tailsClassifier saturates when the posterior is much narrower than the prior

Sequential versions of all three run in rounds, concentrating simulations near the observed data. They are the neural counterpart of ABC-SMC and are described under amortisation. A fourth family, which learns a score in place of a density, has its own chapter.

More worked examples

Worked example: from a classifier's output to a posterior

A classifier has been trained to tell matched pairs (θ, x), where x was simulated from θ, from mismatched pairs. For the observed data and a trial value of θ it outputs 0.8: it is 80 per cent sure the pair is matched. The ratio of likelihood to evidence is d/(1 − d) = 0.8/0.2 = 4. The prior is flat between 0 and 10, a density of 0.1.

Result. The posterior density at that value is 4 × 0.1 = 0.4. An output of 0.5 means the data do not distinguish that value from the prior, and an output of 0.1 gives a density of 0.011. The whole posterior is the prior multiplied by this ratio, evaluated on a grid or explored by MCMC. The log of the ratio, ln 4 = 1.39, is a free-energy difference between two ensembles.
Show the code 
prior_density = 0.1                              # flat between 0 and 10
for d in (0.8, 0.5, 0.1):
    ratio = d / (1 - d)
    print("classifier output %.1f: ratio %.3f, posterior density %.3f" % (d, ratio, ratio * prior_density))

Output

classifier output 0.8: ratio 4.000, posterior density 0.400
classifier output 0.5: ratio 1.000, posterior density 0.100
classifier output 0.1: ratio 0.111, posterior density 0.011

Worked example: why few training points give narrow error bars

A density estimator is fitted, by maximum likelihood, to N samples from a unit Gaussian. The fitted variance is the mean squared deviation from the fitted mean, and on average that is (N − 1)/N of the true variance, because the fitted mean sits closer to the samples than the true mean does.

N = 10:  width 0.949     N = 50:  width 0.990     N = 200:  width 0.997
Result. With 10 effective training points the fitted distribution is 5 per cent too narrow, and the direction of the error is always the same. A flexible network has many more adjustable quantities than a mean, and each one fits the training set a little too well. This is the origin of the overconfidence seen in the curve-fitting chapter, and why it shrank with more simulations.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
for N in (10, 50, 200):
    samples = rng.standard_normal((200_000, N))
    fitted_var = samples.var(axis=1).mean()      # maximum-likelihood variance, averaged over repeats
    print("N = %3d: fitted width %.3f (formula %.3f)" % (N, np.sqrt(fitted_var), np.sqrt((N - 1) / N)))

Output

N =  10: fitted width 0.949 (formula 0.949)
N =  50: fitted width 0.990 (formula 0.990)
N = 200: fitted width 0.997 (formula 0.997)

Questions people ask

Which of the three should I start with? Neural posterior estimation, in most cases. It needs no MCMC afterwards and its samples can be tested for coverage cheaply. Use likelihood estimation when you want to combine independent data sets, because learned likelihoods multiply. Use ratio estimation when the data are high-dimensional and only a few parameters matter.

How many simulations are needed? There is no general rule. For a handful of parameters and well-chosen summaries, a few thousand to a few tens of thousands is typical. The curve-fitting chapter shows a case where five thousand gave error bars up to 18 per cent too narrow and fifty thousand gave 1 to 5 per cent.

Why are the error bars sometimes too narrow? A network with finite training data learns a density that is somewhat too sharp, in the same way that a fitted model always fits its own training set too well. Proposals concentrated on one region make it worse unless they are corrected for. The only reliable defence is a coverage test.

Is the network learning physics? No. It is learning a statistical relationship between the simulator's inputs and outputs. It knows nothing the simulator does not, and it inherits every error the simulator has.

What exactly is the variational principle doing here? The loss of neural posterior estimation is the relative entropy from the true posterior to the network's, averaged over data sets. It is never negative, and it is zero only when the two agree. Minimising it is the same operation as minimising a variational free energy over a family of trial states.

constructionThree ways physicists already estimate a distribution

Each neural method is a learned version of a technique that computational physics developed for free energies.

NRE is free-energy perturbation. The ratio of two partition functions can be written as an average in one ensemble (Zwanzig, 1954):

Z1 / Z0 = ⟨ e−(E1 − E0) ⟩0

The practical version uses samples from both ensembles. Bennett's acceptance-ratio method (1976) gives the minimum-variance estimate of the free-energy difference, and it is a self-consistency condition on Fermi functions of the energy difference. That condition is the same equation as the maximum-likelihood fit of a logistic classifier that separates the two sets of samples. So "train a classifier to tell two ensembles apart and read off the log-odds" was the optimal free-energy estimator decades before it was called ratio estimation.

NLE is the inverse problem. Statistical mechanics usually goes from a Hamiltonian to its samples. The inverse problem, inferring couplings from observed configurations, is known as the inverse Ising problem or Boltzmann-machine learning. NLE does this for a simulator: from samples of data at each θ it learns an effective energy function for the data, which then stands in for the microscopic model.

NPE is a learned equilibrium sampler. Generating independent equilibrium configurations without a Markov chain is the aim of Boltzmann generators in molecular physics and of flow-based sampling in lattice field theory. NPE is the same construction with one addition: the distribution to be sampled depends on the data, so the network learns a whole family of Boltzmann distributions at once.

Why the Gaussian case is exactly solvable. The three worked examples in this chapter all closed in a line because the model is a free theory: a quadratic action, for which the classical solution and the propagator contain everything. Real posteriors are interacting theories, and a neural network is the non-perturbative tool for them, playing the role that lattice simulation plays for QCD.

← 16. Inference in time18. Curve fitting →

Chapter 18 of 39

A worked example: fitting a line, a quadratic and a cubic

One small data set, three models, and every method of the last five chapters run on it. The exact answer is known, so each method can be marked.

physics

The connection. A curve fit with Gaussian noise is a set of masses on springs in a heat bath: a free theory, in which every integral can be done.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Best-fit coefficientsRest position, K⁻¹f
Covariance of the coefficientsThermal fluctuations, K⁻¹
Evidence of a modelPartition function
Occam penalty for an extra coefficientChemical potential for an extra particle

Background: why start with a problem that is already solved

Fitting a polynomial by least squares is one of the oldest procedures in quantitative science. Legendre published it in 1805 and Gauss, who had used it earlier to recover the orbit of the asteroid Ceres, gave it its probabilistic justification in 1809: if the errors are Gaussian, the least-squares fit is the most probable one. With a Gaussian prior added, the whole posterior is Gaussian and can be written in closed form.

That is exactly why it is useful here. A method for intractable problems can only be judged on a problem where the answer is known. Physics uses the harmonic oscillator and the free field for the same purpose. They are the cases in which every integral can be done, and any new approximation scheme is first tested on them. A curve fit with Gaussian noise is the harmonic oscillator of data analysis.

The example is also less trivial than it looks. It contains a model that is too simple, a model with a redundant parameter, a degeneracy between two parameters, and a model-selection question. Each of these appears in real analyses, where it is harder to see.

The problem

There are 20 measurements yi at equally spaced positions xi between −1 and 1. Each has Gaussian noise of standard deviation σ = 0.3. We fit a polynomial,

yi = θ0 + θ1 xi + θ2 xi² + θ3 xi³ + noise line: θ0, θ1 only. Quadratic: add θ2. Cubic: add θ3.

The prior on every coefficient is a Gaussian of mean 0 and standard deviation τ = 2. The data were made from a quadratic with θ = (1.0, 0.8, −0.6). So the line is too simple, the quadratic is right, and the cubic has one parameter it does not need.

It helps to write the model with a matrix. Let X be the table whose row i is (1, xi, xi², …). Then the prediction for all 20 points at once is Xθ, and the data are y = Xθ + noise.

The physics of a curve fit: masses on springs

Take minus the log of likelihood times prior as an energy, as in the dictionary chapter. For this problem the energy is a quadratic function of the parameters. A quadratic energy is the energy of a set of masses joined by springs, so the two problems have the same solution, line for line.

physics

Coupled springs in a heat bath

Energy. E(q) = ½ qᵀK q − fᵀq

q: the displacements of the masses. K: the stiffness matrix; its entry Kjk says how hard displacing mass k pulls on mass j. f: the constant force applied to each mass.

Rest position. ⟨q⟩ = K⁻¹ f
Force balances stiffness.

Thermal jiggling. ⟨δq δqᵀ⟩ = kBT · K⁻¹
kBT is the temperature in energy units. Soft directions (small stiffness) jiggle most.

Partition function. Z = (2π kBT)d/2 (det K)−½ exp( fᵀK⁻¹f / 2kBT )
d is the number of masses.

inference

A polynomial fit with a Gaussian prior

Energy. E(θ) = |y − Xθ|² / 2σ² + |θ|² / 2τ²

Multiply out the square and compare. θ plays the displacements. The stiffness is K = XᵀX/σ² + 1/τ²: the Fisher matrix of the data plus the prior. The force is f = Xᵀy/σ²: the data pull the parameters.

Best fit. ⟨θ⟩ = K⁻¹ f
This is least squares, with a small pull towards zero from the prior.

Error bars. Cov(θ) = K⁻¹
The temperature is 1. A poorly constrained combination of parameters is a soft spring.

Evidence. The same Gaussian integral, with the constant terms kept. It is used for model comparison at the end of the chapter.

Because the energy is exactly quadratic, the posterior is exactly Gaussian. In field-theory language this is a free theory: no interactions, and every integral can be done. That is what makes it a good test bed.

The exact answer

Putting the 20 numbers into the formulas above gives the three posteriors.

Modelθ0 (intercept)θ1 (slope)θ2θ3
Truth1.0000.800−0.6000
Line0.773 ± 0.0670.724 ± 0.110––
Quadratic0.996 ± 0.1000.724 ± 0.110−0.607 ± 0.203–
Cubic0.996 ± 0.1000.914 ± 0.272−0.607 ± 0.203−0.288 ± 0.377

Two things are worth noticing before any simulation is run.

  • The line is confidently wrong. Its intercept is 0.773 ± 0.067, which is 3.4 standard deviations below the true value of 1. The error bar is correct for the model and wrong for the world, because the model is too simple. This is misspecification, and no sampler can detect it from the posterior alone.
  • An extra parameter can loosen the others. Adding the cubic term raises the error on the slope from 0.110 to 0.272. Over this range x and x³ look alike, so the data cannot tell a larger slope with a negative cubic term from a smaller slope without one. In the spring picture this is a soft mode: one combination of the two masses can move a long way at little cost in energy.

Step 1: summaries

ABC needs a few numbers to compare, not 20. The natural choice here is the ordinary least-squares coefficients, s = (XᵀX)⁻¹Xᵀy: two numbers for the line, three for the quadratic, four for the cubic. For this model they are sufficient: they hold everything the data say about θ, and the rest of the 20 numbers is pure noise.

Step 2: ABC

The recipe is the one from the ABC chapter. Draw θ from the prior. Simulate 20 noisy points. Compute their least-squares coefficients. Keep θ if those coefficients are within a distance ε of the observed ones. The distance is measured in units of the noise on each coefficient, so ε = 1 means "within about one standard deviation".

The table gives the result of four million simulations for each model.

Tolerance εLine (2 parameters)Quadratic (3)Cubic (4)Error bars too wide by
2.01 in 3101 in 3,4001 in 18,00030 to 40%
1.01 in 1,3001 in 24,0001 in 400,00011 to 22%
0.51 in 4,8001 in 220,000none in 4 million3 to 6% (line)

Entries are the fraction of simulations kept. The last column is the ratio of the ABC error bar to the exact one, where enough samples were kept to measure it.

Each extra parameter costs a factor of about 20 in simulations at ε = 1. This is the curse of dimensionality in its smallest form: the kept fraction is the volume of a small ball divided by the volume of the prior, and volume falls as a power of the number of dimensions.

Summaries matter as much as the tolerance. For the line, matching all 20 raw points instead of the two coefficients, and keeping the same number of simulations, gives error bars 1.8 times too wide in place of 1.1 times. The 18 extra numbers add noise to the distance and no information.

The physics reading: ABC keeps the states inside a thin shell around the observed summaries. That is a microcanonical ensemble, and the difficulty is the same as finding states at one exact energy by drawing them at random.

Try it

The experiment below runs ABC in your browser on the same 20 points. Blue dots are kept simulations. The red ellipses are the exact posterior (68 and 95 per cent regions). Move the tolerance and change the model.

The data, the exact best fit, and up to 40 kept curves
Intercept and slope: kept simulations against the exact posterior
Kept by ABCExact posteriorLinear fit to every simulation (dashed)
0simulations run
0kept by ABC (0%)
–ABC error bar ÷ exact (intercept, slope)
–linear fit error bar ÷ exact (intercept, slope)

Things to try:

  • With the line, lower the tolerance from 3 to 0.5. The blue cloud shrinks onto the red ellipses and the number kept collapses.
  • Switch to the cubic at ε = 1. Almost nothing is kept, however often you press the button.
  • Watch the dashed ellipses. They come from the same simulations, they sit on the exact answer for all three models, and they do not depend on the tolerance.

Step 3: use every simulation

The dashed ellipses are the simplest version of neural posterior estimation. No simulation is thrown away. A function is fitted to all of the pairs (θ, s): here a straight-line rule for the mean of θ given s, with a constant scatter around it. This is the worked example of the neural methods chapter with more than one parameter. Simulations far from the observed data are useless to ABC, but they still teach the fit how θ changes with s.

For this problem a straight-line rule is exactly right, and each of the three neural methods has a closed form to find:

MethodWhat it must learn hereIn the spring picture
NPEThe posterior of θ given s: a Gaussian with mean K⁻¹(XᵀX/σ²) s and covariance K⁻¹.The rest position and the jiggling, as a function of the applied force.
NLEThe distribution of s given θ: a Gaussian with mean θ and covariance σ²(XᵀX)⁻¹.The energy function itself, learned from examples and then sampled.
NREThe log of likelihood over evidence: a quadratic expression in θ and s.The energy difference between "this θ made this s" and "they are unrelated".

A real analysis does not know the answer is Gaussian, so it uses a general network. The table below is that test: the three methods as implemented in the sbi package with its default networks, each trained on 5,000 simulations drawn from the prior, twice with different random seeds.

Error bar ÷ exactLineQuadraticCubicLargest shift of the mean
NPE0.90 to 1.000.82 to 0.930.82 to 0.890.33 σ
NLE0.87 to 0.980.84 to 0.970.82 to 0.900.26 σ
NRE0.96 to 1.370.98 to 1.690.75 to 1.490.65 σ
ABC, ε = 1, same 5,000 simulations4 curves kept0.2 kept0.01 kept–

Each range covers all the coefficients of that model and both seeds. A value of 1.00 is the exact error bar. The ABC row is the expected number of kept simulations out of 5,000, from the table above.

All three methods give a usable posterior for all three models from 5,000 simulations, where ABC keeps a handful of curves or none. Going from two parameters to four did not need more simulations. The means are within a third of an error bar of the exact ones for NPE and NLE, and within two thirds for NRE.

The error bars are not exact, and the errors have a pattern. NPE and NLE are 0 to 18 per cent too narrow. NRE ranges from 25 per cent too narrow to 69 per cent too wide, and changes most between seeds. Narrow error bars are the dangerous direction, and nothing in the output warns of them. With ten times more simulations (50,000) NPE on the quadratic gives 0.95 to 0.99, so the shortfall here is a matter of too little training data and not a flaw of the method. In a real problem there is no exact answer to compare with, so the test has to be a coverage check.

Step 4: a diffusion model

NPE learns the posterior as a density. A diffusion model learns a force field instead: at every point in parameter space, which way to push a guess, and how hard, to move it towards the posterior. It is trained on the same pairs (θ, s) as before.

The construction has two halves. First, blur: add Gaussian noise of standard deviation n to the parameters, for every n from tiny to large. Second, learn to undo the blur: train a network to say, for a noisy θ, the noise level n and the summaries s, in which direction the clean θ lies. For the curve fit both halves can be written out exactly, and the physics is ordinary diffusion.

physics

A drop of ink spreading in water

Diffusion equation. ∂p/∂t = D ∇²p

p(q, t): the density of ink at position q and time t. D: the diffusion coefficient. ∇²: the sum of second derivatives in space.

Solution for a Gaussian drop. A drop with covariance C keeps its centre and becomes a Gaussian with covariance C + 2Dt. Every direction gains the same variance.

Running it backwards. Reversing the film needs a drift velocity v = −D ∇ln p on each particle. The gradient ∇ln p points towards where the ink is denser, so the reversed particles climb back towards the drop.

inference

A blurred posterior and its score

Blurred posterior. pn(θ | s) = N( μ, K⁻¹ + n² )

μ = K⁻¹f and K⁻¹ are the exact mean and covariance from the springs. n is the noise level, with n² in the role of 2Dt.

The score. ∇ln pn = −(K⁻¹ + n²)⁻¹ (θ − μ)
A spring force pulling θ towards the best fit. The spring is weak when n is large and reaches the stiffness K of the true posterior when n is zero.

Sampling. Start from a broad cloud and lower n while moving each point by dθ/dn = −n ∇ln pn. For this problem the path is θ = μ + (K⁻¹ + n²)½ z, with z a fixed standard normal draw for each point.

So for a curve fit the network has one thing to discover: a force that is linear in θ and in the summaries, with a stiffness that depends on the noise level. The experiment below uses the exact force for the straight-line fit and shows what sampling looks like. Each dot is one guess for (intercept, slope), and the same guesses are drawn as lines over the data on the left. The view on the right zooms in as the noise falls.

The data, the exact best fit, and 40 of the guesses drawn as lines
Intercept and slope: 1,500 guesses at this noise level
Guesses at this noise levelExact posterior
–noise level n
–width of the cloud (intercept, slope)
–exact error bars (intercept, slope)
–cloud ÷ exact

At n = 2 the guesses are as broad as the prior and the lines on the left point everywhere. Once the noise falls below the size of the error bars, about 0.1, the cloud stops shrinking and takes the shape of the posterior, tighter in the intercept than in the slope. The final cloud is a set of independent posterior samples.

A real diffusion model does not know the force and has to learn it. The table gives two such methods from the sbi package on the three fits, with the same 5,000 simulations and two seeds as before. The first is score-based posterior estimation, which is the construction above. The second is flow matching, a close relative that learns the velocity carrying each noise point to its posterior point directly.

Error bar ÷ exactLineQuadraticCubicLargest shift of the mean
Score-based (NPSE)0.90 to 1.130.96 to 1.150.98 to 1.160.38 σ
Flow matching (FMPE)0.96 to 1.040.85 to 1.070.95 to 1.020.26 σ
NPE with a flow, from Step 30.90 to 1.000.82 to 0.930.82 to 0.890.33 σ

On this problem both diffusion-type methods came closer to the exact error bars than the flow of Step 3. Flow matching is right on average (mean 1.00) and within 15 per cent everywhere (0.85 to 1.07); the flow of Step 3 averaged 0.88. The score-based model runs from 0.90 to 1.16, and is 5 per cent too wide on average, which is the safer direction. This is one small Gaussian problem with two seeds, and it should not be read as a ranking of the methods in general.

The cost is at sampling time. NPE returns a posterior sample from one pass through its network. A diffusion model has to follow the force through many small steps of falling noise for each sample, as the experiment shows.

Step 5: which model?

The evidence Z is the probability of the data under a model, averaged over its prior. For a Gaussian problem it is the partition function of the spring system, and it can be computed exactly.

ModelParametersMisfit χ²ln ZOdds against the quadratic
Constant179.2−37.371 in 109
Line236.0−18.751 in 8.8
Quadratic326.9−16.57–
Cubic426.2−17.951 in 4.0
Quartic526.2−19.011 in 11

The misfit can only fall as parameters are added: the cubic fits a little better than the quadratic (26.2 against 26.9). The evidence still prefers the quadratic, by a factor of 4. This is Occam's razor as an entropy cost. The cubic's extra parameter could have been anywhere within its prior width of 2, and the data narrow it to 0.377. That shrinkage costs about ln(2 / 0.377) = 1.7 in ln Z, and the better fit pays back only 0.35.

The physics reading: letting the number of parameters vary is a grand canonical ensemble. Each added parameter is an added particle, and the Occam cost is its chemical potential. A particle is added only if it lowers the energy by more than that cost.

More worked examples

Worked example: predicting beyond the data

The data lie between x = −1 and 1. Use each fitted model to predict the curve at x = 1.5 and x = 2, outside that range. The true quadratic gives 0.85 and 0.20.

ModelPrediction at x = 1.5Prediction at x = 2
Truth0.850.20
Line1.86 ± 0.182.22 ± 0.23
Quadratic0.72 ± 0.420.02 ± 0.77
Cubic0.03 ± 0.99−1.91 ± 2.64
Result. The line is the most confident and the most wrong: at x = 2 it misses by nearly nine of its own standard deviations. The quadratic is right within its error. The cubic is right only because its error bar is enormous. Inside the data all three fits look acceptable to the eye. Extrapolation is where a wrong model is exposed and an over-flexible one is useless.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
x = np.linspace(-1, 1, 20)
y = np.array([-0.0578, -0.4708, 0.2324, 0.0531, 0.1977, 0.7224, 0.7013, 0.6845, 1.3723, 0.2087,
              0.9230, 0.9663, 1.6518, 1.0614, 1.3489, 1.0267, 1.7997, 1.3545, 0.4366, 1.2630])
sigma, tau = 0.3, 2.0            # noise per point, prior width of each coefficient
print("truth at 1.5 and 2.0: %.2f, %.2f" % (1 + 0.8*1.5 - 0.6*1.5**2, 1 + 0.8*2 - 0.6*4))
for d, name in ((2, "line"), (3, "quadratic"), (4, "cubic")):
    X = np.vander(x, d, increasing=True)
    C = np.linalg.inv(X.T @ X / sigma**2 + np.eye(d) / tau**2)
    mu = C @ X.T @ y / sigma**2
    for xs in (1.5, 2.0):
        v = xs ** np.arange(d)
        print("%-9s at x = %.1f: %5.2f +/- %.2f" % (name, xs, v @ mu, np.sqrt(v @ C @ v)))

Output

truth at 1.5 and 2.0: 0.85, 0.20
line      at x = 1.5:  1.86 +/- 0.18
line      at x = 2.0:  2.22 +/- 0.23
quadratic at x = 1.5:  0.72 +/- 0.42
quadratic at x = 2.0:  0.02 +/- 0.77
cubic     at x = 1.5:  0.03 +/- 0.99
cubic     at x = 2.0: -1.91 +/- 2.64

Questions people ask

If the answer is known exactly, why run ABC or a neural network at all? To measure them. The table of ratios to the exact error bar is the only kind of evidence that a method works, and it can be produced only where the truth is available. On a real problem you have the method's answer and nothing to compare it with.

Why did ABC need about 300 times more simulations for the cubic than for the line? Because it has to land inside a small ball in a space with one dimension per summary, and the chance of doing so falls by a roughly constant factor for each dimension added. Here that factor was about 20 per parameter.

The neural error bars were too narrow by up to 18 per cent. Is that acceptable? It depends on the use. For a forecast it may be. For a published constraint it is not, and the chapter shows that more training simulations removed most of it. The important point is that nothing in the network's output revealed the problem. Only the comparison did.

Would the same ranking of methods hold on a harder problem? Not necessarily. This problem is linear and Gaussian, which favours every method. Its value is as a calibration exercise, not as a league table.

Why is the straight line's intercept so far from the truth? Because the data curve downwards at both ends, and a line cannot. It compromises by sitting lower in the middle. The model is wrong, the fit is the best wrong answer, and its error bar describes only the scatter of that wrong answer.

What this example shows

  • A curve fit with Gaussian noise is a set of springs in a heat bath. Best fit is the rest position, the covariance is the inverse stiffness, and the evidence is the partition function.
  • ABC is correct but wasteful. Each extra parameter cost about 20 times more simulations here, and at 5,000 simulations it kept four curves for the line and none for the cubic.
  • A diffusion model learns a force field in place of a density. For a curve fit that force is a spring pulling towards the best fit, and sampling is diffusion run backwards.
  • Methods that fit a function to every simulation gave an answer for all three models from the same 5,000 simulations. Their error bars were not exact, which is why the checks chapter exists.
  • A model that is too simple gives a small error bar around the wrong value. A model with an unneeded parameter is penalised by the evidence, not by the fit.

Chapter 19 of 39

Flows: reshaping a Gaussian into anything

A normalising flow is an invertible map that turns a simple distribution into a complicated one. The whole idea rests on one line of calculus, and you can watch it work below.

physics

The connection. A normalising flow is a change of variables that carries probability with it. Physics calls this transport of a density, and it obeys the continuity equation.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Invertible map from noise to the targetFlow of a fluid; a canonical transformation
Jacobian determinantChange of phase-space volume (Liouville's theorem when it is 1)
Continuous flowContinuity equation ∂ρ/∂t + ∇·(ρv) = 0
Flow that turns the target into a GaussianTrivialising map of lattice field theory

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: reshaping a distribution

The rule for changing variables in a probability density is as old as the calculus. Using it as a modelling tool is recent. Tabak and Vanden-Eijnden suggested in 2010 that a complicated distribution could be described by the map that turns it into a Gaussian. Rezende and Mohamed made the idea practical for neural networks in 2015 and named it a normalising flow. The technical obstacle is the Jacobian determinant, which is expensive for a general map. The architectures that followed, by Dinh and colleagues in 2016 and by Papamakarios, Pavlakou and Murray in 2017, are built so that the determinant is cheap. Durkan and colleagues added flexible spline transformations in 2019. In 2018 Chen and colleagues showed that the map could instead be defined as the solution of a differential equation.

Physics has two long traditions here. Fluid mechanics describes exactly this: a density carried along by a velocity field and obeying the continuity equation. And in 2009 Lüscher proposed, for lattice field theory, a change of variables that turns an interacting theory into a trivial one. From 2019 Albergo, Kanwar and Shanahan used normalising flows to construct such maps numerically. The method went from physics to machine learning and back within a decade.

In SBI a flow is usually the object that represents the posterior in neural posterior estimation, and the likelihood in neural likelihood estimation.

The one-line rule

Take a random number u with a known density pu, and pass it through a smooth, invertible function: θ = f(u). Probability is conserved. The chance of finding u in a small interval du must equal the chance of finding θ in the interval dθ it is mapped to:

pθ(θ) |dθ| = pu(u) |du|   ⇒   pθ(θ) = pu(u) · |du/dθ|,    u = f−1(θ)

Where the map stretches (dθ/du large) the probability is spread thin and the density falls. Where it squeezes, the density rises. That factor |du/dθ| is the Jacobian. It is the same bookkeeping as the continuity equation for a fluid: mass is neither created nor destroyed, so density changes only by compression or expansion.

Worked example 1: shift and stretch

Let u be a standard Gaussian and θ = μ + σ u. Then u = (θ − μ)/σ and du/dθ = 1/σ:

p(θ) = (1/σ) · (1/√2π) exp[ −(θ − μ)² / 2σ² ]
Result. A Gaussian of mean μ and width σ. The 1/σ in front of every Gaussian is a Jacobian: stretching the axis by σ lowers the density by the same factor.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
mu, sigma = 1.0, 0.5
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
theta = mu + sigma * rng.standard_normal(1_000_000)          # push standard Gaussian numbers through the map
t = 1.3
rule = stats.norm.pdf((t - mu) / sigma) / sigma               # base density at u, times the Jacobian 1/sigma
hist = ((theta > t - 0.01) & (theta < t + 0.01)).mean() / 0.02
print("samples: mean %.3f, sd %.3f" % (theta.mean(), theta.std()))
print("density at %.1f: rule %.4f, N(mu, sigma) %.4f, histogram %.3f" % (t, rule, stats.norm(mu, sigma).pdf(t), hist))

Output

samples: mean 1.000, sd 0.500
density at 1.3: rule 0.6664, N(mu, sigma) 0.6664, histogram 0.676

Worked example 2: a lognormal from a normal

Now exponentiate: θ = exp(μ + σ u). The map is invertible, with u = (ln θ − μ)/σ, and it sends the whole real line to positive numbers. Differentiate the inverse:

du/dθ = 1 / (σ θ)

Insert into the rule:

p(θ) = [ 1 / (θ σ √2π) ] exp[ −(ln θ − μ)² / 2σ² ],    θ > 0
Result. This is the lognormal distribution. The extra 1/θ is the Jacobian of the exponential: the map stretches large values more than small ones, so the right-hand side is thinned into a long tail and the left is piled up.

The three usual summaries no longer coincide, because the map is not linear:

QuantityFormulaμ = 0, σ = 0.5Why
Medianeμ1.000The median of u is 0, and a monotonic map carries medians to medians
Meaneμ + σ²/21.133The long right tail pulls the average up
Peak (mode)eμ − σ²0.779The 1/θ Jacobian favours small values

Quantiles pass straight through a monotonic map; means and peaks do not. That is why a posterior should be summarised by quantiles when it has been transformed.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
mu, sigma = 0.0, 0.5
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
theta = np.exp(mu + sigma * rng.standard_normal(2_000_000))
grid = np.linspace(0.01, 5, 50000)
density = stats.norm.pdf((np.log(grid) - mu) / sigma) / (sigma * grid)      # base density times Jacobian 1/(sigma theta)
print("median: samples %.3f, formula %.3f" % (np.median(theta), np.exp(mu)))
print("mean:   samples %.3f, formula %.3f" % (theta.mean(), np.exp(mu + sigma**2 / 2)))
print("peak:   of the density %.3f, formula %.3f" % (grid[density.argmax()], np.exp(mu - sigma**2)))

Output

median: samples 1.000, formula 1.000
mean:   samples 1.134, formula 1.133
peak:   of the density 0.779, formula 0.779

See it

The left panel is the starting Gaussian. The middle panel is the map. The right panel shows 6,000 Gaussian samples after passing through the map (bars) against the density from the one-line rule (line). They agree because the rule is exact.

1. Start: standard Gaussian in u
2. The map θ = f(u)
3. Result: density in θ
Mapped samplesDensity from the change-of-variables rule
–median of θ
–mean of θ
–stretch dθ/du at u = 0
–density at f(0)

Things to try

  • Lognormal, raise σ. The map curves more steeply, the tail lengthens, and the mean pulls away from the median while the median stays at eμ.
  • Shift and stretch. A straight-line map can only move and rescale. The shape stays Gaussian whatever you do.
  • Two peaks, raise a. The map is steep at the centre, so the middle of the Gaussian is spread thin and a dip opens. One smooth, monotonic function has turned one peak into two. The density at the centre is the Gaussian's peak value divided by the stretch there, 0.399/(1 + a k).

From one map to a neural network

Chain several maps

Apply maps in sequence, θ = f3(f2(f1(u))). By the chain rule the Jacobians multiply, so their logarithms add:

ln p(θ) = ln pu(u) − Σlayers ln |dfi/dz|

Each layer is simple. The composition can be very flexible. The lognormal above is already a two-layer flow: a shift and stretch followed by an exponential.

Several parameters at once

For a vector θ the derivative becomes a matrix and the factor becomes the absolute value of its determinant:

p(θ) = pu(u) · | det ∂u/∂θ |

A determinant of a general matrix is expensive, so flow layers are designed to have triangular Jacobians, whose determinant is just the product of the diagonal entries. Two standard designs do this. An autoregressive layer transforms each coordinate using only the ones before it. A coupling layer leaves half the coordinates alone and uses them to set the shift and stretch of the other half. The shifts and stretches are the outputs of ordinary neural networks, which is where the learning happens.

Training

Given example values of θ, adjust the network so the examples are probable, that is, minimise the average of −ln p(θ) computed from the formula above.

Worked example 3: training the simplest flow

Fit θ = μ + σ u to n examples θi. The loss is

L(μ, σ) = ln σ + (1/n) Σ (θi − μ)² / 2σ²

Setting ∂L/∂μ = 0 gives μ = the sample average. Setting ∂L/∂σ = 0 gives σ² = the sample variance.

Result. Training the one-layer flow is computing a mean and a standard deviation. The term ln σ, the log-Jacobian, is what stops the fit from cheating: without it the loss could be made as small as you like by taking σ to infinity.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import minimize
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
data = rng.normal(1.5, 0.7, 5000)                # examples to fit
def loss(params):
    mu, log_sigma = params
    return log_sigma + ((data - mu)**2).mean() / (2 * np.exp(2 * log_sigma))
fit = minimize(loss, [0.0, 0.0])
print("trained flow:  mu = %.4f, sigma = %.4f" % (fit.x[0], np.exp(fit.x[1])))
print("sample values: mean %.4f, sd %.4f" % (data.mean(), data.std()))

Output

trained flow:  mu = 1.4968, sigma = 0.6968
sample values: mean 1.4968, sd 0.6968

Making it conditional

For NPE the map must depend on the data. Let the shift and stretch be functions of x: θ = μ(x) + σ(x) u. Each observation then selects its own map and so its own posterior. The linear-Gaussian example of the neural methods chapter is exactly this, with μ(x) = a x.

What a flow gives you, and what it costs

You getBecause
Exact samples in one passDraw a Gaussian, apply the map
An exact, normalised densityInvert the map and apply the rule. No partition function to compute.
Positive-only or bounded parameters handled cleanlyEnd with an exponential or a sigmoid, as the lognormal does

The cost is the restriction to invertible maps with cheap determinants. A smooth invertible map cannot tear space, so a flow from one Gaussian finds it hard to represent peaks that are completely separated: it leaves a thin bridge of probability between them, as the two-peak example shows at its centre. Score-based models (scores chapter) drop the invertibility requirement and pay with slower sampling.

Physics reading. A flow is a change of coordinates under which a complicated distribution becomes a free, Gaussian one. In lattice field theory the same construction is used to map a free field onto an interacting one, so that field configurations can be sampled without a long Markov chain.

More worked examples

Worked example: a map that is not invertible

Take a standard Gaussian variable u and square it: θ = u². Two values of u, plus and minus √θ, land on the same θ. The density of θ is the sum of the contributions from both, each with the Jacobian factor 1/(2√θ):

p(θ) = 2 × e−θ/2/√(2π) × 1/(2√θ) = e−θ/2 / √(2πθ)
Result. This is the chi-squared distribution with one degree of freedom. At θ = 1 it gives 0.242. The density can still be found, but only by summing over branches, and the inverse is not unique. A normalising flow forbids such folds so that every point has exactly one pre-image and the density is a single term.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
theta = 1.0
density = 2 * stats.norm.pdf(np.sqrt(theta)) / (2 * np.sqrt(theta))      # two branches, each with its Jacobian
print("density at 1: %.3f   chi-squared(1): %.3f" % (density, stats.chi2(1).pdf(theta)))

Output

density at 1: 0.242   chi-squared(1): 0.242

Worked example: turning a uniform number into an exponential one

Take u uniform between 0 and 1 and set θ = −ln(1 − u). The inverse is u = 1 − e−θ, and its derivative is e−θ. The density of u is 1, so the change-of-variables rule gives p(θ) = 1 × e−θ.

Result. An exponential distribution, made from a uniform one by a single invertible map. This is how computers generate random numbers from most distributions, and it is the simplest normalising flow. The map is the inverse of the cumulative distribution. A neural flow learns such a map when the cumulative distribution is not known.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
theta = -np.log(1 - rng.random(1_000_000))       # the map applied to uniform numbers
print("mean %.3f, sd %.3f (exponential: 1, 1);  P(theta > 2) = %.4f (exp(-2) = %.4f)" % (theta.mean(), theta.std(), (theta > 2).mean(), np.exp(-2)))

Output

mean 1.001, sd 1.002 (exponential: 1, 1);  P(theta > 2) = 0.1357 (exp(-2) = 0.1353)

Questions people ask

Why must the map be invertible? Because the density at a point is computed by sending that point back to the simple distribution and reading off the density there. Without an inverse there is no way to evaluate the probability of a given value, only to sample.

Can a flow represent any distribution? In principle, with enough layers. In practice smooth maps find it hard to turn one blob into two well-separated blobs, because the region between them must be squeezed to almost nothing. Posteriors with separated modes are where flows most often fail.

What is the Jacobian doing, physically? It records how much a small volume is stretched or squeezed by the map. Probability is conserved, so where volume expands the density must fall. In Hamiltonian mechanics the determinant is exactly one. That is Liouville's theorem, and it is why phase-space density is conserved along trajectories.

How is a flow different from a diffusion model? A flow is a deterministic map with a density that can be evaluated. A diffusion model reaches the same target by a noisy process and learns a force, not a map. The continuous versions of the two are closely related: every diffusion has an equivalent deterministic flow with the same distributions at each time.

constructionJacobians, Liouville and trivialising maps

The Jacobian is the change of measure. In a path integral, changing integration variables multiplies the measure by a Jacobian determinant, and that factor can carry physics: it must be kept or the answer is wrong. The log-Jacobian term in a flow's training loss is the same object. Dropping it is the error of changing variables in an integral and forgetting the du/dθ.

Volume-preserving flows are Hamiltonian. Liouville's theorem says Hamiltonian evolution has unit Jacobian. Flow layers built to preserve volume exactly are discrete versions of such evolution. They can rearrange probability but cannot concentrate it, which is why practical flows add layers that stretch and squeeze.

A flow is a trivialising map. In lattice field theory a trivialising map (Lüscher, 2010) is a change of field variables under which an interacting theory becomes a free one, so that sampling is immediate. A normalising flow is that map, found numerically. The name says it: "normalising" means carrying the distribution to the normal, non-interacting form. Flow-based samplers for lattice field theory, introduced in 2019, train exactly the networks described in this chapter, with the action as the target.

The continuous version obeys a continuity equation. If the map is built up over a fictitious time by moving every point with a velocity field v, probability is transported like the density of a fluid:

∂p/∂t + ∇·(p v) = 0

The log-density of a moving point changes at the rate −∇·v, the continuous form of adding log-Jacobians layer by layer. This is the bridge to the diffusion chapter, where the velocity field is fixed by a score.

The renormalisation group is a flow to a Gaussian. Coarse-graining a system near a trivial fixed point drives its distribution toward a free theory, shedding interaction terms scale by scale. A normalising flow run in the "normalising" direction does the same thing, layer by layer, and multi-scale flow architectures make the correspondence explicit by assigning layers to length scales.

← 18. Curve fitting20. Amortisation →

Chapter 20 of 39

Amortisation: train once, infer many times

A trained NPE network returns a posterior for any data set in milliseconds. That speed is paid for in advance, and it is worth knowing when the deal is good.

physics

The connection. Amortised inference learns the answer as a function of the data, as an equation of state gives the state for every value of the external field and not just one.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
One network for every data setEquation of state: the solution as a function of external conditions
Inference for one observed data setSolving at one value of the field
Amortisation gapError of a global approximation where a local one would be exact

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: paying once

The word comes from finance: a large cost paid once and spread over many uses. In machine learning it was introduced by Gershman and Goodman in 2014, for inference networks that learn to output a posterior for any input and so avoid solving a fresh optimisation problem each time. The variational autoencoder of Kingma and Welling, from 2013, is the best-known example.

For SBI the attraction is clearest in gravitational-wave astronomy. Analysing one binary merger with conventional samplers takes hours to days. A network trained once on simulated signals, as in the DINGO work of Dax and colleagues from 2021, returns a posterior in seconds, for every event the detector records. Coverage tests also become affordable, because testing a method on a thousand simulated data sets requires a thousand posteriors.

The physical counterpart is the difference between solving a problem at one value of an external field and knowing the equation of state. The equation of state costs more to obtain and is less accurate at any single point than a dedicated calculation there, but it answers every question at once.

What is being traded

A sampler does all its work after the data arrive, and does it again for every new data set. An amortised network does its work before any data arrive, by learning the map from data to posterior over the whole range the prior allows. "Amortised" is the accountant's word for a fixed cost spread over many uses.

Worked example: when does amortisation pay?

Suppose a sampler needs Cs simulator calls for each data set, and a network needs Ct calls once, for training. For M data sets the costs are M Cs and Ct.

Result. Amortisation wins when M > Ct/Cs. With 100,000 training simulations against 5,000 per sampling run, the break-even is 20 data sets. For one data set it is a poor bargain. For a survey of a million objects, or for the hundreds of repeated analyses that a coverage test needs, it is the only practical route.
Show the code 
training, per_run = 100_000, 5_000
print("break-even at %d data sets" % (training // per_run))
for M in (1, 20, 1000):
    print("%4d data sets: sampler %9d calls, network %d calls" % (M, M * per_run, training))

Output

break-even at 20 data sets
   1 data sets: sampler      5000 calls, network 100000 calls
  20 data sets: sampler    100000 calls, network 100000 calls
1000 data sets: sampler   5000000 calls, network 100000 calls

The physics analogue is the difference between an equation of state and a single simulation. One run tells you the pressure at one density and temperature. An equation of state, fitted once to many runs, answers for every state point at once.

The amortisation gap

A network trained over the whole prior is never quite as accurate at one particular data set as a method that concentrated on it. The shortfall is called the amortisation gap, and it is largest where training examples were sparse.

Worked example: the gap for the linear-Gaussian NPE
