In the neural methods chapter the simplest NPE fitted a slope a = τ²/(τ² + s²) by least squares. From N training pairs a least-squares slope carries a statistical error of

σa = √[ v / (N (τ² + s²)) ]

where v is the posterior variance. The predicted posterior mean is a x, so its error is x σa: it grows with distance from the centre of the training data. For τ = 2, s² = 0.25 (posterior s.d. 0.485):

Training pairs NError at x = 1.2 (typical)Error at x = 6 (rare, 2.9σ)
1000.028 (6% of the error bar)0.141 (29% of the error bar)
10,0000.003 (0.6%)0.014 (3%)
Result. The network is five times worse on the unusual data set than on the typical one, at any training size. An amortised posterior is most trustworthy for data that look like the bulk of its training set and least trustworthy in the tails.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
tau2, s2 = 4.0, 0.25
v = tau2 * s2 / (tau2 + s2)                      # posterior variance
for N in (100, 10_000):
    sigma_a = np.sqrt(v / (N * (tau2 + s2)))     # statistical error of the fitted slope
    print("N = %5d: error at x = 1.2: %.3f (%.1f%% of the error bar), at x = 6: %.3f (%.0f%%)" % (N, 1.2 * sigma_a, 120 * sigma_a / np.sqrt(v), 6 * sigma_a, 600 * sigma_a / np.sqrt(v)))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)                   # check N = 100 by refitting many times
slopes = []
for _ in range(20000):
    th = rng.normal(0, 2, 100); x = th + rng.normal(0, 0.5, 100)
    slopes.append((th * x).sum() / (x * x).sum())
print("scatter of the slope over 20,000 training sets of 100: %.4f (formula %.4f)" % (np.std(slopes), np.sqrt(v / (100 * (tau2 + s2)))))

Output

N =   100: error at x = 1.2: 0.028 (5.8% of the error bar), at x = 6: 0.141 (29%)
N = 10000: error at x = 1.2: 0.003 (0.6% of the error bar), at x = 6: 0.014 (3%)
scatter of the slope over 20,000 training sets of 100: 0.0239 (formula 0.0235)

Sequential training: give up amortisation to gain accuracy

If you care about one data set, most prior simulations are wasted on regions the data rule out. With a prior of width τ and a posterior of width σp, only a fraction of about σp/τ of simulations fall where they matter, and (σp/τ)d for d parameters. Sequential methods run in rounds:

  1. Train on simulations from the prior and get a rough posterior for the observed data.
  2. Draw the next batch of parameters from that rough posterior and simulate.
  3. Retrain, correcting for the fact that the parameters no longer came from the prior.
  4. Repeat until the posterior stops changing.

The correction in step 3 is importance weighting, the same device as umbrella sampling in molecular simulation: sample where it is useful, then reweight to undo the distortion.

AmortisedSequential
Simulations for one data setManyFar fewer
A second data setFreeStart again
Coverage testsCheap: hundreds of posteriors at no extra costExpensive: each test case needs its own training
Best whenMany data sets, or the posterior is not much narrower than the priorOne data set and a posterior far narrower than the prior

More worked examples

Worked example: how many prior simulations land near the answer

Two parameters each have a prior range of 10. The posterior for the observed data has a width of 0.1 in each. Count a simulation as useful for learning this posterior if both its parameters fall within three posterior standard deviations of the answer, a box of side 0.6.

Result. (0.6/10)² = 0.36 per cent. Of 10,000 simulations drawn from the prior, 36 are near the observation. With six parameters the fraction would be 5 × 10−8. This is the case for sequential methods, which draw later simulations from the current estimate of the posterior. It is also the price of amortisation: a network that must serve every possible observation cannot concentrate on one.
Show the code 
for d in (2, 6):
    frac = (0.6 / 10) ** d                       # box of +/- 3 posterior sd in a prior of width 10
    print("%d parameters: %.2e of prior draws, %.3g out of 10,000" % (d, frac, 1e4 * frac))

Output

2 parameters: 3.60e-03 of prior draws, 36 out of 10,000
6 parameters: 4.67e-08 of prior draws, 0.000467 out of 10,000

Questions people ask

Is an amortised posterior less accurate? Usually somewhat, for any one data set. The network has to spread its capacity and its training simulations over all the data sets the prior can produce. The loss in accuracy at a particular observation is called the amortisation gap.

When is amortisation not worth it? When there is one data set, a broad prior and a narrow posterior. Then nearly every training simulation is far from the observation and teaches the network little about the region that matters. Sequential methods, which concentrate simulations near the data, do better.

Can I have both? Partly. One can train an amortised network and then refine it for a particular observation, or correct its samples by importance weights. What cannot be kept is the validity of a sequentially trained network for other data sets.

Why does amortisation matter for checking a result? Because the tests of the checks chapter need the posterior for hundreds of simulated data sets. With an amortised network that takes minutes. With a sampler it is often out of reach, and the test is then not done.

constructionThe evidence as a generating functional, with the data as source

In field theory one does not compute correlation functions one at a time. One computes the generating functional Z[J] once, as a function of an external source J, and obtains every correlation function by differentiating. Amortised inference has the same structure, and for Gaussian noise the correspondence is exact.

Worked example: every posterior moment from one function of the data

Let x = θ + noise of variance s², with any prior p(θ). Expanding the square in the likelihood, the evidence is

p(x) ∝ e−x²/2s² Z(J),    Z(J) = ∫ p(θ) e−θ²/2s² eJθ dθ,    J = x / s²

The data enter only as a source term Jθ. Differentiating W = ln Z with respect to the source gives the connected correlation functions, here the posterior cumulants:

⟨θ | x⟩ = ∂W/∂J = x + s² ∂ ln p(x)/∂x,     Var(θ | x) = ∂²W/∂J² = s² + s⁴ ∂² ln p(x)/∂x²

Check with a Gaussian prior of variance τ²: ln p(x) = −x²/2(τ² + s²), so the mean is x − s² x/(τ² + s²) = x τ²/(τ² + s²) and the variance is s² − s⁴/(τ² + s²) = s² τ²/(τ² + s²). Both are the results of the dictionary chapter.

Result. The log-evidence, as a function of the data, is the generating function of the posterior. Know it for all x and you know every posterior for every data set. An amortised network is a numerical representation of this one function, and the first formula (Tweedie's) is also the denoising rule of the diffusion chapter.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
s2, x = 0.25, 1.2
theta = np.linspace(-10, 10, 400001)
prior = 0.5 * np.exp(-(theta + 1.5)**2 / 2) + 0.5 * np.exp(-(theta - 2)**2 / 0.5)    # any prior: here two bumps
def evidence(xx): return np.trapezoid(prior * np.exp(-(xx - theta)**2 / (2 * s2)), theta)
h = 1e-3
d1 = (np.log(evidence(x + h)) - np.log(evidence(x - h))) / (2 * h)
d2 = (np.log(evidence(x + h)) - 2 * np.log(evidence(x)) + np.log(evidence(x - h))) / h**2
post = prior * np.exp(-(x - theta)**2 / (2 * s2)); post /= np.trapezoid(post, theta)
mean = np.trapezoid(theta * post, theta); var = np.trapezoid((theta - mean)**2 * post, theta)
print("posterior mean:     direct %.4f, from the evidence %.4f" % (mean, x + s2 * d1))
print("posterior variance: direct %.4f, from the evidence %.4f" % (var, s2 + s2**2 * d2))

Output

posterior mean:     direct 1.4919, from the evidence 1.4919
posterior variance: direct 0.2235, from the evidence 0.2235

Linear response gives the amortisation gap its physical reading. Near the centre of the training data the network has learned the response to small sources accurately. Far out it is being asked for the response to a large source, where the nonlinear terms it saw few examples of dominate.

← 19. Flows21. Scores and diffusion →

Chapter 21 of 39

Scores and diffusion models

The newest way to represent a posterior learns a force field instead of a probability. It is Langevin dynamics run in reverse, and it removes the partition function from the problem at the first step.

physics

The connection. A diffusion model is Brownian motion run backwards. It learns the force that undoes the spreading.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Score ∇ln pForce −∇E/kBT
Adding noise step by stepDiffusion; the Fokker-Planck equation
Sampling by removing noiseTime-reversed Langevin dynamics
Sampling a distribution by a stochastic processStochastic quantisation (Parisi and Wu)

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: from thermodynamics to image generators and back

The 2015 paper that introduced diffusion models, by Sohl-Dickstein and colleagues, is titled "Deep unsupervised learning using nonequilibrium thermodynamics". The idea is taken directly from physics: a structured distribution is destroyed by slowly adding noise, a process whose every step is simple, and a network is trained to reverse each step. The mathematics of reversing a diffusion had been worked out by Anderson in 1982.

A second line began with Hyvärinen, who showed in 2005 that the gradient of a log-density, the score, can be learned without knowing the normalising constant. Vincent in 2011 connected this to removing noise. Song and Ermon in 2019, and Ho, Jain and Abbeel in 2020, made the approach work at scale. In 2021 Song and colleagues showed that the two lines are one, by writing both as stochastic differential equations. The image generators that became widely known in 2022 are built on this.

The link to field theory is older than any of it. In 1981 Parisi and Wu proposed computing the averages of a quantum field theory by running a Langevin equation in an extra, fictitious time until it reaches equilibrium. That is sampling a Boltzmann weight by diffusion, the forward half of a diffusion model. In SBI, score-based posterior estimation was introduced from 2022 and flow matching in 2023.

The score is a force

The score of a distribution is the gradient of its log-probability. For a Boltzmann distribution p = e−E/Z,

score(θ) = ∇ ln p(θ) = −∇E(θ) the force; ln Z is a constant and its gradient is zero

So a model of the score never has to be normalised. The hardest quantity in the subject disappears on differentiation, which is the main reason this approach has spread.

From a force to samples: Langevin dynamics

A particle in a potential, pushed by the force and kicked by thermal noise, obeys the overdamped Langevin equation:

dθ = −∇E dt + √2 dW drift down the potential plus random kicks

Its probability density evolves by the Fokker–Planck equation and settles to e−E/Z. So if a network supplies the score, you can draw samples by simulating Brownian motion in the corresponding force field.

Diffusion models: learn to undo noise

A diffusion model adds a schedule of noise levels. Take samples of θ, blur them with Gaussian noise of increasing size until nothing but noise is left, and train a network to estimate the score at every noise level. To generate, start from pure noise and step back down the levels, following the learned score. The blurring plays the part of temperature: at high noise the landscape is smooth with a single basin, and separate peaks emerge gradually as it cools, as in annealing.

Worked example: one denoising step for a Gaussian

Let the posterior be N(m, v) and add noise of variance σt². The blurred distribution is N(m, v + σt²), whose score is

scoret(θt) = −(θt − m) / (v + σt²)

The best estimate of the clean value from a noisy one is the noisy value moved along the score by σt² (Tweedie's formula):

θ̂0 = θt + σt² · scoret(θt)

Numbers: m = 1.129 and v = 0.235 (the posterior of the ABC chapter), noise σt = 1, noisy value θt = 3. The score is −(3 − 1.129)/1.235 = −1.514, so the denoised estimate is 3 − 1.514 = 1.486.

Result. Check by Bayes' rule: the mean of the clean value given the noisy one is m + [v/(v + σt²)](θt − m) = 1.129 + 0.190 × 1.871 = 1.486. The two agree. Denoising is following the force toward where the probability is.
Show the code 
m, v = 1.2 * 4 / 4.25, 1 / (1 / 4 + 1 / 0.25)    # the posterior of the ABC chapter: 1.129, 0.235
noise, theta_t = 1.0, 3.0
score = -(theta_t - m) / (v + noise**2)
print("score %.3f, denoised estimate %.3f" % (score, theta_t + noise**2 * score))
print("Bayes' rule:              %.3f" % (m + v / (v + noise**2) * (theta_t - m)))

Output

score -1.514, denoised estimate 1.486
Bayes' rule:              1.486

The targets of the flows chapter, made by diffusion

The flows chapter built three distributions by pushing a Gaussian through a map in one step. A diffusion model reaches the same three by many small steps, guided by the score. For the first the whole calculation can be done by hand. For the other two the score has no neat formula, which is the situation a network is for.

Worked example: shift and stretch, by diffusion

Target: N(μ, σ²). At noise level s the blurred distribution is N(μ, σ² + s²) and the score is −(θ − μ)/(σ² + s²). To generate, lower the noise level gradually and move each sample so that the cloud always has the right blurred distribution. The rule for that motion is

dθ/ds = −s · score(θ, s) = s (θ − μ) / (σ² + s²)

This separates. Integrating from a large starting noise smax down to s:

θ(s) − μ = [ θ(smax) − μ ] · √[ (σ² + s²) / (σ² + smax²) ]

At the start the sample is Gaussian with variance σ² + smax², so u = [θ(smax) − μ]/√(σ² + smax²) is a standard Gaussian number. Setting s = 0:

θ(0) = μ + σ u
Result. For a Gaussian target, running the diffusion to the end reproduces the shift-and-stretch map of the flows chapter exactly. The flow applies it in one step. The diffusion applies it as a continuous squeeze toward the mean, with the squeezing rate set by the score at each noise level.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
mu, sigma, s_max, n = 1.0, 0.5, 20.0, 20_000
u = rng.standard_normal(n)
theta = mu + np.sqrt(sigma**2 + s_max**2) * u    # start from the fully blurred distribution
levels = np.geomspace(s_max, 1e-4, 20000)
for s, s_next in zip(levels[:-1], levels[1:]):   # lower the noise, moving each sample along the rule
    mid = 0.5 * (s + s_next)
    theta += (s_next - s) * mid * (theta - mu) / (sigma**2 + mid**2)
print("after the diffusion: mean %.3f, sd %.3f (target %.1f, %.1f)" % (theta.mean(), theta.std(), mu, sigma))
print("largest difference from the flow map mu + sigma*u: %.4f" % np.abs(theta - (mu + sigma * u)).max())

Output

after the diffusion: mean 1.002, sd 0.498 (target 1.0, 0.5)
largest difference from the flow map mu + sigma*u: 0.0020

Worked example: what the network is trained on

The score is never given. The network sees pairs made by taking a clean sample θ0 and adding noise, θs = θ0 + s ε, and is trained to predict the quantity −(θs − θ0)/s², which is minus the added noise in units of s. For the Gaussian target take the simplest model, a straight line −k(θs − μ), and fit k by least squares:

k = −Cov[ −(θs − θ0)/s², θs ] / Var(θs) = (s²/s²) / (σ² + s²) = 1 / (σ² + s²)
Result. Regressing "the noise that was added" on the noisy value returns the true score, −(θ − μ)/(σ² + s²). Each individual target is random, but its average at a given noisy value is the score. That is why a diffusion model can be trained from samples alone, with no density and no partition function.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
mu, sigma, s, n = 1.0, 0.5, 0.8, 2_000_000
clean = rng.normal(mu, sigma, n)
noisy = clean + s * rng.standard_normal(n)
target = -(noisy - clean) / s**2                 # what the network is asked to predict
k = -np.cov(target, noisy)[0, 1] / noisy.var()   # least-squares slope of the target against the noisy value
print("fitted k = %.4f, true 1/(sigma^2 + s^2) = %.4f" % (k, 1 / (sigma**2 + s**2)))

Output

fitted k = 1.1238, true 1/(sigma^2 + s^2) = 1.1236

Worked example: the lognormal

Target: the lognormal of the flows chapter. Blurring it with Gaussian noise gives an integral with no closed form, so the score at a general noise level must be computed numerically or learned. Two things can still be said exactly.

At vanishing noise the score is the derivative of the log of the lognormal density:

score(θ, 0) = −1/θ − (ln θ − μ) / (σ² θ)

It vanishes at ln θ = μ − σ², the peak found in the flows chapter (0.779 for μ = 0, σ = 0.5). To the left the force pushes right, increasingly hard as θ → 0: the wall that keeps samples positive. To the right it pushes left only weakly, which is the long tail.

At any noise level the score is given by an average, not a formula:

score(θ, s) = [ ⟨θ0 | θ⟩ − θ ] / s²

where ⟨θ0 | θ⟩ is the mean clean value among all samples that noise could have carried to θ. The experiment below evaluates that average on a grid. A network estimates the same average from training pairs.

Result. Noise spills some probability to negative θ, where the lognormal has none. There the average clean value is positive, so the score points firmly to the right. The model learns the positivity of the parameter as a force, without being told about it.

A shortcut used in practice: diffuse in u = ln θ, where the target is Gaussian and the first worked example applies, then exponentiate at the end. That is one fixed flow layer placed after a diffusion model, and it guarantees positive samples.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
mu, sigma = 0.0, 0.5
score0 = lambda t: -1 / t - (np.log(t) - mu) / (sigma**2 * t)        # score at vanishing noise
print("score at the peak 0.779: %.4f;  at 0.3: %+.1f;  at 3: %+.2f" % (score0(np.exp(mu - sigma**2)), score0(0.3), score0(3.0)))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
clean = np.exp(mu + sigma * rng.standard_normal(2_000_000))
s = 0.3
for theta in (-0.2, 0.8, 2.5):                   # score at noise level s: (mean clean value given theta - theta) / s^2
    w = np.exp(-(theta - clean)**2 / (2 * s**2))
    print("noise 0.3, theta = %+.1f: mean clean value %.2f, score %+.2f" % (theta, (w * clean).sum() / w.sum(), ((w * clean).sum() / w.sum() - theta) / s**2))

Output

score at the peak 0.779: 0.0000;  at 0.3: +12.7;  at 3: -1.80
noise 0.3, theta = -0.2: mean clean value 0.45, score +7.18
noise 0.3, theta = +0.8: mean clean value 0.83, score +0.36
noise 0.3, theta = +2.5: mean clean value 2.33, score -1.86

See it

Choose a target and move the noise slider from left (pure noise) to right (finished samples). The left panel shows the blurred target and 2,500 samples being carried along. The middle panel shows the score, the force acting on them, at that noise level. The right panel shows forty sample paths from noise to data.

1. Density at this noise level
2. Score: the force on a sample
3. Sample paths, noise to data
Samples carried by the scoreExact blurred densityFinal target
–noise level s
–samples: mean ± s.d.
–exact: mean ± s.d.
–steps taken

Things to try

  • Gaussian. The score is a straight line at every noise level and only its slope changes, 1/(σ² + s²). The paths converge like a fan closing. This is the first worked example.
  • Lognormal, final steps. The score becomes steep and positive on the left and shallow on the right. Watch the left edge of the sample cloud stop at zero while the right keeps its tail.
  • Two peaks. At high noise there is one zero of the score, at the centre, and it is stable: every sample is pulled inward. Lower the noise and the centre turns unstable while two new stable points appear on either side. In the right panel the paths split at that noise level. Each sample has chosen its peak, and the choice was made early, from the sign of its starting value.
  • Compare with the flows chapter. The finished histograms match the ones produced there by a single map. Same distributions, two routes.

How the experiment works: the score is computed exactly, from the average in the lognormal example, on a grid of 935 points at 60 noise levels. The samples start as a broad Gaussian and are moved by the rule dθ/ds = −s · score, with a second-order step. Nothing is learned here. In a real diffusion model a network supplies the score and everything else is the same.

Questions people ask

Why learn a force when you could learn the density? Because the force does not involve the normalising constant. The gradient of ln p is the same whatever Z is. A network that outputs a force has no constraint to satisfy, where a network that outputs a density must integrate to one. That freedom is the reason diffusion models scale to very complicated distributions.

Why is noise added at many levels and not one? At low noise the learned force is accurate only close to where the data are, and a sampler starting far away gets no guidance. At high noise the distribution is smooth and easy to learn everywhere, but blurred. Using all levels in sequence carries a sample from anywhere to the sharp target.

Is the reverse process just running the film backwards? Not for each particle. Diffusion is irreversible, and an individual path cannot be retraced. What can be reversed is the evolution of the distribution, and doing so requires an extra drift proportional to the score. That extra term is what the network supplies.

What does it cost? Time at the sampling stage. A flow produces a sample in one pass through the network. A diffusion model takes tens to hundreds of small steps. For a posterior in a few dimensions the difference is seconds, and for large problems it matters.

How does this differ from Langevin MCMC? Langevin MCMC needs the true score, and so the true likelihood, and runs at one noise level until equilibrium. A diffusion model uses a learned score, moves through a sequence of noise levels, and is never in equilibrium at any of them.

constructionThe physics of diffusion

The name is literal. A diffusion model is the physics of Brownian motion, run forwards to destroy structure and backwards to create it. The paper that introduced the method (2015) was titled "Deep unsupervised learning using nonequilibrium thermodynamics".

Diffusion modelDiffusion physics
Adding noise step by stepBrownian motion: a particle kicked by random collisions
How the density blursThe heat equation, ∂p/∂t = D ∂²p/∂θ², the one that spreads a drop of ink
Noise with a restoring pullOrnstein–Uhlenbeck process: a Brownian particle on a spring, relaxing to a Gaussian
Evolution of the whole distributionFokker–Planck equation: drift plus diffusion
The scoreThe entropic force that drives diffusion
Fully noised stateThermal equilibrium: all memory of the start is lost
Generating a sampleThe time-reversed process
Noise scheduleA cooling schedule

Worked example: the heat equation sets the noise level

Start the heat equation from a Gaussian of variance v. Try a Gaussian of variance V(t) as the solution. Substituting, the equation is satisfied if dV/dt = 2D:

p(θ, t) = N(θ; m, v + 2Dt)
Result. The "noise level" s used throughout this chapter is elapsed diffusion time in disguise: s² = 2Dt. This is also the path integral of the path-integral chapter, where summing over random-walk histories gave a variance of 2Dt. Blurring a distribution, solving the heat equation, and summing over Brownian paths are one operation.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
D, v, m = 0.5, 0.2, 1.0
x = np.linspace(-10, 12, 2201); dx = x[1] - x[0]; dt = 0.2 * dx**2 / D
p = np.exp(-(x - m)**2 / (2 * v)) / np.sqrt(2 * np.pi * v)   # start: a Gaussian of variance v
t = 0.0
for target in (0.5, 2.0):
    while t < target - 1e-12:                    # explicit finite-difference solution of dp/dt = D d2p/dx2
        p[1:-1] += D * dt / dx**2 * (p[2:] - 2 * p[1:-1] + p[:-2]); t += dt
    mean = np.trapezoid(x * p, x); var = np.trapezoid((x - mean)**2 * p, x)
    print("t = %.1f: mean %.3f, variance %.3f (v + 2Dt = %.3f)" % (t, mean, var, v + 2 * D * t))

Output

t = 0.5: mean 1.000, variance 0.700 (v + 2Dt = 0.700)
t = 2.0: mean 1.000, variance 2.200 (v + 2Dt = 2.200)

The score is the force behind Fick's law. The diffusion current is J = −D ∇p, which can be written as density times velocity:

J = p · ( −D ∇ ln p ) so each particle drifts, on average, with velocity −D × score

Nothing pushes an individual molecule down the concentration gradient. The drift is entropic: there are simply more ways to move from a crowded place to an empty one than the reverse. The score is that statistical force. Moving every particle with velocity −D × score, with no random kicks at all, reproduces the same evolving density. That deterministic version is the rule dθ/ds = −s × score used in the experiment above, and it is what makes a diffusion model a continuous flow.

Running it backwards is exact. A diffusion with drift f and noise strength g, run backwards in time, is again a diffusion, with drift f − g² × score (Anderson, 1982). No approximation is involved. The reverse process needs exactly one thing the forward process did not, the score at every time, and that is the one thing the network is trained to supply.

The second law, and where the information goes. Ink spreading in water needs no instruction, and entropy rises. Un-mixing it would require knowing where every molecule should go. The forward process of a diffusion model is the approach to equilibrium, in which the information that distinguished the data from noise is lost. The network stores that information, as a force field, at every stage of the loss. Generation is then not a violation of the second law but a controlled process with the missing information fed back in, in the way a Maxwell demon can lower entropy only by using what it knows.

Paths have an action. Each noising trajectory carries the Onsager–Machlup weight of the path-integral chapter, and the forward and reverse processes are two measures on the same space of paths. The training loss is a bound on the Kullback–Leibler divergence between them, which in stochastic thermodynamics is the entropy produced along the path. Training a diffusion model is making a stochastic process as nearly reversible as possible.

Stochastic quantisation. Parisi and Wu showed that a Euclidean field theory can be defined as the equilibrium of a Langevin dynamics in a fictitious time. A diffusion model accepts that definition and learns the drift. Applied to the field configurations of a lattice theory, a diffusion model is stochastic quantisation with a learned force.

Noising is a renormalisation flow. Adding noise at increasing levels erases structure from the smallest scales upward, and the distribution flows to a Gaussian, the trivial fixed point. The exact renormalisation-group equation for a field theory has the form of a heat equation in the space of fields, and recent work treats a diffusion model as that flow and generation as its inverse. This is a current research direction more than a settled result, but it is the most direct statement of the theme of this course: the machinery is the same.

How this is used in SBI

  • Posterior score estimation. Train the score network on simulated pairs with the data as an extra input, so it learns the score of p(θ | x) at every noise level. Sampling the posterior is then running the reverse diffusion.
  • Adding up evidence. Scores are gradients of log-probabilities, so scores from independent measurements add, as forces from independent sources add. This makes it natural to combine many observations.
  • Flow matching. A close relative replaces the random path from noise to posterior with a deterministic velocity field, a flow in the fluid-dynamical sense, and is often faster to sample.

Compared with a normalising flow, a score model places no restrictions on the network (it need not be invertible), which helps for complicated or many-peaked posteriors. The cost is that sampling takes many small steps where a flow takes one.

← 20. Amortisation22. Energy-based models →

Chapter 22 of 39

Energy-based models: learning the energy itself

So far the energy came from a model we wrote down. An energy-based model turns that round: it is given samples and has to find the energy that would produce them.

physics

The connection. An energy-based model writes the unknown distribution as a Boltzmann weight and learns the energy. The Boltzmann machine is an Ising model run backwards.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Learned energy functionHamiltonian
Learning couplings from correlationsThe inverse Ising problem
Normalising constantPartition function, and the same obstacle
Score matching, contrastive learningLearning the force; measuring a free-energy difference

Background: memory as an energy landscape

In 1982 Hopfield, a physicist, published a model of memory based on the physics of magnets. A network of units that are each on or off is given an energy function like that of a spin glass, with couplings chosen so that stored patterns are minima. Started near a stored pattern, the network rolls downhill into it. Recall is relaxation to a nearby minimum. The capacity of such a network was soon calculated with the methods of spin-glass theory.

In 1985 Ackley, Hinton and Sejnowski added temperature. Their Boltzmann machine samples its states from a Boltzmann distribution and adjusts its couplings until the samples resemble the data. Training was slow, because every step needs the model's own correlations, and for two decades the method remained mostly of theoretical interest. Hinton's contrastive divergence of 2002 made a restricted version practical, and stacks of such machines started the revival of deep networks in 2006. Hopfield and Hinton shared the 2024 Nobel Prize in Physics for this work.

The reverse problem has its own history in physics: inferring the couplings of a spin model from measured correlations. It has been used since the 2000s to analyse recordings of neurons, the flight of bird flocks, and protein sequences, where it predicts which amino acids are in contact.

The idea

Any probability distribution can be written as a Boltzmann weight, p(x) = exp(−E(x)) / Z. An energy-based model takes this as its definition. It represents the energy E(x) by a flexible function with adjustable parameters, and tunes them until the samples it would produce look like the data.

The oldest example is taken directly from magnetism. A Boltzmann machine is an Ising model whose couplings are not given by nature but learned from data.

physics

The Ising model

Energy. E(s) = −Σi<j Jij si sj − Σi hi si

si = ±1: the spin at site i. Jij: the coupling between spins i and j; positive means they prefer to align. hi: the field on spin i.

The usual question. The couplings are known. What are the correlations ⟨sisj⟩?

learning

The Boltzmann machine

Energy. The same expression. The spins are now binary features of the data, such as pixels that are on or off.

The inverse question. The correlations are measured in the data. What couplings would produce them?

Learning rule. ΔJij ∝ ⟨sisj⟩data − ⟨sisj⟩model
Strengthen a coupling if the data are more correlated than the model, weaken it if less.

The learning rule is not a guess. It is the gradient of the log-likelihood of the data, and it has a clean form because ∂ln Z/∂Jij is the model's own correlation ⟨sisj⟩. Learning stops when the model reproduces the measured correlations. Physicists call this the inverse Ising problem.

Worked example: two spins

Two spins and one coupling J, no fields. There are four states. The two aligned states have energy −J and the two opposed states have energy +J, so

Z = 2eJ + 2e−J,     ⟨s1s2⟩ = (2eJ − 2e−J) / Z = tanh J

Suppose the data show a correlation of 0.6: the two features agree 80 per cent of the time. Matching the model to the data means tanh J = 0.6.

Result. J = atanh 0.6 = 0.693, which is ln 2. Aligned states are then e2J = 4 times as probable as opposed ones: 40 per cent each against 10 per cent each, as in the data.

The learning rule reaches the same place by small steps. Starting from J = 0 with a step size of 0.25, the coupling is 0.15 after one step, 0.47 after five, 0.61 after ten and 0.69 after thirty.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
target = 0.6                                     # correlation in the data
print("exact coupling atanh(0.6) = %.3f, ln 2 = %.3f" % (np.arctanh(target), np.log(2)))
J = 0.0
for step in range(1, 31):
    J += 0.25 * (target - np.tanh(J))            # data correlation minus model correlation
    if step in (1, 5, 10, 30): print("after %2d steps: J = %.2f" % (step, J))

Output

exact coupling atanh(0.6) = 0.693, ln 2 = 0.693
after  1 steps: J = 0.15
after  5 steps: J = 0.47
after 10 steps: J = 0.61
after 30 steps: J = 0.69

See it

Choose the correlation in the data and press Learn. The bars are the model's probabilities for the four states, and the red marks are the data.

The coupling as it is learned
Probabilities of the four states
ModelData, and the exact coupling (dashed)
0learning steps
–coupling J now
–model correlation tanh J
–exact answer atanh(correlation)

Set a negative correlation and the learned coupling is negative: an antiferromagnet. Set a correlation near ±0.9 and learning slows down, because tanh J flattens and the gradient becomes small.

The difficulty: the partition function again

With two spins the model's correlation is a formula. With a thousand spins it is a sum over 21000 states. Every learning step needs ⟨sisj⟩ from the model, so every learning step contains the hard problem of statistical mechanics. The history of energy-based models is a list of ways round it.

MethodHow it avoids computing ZPhysics counterpart
Monte Carlo samplingEstimate the model's correlations by simulating it.Monte Carlo simulation of a spin system
Contrastive divergenceStart the simulation at the data and run it only a few steps.A short quench away from a prepared state
Mean-field learningReplace the correlations by a self-consistent approximation.Mean-field theory, the TAP equations
Score matchingFit the gradient of the energy. Z does not depend on x, so it drops out.Learning the force instead of the potential
Noise-contrastive estimationTrain a classifier to tell data from noise; its output is the energy difference.A free-energy difference between two systems

The last two rows are methods you have already met. Score matching is the basis of diffusion models. Noise-contrastive estimation is the idea behind neural ratio estimation.

Where this sits in SBI

The three neural methods of the neural methods chapter can all be read as learning an energy.

  • NLE learns the energy of the data given the parameters, −ln p(x | θ). Its network is built so that Z = 1 by construction, which is what a normalising flow is for.
  • NRE learns an energy difference, ln p(x | θ) − ln p(x), by classification. It never needs a normalised density, and pays for that by needing MCMC afterwards.
  • Diffusion-based estimators learn the force, the gradient of the energy in θ, and never need Z either.

Hopfield's network of 1982, which stores memories as energy minima of a spin system, and Hinton and Sejnowski's Boltzmann machine of 1985 are the two models for which the 2024 Nobel Prize in Physics was awarded. Modern attention layers can be written as one update of a Hopfield network with a steeper energy function.

More worked examples

Worked example: one spin and a field

A binary feature is on in 73 per cent of the data. Model it as a spin in a field h, with probabilities proportional to eh and e−h. The mean of the spin is then tanh h. In the data the mean is 0.73 − 0.27 = 0.46.

Result. h = atanh 0.46 = 0.497. The learning rule Δh ∝ ⟨s⟩data − ⟨s⟩model stops there. Fields are fixed by averages and couplings by correlations.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
mean_spin = 0.73 - 0.27
print("mean spin %.2f, field h = %.3f" % (mean_spin, np.arctanh(mean_spin)))

Output

mean spin 0.46, field h = 0.497

Worked example: direct and indirect correlations

Three spins sit in a row. Spins 1 and 2 are coupled with J = ln 2, and so are spins 2 and 3. Spins 1 and 3 are not coupled at all. Neighbours then have correlation tanh J = 0.6. For a chain, the correlation between the ends is the product of the correlations along the way.

Result. Spins 1 and 3 have a correlation of 0.6 × 0.6 = 0.36 with no coupling between them. A correlation does not show a direct interaction. Inferring the couplings, and not only measuring correlations, separates the two. This is how contacts between amino acids in a protein are predicted from sequence data: pairs with a strong inferred coupling touch, while pairs that are merely correlated need not.
Show the code 
import numpy as np, itertools
J = np.log(2)
states = np.array(list(itertools.product([-1, 1], repeat=3)))
w = np.exp(J * (states[:, 0] * states[:, 1] + states[:, 1] * states[:, 2]))    # chain 1-2-3
p = w / w.sum()
corr = lambda i, j: (p * states[:, i] * states[:, j]).sum()
print("neighbours: %.2f   ends of the chain: %.2f" % (corr(0, 1), corr(0, 2)))

Output

neighbours: 0.60   ends of the chain: 0.36

Questions people ask

If energy-based models are so natural, why are they not used everywhere? Because of the partition function. Evaluating the probability of a point, or taking one exact learning step, needs a sum over all states. Models built so that the normalisation is automatic, such as flows, avoid that cost and are easier to train.

What are hidden units for? A Boltzmann machine with only pairwise couplings between visible units can match pairwise correlations and nothing more. Adding units that are never observed, and summing over them, produces effective interactions of all orders among the visible ones. In physics terms, integrating out degrees of freedom generates new couplings.

Is a diffusion model an energy-based model? Close to it. It learns the gradient of an energy at each noise level and never the energy itself, which avoids the partition function completely.

How does this connect to transformers? A modern Hopfield network, with an energy that rises steeply away from stored patterns, retrieves a pattern in a single update. That update has the same mathematical form as the attention operation in a transformer.

What to remember

  • An energy-based model writes the unknown distribution as a Boltzmann weight and learns the energy from samples.
  • A Boltzmann machine is an Ising model run backwards: correlations in, couplings out. The learning rule compares data correlations with model correlations.
  • The partition function is the obstacle, in learning as in physics. Score matching and classification are the two standard ways to avoid it, and they are the ideas behind diffusion models and NRE.
← 21. Scores and diffusion23. Training as physics →

Chapter 23 of 39

Training as physics: gradient descent with noise

Every neural estimator in this course is fitted by stochastic gradient descent. That procedure is itself a physical process: a particle sliding downhill while being shaken.

physics

The connection. Stochastic gradient descent is a particle sliding downhill in the loss while a heat bath shakes it.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
SGD updateLangevin equation
Learning rate ÷ batch sizeTemperature
Lowering the learning rateAnnealing
Loss landscape of a large networkEnergy landscape of a glass

Background: an old algorithm with a physical life

Stochastic gradient descent dates from 1951, when Robbins and Monro showed that a quantity can be minimised using only noisy measurements of its slope, provided the step size is reduced gradually. It became the standard way to train neural networks after 1986, when Rumelhart, Hinton and Williams popularised an efficient way of computing the gradients. For decades the noise from using small batches was regarded as a price paid for speed.

The physical reading developed in the 2010s. Welling and Teh showed in 2011 that adding a calibrated amount of noise to gradient descent turns it into a sampler of the posterior. Mandt, Hoffman and Blei in 2017 analysed ordinary SGD as a stochastic process with a stationary distribution. Smith and Le in 2018 identified the ratio of learning rate to batch size as an effective temperature. Others, including Choromanska and colleagues in 2015, compared the loss surface of a large network with the energy landscape of a spin glass.

The picture is useful and incomplete. It explains why schedules resemble annealing, why batch size and learning rate can be traded against each other, and why repeated training runs differ. It does not yet explain, in a way everyone accepts, why very large networks generalise as well as they do.

What training does

A network has weights w. A loss function L(w) says how badly the network does on the training simulations. Training lowers the loss by repeated small steps downhill:

w ← w − η · (gradient of L) η: the learning rate, the size of each step

Computing the gradient on all the simulations at once is expensive. So each step uses a random handful, a batch of B simulations. The gradient from a batch is the true gradient plus a random error, and the error is larger for smaller batches. This is stochastic gradient descent, SGD. The random error looks like a nuisance. It turns out to be the temperature.

physics

A particle in a viscous fluid

Langevin equation. dq/dt = −∇U(q) + √(2kBT) ξ(t)

q: position. U: potential energy. −∇U: the force, pointing downhill. ξ(t): random kicks from the molecules of the fluid. kBT: temperature.

Where it ends up. Not at the bottom, but in the Boltzmann distribution p(q) ∝ exp(−U(q)/kBT), spread over the region within about kBT of the bottom.

learning

Stochastic gradient descent

Update. w ← w − η [ ∇L(w) + noise ]

w: weights. L: the loss, in the role of the potential. The noise has variance s²/B, where s² is the spread of single-example gradients and B the batch size.

Effective temperature. Teff = η s² / 2B
A larger learning rate or a smaller batch means a hotter system. The weights do not settle at the minimum of the loss. They fluctuate around it.

Worked example: one weight in a quadratic loss

Take one weight and the loss L = ½ κw², with curvature κ = 1. Let the gradient noise have s² = 1, the batch size be 10 and the learning rate 0.1. Each step is

w ← (1 − ηκ) w − η · noise

In the steady state the variance V of w must reproduce itself: V = (1 − ηκ)² V + η² s²/B. Solving,

V = η s² / [ B κ (2 − ηκ) ] = 0.1 / (10 × 1.9) = 0.00526

The Boltzmann prediction is Teff/κ = 0.005. A simulation of 400,000 steps gives 0.00526.

Result. The weight jiggles around the minimum with standard deviation 0.073. For a small learning rate this is the Boltzmann distribution at temperature ηs²/2B. The factor (2 − ηκ) is the correction for taking finite steps, and at ηκ = 2 the spread becomes infinite: training diverges.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
eta, B, kappa = 0.1, 10, 1.0
noise = rng.standard_normal(400_000) / np.sqrt(B)
w, ws = 0.0, []
for n in noise:
    w -= eta * (kappa * w + n)                   # one SGD step with a noisy gradient
    ws.append(w)
print("simulated variance %.5f, formula %.5f, Boltzmann T/kappa %.5f, sd %.3f" % (np.var(ws[1000:]), eta / (B * kappa * (2 - eta * kappa)), eta / (2 * B), np.std(ws[1000:])))

Output

simulated variance 0.00526, formula 0.00526, Boltzmann T/kappa 0.00500, sd 0.073

See it

The red dot is the weight. Change the learning rate and the batch size, and compare its spread with the formula.

The loss, and the weight now
Where the weight has been, against the prediction
Weight now, and the predicted distributionRecent and recorded values
–effective temperature η/2B
–predicted spread of the weight
–measured spread

Doubling the batch size and doubling the learning rate together leaves the temperature unchanged, which is a rule of thumb practitioners found by experiment. Above a learning rate of 1 the weight overshoots the minimum on every step, and as the rate approaches 2 its spread grows without limit.

What follows from this picture

  • Learning-rate schedules are annealing. Starting with a large learning rate and lowering it is cooling a system slowly so that it settles into a deep minimum and not the first one it meets.
  • Noise prefers wide minima. A hot particle spends its time where there is room. In a wide, flat basin the Boltzmann weight includes a large volume; in a narrow one, a small volume. This is the same entropy term as the Occam factor of the model comparison chapter, and it is one explanation offered for why networks trained with small batches often generalise better.
  • SGD can be made into a posterior sampler. If the loss is minus the log-posterior and noise of exactly the right size is added, the stationary distribution of the weights is the posterior. This is stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics, the link between training a network and the samplers chapter.
  • The landscape is glassy. A real loss has very many minima separated by barriers, like the energy of a spin glass. Much of the modern theory of training borrows from the physics of glasses for that reason.

The analogy has limits. The noise of SGD is not the same in all directions, and it depends on where the weights are, so the stationary distribution is only approximately a Boltzmann distribution of the loss.

Why this matters for SBI

The curve-fitting chapter found that two networks trained on the same simulations with different random seeds gave error bars that differed by 10 per cent or more, and for NRE by tens of per cent. This is the reason. A trained network is one sample from a distribution over weights at a finite temperature, not the unique minimum of the loss. Training several networks and comparing them, or averaging them, measures that spread, which is why the checks chapter asks for more than one seed.

More worked examples

Worked example: cooling by halving the learning rate

In the worked example above the weight had variance ηs²/[Bκ(2 − ηκ)] = 0.00526 at a learning rate of 0.1. Halve the learning rate to 0.05.

Result. The variance becomes 0.05/(10 × 1.95) = 0.00256, almost exactly half. The spread of the weight about the minimum falls from 0.073 to 0.051. Each halving of the learning rate halves the temperature. A schedule that lowers the rate in stages is a cooling schedule, and the network at the end of training is a system that has been annealed.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
B, kappa = 10, 1.0
for eta in (0.1, 0.05):
    var = eta / (B * kappa * (2 - eta * kappa))
    print("learning rate %.2f: variance %.5f, spread %.3f" % (eta, var, np.sqrt(var)))

Output

learning rate 0.10: variance 0.00526, spread 0.073
learning rate 0.05: variance 0.00256, spread 0.051

Worked example: escaping a poor minimum

The weights sit in a shallow minimum separated from a better one by a barrier of height 0.02 in the loss. At effective temperature T the rate of escape over a barrier is proportional to e−barrier/T, which is Kramers' formula from chemical kinetics. With T = 0.005 that factor is e−4 = 0.018. Halve the temperature and it is e−8 = 0.00034.

Result. Halving the temperature makes escape 55 times slower. Early in training a high temperature lets the weights cross barriers and explore. Late in training a low temperature holds them in place. Lowering the learning rate too soon traps the network in whichever basin it happens to occupy, as quenching a liquid too fast produces a glass.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
barrier = 0.02
for T in (0.005, 0.0025):
    print("T = %.4f: exp(-barrier/T) = %.5f" % (T, np.exp(-barrier / T)))
print("ratio: %.0f" % np.exp(barrier / 0.0025 - barrier / 0.005))

Output

T = 0.0050: exp(-barrier/T) = 0.01832
T = 0.0025: exp(-barrier/T) = 0.00034
ratio: 55

Questions people ask

Is the noise in SGD really thermal? Only approximately. Thermal noise is equally strong in every direction and independent of position. The noise of SGD is stronger along some directions of weight space than others, and changes as the weights move. The stationary distribution is therefore not exactly a Boltzmann distribution of the loss.

Does a lower final loss mean a better network? Not necessarily. The loss is measured on the training simulations. A network at a very sharp minimum can do worse on new data than one in a broad basin with a slightly higher loss, because a small shift in the landscape matters more where the walls are steep.

Why do two training runs give different posteriors? Different random initial weights and a different order of batches lead to different basins. The spread between runs is a real uncertainty of the method. It is not included in the error bars any single network reports.

What should I do about that? Train several networks. If they agree, report any one. If they differ by an amount that matters, average their posteriors, and treat the disagreement as a sign that more simulations are needed.

What to remember

  • Stochastic gradient descent is a particle sliding downhill in the loss while being shaken, with temperature ηs²/2B.
  • The weights end up fluctuating around a minimum, not sitting in it. A larger learning rate or a smaller batch is a higher temperature.
  • Lowering the learning rate is annealing, and the noise favours wide minima.
  • A trained network is one draw from a distribution, so results from a single training run carry a seed-to-seed scatter.
← 22. Energy-based models24. Free energy →

Chapter 24 of 39

Free energy: what training minimises

Fitting a distribution to a posterior is the variational method of statistical mechanics. The same inequality, the same trade between energy and entropy, and the same known weakness.

physics

The connection. Variational inference is the variational method of statistical mechanics, unchanged. The quantity that is maximised is minus a free energy.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Evidence lower bound (ELBO)Minus the variational free energy
ELBO ≤ ln(evidence)Gibbs-Bogoliubov inequality F ≤ Ftrial
Factorised approximationMean-field theory
Gap between bound and truthRelative entropy to the true equilibrium state

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: bounding what cannot be computed

The variational method of statistical mechanics rests on an inequality proved, in different forms, by Gibbs, Bogoliubov and Feynman. The true free energy of a system is never higher than the value computed with any trial distribution. One therefore chooses a family of distributions simple enough to calculate with and adjusts its parameters to make the bound as tight as possible. The earliest example is from 1907, when Weiss replaced the interaction of each atomic magnet with its neighbours by an average field. Mean-field theory, as it came to be called, gave the first explanation of why iron loses its magnetism above a critical temperature.

Machine learning adopted the method in the 1990s. Hinton and van Camp in 1993, and Jordan and colleagues in a 1999 review, used it to make inference tractable in models with many hidden variables. The quantity being maximised is now called the evidence lower bound. Kingma and Welling's variational autoencoder of 2013 combined it with neural networks. The loss function of neural posterior estimation is the same bound once more.

The weakness came along with the method. Mean-field theory ignores correlations, and so gets critical behaviour wrong. Variational inference with a factorised distribution ignores correlations between parameters, and so underestimates their uncertainties. The failure and its cause are the same in both subjects.

The inequality

Let p(θ) = e−E(θ)/Z be the posterior and q(θ) any trial distribution. Define the trial free energy as average energy minus entropy:

F[q]  =  ⟨E⟩q  −  S[q],     S[q] = −∫ q ln q dθ

Derivation in three lines

The mismatch between q and p is measured by the Kullback–Leibler divergence, KL = ∫ q ln(q/p) dθ, which is never negative and is zero only when q = p. Substitute ln p = −E − ln Z:

KL(q ‖ p) = ∫ q ln q + ∫ q E + ln Z = F[q] − (−ln Z)
Result. F[q] ≥ −ln Z, with equality only for the true posterior. Any trial distribution overestimates the free energy, and the gap is exactly the mismatch. This is the Gibbs–Bogoliubov inequality. In machine learning −F[q] is called the evidence lower bound, or ELBO.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
theta = np.linspace(-8, 8, 400001)
E = 0.25 * theta**4 - theta**2 + 0.3 * theta     # any energy will do: here a lopsided double well
lnZ = np.log(np.trapezoid(np.exp(-E), theta))
lnp = -E - lnZ                                   # log of the true posterior
for m, w in ((1.3, 0.6), (-1.5, 0.5), (0.0, 1.5)):               # three Gaussian trial distributions
    q = np.exp(-(theta - m)**2 / (2 * w**2)) / np.sqrt(2 * np.pi * w**2)
    F = np.trapezoid(q * E, theta) + np.trapezoid(q * np.log(q + 1e-300), theta)     # <E> - S
    KL = np.trapezoid(q * (np.log(q + 1e-300) - lnp), theta)
    print("trial N(%.1f, %.1f): F[q] = %.4f, -ln Z = %.4f, difference %.4f, KL = %.4f" % (m, w, F, -lnZ, F + lnZ, KL))

Output

trial N(1.3, 0.6): F[q] = -0.8443, -ln Z = -2.1001, difference 1.2559, KL = 1.2559
trial N(-1.5, 0.5): F[q] = -1.5195, -ln Z = -2.1001, difference 0.5806, KL = 0.5806
trial N(0.0, 1.5): F[q] = -0.2776, -ln Z = -2.1001, difference 1.8225, KL = 1.8225

So "minimise the mismatch" and "minimise the free energy" are one instruction. The two terms pull opposite ways, as in thermodynamics. Low energy wants q packed onto the best fit. High entropy wants q spread out. Their balance sets the error bar.

Worked example: a Gaussian trial for a quadratic energy

Take E(θ) = κ(θ − θ0)²/2 and a Gaussian trial q = N(m, w²). Then ⟨E⟩ = κ[(m − θ0)² + w²]/2 and the entropy of a Gaussian is ln w plus a constant:

F(m, w) = κ (m − θ0)² / 2 + κ w² / 2 − ln w + const

Minimise: ∂F/∂m = 0 gives m = θ0. ∂F/∂w = κ w − 1/w = 0 gives w² = 1/κ.

Result. The variational answer is the exact posterior, as it must be when the trial family contains the truth. The width comes from the balance κ w = 1/w: the energy cost of spreading against the entropy gain.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import minimize
kappa, theta0 = 4.0, 1.0
F = lambda z: kappa * (z[0] - theta0)**2 / 2 + kappa * np.exp(2 * z[1]) / 2 - z[1]      # z = (m, ln w)
fit = minimize(F, [0.0, 0.0])
print("variational: m = %.4f, w = %.4f;  exact posterior: mean %.4f, sd %.4f" % (fit.x[0], np.exp(fit.x[1]), theta0, kappa**-0.5))

Output

variational: m = 1.0000, w = 0.5000;  exact posterior: mean 1.0000, sd 0.5000

The known weakness: mean-field theory

In practice the trial family does not contain the truth. The commonest shortcut treats the parameters as independent, q(θ1, θ2) = q1(θ1) q2(θ2). Physicists call this mean-field theory: each spin feels only the average of its neighbours, giving the self-consistent equation m = tanh(J z m/kT) for the Ising magnet. It is simple, often useful, and it ignores correlations.

Worked example: mean field on two correlated parameters

Let the true posterior be a two-dimensional Gaussian with unit variances and correlation ρ. Its energy is

E = ( θ1² − 2ρ θ1 θ2 + θ2² ) / 2(1 − ρ²)

Minimising F over independent Gaussians gives each factor the stiffness of the well with the other parameter held fixed, which is 1/(1 − ρ²).

Result. The mean-field variance is 1 − ρ², against a true variance of 1. The centre is right and the error bar is too small.
Correlation ρTrue s.d.Mean-field s.d.Too small by
01.001.001.0×
0.51.000.871.15×
0.91.000.442.3×
0.991.000.147.1×
Show the code 
import numpy as np
for rho in (0.0, 0.5, 0.9, 0.99):
    K = np.linalg.inv(np.array([[1.0, rho], [rho, 1.0]]))        # stiffness matrix of the true posterior
    mean_field_sd = K[0, 0]**-0.5                # each factor sees the stiffness with the other held fixed
    print("rho = %.2f: true sd 1.00, mean-field sd %.2f, too small by %.2fx" % (rho, mean_field_sd, 1 / mean_field_sd))

Output

rho = 0.00: true sd 1.00, mean-field sd 1.00, too small by 1.00x
rho = 0.50: true sd 1.00, mean-field sd 0.87, too small by 1.15x
rho = 0.90: true sd 1.00, mean-field sd 0.44, too small by 2.29x
rho = 0.99: true sd 1.00, mean-field sd 0.14, too small by 7.09x

This is the general tendency of this kind of variational fit: where the trial family is too simple it hugs one part of the true distribution and reports too little uncertainty. Mean-field theory makes the matching error in physics, overestimating order because it neglects fluctuations.

NPE as described in the neural methods chapter minimises the divergence the other way round, KL(p ‖ q), averaged over simulated data. That direction penalises missing probability mass, so a well-trained NPE tends to be broad where it is unsure. It can still be overconfident when training is short or the data are unlike the training set, which is why the checks chapter exists.

Three more links

  • Model comparison is a free-energy difference. The ratio of the evidences of two models is e−ΔF, and the penalty for an unnecessary parameter is an entropy cost. Worked through in the next chapter.
  • Tempering. Raise the likelihood to a power β between 0 and 1 and you have the same system at temperature 1/β. Samplers heat the posterior to cross barriers between peaks and then cool it. Integrating the average energy over β from 0 to 1 gives ln Z, which is thermodynamic integration.
  • Multiple peaks are coexisting phases. A posterior with two separated peaks is a system with two phases. A sampler stuck in one is in a metastable state, and for the same reason: the barrier is high compared with the temperature.

Questions people ask

Why does variational inference give error bars that are too small? Because of the direction of the relative entropy it minimises. That direction penalises the trial distribution heavily for putting weight where the true posterior has little, and lightly for missing regions the posterior covers. Faced with a correlated posterior, a factorised trial distribution shrinks to fit inside it.

Is neural posterior estimation variational inference? It minimises a relative entropy in the opposite direction, averaged over simulated data sets. That direction penalises missing mass, so it tends to be broader, not narrower, when the family is too restrictive. In practice finite training data can still make it overconfident.

What is the 'free energy' of a statistical model? Minus the log of the evidence. The bound says that the average energy under the trial distribution, minus its entropy, is never below it. Energy favours concentrating on the best fit, and entropy favours spreading out. The optimum balances them, as in thermodynamics.

How good is the bound? The gap is exactly the relative entropy between the trial distribution and the true posterior. It vanishes only if the family contains the truth. This is why making the family more flexible, for instance with a normalising flow, tightens it.

constructionSaddle points and loops: the Laplace approximation as a one-loop calculation

The standard way to evaluate a path integral is to expand about the configuration of least action. The leading term is the classical action, the next comes from Gaussian fluctuations about it (one loop), and higher terms are organised by a small parameter. Statistics calls the first two terms the Laplace approximation:

−ln Z ≈ E(θ*) + ½ ln det[ H / 2π ] classical action plus one-loop determinant; H is the matrix of second derivatives at the best fit θ*

Worked example: Stirling's formula as a loop expansion

The factorial is an integral: n! = ∫0∞ e−E(t) dt with energy E(t) = t − n ln t. The energy is least at t* = n, where E = n − n ln n and the curvature is E″ = 1/n.

OrderApproximation to n!Value for n = 5
Classicale−E(t*) = nn e−n21.06
One loop× √(2πn)118.02
Two loops× (1 + 1/12n)119.99
Exact120
Result. The classical term alone is wrong by a factor of nearly six. The Gaussian fluctuation factor √(2πn), a one-loop determinant, repairs almost all of it, and the next correction is suppressed by 1/n. Here 1/n plays the part of ħ.
Show the code 
import numpy as np, math
n = 5
classical = n**n * np.exp(-n)
one_loop = classical * np.sqrt(2 * np.pi * n)
two_loop = one_loop * (1 + 1 / (12 * n))
print("classical %.2f, one loop %.2f, two loops %.2f, exact %d" % (classical, one_loop, two_loop, math.factorial(n)))

Output

classical 21.06, one loop 118.02, two loops 119.99, exact 120

In inference the small parameter is one over the number of data. The theorem that posteriors become Gaussian with many data (Bernstein–von Mises) says that the loop expansion converges, and the Fisher forecast is its leading term. Because the determinant of H grows as nd for d parameters, the one-loop term contributes (d/2) ln n to the free energy, which is the penalty in the Bayesian information criterion. Non-Gaussian features of a posterior, such as skewness and long tails, are the higher-loop terms, and a posterior with several peaks has several saddle points, with tunnelling between them that no finite order of the expansion describes.

The variational method of this chapter is the complementary tool, and Feynman used it on path integrals for the same reason it is used here: it is not an expansion in a small parameter, so it survives when the fluctuations are large.

← 23. Training as physics25. Gaussian processes →

Chapter 25 of 39

Gaussian processes: when the unknown is a function

So far the unknowns have been a few numbers. Often the unknown is a whole curve: a brightness across the sky, a response against frequency, a signal against time. A Gaussian process is the simplest prior over curves, and it is a free field theory.

physics

The connection. A Gaussian process is a free field theory. Its kernel is the propagator.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Prior over functionsGaussian weight exp(−S[φ]) with a quadratic action
Kernel k(x, x′)Two-point function, or propagator
Posterior mean given dataClassical field in the presence of sources
Non-Gaussian priorsInteracting field theory

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: a distribution over functions

The Gaussian process has been discovered several times. Wiener and Kolmogorov used it in the 1940s to predict and filter time series. In the 1950s the South African mining engineer Krige used it to estimate gold concentrations between boreholes, and Matheron formalised the procedure as kriging, which is still the standard method in geostatistics. Machine learning took it up in the 1990s, and Rasmussen and Williams' book of 2006 is the usual reference. In 1996 Neal showed that a neural network with one very wide hidden layer and random weights is a Gaussian process.

In physics the same object is a free field. A field whose values at different points are jointly Gaussian, with a covariance that depends on their separation, is the starting point of field theory: its covariance is the propagator, and everything about it can be computed. The cosmic microwave background is modelled as a Gaussian random field on the sphere, and its power spectrum is the kernel written in a basis of spherical harmonics.

In simulation-based work Gaussian processes are most used as emulators: fast stand-ins for an expensive simulator, trained on a few hundred runs, that also report how uncertain they are between those runs. The approach goes back to Sacks and colleagues in 1989 and has been standard in cosmology since the mid-2000s.

A prior over curves

A function has a value at every point, so it is like a parameter vector with infinitely many entries. That sounds unmanageable, but only two ingredients are needed. First, an average curve, usually taken to be zero. Second, a rule saying how strongly the values at two points x and x′ are tied together, called the kernel. A common choice:

k(x, x′) = exp[ −(x − x′)² / 2ℓ² ]

Points much closer than the length ℓ have nearly equal values. Points much farther apart are unrelated. The kernel therefore says "the curve is smooth on the scale ℓ" without saying anything about its shape. A Gaussian process is the statement that any finite set of values of the curve is jointly Gaussian with these covariances.

Learning the curve from a few points

Measure the curve at a few places, with noise. Because everything is Gaussian, the posterior for the value anywhere else is Gaussian too, and it is given by the same formula that combined a prior and a measurement in the dictionary chapter, written with matrices:

mean(x*) = k*T (K + σn² I)−1 y,     variance(x*) = k(x*, x*) − k*T (K + σn² I)−1 k*

Here K holds the kernel between measured points, k* the kernel between the new point and the measured ones, and σn is the measurement noise.

Worked example: two measurements

Take ℓ = 1 and unit prior variance. Measure 1.0 at x = 0 and 0.5 at x = 1, each with noise 0.1. The predictions:

WherePredicted valueError barWhy
x = 0.5, between the points0.820.19Both neighbours inform it
x = 2, one length beyond0.050.74Little information reaches this far
x = 4, far away0.001.00Back to the prior
Result. The error bar is small near the data and grows to the prior value away from them. The method knows where it is ignorant, which a fitted polynomial does not. Notice that the value midway, 0.82, is above the average of the two measurements, 0.75: the smoothness assumption prefers a curve that bulges between them.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
k = lambda a, b: np.exp(-0.5 * (a - b)**2)       # length 1, unit prior variance
xo, yo, noise = np.array([0.0, 1.0]), np.array([1.0, 0.5]), 0.1
K = k(xo[:, None], xo[None, :]) + noise**2 * np.eye(2)
for xs in (0.5, 2.0, 4.0):
    ks = k(xs, xo)
    print("x = %.1f: predicted %.2f, error bar %.2f" % (xs, abs(ks @ np.linalg.solve(K, yo)), np.sqrt(1 - ks @ np.linalg.solve(K, ks))))

Output

x = 0.5: predicted 0.82, error bar 0.19
x = 2.0: predicted 0.05, error bar 0.74
x = 4.0: predicted 0.00, error bar 1.00

Uses in simulation-based inference

  • Emulators. If each simulation takes hours, run it at a few dozen parameter values and let a Gaussian process interpolate, with honest error bars between the runs. This was the standard surrogate before neural networks and is still the better choice when simulations are few.
  • Choosing the next simulation. Run next where the emulator is least certain, or where a new run is expected to help most. This is the experimental design of the information chapter, applied to the simulator.
  • Priors on nuisance functions. A slowly varying instrumental gain, or an unknown smooth background, can be given a Gaussian-process prior and marginalised.

More worked examples

Worked example: interpolating, and forgetting

A function has a Gaussian-process prior with mean zero, unit variance and a squared-exponential kernel of length 1, so the correlation between points a distance r apart is e−r²/2. It is observed without noise to be 1 at x = 0 and 0 at x = 2. Predict it at x = 1, midway. By symmetry the two observations get equal weights, w = e−1/2/(1 + e−2) = 0.534.

at x = 1:   mean = 0.534 × 1 + 0.534 × 0 = 0.53,    sd = √(1 − 2 × 0.607 × 0.534) = 0.59

Now predict at x = 5, three lengths beyond the last point. The correlations with both observations are below 0.012.

Result. At x = 5 the prediction is 0.00 ± 1.00, which is the prior. A Gaussian process interpolates between nearby points and honestly reverts to ignorance far from them. The correlation length of the kernel is the distance over which information travels, exactly as the correlation length of a field sets how far a disturbance is felt.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
k = lambda a, b: np.exp(-0.5 * (a - b)**2)       # squared-exponential kernel, length 1
xo, fo = np.array([0.0, 2.0]), np.array([1.0, 0.0])
K = k(xo[:, None], xo[None, :])
for xs in (1.0, 5.0):
    ks = k(xs, xo)
    w = np.linalg.solve(K, ks)
    print("x = %.0f: weights %s, mean %.2f, sd %.2f" % (xs, w.round(3), w @ fo, np.sqrt(1 - ks @ w)))

Output

x = 1: weights [0.534 0.534], mean 0.53, sd 0.59
x = 5: weights [-0.002  0.011], mean -0.00, sd 1.00

Questions people ask

How can there be a probability distribution over functions? By only ever asking about finitely many points. A Gaussian process is the rule that the values of the function at any finite set of points are jointly Gaussian, with covariances given by the kernel. That rule is consistent however the points are chosen, which is all a definition requires.

How do I choose the kernel? It encodes what you believe about the function before seeing data: how smooth it is, over what length it varies, whether it is periodic. Its parameters are usually set by maximising the evidence, which is the subject of the hyperparameters chapter.

Why do Gaussian processes not scale to large data sets? The exact calculation inverts a matrix with one row per data point, which costs the cube of the number of points. A few thousand points are routine, and a million are not without approximations.

What does field theory add? A way to go beyond Gaussian. A prior with interactions, in the sense of terms beyond quadratic in the action, describes non-Gaussian functions, and its corrections can be organised by the same diagrams as in an interacting field theory. This is how the non-Gaussian features of the galaxy distribution are treated.

constructionA free field theory, with the data as source

The prior is e−S with a quadratic action. Write the Gaussian-process prior as a weight on functions, p[f] ∝ exp(−S[f]) with

S[f] = ½ ∫∫ f(x) k−1(x, x′) f(x′) dx dx′

This is the action of a free field. The kernel is its two-point function, the propagator, and its inverse is the operator in the action.

A kernel that is literally a propagator. Take the exponential kernel k = e−|x − x′|/ℓ. Up to a constant it is the Green's function of the operator −d²/dx² + m², with m = 1/ℓ:

( −d²/dx² + m² ) e−m|x| / 2m = δ(x)

So the action is ∫ [ (df/dx)² + m² f² ] dx, a free scalar field of mass m in one dimension. The correlation length is the inverse mass, as always. Read x as imaginary time and the same object is the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process of the scores chapter and the ground state of a quantum harmonic oscillator. One Gaussian process, three names.

The data are a source. Noisy measurements add to the action a term coupling the field to the observed values, and a term quadratic in the field at the measured points. Completing the square gives the posterior mean as the classical field in the presence of the source, and the posterior covariance as the propagator in the presence of the measurement term. The two formulas above are those statements.

The Wiener filter. If the process is statistically the same everywhere, the kernel is diagonal in Fourier space, with power spectrum S(k). For noise of spectrum N(k) the posterior mean is, mode by mode,

f̂(k) = [ S(k) / ( S(k) + N(k) ) ] · y(k)

the shrinkage factor τ²/(τ² + s²) of the dictionary chapter applied separately to each Fourier mode. Modes where the signal dominates are kept. Modes where the noise dominates are suppressed. This is the optimal linear filter used throughout astronomy and cosmology.

Beyond the free theory. Real fields are often not Gaussian: a brightness must be positive, a density field has been made non-Gaussian by gravity. Adding such knowledge adds interaction terms to the action, and the reconstruction then needs the non-perturbative tools of this course. That programme is information field theory, and it is the most direct instance of the correspondence this course describes: the inference problem is a field theory, and is solved as one.

← 24. Free energy26. The Wiener filter →

Chapter 26 of 39

The Wiener filter: a field answering a source

The best estimate of a signal buried in noise is given by a formula from 1942. Read as physics, it says that the data act as a source and the estimate is the field that the source produces.

physics

The connection. The optimal reconstruction of a Gaussian signal from noisy data is the classical field produced by a source. The data are the source and the posterior covariance is the propagator.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Data weighted by inverse noise, j = N⁻¹dSource J
Posterior covariance D = (S⁻¹ + N⁻¹)⁻¹Propagator G
Wiener-filtered map m = D jClassical field φcl = G J
Non-Gaussian or non-linear reconstructionInteracting field theory (information field theory)

Background: from anti-aircraft guns to the microwave sky

Wiener worked out the optimal linear filter during the Second World War, for predicting the path of an aircraft from noisy radar. Kolmogorov had solved the discrete version in 1941. The report was classified and so mathematical that engineers called it the yellow peril, after the colour of its cover. The filter weighs each frequency of the data by how much of the power at that frequency is signal.

The same formula became the standard tool for making maps in astronomy. The maps of the microwave background, of the large-scale distribution of galaxies and of gravitational lensing are Wiener-filtered reconstructions or close relatives. From 2009 Enßlin and collaborators recast the whole subject as information field theory: the unknown signal is a field, its prior and likelihood define an action, and the posterior is the corresponding statistical field theory. For Gaussian signal and noise the theory is free and the Wiener filter is its exact solution. Anything non-Gaussian or non-linear adds interaction terms, treated with Feynman diagrams.

The formula

The data are signal plus noise, d = s + n. The signal has prior covariance S and the noise has covariance N, both Gaussian with mean zero. Minus the log of the posterior is a quadratic function of the signal, so the posterior is Gaussian, and completing the square gives its mean and covariance.

physics

A free field with a source

Action. S[φ] = ½ φ G⁻¹ φ − J φ

φ: the field. G: the propagator, the response of the field at one point to a unit source at another. J: the source.

Classical field. φcl = G J
The field that minimises the action.

Fluctuations. ⟨δφ δφ⟩ = G (with kBT = 1). The same propagator gives the response and the fluctuations.

inference

Signal reconstruction

Energy. E(s) = ½ sᵀS⁻¹s + ½ (d − s)ᵀN⁻¹(d − s)

Collect terms: E = ½ sᵀD⁻¹s − jᵀs + constant, with D = (S⁻¹ + N⁻¹)⁻¹ and j = N⁻¹d.

Posterior mean. m = D j = S (S + N)⁻¹ d
This is the Wiener filter.

Posterior covariance. D. The data, weighted by the inverse noise, are the source. D is the propagator.

Worked example: one number

A signal with prior variance 4 is measured once with noise variance 1. The reading is 3.0. The filter multiplies the reading by S/(S + N) = 4/5.

Result. The estimate is 0.8 × 3.0 = 2.4, with variance SN/(S + N) = 0.8, so ±0.89. The filter always shrinks the data towards the prior mean, by more when the noise is larger. This is the shrinkage of the hierarchical chapter and the first step of the Kalman filter in the inference-in-time chapter. They are the same formula.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
S, N, d = 4.0, 1.0, 3.0
print("weight %.1f, estimate %.1f, variance %.1f, sd %.2f" % (S / (S + N), S / (S + N) * d, S * N / (S + N), np.sqrt(S * N / (S + N))))

Output

weight 0.8, estimate 2.4, variance 0.8, sd 0.89

Worked example: filling a gap

Two neighbouring pixels have signal variance 1 and correlation 0.8. Only the first is observed, with noise variance 0.5, and reads 1.2. The second has no data at all. The filter gives it the value S21/(S11 + N) times the reading.

Result. The unobserved pixel is estimated as 0.8/1.5 × 1.2 = 0.64, with variance 1 − 0.8²/1.5 = 0.57, so ±0.76. Information reached it through the prior correlation, which is the propagator carrying the influence of a source from one point to another. Its uncertainty is reduced from 1.0 but remains large. Filling masked regions of a sky map is this calculation with millions of pixels.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
S = np.array([[1.0, 0.8], [0.8, 1.0]])           # signal covariance of the two pixels
N, d1 = 0.5, 1.2                                 # noise variance and reading of pixel 1
est = S[1, 0] / (S[0, 0] + N) * d1
var = S[1, 1] - S[1, 0]**2 / (S[0, 0] + N)
print("unobserved pixel: %.2f +/- %.2f (variance %.2f)" % (est, np.sqrt(var), var))

Output

unobserved pixel: 0.64 +/- 0.76 (variance 0.57)

Worked example: filtering by wavelength

A stationary signal has power spectrum Ps(k) = 1/(k² + 1), which is the propagator of a free field of unit mass. The noise is white, with Pn = 0.1 at every k. In Fourier space the filter is diagonal: each mode is multiplied by Ps/(Ps + Pn).

Show the code 
for k in (0, 1, 3, 10):
    Ps, Pn = 1 / (k**2 + 1), 0.1
    print("k = %2d: signal power %.3f, filter weight %.2f" % (k, Ps, Ps / (Ps + Pn)))

Output

k =  0: signal power 1.000, filter weight 0.91
k =  1: signal power 0.500, filter weight 0.83
k =  3: signal power 0.100, filter weight 0.50
k = 10: signal power 0.010, filter weight 0.09
Wavenumber kSignal powerFilter weight
01.0000.91
10.5000.83
30.1000.50
100.0100.09
Result. Long wavelengths pass almost unchanged and short ones are suppressed. The changeover is at k = 3, where signal and noise power are equal. The filtered map is smooth because the fine detail in the data is mostly noise, and the filter knows it. A filtered map therefore has less power than the true signal on small scales, which must be remembered when it is analysed further.

Beyond the free theory

The Wiener filter is exact only when signal and noise are Gaussian and the measurement is linear. A signal that must be positive, counts with Poisson noise, or an instrument with a non-linear response all add terms to the energy beyond quadratic. The posterior is then an interacting field theory. Its mean can be found by perturbation theory about the Wiener solution, by a variational approximation, or by sampling, which are the methods of the earlier chapters applied to a field. When even the likelihood is unavailable, the reconstruction is done with simulations and a network, and that is SBI for fields.

Questions people ask

Is the Wiener filter a Bayesian method? It was derived as the linear estimate with the smallest mean squared error, with no mention of priors. It coincides with the Bayesian posterior mean when signal and noise are Gaussian. For non-Gaussian signals it is still the best linear estimate, and no longer the best estimate.

Where do S and N come from? The noise covariance comes from knowledge of the instrument. The signal covariance is the difficulty: it is often what one wants to learn. The usual answer is to treat the power spectrum as a hyperparameter and estimate it together with the map.

Why is a Wiener-filtered map biased low? Because it is the posterior mean, and a mean is smoother than any single realisation. Where the data are uninformative the estimate falls to the prior mean of zero. Samples drawn from the posterior have the right amount of power, and the mean does not.

What does the field-theory language buy? A systematic way to go beyond the Gaussian case. The corrections for non-linear and non-Gaussian problems are organised by diagrams built from the propagator D and the source j, and the rules for computing them are already known.

What to remember

  • The Wiener filter is the posterior mean of a Gaussian signal in Gaussian noise: m = S(S + N)⁻¹d.
  • In field-theory terms the data are a source, the posterior covariance is the propagator, and the estimate is the classical field.
  • Each mode is weighted by signal power over total power, so the filter passes what is mostly signal and suppresses what is mostly noise.
