  • Non-Gaussian and non-linear problems are interacting theories built on this free one.
← 25. Gaussian processes27. Nuisance parameters →

Chapter 27 of 39

Nuisance parameters: integrating out what you do not want

Most parameters in an analysis are not the ones you care about. Removing them properly is an integral, and field theory calls the result an effective action.

physics

The connection. Marginalising over a nuisance parameter is integrating out a degree of freedom. What remains is an effective action for the parameters that were kept.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Nuisance parametersHeavy fields, or fast degrees of freedom
Marginal likelihoodEffective action
Schur complement Fθθ − Fθν²/FννEffective stiffness k1 − c²/k2
Profiling (maximising over the nuisance)Tree-level effective action
The determinant kept by marginalisingOne-loop correction

Background: effective theories

A model of a real experiment has parameters of two kinds. A few are the object of the study. The others describe the apparatus and the foreground: calibrations, backgrounds, beam shapes, amplitudes of contaminating sources. They are called nuisance parameters, and there are often far more of them than parameters of interest. The Bayesian instruction is to integrate over them. What is left is the marginal posterior of the parameters of interest, with uncertainties that include ignorance of the nuisances.

Field theory has done the same since the 1930s. A complete description of a process at low energy does not need the heavy particles explicitly. Integrating them out of the path integral leaves a theory of the light particles alone, the effective theory, whose couplings have been shifted by the particles that were removed. Fermi's theory of the weak interaction is what remains of the modern theory after the heavy W boson has been integrated out. Wilson made this the foundation of the renormalisation group in the 1970s.

The correspondence is exact. The marginal likelihood is an effective action for the parameters that were kept, and the two characteristic effects of integrating out, a shift in the couplings and a determinant, have direct statistical meanings.

The Gaussian case, done exactly

physics

Two coupled oscillators

Energy. E(x, y) = ½ k1x² + ½ k2y² + c x y

x: the light degree of freedom to be kept. y: the one to be removed. c: their coupling.

Integrate out y. ∫ e−E dy = √(2π/k2) · exp(−½ keff x²)

Effective stiffness. keff = k1 − c²/k2
The coupling to y has softened the spring on x.

Determinant. The prefactor √(2π/k2) is the fluctuation volume of y. If k2 depends on x, it becomes a new term in the effective energy.

inference

A parameter of interest and a nuisance

Energy. E(θ, ν) = ½ Fθθθ² + ½ Fννν² + Fθνθν

θ: the parameter of interest. ν: the nuisance. F: the Fisher matrix.

Marginalise over ν. The same integral.

Marginal Fisher information. Feff = Fθθ − Fθν²/Fνν
The degeneracy with ν has reduced what is known about θ. This is the Schur complement.

Determinant. The volume of nuisance values compatible with the data. Dropping it is the difference between marginalising and merely maximising over ν.

Worked example: the cost of one nuisance parameter

Two parameters have a Fisher matrix with 10 on the diagonal and 8 off it, as in the soft-mode example of the error-bars chapter. Treat the second as a nuisance and integrate it out.

Result. Feff = 10 − 8²/10 = 3.6, so the error bar on the parameter of interest is 1/√3.6 = 0.527. With the nuisance known exactly it would be 1/√10 = 0.316. The marginal error is the one obtained earlier by inverting the full matrix, as it must be: marginalising and reading the diagonal of the inverse are the same operation. Not knowing the nuisance costs a factor of 1.67 in precision.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
F = np.array([[10., 8.], [8., 10.]])
F_eff = F[0, 0] - F[0, 1]**2 / F[1, 1]           # Schur complement
print("F_eff = %.1f: marginal error %.3f, from the full inverse %.3f, with the nuisance known %.3f" % (F_eff, F_eff**-0.5, np.sqrt(np.linalg.inv(F)[0, 0]), F[0, 0]**-0.5))

Output

F_eff = 3.6: marginal error 0.527, from the full inverse 0.527, with the nuisance known 0.316

Worked example: a calibration offset sets a floor

A quantity is measured n times with noise 1. Every reading shares an unknown calibration offset, with a prior of width 0.3. The offset is completely degenerate with the quantity in the data and is constrained only by its prior. Marginalising adds its variance to that of the average.

n = 4:  √(1/4 + 0.3²) = 0.58      n = 100:  √(1/100 + 0.3²) = 0.32      n → ∞:  0.30
Result. With 4 readings the offset raises the error from 0.50 to 0.58. With 100 readings it raises it from 0.10 to 0.32. More data cannot take the error below 0.30. A nuisance that the data cannot constrain is a systematic floor, and it enters the final error exactly as an integrated-out field enters an effective theory: as a term that does not scale away.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
for n in (4, 100, 10**8):
    print("n = %9d: error without offset %.2f, with offset %.2f" % (n, 1 / np.sqrt(n), np.sqrt(1 / n + 0.3**2)))

Output

n =         4: error without offset 0.50, with offset 0.58
n =       100: error without offset 0.10, with offset 0.32
n = 100000000: error without offset 0.00, with offset 0.30

Worked example: an unknown noise level

Five readings are averaged, and the noise level is not known in advance. It has to be estimated from the scatter of the same five readings. Integrating over the unknown noise level turns the Gaussian for the mean into a Student t distribution with 4 degrees of freedom.

Result. The 68 per cent interval is 1.14 times the naive one, and the 95 per cent interval is 2.78 standard errors in place of 1.96. With 20 readings the factors are 1.03 and 2.09. Here the determinant term matters: the volume of noise levels compatible with the data depends on where the mean is, and that dependence is what fattens the tails. Plugging in the best-fit noise level, which is profiling, would give error bars that are too small.
Show the code 
from scipy import stats
for n in (5, 20):
    t = stats.t(n - 1)
    print("%2d readings: 68%% interval x %.2f, 95%% interval %.2f standard errors (Gaussian 1.96)" % (n, t.ppf(0.8413), t.ppf(0.975)))

Output

 5 readings: 68% interval x 1.14, 95% interval 2.78 standard errors (Gaussian 1.96)
20 readings: 68% interval x 1.03, 95% interval 2.09 standard errors (Gaussian 1.96)

Profiling against marginalising

There are two ways to remove a nuisance parameter. Marginalising integrates over it. Profiling sets it, for each value of the parameters of interest, to whatever value fits best. In the Gaussian case with a fixed Fisher matrix the two give the same width, because the determinant is a constant. They differ when the range of acceptable nuisance values changes from place to place. Then the determinant term, the log of the nuisance volume, adds to the effective energy, and profiling misses it.

In field-theory terms profiling is the tree-level effective action, obtained by solving the equation of motion of the heavy field and substituting the solution. Marginalising adds the one-loop determinant. For linear parameters with Gaussian priors, such as the amplitudes of known templates, the integral can be done analytically, and the analysis proceeds with an effective likelihood for the remaining parameters that includes the determinant.

Questions people ask

Should I marginalise or profile? In a Bayesian analysis, marginalise: it is what the rules of probability require. Profiling is the standard frequentist procedure and is often a good approximation, but it can understate uncertainty when the nuisance is poorly constrained in part of the parameter space.

Does marginalising always increase the error bar? Compared with fixing the nuisance at a known value, yes, or leaves it the same if the two parameters are uncorrelated. That is the price of not knowing the nuisance, and it is the honest price.

What if there are thousands of nuisance parameters? Then integrate analytically where possible, which covers anything that enters linearly with a Gaussian prior, and sample the others. Neural methods offer a third route: simulate with the nuisances drawn from their priors and train only on the parameters of interest. The network then learns the marginal posterior directly, and the integral is done by the simulator.

Is that last point not too good to be true? It is the main practical advantage of SBI. Marginalisation costs nothing extra, because simulating with random nuisance values and ignoring them is integrating over them. The cost is that the network must be shown enough variety in the nuisances to learn their effect.

What to remember

  • Marginalising over a nuisance parameter is integrating out a degree of freedom. The marginal likelihood is an effective action.
  • For Gaussians the result is the Schur complement: the information about the parameter of interest is reduced by its degeneracy with the nuisance.
  • A nuisance the data cannot constrain leaves a floor in the error that more data do not remove.
  • Profiling keeps the tree-level term and drops the determinant. The two agree only when the volume of acceptable nuisance values is constant.
  • In SBI, marginalising is free: draw the nuisances at random in the simulator and do not ask the network for them.
← 26. The Wiener filter28. Model comparison →

Chapter 28 of 39

Model comparison: evidence, Bayes factors and Occam's razor

Is the extra parameter needed? The question is answered by a free-energy difference, and the answer contains a penalty for complexity that nobody had to put in by hand.

physics

The connection. Comparing two models is comparing two free energies. Occam's razor is the entropy term.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
EvidencePartition function
ln(Bayes factor)Free-energy difference
Occam factorFluctuation determinant: the entropy of the fluctuations about the saddle
Best fit against simplicityEnergy against entropy

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: weighing hypotheses

The use of probability to compare theories, not only to estimate parameters, was set out by Jeffreys in his 1939 book. He introduced the ratio of evidences, now called the Bayes factor, with a scale for interpreting it that is still quoted. The automatic penalty against needless complexity was noticed early, and was explained most clearly by MacKay in 1992: a model with more freedom spreads its predictions over more possible data sets, so it assigns less probability to any particular one, including the one observed.

Schwarz derived a large-sample approximation in 1978, the Bayesian information criterion, in which each extra parameter costs half the logarithm of the number of data points. Kass and Raftery's 1995 review made Bayes factors a standard tool. Their dependence on the prior was pointed out by Lindley in 1957, in a paradox that carries his name.

In physics the comparison of two phases is the comparison of two free energies, and the phase with the lower one is the one observed. The free energy contains an energy and an entropy, and a phase can win on either. The Bayes factor is the same contest: goodness of fit against the volume of parameter space the model had to commit.

The evidence

For a model M, the evidence is the probability of the data averaged over everything the model's prior allows:

ZM = p(x | M) = ∫ p(x | θ, M) p(θ | M) dθ,     FM = −ln ZM

Two models are compared by the ratio of their evidences, the Bayes factor B = Z1/Z0 = e−ΔF. In physics the phase with the lower free energy is the one you find. Here the model with the lower free energy is the one the data favour.

Where Occam's razor comes from

A flexible model spreads its prior over many possible data sets, so it assigns less probability to any one of them. It is favoured only if the improvement in fit outweighs that dilution. In thermodynamic terms the extra parameter lowers the energy (a better fit) but costs entropy (prior volume that the data ruled out).

Worked example: is there a signal?

Model 0: no signal, θ = 0. Model 1: a signal of unknown size, θ ~ N(0, τ²). The measurement x̄ has noise variance s². Under model 0 the data are N(0, s²). Under model 1 they are N(0, s² + τ²). Dividing the two Gaussians:

ln B10 = (x̄² / 2) [ 1/s² − 1/(s² + τ²) ] − ½ ln(1 + τ²/s²)

The first term rewards the fit. The second is the Occam penalty, and it does not depend on the data at all. With τ = 2 and s = 0.5 the penalty is ½ ln 17 = 1.42.

Measured x̄Significanceln B10Bayes factorVerdict
0.51.0σ−0.950.39Favours no signal, 2.6 to 1
0.871.7σ0.001.0Even
1.22.4σ1.293.6Mild support for a signal
2.04.0σ6.11450Strong support
Result. A 1σ excess counts against the signal model even though that model fits it better, and a 2.4σ excess is worth odds of less than 4 to 1. The break-even is at 1.7σ. Widening the prior τ raises the penalty: a model that could have predicted almost anything gets little credit for predicting what was seen.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
tau, s = 2.0, 0.5
penalty = 0.5 * np.log(1 + tau**2 / s**2)
print("Occam penalty %.2f; break-even at %.2f = %.1f sigma" % (penalty, np.sqrt(2 * penalty / (1 / s**2 - 1 / (s**2 + tau**2))), np.sqrt(2 * penalty / (1 / s**2 - 1 / (s**2 + tau**2))) / s))
for xbar in (0.5, 0.87, 1.2, 2.0):
    lnB = xbar**2 / 2 * (1 / s**2 - 1 / (s**2 + tau**2)) - penalty
    print("xbar = %.2f (%.1f sigma): ln B = %+.2f, Bayes factor %.3g" % (xbar, xbar / s, lnB, np.exp(lnB)))

Output

Occam penalty 1.42; break-even at 0.87 = 1.7 sigma
xbar = 0.50 (1.0 sigma): ln B = -0.95, Bayes factor 0.388
xbar = 0.87 (1.7 sigma): ln B = +0.01, Bayes factor 1.01
xbar = 1.20 (2.4 sigma): ln B = +1.29, Bayes factor 3.65
xbar = 2.00 (4.0 sigma): ln B = +6.11, Bayes factor 452

Doing it with simulations

  • Nested sampling returns the evidence directly (samplers chapter), when a likelihood or a learned stand-in for one is available.
  • Thermodynamic integration. Raise the likelihood to a power β, the inverse temperature, and integrate the average log-likelihood from β = 0 to 1. This is how free-energy differences are measured in molecular simulation.
  • Classify the models. Simulate data from each model and train a classifier to tell which produced them. As in NRE, the odds of a perfect classifier are the Bayes factor. The model index is treated as one more parameter.

Cautions

  • The Bayes factor depends on the prior width even when the posterior does not. State the prior, and show how the answer moves when it is changed.
  • A common descriptive scale: ln B below 1 is not worth more than a mention, 1 to 2.5 is moderate, 2.5 to 5 is strong, above 5 is decisive. These are conventions, not thresholds with a derivation.
  • Both models may be wrong. A large Bayes factor says one is better than the other, not that it is good. That needs the tests in the checks chapter.

More worked examples

Worked example: three sigma is not always evidence

A parameter is measured as 0.30 ± 0.10, three standard deviations from zero. Is it really non-zero? Compare the model in which it is exactly zero with one in which it is free, with a Gaussian prior of width 2. For nested models the Bayes factor in favour of zero is the posterior density at zero divided by the prior density at zero.

posterior density at 0:  0.0443     prior density at 0:  0.1995     ratio: 0.22
Result. The odds are 4.5 to 1 for a non-zero value, far weaker than the 370 to 1 that "three sigma" suggests. With a prior ten times wider, the same data give odds of 2.2 to 1 in favour of zero. The measurement did not change. The wider model simply made a vaguer prediction. This is Lindley's paradox, and it is the Occam factor at work.
Show the code 
from scipy import stats
post = stats.norm(0.30, 0.10)
for width in (2, 20):
    bf = post.pdf(0) / stats.norm(0, width).pdf(0)           # Savage-Dickey ratio, in favour of zero
    print("prior width %2d: posterior %.4f, prior %.4f, ratio %.2f -> odds %.1f to 1 %s" % (width, post.pdf(0), stats.norm(0, width).pdf(0), bf, max(bf, 1 / bf), "for zero" if bf > 1 else "against zero"))
print("two-sided p-value at 3 sigma: 1 in %.0f" % (1 / (2 * stats.norm.sf(3))))

Output

prior width  2: posterior 0.0443, prior 0.1995, ratio 0.22 -> odds 4.5 to 1 against zero
prior width 20: posterior 0.0443, prior 0.0199, ratio 2.22 -> odds 2.2 to 1 for zero
two-sided p-value at 3 sigma: 1 in 370

Worked example: the BIC against the exact evidence

For the 20 data points of the curve-fitting chapter the Bayesian information criterion approximates −ln Z by χ²/2 + (k/2) ln 20, with k the number of coefficients. That gives 21.01 for the line, 17.95 for the quadratic and 19.11 for the cubic.

Result. The criterion prefers the quadratic by 3.06 over the line and by 1.15 over the cubic. The exact evidence gave 2.17 and 1.38. The ranking is the same and the margins differ by up to 40 per cent, because the criterion ignores the prior width and is derived for much more than 20 points. It is a useful first look, not a substitute for the evidence.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
x = np.linspace(-1, 1, 20)
y = np.array([-0.0578, -0.4708, 0.2324, 0.0531, 0.1977, 0.7224, 0.7013, 0.6845, 1.3723, 0.2087,
              0.9230, 0.9663, 1.6518, 1.0614, 1.3489, 1.0267, 1.7997, 1.3545, 0.4366, 1.2630])
sigma, tau = 0.3, 2.0            # noise per point, prior width of each coefficient
bic, lnZ = {}, {}
for d, name in ((2, "line"), (3, "quadratic"), (4, "cubic")):
    X = np.vander(x, d, increasing=True)
    chi2 = ((y - X @ np.linalg.lstsq(X, y, rcond=None)[0])**2).sum() / sigma**2
    bic[name] = chi2 / 2 + d / 2 * np.log(len(y))
    K = sigma**2 * np.eye(len(y)) + tau**2 * X @ X.T         # covariance of the data under the model
    lnZ[name] = -0.5 * (y @ np.linalg.solve(K, y) + np.linalg.slogdet(K)[1] + len(y) * np.log(2 * np.pi))
    print("%-9s BIC/2 = %.2f   -ln Z = %.2f" % (name, bic[name], -lnZ[name]))
print("quadratic preferred over line, cubic:  BIC %.2f, %.2f   exact %.2f, %.2f" % (bic["line"] - bic["quadratic"], bic["cubic"] - bic["quadratic"], lnZ["quadratic"] - lnZ["line"], lnZ["quadratic"] - lnZ["cubic"]))

Output

line      BIC/2 = 21.01   -ln Z = 18.75
quadratic BIC/2 = 17.95   -ln Z = 16.57
cubic     BIC/2 = 19.11   -ln Z = 17.95
quadratic preferred over line, cubic:  BIC 3.06, 1.15   exact 2.17, 1.38

Questions people ask

Why does the prior matter so much here, when it barely affects parameter estimates? Because the evidence is an average of the likelihood over the prior. Doubling the prior width of a parameter the data pin down well leaves the posterior unchanged and halves the evidence. A model is penalised for every bit of prior range the data ruled out.

What is Lindley's paradox? A result can be significant by a classical test while the Bayes factor favours the simpler model. It happens when the prior on the extra parameter is wide: the simpler model predicted the data poorly, and the wider model predicted them even more diffusely.

Is the Bayesian information criterion good enough? As a rough guide, with plenty of data and a posterior that is a single peak. It ignores the prior completely and fails for models with flat directions, a point the phase-transitions chapter returns to.

How is evidence computed in SBI, with no likelihood? In three ways. Train a classifier to tell simulations of one model from those of the other, and its output gives the Bayes factor. Learn the likelihood and integrate it by nested sampling. Or embed the models in a larger one and read the comparison from the posterior of the extra parameter.

constructionThe Occam factor is a fluctuation determinant

Apply the saddle-point formula of the free-energy chapter to the evidence of a model with a flat prior of width Δθ. The integral over the likelihood gives its peak value times the width σpost of the region the data allow:

Z ≈ Lmax × ( σpost √2π / Δθ ) best fit × fraction of prior volume that survives

The second factor is the Occam factor. It is the one-loop fluctuation determinant divided by the prior volume: the ratio of the phase space the system actually explores to the phase space it was offered. Its logarithm is an entropy difference, and it is always negative.

So the free energy of a model has the thermodynamic form F = E − TS: an energy term, the misfit at the best fit, and an entropy term that counts how finely the parameters had to be tuned. Model selection is the competition between them. With few data the entropy term dominates and the simple model wins. With many data the energy term, which grows with the number of data, overtakes the entropy term, which grows only logarithmically. The preferred model then changes abruptly at a definite amount of data, in the way a system changes phase when the free energies of two phases cross.

Check against the worked example: with τ = 2 and s = 0.5 the posterior width is 0.485, and ln(σpost/τ) = ln(0.485/2) = −1.42, which is the Occam penalty ½ ln 17 found there.

← 27. Nuisance parameters29. Computing the evidence →

Chapter 29 of 39

Computing the evidence: thermodynamic integration and information criteria

The model comparison chapter said what the evidence means. This one is about getting its value, by the method chemists use to measure a free energy, and about the shortcuts that avoid computing it at all.

physics

The connection. The evidence is a free energy, and it is computed as chemists compute one: integrate the mean energy from infinite temperature down to the working temperature.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Power β applied to the likelihoodInverse temperature
∂ ln Z/∂β = ⟨ln L⟩β∂ ln Z/∂β = −⟨E⟩β
Thermodynamic integration; power posteriorsKirkwood's coupling-parameter method
Effective number of parameters, 2 Var(ln L)Heat capacity
BICLarge-N expansion of the free energy

Background: a free energy cannot be measured directly

A free energy is not the average of anything, so it cannot be obtained by sampling a system and averaging. In 1935 Kirkwood showed a way round. The derivative of a free energy with respect to a parameter is an average, so the free-energy difference between two systems can be found by changing one gradually into the other and integrating the average along the way. Chemists have used this thermodynamic integration ever since, for example to compute how strongly a drug binds to a protein.

Statistics imported it twice: Gelman and Meng in 1998 under the name path sampling, and Friel and Pettitt in 2008 as the power posterior. Before that, the difficulty of computing the evidence had led to approximations that avoid the integral. Akaike's criterion of 1973 and Schwarz's of 1978 need only the best fit and a count of parameters. Later criteria by Spiegelhalter and colleagues in 2002 and by Watanabe in 2010 replace the count by an effective number of parameters estimated from the posterior.

Thermodynamic integration

Raise the likelihood to a power β between 0 and 1. At β = 0 the data are ignored and the distribution is the prior. At β = 1 it is the posterior. In between, the data are partly switched on. The power β is an inverse temperature: β = 0 is infinitely hot and β = 1 is the working temperature.

physics

Free energy from the mean energy

Partition function. Z(β) = Σ exp(−βE)

Its derivative. ∂ ln Z / ∂β = −⟨E⟩β
The mean energy at inverse temperature β. Unlike Z, it is an average and can be measured in a simulation.

Integrate. ln Z(β1) − ln Z(β0) = −∫ ⟨E⟩β dβ

Heat capacity. C = ⟨δE²⟩ / kBT²
The variance of the energy.

inference

Evidence from the mean log-likelihood

Tempered evidence. Z(β) = ∫ L(θ)β π(θ) dθ, with Z(0) = 1.

Its derivative. ∂ ln Z / ∂β = ⟨ln L⟩β
The average log-likelihood over the tempered posterior, which a sampler can estimate.

Integrate. ln Z = ∫01 ⟨ln L⟩β dβ

Number of constrained parameters. d = 2 Var(ln L) over the posterior: the heat capacity.

Worked example: thermodynamic integration done exactly

One parameter has a Gaussian prior of variance 4. One measurement with noise variance 1 gives 1.0. The evidence is known in closed form: the data are Gaussian with variance 4 + 1, so ln Z = ln N(1; 0, 5) = −1.824. Now do it by integration. At power β the tempered posterior is Gaussian with precision β + ¼, and the average log-likelihood can be written down.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats, integrate
tau2, s2, d = 4.0, 1.0, 1.0                      # prior variance, noise variance, the measurement
def mean_lnL(beta):                              # average log-likelihood over the tempered posterior
    prec = beta / s2 + 1 / tau2
    mu, var = (beta * d / s2) / prec, 1 / prec
    return -0.5 * np.log(2 * np.pi * s2) - 0.5 * ((d - mu)**2 + var) / s2
print("<ln L> at beta = 0, 0.1, 0.5, 1:", ", ".join("%.2f" % mean_lnL(b) for b in (0, 0.1, 0.5, 1)))
print("integral %.3f   direct ln Z %.3f" % (integrate.quad(mean_lnL, 0, 1)[0], stats.norm(0, np.sqrt(tau2 + s2)).logpdf(d)))

Output

<ln L> at beta = 0, 0.1, 0.5, 1: -3.42, -2.60, -1.64, -1.34
integral -1.824   direct ln Z -1.824
Power β00.10.51
⟨ln L⟩−3.42−2.60−1.64−1.34
Result. Integrating ⟨ln L⟩ from 0 to 1 gives −1.824, in agreement with the direct answer. The integrand changes fastest near β = 0, where the data first begin to act, so the temperatures used in practice are spaced closely there. This is why tempered samplers use a ladder that is dense at the hot end.

Worked example: how many parameters did the data constrain?

For the same problem, compute the variance of the log-likelihood over the posterior. The posterior has variance 0.8 and mean 0.8, and the result is Var(ln L) = 0.352.

Result. d = 2 × 0.352 = 0.70. The data constrained 0.7 of a parameter, not 1, because the prior was already supplying a fifth of the information. With a very wide prior the answer tends to exactly 1. In physics each fully excited quadratic degree of freedom contributes ½ to the heat capacity, and a partly frozen one contributes less. This count, introduced by Handley and Lemos in 2019, is the heat capacity of the posterior.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
tau2, s2, d = 4.0, 1.0, 1.0
var = 1 / (1 / s2 + 1 / tau2); mu = d / s2 * var
theta = rng.normal(mu, np.sqrt(var), 2_000_000)  # posterior samples
lnL = -0.5 * (d - theta)**2 / s2
print("posterior mean %.1f, variance %.1f, Var(ln L) = %.3f, d = %.2f" % (mu, var, lnL.var(), 2 * lnL.var()))

Output

posterior mean 0.8, variance 0.8, Var(ln L) = 0.352, d = 0.70

Shortcuts: information criteria

When the evidence is too expensive, a criterion built from the best fit is used instead. All have the form misfit plus penalty, and the model with the lowest value is preferred.

CriterionFormulaWhat the penalty represents
AIC (Akaike)χ² + 2kExpected improvement in fit from k parameters fitting the noise
BIC (Schwarz)χ² + k ln nLarge-n expansion of −2 ln Z: the Occam factor
DIC, WAICmisfit + 2deffAs AIC, with the number of parameters the data actually constrain
Cross-validationerror on held-out dataDirect measurement of predictive accuracy

The first and the second answer different questions. AIC and cross-validation ask which model will predict new data best. BIC and the evidence ask which model is more probable. With little data they often agree, and with much data BIC penalises extra parameters more heavily.

Worked example: four criteria on one data set

Apply the criteria to the three fits of the curve-fitting chapter, 20 points with a line, a quadratic and a cubic. Leave-one-out cross-validation refits with each point removed in turn and measures the error in predicting it.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
x = np.linspace(-1, 1, 20)
y = np.array([-0.0578, -0.4708, 0.2324, 0.0531, 0.1977, 0.7224, 0.7013, 0.6845, 1.3723, 0.2087,
              0.9230, 0.9663, 1.6518, 1.0614, 1.3489, 1.0267, 1.7997, 1.3545, 0.4366, 1.2630])
sigma, tau = 0.3, 2.0            # noise per point, prior width of each coefficient
n = len(y)
for d, name in ((2, "line"), (3, "quadratic"), (4, "cubic")):
    X = np.vander(x, d, increasing=True)
    H = X @ np.linalg.solve(X.T @ X, X.T)        # hat matrix of the least-squares fit
    r = y - H @ y
    chi2 = (r**2).sum() / sigma**2
    K = sigma**2 * np.eye(n) + tau**2 * X @ X.T
    lnZ = -0.5 * (y @ np.linalg.solve(K, y) + np.linalg.slogdet(K)[1] + n * np.log(2 * np.pi))
    loo = r / (1 - np.diag(H))                   # leave-one-out prediction errors
    print("%-9s chi2 %.1f  AIC %.1f  BIC %.1f  -2lnZ %.1f  cross-val %.3f  in-sample %.3f" % (name, chi2, chi2 + 2*d, chi2 + d*np.log(n), -2*lnZ, np.sqrt((loo**2).mean()), np.sqrt((r**2).mean())))
aic = np.array([40.0, 32.9, 34.2]); w = np.exp(-0.5 * (aic - aic.min()))
print("AIC weights (line, quadratic, cubic):", (w / w.sum()).round(2))

Output

line      chi2 36.0  AIC 40.0  BIC 42.0  -2lnZ 37.5  cross-val 0.453  in-sample 0.403
quadratic chi2 26.9  AIC 32.9  BIC 35.9  -2lnZ 33.1  cross-val 0.419  in-sample 0.348
cubic     chi2 26.2  AIC 34.2  BIC 38.2  -2lnZ 35.9  cross-val 0.439  in-sample 0.344
AIC weights (line, quadratic, cubic): [0.02 0.64 0.34]
Modelχ²AICBIC−2 ln Z (exact)Cross-validation errorIn-sample error
Line36.040.042.037.50.4530.403
Quadratic26.932.935.933.10.4190.348
Cubic26.234.238.235.90.4390.344
Result. Every criterion picks the quadratic, which is the model that generated the data. Only the in-sample error prefers the cubic, and it always prefers the model with more parameters. The margins differ: over the cubic, AIC prefers the quadratic by 1.3, BIC by 2.3 and the exact evidence by 2.8. Turned into weights, AIC gives the cubic a 34 per cent share against 64 per cent for the quadratic, where the evidence gave 17 against 69.

Questions people ask

If nested sampling gives the evidence, why use thermodynamic integration? Nested sampling is usually the better tool for a moderate number of parameters. Thermodynamic integration comes almost free when a tempered sampler is already being run to cope with several modes, and it extends more easily to very many parameters.

Which criterion should I report? The evidence, if it can be computed and the priors are defensible. Otherwise say which question is being asked. For prediction, use cross-validation or WAIC. For choosing between physical hypotheses, use the evidence or, as an approximation, BIC.

What is an effective number of parameters? The number of directions in parameter space that the data, and not the prior, determine. A parameter the data cannot constrain counts as zero and one fully fixed by the data counts as one. It is the same count as the heat capacity of a system some of whose modes are frozen.

How is the evidence obtained in SBI? With a learned likelihood, by either method in this chapter. Without one, by training a classifier to tell simulations of one model from those of another, which gives the ratio of evidences directly.

What to remember

  • The evidence is a free energy, and it can be computed in the way chemists compute one: integrate the mean log-likelihood from the prior to the posterior.
  • The power applied to the likelihood is an inverse temperature. The prior is the infinitely hot state.
  • Twice the variance of the log-likelihood over the posterior counts the parameters the data constrained. It is the heat capacity.
  • Information criteria are cheap stand-ins. AIC and cross-validation target prediction, and BIC targets the evidence.
← 28. Model comparison30. Three ensembles →

Chapter 30 of 39

Three ensembles, three kinds of inference

Statistical mechanics has three standard ways of setting up a problem, which differ in what is held fixed. Each one is a method of inference you have already met.

physics

The connection. The three ensembles of statistical mechanics are three kinds of inference.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Rejection ABC; nested samplingMicrocanonical ensemble
The ordinary posterior for one modelCanonical ensemble
Unknown number of parametersGrand canonical ensemble
Prior odds for an extra parameterChemical potential

Background: Gibbs and the idea of an ensemble

Gibbs introduced ensembles in his 1902 book. An ensemble is an imagined collection of copies of a system, each in a different microscopic state, all consistent with what is known macroscopically. He defined three, according to what is held fixed, and showed that for large systems they give the same thermodynamics. That equivalence is what lets physicists choose whichever is easiest to calculate with.

The choice is not only a matter of convenience. For small systems, and for systems with long-range forces such as self-gravitating gas, the ensembles are not equivalent, and the microcanonical one can show behaviour, such as negative heat capacity, that the canonical one cannot. Which ensemble applies depends on how the system is actually coupled to its surroundings.

Statistics met the third ensemble in 1995, when Green introduced reversible-jump MCMC, a sampler that moves between models with different numbers of parameters. It was the first general method for problems in which the number of components is itself unknown, such as the number of sources in an image or of change points in a record.

What an ensemble is

An ensemble is a rule for which states of a system are allowed and with what weight. The three standard ones differ in what is fixed exactly and what is left free to fluctuate.

EnsembleFixed exactlyFree to fluctuateCentral quantity
MicrocanonicalEnergy E, particle number NNothingS = kB ln Ω(E)
CanonicalTemperature T, particle number NEnergyZ = Σ exp(−E/kBT)
Grand canonicalTemperature T, chemical potential μEnergy and particle numberΞ = ΣN exp(μN/kBT) ZN

Ω(E): the number of states with energy E. S: entropy. Z: partition function, a sum over all states. Ξ: grand partition function, a sum over all particle numbers. μ: the energy gained or paid for adding one particle.

The running example is the data set of the curve-fitting chapter: 20 points fitted with polynomials.

Microcanonical: a hard constraint

In the microcanonical ensemble only states on one energy shell are allowed, and all of them count equally. The statistical version is a hard constraint: keep the parameter values whose predictions match the data within a tolerance, and give each of them equal weight.

That is rejection ABC. The tolerance ε is the thickness of the shell. The fraction of prior draws that land in the shell is the number of allowed states, and its logarithm is an entropy. For the curve fit at ε = 1 the kept fractions were 1 in 1,300 for the line and 1 in 400,000 for the cubic, so the entropy of the shell falls by about 3 for each added parameter.

Nested sampling is microcanonical in a second way. It works through shells of constant likelihood, from the outside in, and adds up their volumes: the evidence is ∫ L dX, with X the prior volume enclosed by the shell of likelihood L. In physics this is computing the partition function from the density of states, Z = ∫ Ω(E) exp(−E/kBT) dE.

Canonical: the ordinary posterior

In the canonical ensemble every state is allowed, weighted by exp(−E/kBT). The energy can fluctuate, and the temperature says by how much. This is the ordinary posterior: every parameter value is allowed, weighted by likelihood times prior. The model, and so the number of parameters, is fixed. The noise level plays the temperature.

MCMC, Hamiltonian Monte Carlo and neural posterior estimation all sample this ensemble. Hamiltonian Monte Carlo uses the first ensemble inside the second: each trajectory conserves its energy, a microcanonical move, and the fresh random momentum drawn between trajectories is contact with the heat bath.

Grand canonical: how many parameters?

In the grand canonical ensemble the number of particles is not fixed either. A particle is added when the energy it gains exceeds a price, the chemical potential. The statistical version is a problem where the number of components is itself unknown: how many sources are in the image, how many terms the polynomial needs.

physics

A gas in contact with a reservoir of particles

Probability of N particles. P(N) = exp(μN/kBT) ZN / Ξ

ZN: the partition function with exactly N particles. μ: chemical potential. Ξ: the sum of the numerators over all N.

Average number. ⟨N⟩ = kBT ∂ln Ξ / ∂μ

inference

A model with k parameters, k unknown

Probability of k parameters. P(k | data) = π(k) Zk / Σj π(j) Zj

Zk: the evidence of the model with k parameters. π(k): the prior probability of that model. Writing π(k) ∝ exp(μk) makes μ the log of the prior odds for each extra parameter.

Average number. ⟨k⟩ = Σ k P(k | data)

Worked example: how many coefficients does the curve need?

The curve-fitting chapter gave the evidence for five polynomial models of the same 20 points: ln Z = −37.37 (constant), −18.75 (line), −16.57 (quadratic), −17.95 (cubic) and −19.01 (quartic). With no prior preference between them, μ = 0, the probability of each is its Z divided by the sum.

constant 0.0%    line 7.8%    quadratic 68.9%    cubic 17.3%    quartic 6.0%
Result. The average number of coefficients is 3.22. The data were made from a quadratic, which has 3. The answer is not a single model but a distribution over models, as the particle number of an open system is a distribution.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
x = np.linspace(-1, 1, 20)
y = np.array([-0.0578, -0.4708, 0.2324, 0.0531, 0.1977, 0.7224, 0.7013, 0.6845, 1.3723, 0.2087,
              0.9230, 0.9663, 1.6518, 1.0614, 1.3489, 1.0267, 1.7997, 1.3545, 0.4366, 1.2630])
sigma, tau = 0.3, 2.0            # noise per point, prior width of each coefficient
n, lnZ = len(y), []
for d in (1, 2, 3, 4, 5):
    X = np.vander(x, d, increasing=True)
    K = sigma**2 * np.eye(n) + tau**2 * X @ X.T
    lnZ.append(-0.5 * (y @ np.linalg.solve(K, y) + np.linalg.slogdet(K)[1] + n * np.log(2 * np.pi)))
lnZ = np.array(lnZ)
p = np.exp(lnZ - lnZ.max()); p /= p.sum()
print("ln Z:", lnZ.round(2))
print("probabilities (%):", (100 * p).round(1), " mean number of coefficients %.2f" % (p @ np.arange(1, 6)))

Output

ln Z: [-37.37 -18.75 -16.57 -17.95 -19.01]
probabilities (%): [ 0.   7.8 68.8 17.4  6. ]  mean number of coefficients 3.22

The experiment below lets you change the price of a parameter.

–prior odds for each extra coefficient
–average number of coefficients
–most probable model

A negative μ makes parameters expensive and moves the weight towards the line. A positive μ subsidises them and moves it towards the quartic. The quadratic stays the most probable model over a wide range, from prior odds of about 9 to 1 against each extra coefficient to about 3 to 1 for. Outside that range the prior outvotes the data. Samplers that jump between models of different size, such as reversible-jump MCMC, are sampling this ensemble.

Equivalence of ensembles

For a large system the three ensembles give the same answers. The energy of a canonical system fluctuates, but only by a fraction of order 1/√N, so for 10²³ particles it may as well be fixed. Statistics has the same result with the amount of data in place of N.

  • Micro and canonical. ABC with a thin shell reproduces the posterior. For the line fit, the ABC error bars were 3 to 6 per cent too wide at ε = 0.5 and 30 to 40 per cent at ε = 2. The thinner the shell, the closer the two ensembles.
  • Canonical and grand canonical. With enough data one model takes all the probability, and averaging over models gives the same answer as choosing the best one. In the example above 20 points are not enough for that: the quadratic has 69 per cent.

The ensembles differ for small systems and near phase transitions, and the methods of inference differ in the same places: with little data, and where two explanations compete.

Questions people ask

Are these three really different methods, or one method described three ways? They are different calculations that agree in a limit. ABC with a finite tolerance, an exact posterior and a model-averaged posterior give different numbers for a small data set. With much data they converge, as the ensembles of physics do for a large system.

What sets the chemical potential in a real analysis? Your prior on the number of components. A prior that makes each additional source half as likely as the last is a chemical potential of −ln 2 per source. Analyses that fix the number of components have chosen an infinite potential beyond that number, often without saying so.

Is model averaging better than model selection? When the data do not single out one model, yes: predictions made from the best model alone ignore the uncertainty about which model is right. When one model has nearly all the probability the two coincide.

Where is nested sampling in this picture? It is the microcanonical route to a canonical answer. It measures the volume inside each contour of constant likelihood, which is the density of states, and from that the evidence at any temperature follows by a one-dimensional integral.

What to remember

  • Microcanonical means a hard constraint: rejection ABC keeps a shell around the data, and nested sampling counts shells of constant likelihood.
  • Canonical means the ordinary posterior for a fixed model, with the noise level as temperature.
  • Grand canonical means the number of parameters is unknown. The prior odds for an extra parameter are a chemical potential, and the answer is a distribution over models.
  • With much data the three agree. They part company with little data and where explanations compete.
← 29. Computing the evidence31. Phase transitions →

Chapter 31 of 39

Phase transitions in inference

Water does not freeze gradually. Sometimes an inference does not change its mind gradually either. The mathematics is the same, and it lives in the zeros of the partition function.

physics

The connection. A sudden change of conclusion as data accumulate is a phase transition, and it is governed by the zeros of the evidence in the complex plane.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Amount of data nSystem size N
Zeros of the evidenceLee-Yang zeros of the partition function
Two explanations exchanging placesFirst-order transition
Detection thresholdCritical point of a second-order transition

Background: thresholds in inference

In 1952 Lee and Yang explained how a partition function that is perfectly smooth for any finite system can produce the sharp transitions seen in nature. They let the magnetic field take complex values and studied where the partition function vanishes. Fisher did the same for complex temperature in 1965. The picture that resulted, of zeros pinching the real axis as the system grows, is the standard explanation of why phase transitions exist only in the limit of infinite size.

From the 1980s physicists applied the methods of disordered systems to problems of inference and computation. Sharp thresholds were found in the capacity of neural networks, in the satisfiability of random logical formulas, in error-correcting codes and in compressed sensing. In 2005 Baik, Ben Arous and Péché proved the threshold for detecting a weak signal in a large random matrix, the result used in this chapter. A 2016 review by Zdeborová and Krzakala collects many such results under the title "Statistical physics of inference".

The practical message is that the difficulty of an inference problem does not always change gradually with the quality of the data. There can be a signal-to-noise ratio below which no method recovers anything, and another below which the answer exists but no efficient algorithm finds it.

Where sharp changes come from

A partition function Z is a sum of positive terms, each a smooth function of temperature or field. A finite sum of smooth positive terms is smooth and never zero. So for any finite system nothing sudden can happen, and the free energy −ln Z has no kinks.

Lee and Yang found where the sharpness is hiding. Let the temperature or the field be a complex number. Then Z can vanish, and at a zero of Z the free energy −ln Z is singular. For a finite system the zeros sit away from the real axis, where they do no harm. As the system grows they move closer. If they reach the real axis in the limit of infinite size, the free energy has a kink at that point. That is a phase transition.

In inference the evidence is the partition function (the dictionary), and the size of the system is the amount of data. So the same question can be asked: as the data grow, does the answer change smoothly or suddenly? The simplest case can be done in full.

A simple example: is the coin fair?

A coin is tossed n times and a fraction h of the tosses are heads. There are two hypotheses, equally likely beforehand. Either the coin is fair, with probability of heads 0.5, or it is biased, with probability of heads 0.7.

Worked example: the posterior probability that the coin is biased

The probability of the observed sequence under each hypothesis is

fair:  0.5n      biased:  0.7nh · 0.3n(1−h)

The evidence is their average. Dividing the biased term by the fair term and taking the logarithm gives a quantity that is proportional to n:

Z = ½ · 0.5n · [ 1 + e n c (h − hc) ] c = ln(7/3) = 0.847,   hc = ln(5/3) / ln(7/3) = 0.603

The posterior probability of the biased coin is the second term over the sum:

P(biased) = 1 / [ 1 + e −n c (h − hc) ]
Result. The answer depends on the data through one combination, n(h − hc). Below hc = 0.603 the fair coin wins, above it the biased coin wins, and the changeover gets sharper in proportion to n.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
c = np.log(7 / 3); hc = np.log(5 / 3) / c
print("c = %.3f, h_c = %.3f" % (c, hc))
for n in (10, 100, 1000, 10000):
    P = lambda h: 1 / (1 + np.exp(-n * c * (h - hc)))
    print("n = %5d: P(biased) at h = 0.58, 0.60, 0.62: %.3f %.3f %.3f   width %.5f" % (n, P(0.58), P(0.60), P(0.62), 2 * np.log(9) / (c * n)))

Output

c = 0.847, h_c = 0.603
n =    10: P(biased) at h = 0.58, 0.60, 0.62: 0.452 0.494 0.536   width 0.51864
n =   100: P(biased) at h = 0.58, 0.60, 0.62: 0.126 0.439 0.810   width 0.05186
n =  1000: P(biased) at h = 0.58, 0.60, 0.62: 0.000 0.080 1.000   width 0.00519
n = 10000: P(biased) at h = 0.58, 0.60, 0.62: 0.000 0.000 1.000   width 0.00052
Tosses nP(biased) at h = 0.58at h = 0.60at h = 0.62Width of the changeover in h
1045%49%54%0.52
10013%44%81%0.052
1,0000.0%8%100.0%0.0052
10,0000%0%100%0.00052

The width is the range of h over which P(biased) goes from 10 to 90 per cent.

With ten tosses the data barely move the odds. With ten thousand, a change of one part in a thousand in the fraction of heads flips the conclusion from certain to certain. In the limit the curve is a step.

The same sum in physics

physics

A magnet of N locked spins in a field

Partition function. Z = eNμB/kBT + e−NμB/kBT

Below its critical temperature a magnet's spins point together, all up or all down: two states. N: number of spins. μ: magnetic moment of one spin. B: applied field. kBT: temperature in energy units.

Magnetisation. m = tanh( NμB / kBT )
For large N this is a step at B = 0: the magnet flips.

Zeros. Z = 0 when B = i π (k + ½) kBT / Nμ for whole numbers k. They lie on the imaginary axis, spaced by an amount proportional to 1/N, and close in on B = 0.

inference

Two hypotheses and n tosses

Evidence. Z ∝ 1 + e n c (h − hc)

Two hypotheses in place of two magnetic states. n: number of tosses, in the role of N. h − hc: how far the data are from the tie point, in the role of the field B.

Posterior. 2 P(biased) − 1 = tanh( n c (h − hc) / 2 )
The same tanh. For large n it is a step at h = hc: the conclusion flips.

Zeros. Z = 0 when h = hc + i π (2k + 1) / (c n). They lie on a vertical line through hc, spaced by an amount proportional to 1/n, and close in on the real axis at hc.

A fraction of heads cannot be a complex number, and a magnetic field cannot either. The zeros are not places the system visits. They are a way of seeing, from a distance, how sharp the real behaviour is: the transition is as wide as the nearest zero is far.

See it

Move the number of tosses. On the left is the posterior probability of the biased coin against the fraction of heads. On the right are the zeros of the evidence in the complex plane of h.

Posterior probability that the coin is biased
Zeros of the evidence in the complex plane of h
Posterior at this nLimit of infinite data (dashed)Zeros of the evidence
–tosses
–distance of the nearest zero from the real axis
–width of the changeover in h
–P(biased) at h = 0.62

The two numbers in the middle of the bottom row stay in a fixed ratio. As the zeros pinch the real axis the curve becomes a step.

Two kinds of transition

The coin is a first-order transition: two distinct explanations, each good in its own region, exchanging places. Ice and water do this at 0 °C. In a posterior with continuous parameters it appears as two separate peaks whose weights cross. Just at the crossing both peaks matter, and a sampler that sits in one of them gives the wrong answer with no warning. This is why nested sampling and tempered samplers exist, and why they slow down at exactly these points.

The other kind is second-order, or continuous. Nothing jumps. Instead a quantity that was exactly zero starts to grow from a threshold, as the magnetisation of iron does when it is cooled through the Curie temperature. In inference the growing quantity is how much the data tell you about a hidden signal.

Worked example: a hidden direction in noise

A large symmetric table of noisy numbers hides one preferred direction v with strength λ. In symbols the data are λ v vᵀ + noise, with the noise scaled so that its eigenvalues fill the range −2 to 2. The natural estimate of v is the eigenvector with the largest eigenvalue. Random matrix theory gives the result in the limit of a large table:

largest eigenvalue = λ + 1/λ,     overlap² with the true direction = 1 − 1/λ²     for λ > 1

For λ below 1 the largest eigenvalue stays at 2, the edge of the noise, and the overlap is zero.

Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
n = 1500
for lam in (0.5, 1.5, 3.0):
    W = rng.standard_normal((n, n)); W = (W + W.T) / np.sqrt(2 * n)       # noise with eigenvalues in [-2, 2]
    v = rng.standard_normal(n); v /= np.linalg.norm(v)
    vals, vecs = np.linalg.eigh(W + lam * np.outer(v, v))
    theory = (lam + 1 / lam, 1 - 1 / lam**2) if lam > 1 else (2.0, 0.0)
    print("strength %.1f: top eigenvalue %.2f (theory %.2f), overlap^2 %.2f (theory %.2f)" % (lam, vals[-1], theory[0], (vecs[:, -1] @ v)**2, theory[1]))

Output

strength 0.5: top eigenvalue 1.98 (theory 2.00), overlap^2 0.00 (theory 0.00)
strength 1.5: top eigenvalue 2.18 (theory 2.17), overlap^2 0.58 (theory 0.56)
strength 3.0: top eigenvalue 3.31 (theory 3.33), overlap^2 0.89 (theory 0.89)
Signal strength λLargest eigenvalueOverlap² with the truthWhat you can learn
0.52.000Nothing. The signal is there but invisible.
1.02.000The threshold.
1.22.030.31A first, poor estimate.
1.52.170.56
2.02.500.75
3.03.330.89A good estimate.

A simulation with a table of 1,500 by 1,500 gives 1.98 and 0.00 at λ = 0.5, 2.18 and 0.58 at λ = 1.5, and 3.31 and 0.89 at λ = 3.

Below the threshold no amount of care with this estimator recovers anything: the signal's eigenvalue is buried inside the noise. Above it the overlap grows from zero, as a magnetisation does. This is the usual shape of a detection limit: not a gradual loss of accuracy but a point below which the signal cannot be found at all.

Poles, and models with flat directions

There is a second place where singularities in a complex plane control an inference, and here they are poles and not zeros. It concerns the Occam penalty of the model comparison chapter.

For a model whose posterior is a single clean peak, the evidence for large n behaves as −ln Z ≈ (best misfit) + (d/2) ln n, with d the number of parameters. Each parameter costs ½ ln n. Many models are not like this. A neural network, or a sum of two sources where one may have zero amplitude, has directions in which the parameters can change without changing the prediction. Watanabe showed how to count the cost in that case. Define a function of a complex number z,

ζ(z) = ∫ K(θ)z π(θ) dθ K(θ): the average misfit per data point relative to the truth, zero at the best fit. π(θ): the prior.

This function has poles on the negative real axis. If the pole nearest zero is at z = −λ, and it is a pole of order m, then

−ln Z ≈ n · (best misfit) + λ ln n − (m − 1) ln ln n

Worked example: two parameters, two models

Take two parameters a and b, each with a flat prior between −1 and 1.

Regular model: K = a² + b². The misfit is zero at one point. For one parameter, ζ(z) = ∫ a2z da / 2 = 1/(2z + 1), with a pole at z = −½. Two such parameters give λ = 1, which is d/2. This is the usual rule.

Singular model: K = a²b². The misfit is zero whenever either parameter is zero: a cross, not a point. Think of a as an amplitude and b as a shape parameter that stops mattering when the amplitude vanishes. The integral factorises,

ζ(z) = [ 1 / (2z + 1) ]²

so there is a double pole at z = −½: λ = ½ and m = 2.

Result. The singular model has two parameters but pays the Occam cost of fewer than one: ½ ln n − ln ln n in place of ln n.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import integrate, special
for n in (1e2, 1e4, 1e6):
    regular = (np.sqrt(np.pi / n) * special.erf(np.sqrt(n)) / 2)**2                 # K = a^2 + b^2
    inner = lambda a: np.sqrt(np.pi / (n * a * a)) * special.erf(np.sqrt(n) * a) / 2 if a > 0 else 1.0
    singular = integrate.quad(inner, 0, 1, limit=400, points=[1e-6, 1e-4, 1e-3, 1e-2, 1e-1])[0]   # K = a^2 b^2
    print("n = %7d: regular %.2f (ln n = %.2f)   singular %.2f (rule %.2f)" % (n, -np.log(regular), np.log(n), -np.log(singular), 0.5 * np.log(n) - np.log(np.log(n))))

Output

n =     100: regular 4.85 (ln n = 4.61)   singular 1.23 (rule 0.78)
n =   10000: regular 9.45 (ln n = 9.21)   singular 3.01 (rule 2.38)
n = 1000000: regular 14.06 (ln n = 13.82)   singular 4.96 (rule 4.28)

The evidence integrals can be done numerically to check.

Data size nRegular: −ln Zrule: ln nSingular: −ln Zrule: ½ ln n − ln ln n
1004.854.611.230.78
10,0009.459.213.012.38
1,000,00014.0613.824.964.28

The rules leave out a constant, so the columns to compare are the increases from row to row: 4.6 per row for the regular model, about 1.9 for the singular one, in both the integral and the rule.

In physics the number λ would be called a critical exponent: it says how the free energy scales with system size at a singular point, and like a critical exponent it depends on the geometry of the singularity and not on the details. The practical consequence is that counting parameters overstates the Occam penalty for models with flat directions, which is one reason very large neural networks are not punished by the evidence as a naive count would suggest.

Questions people ask

A real data set is finite. Do these transitions matter? They show up as steep but smooth changes. A posterior probability that goes from 5 to 95 per cent over a narrow range of signal strength is a transition rounded by finite size. Knowing where the underlying threshold is tells you where to expect unstable results.

How would I recognise a first-order transition in my own analysis? The posterior has two separated peaks, and which one dominates changes with a small change in the data, the prior or the model. Samplers started in different places disagree, and nested sampling shows a sudden change in the rate at which the evidence accumulates.

What is the difference between hard and impossible? Below the information threshold the data do not contain the answer. Between that and a second, algorithmic threshold, the answer is in the data but known efficient methods fail to find it, in the way a glass fails to find its crystalline ground state.

Why should the Occam penalty depend on singularities? Because the penalty is the log of the volume of parameter space that fits about as well as the best fit. Near a regular minimum that volume shrinks as a simple power of the amount of data. Where the set of best fits is a line or a cross, it shrinks more slowly, and the power is what the zeta function's pole measures.

What to remember

  • The evidence is a partition function, and the amount of data plays the size of the system. Sharp behaviour appears only in the limit of much data, and is announced by zeros of the evidence closing in on the real axis.
  • First-order: two explanations swap places and the conclusion jumps. Expect samplers to struggle exactly there.
  • Second-order: below a threshold in signal strength the data carry no usable information about the signal; above it the information grows from zero.
  • For models with flat directions the Occam penalty is set by a pole of a zeta function and is smaller than half a log per parameter.
← 30. Three ensembles32. Symmetry and degeneracy →

Chapter 32 of 39

Symmetry and degeneracy: what the data cannot tell you

Sometimes two different sets of parameters predict exactly the same data. No amount of measurement can then choose between them. Recognising this in advance, and deciding what to do about it, is the statistical version of a gauge symmetry.

physics

The connection. A change of parameters that leaves the predictions unchanged is a symmetry, and field theory has a complete toolkit for it.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Exact degeneracySymmetry, or gauge freedom
Flat direction of the likelihoodZero mode; Goldstone mode
Fixing a convention to remove itGauge fixing, with its Jacobian (Faddeev-Popov)
Nearly flat directionSoft mode with large fluctuations

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: what cannot be measured

Some combinations of parameters leave every prediction of a model unchanged. Statisticians call such a model non-identifiable, a notion formalised in econometrics in the mid-twentieth century. The familiar example is a mixture of two components, which gives the same predictions when the labels of the components are swapped.

Physics has more experience of this than any other field, because its fundamental theories are built on such redundancies. The potentials of electromagnetism can be changed in a certain way without changing any field or force. That freedom is called gauge freedom, and handling it correctly in a path integral requires fixing a convention and including the Jacobian of that choice, a procedure found by Faddeev and Popov in 1967. When a symmetry of the energy is not shared by the ground state, Goldstone's theorem of 1961 says that fluctuations along the symmetric direction cost almost no energy.

A related idea came from systems biology in the 2000s. Sethna and colleagues found that models with many parameters typically have a few combinations that the data determine well and many that they hardly constrain at all, with stiffnesses spread evenly over many orders of magnitude. They called such models sloppy. The pattern is now recognised in fields from particle physics to climate modelling.

The simplest case

Suppose a signal passes through two amplifiers in a row, with gains a and b, and you measure the output y = a b + noise. Doubling a and halving b changes nothing you can observe. The data determine the product g = a b and say nothing at all about the ratio a/b.

On a plot of b against a, the parameter values that fit the data lie along a curve, the hyperbola a b = y. The posterior is a ridge along that curve: narrow across it, and as long as the prior allows along it.

Worked example: the Fisher matrix knows

For y = a b with noise s, the derivatives of the prediction are ∂y/∂a = b and ∂y/∂b = a, so the Fisher matrix is

F = (1/s²) [ b²   ab ;   ab   a² ]

Its determinant is a²b² − (ab)² = 0. At a = 2, b = 0.5, s = 0.1 the two eigenvalues and their directions are:

EigenvalueDirection in (a, b)Meaning
425(0.5, 2), i.e. (b, a)Changes the product. Measured to 1/√425 = 0.05.
0(2, −0.5), i.e. (a, −b)Raises a and lowers b in proportion. Product unchanged. Not measured at all.
Result. A zero eigenvalue of the Fisher matrix marks a direction the data are blind to. A Fisher forecast then predicts an infinite error bar along it, and a matrix inversion fails. Finding the zero and near-zero eigenvalues before running anything is the cheapest diagnostic there is.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
a, b, s = 2.0, 0.5, 0.1
grad = np.array([b, a])                          # derivatives of y = a b with respect to (a, b)
F = np.outer(grad, grad) / s**2
lam, vec = np.linalg.eigh(F)
print("determinant %.1f, eigenvalues %.0f and %.0f" % (np.linalg.det(F), lam[1], abs(lam[0])))
print("measured direction", (vec[:, 1] / vec[0, 1] * b).round(2), " blind direction", (vec[:, 0] / vec[0, 0] * a).round(2), " error along the first: %.2f" % lam[1]**-0.5)

Output

determinant 0.0, eigenvalues 425 and 0
measured direction [0.5 2. ]  blind direction [ 2.  -0.5]  error along the first: 0.05

Three things you can do

OptionHowCost
Fix the freedomSet a = 1 by convention and fit only bNone, provided everyone knows the convention
ReparametriseFit the product g and the ratio r separately. The data constrain g; r is left to the prior.Have to find the right variables
Let the prior decideKeep a and b and give both a priorThe answer along the ridge is the prior and nothing else; samplers struggle with the long curved shape

The parameter count that matters is the number of parameters minus the number of such freedoms. With N antennas each having an unknown complex gain, and measurements only of products of pairs of gains, one overall phase can never be determined, so there are 2N − 1 measurable gain parameters and not 2N. Radio astronomers fix it by declaring one antenna's phase to be zero.

Nearly blind directions

More common than an exact degeneracy is an approximate one: a combination of parameters that the data constrain a thousand times more weakly than the others. The posterior is then a long thin needle. Near-circular orbits give a standard example: the angle to the closest point of the orbit and the position along the orbit can be traded against each other almost perfectly, and only their sum is well measured.

For SBI, both kinds are a warning. A network asked for the posterior of an unmeasurable parameter will return the prior, dressed up as a result. Always compare the posterior with the prior, parameter by parameter: where they coincide, the data said nothing.

More worked examples

Worked example: only the product is measured

A signal is y = a × b × x, for example a source brightness times an instrument gain. Near a = 2, b = 3 the derivatives of the model with respect to (a, b) are proportional to (3, 2). The Fisher matrix is the outer product of that vector with itself: rows (9, 6) and (6, 4).

Result. Its eigenvalues are 13 and 0. The zero belongs to the direction (2, −3): raising a by a fraction while lowering b by the same fraction changes nothing. No amount of data of this kind separates the two. The cure is another kind of data that fixes one of them, such as a calibration source of known brightness, or a report of the product alone. This degeneracy between brightness and gain is the central difficulty of calibrating a radio interferometer.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
a, b = 2.0, 3.0
grad = np.array([b, a])                          # derivatives of y = a b x with respect to (a, b)
F = np.outer(grad, grad)
lam, vec = np.linalg.eigh(F)
print("Fisher matrix", F.tolist(), " eigenvalues", lam.round(6), " blind direction", (vec[:, 0] / vec[0, 0] * a).round(2))

Output

Fisher matrix [[9.0, 6.0], [6.0, 4.0]]  eigenvalues [ 0. 13.]  blind direction [ 2. -3.]

Worked example: which component is which?

Data come from two overlapping populations centred at −1 and +1. A model with two components, labelled 1 and 2, fits equally well with component 1 at −1 and component 2 at +1, or the other way round. The posterior for the centre of component 1 therefore has two equal peaks, at −1 and at +1.

Result. The posterior mean of that centre is 0, a value neither component has, and its standard deviation is about 1, though each centre is known precisely. The labels are a symmetry of the model and carry no meaning. Imposing an order, component 1 on the left, is fixing a gauge. A sampler that visits only one of the two peaks gives the right answer by accident.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
label = rng.integers(0, 2, 1_000_000)            # the sampler lands in either labelling equally often
mu1 = np.where(label == 0, -1.0, 1.0) + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(label.size)
print("posterior for the centre of component 1: mean %.2f, sd %.2f (each peak has sd 0.05)" % (mu1.mean(), mu1.std()))

Output

posterior for the centre of component 1: mean 0.00, sd 1.00 (each peak has sd 0.05)

Questions people ask

Is a degeneracy a fault in the model? Not necessarily. It may be a true statement about what the experiment can measure. The fault is in quoting a value for a quantity the data cannot determine. The honest report is of the combinations that are constrained.

Why do degeneracies cause numerical trouble? A flat direction is a zero eigenvalue of the Fisher matrix, so the matrix cannot be inverted and the Gaussian approximation predicts an infinite error bar. Samplers wander along the flat direction without limit unless a prior stops them, and optimisers stop at an arbitrary point on it.

Does fixing one parameter solve the problem? It removes the flat direction, but the choice of which parameter to fix, and at what value, changes the prior on everything else unless the Jacobian of the choice is included. This is the statistical version of the Faddeev-Popov determinant, and leaving it out biases the result.

How do I find the well-constrained combinations? Compute the eigenvectors of the Fisher matrix. Those with large eigenvalues are the stiff, well-measured combinations. Those with small eigenvalues are the soft ones. Reporting results in that basis separates what was learned from what was assumed.

constructionGauge freedom, soft modes and the measure

A redundancy of description. In electromagnetism the potentials can be changed by a gauge transformation without altering any field or any measurement. The potentials are a convenient description with a built-in redundancy. A parameter degeneracy is the same thing: a transformation of the parameters (a → λa, b → b/λ) that leaves the likelihood invariant. The set of parameter values related by it is a gauge orbit, and only quantities constant along orbits, such as the product, are observable.

Gauge fixing and its Jacobian. Integrating over all parameters counts every point of each orbit, and if the orbit is infinitely long the integral diverges. Field theory handles this by choosing one representative per orbit (fixing a gauge) and dividing out the orbit volume, and the change of variables that separates orbit directions from physical ones brings a Jacobian, the Faddeev–Popov determinant. Here the same step is explicit. Changing from (a, b) to the product g and ratio r,

da db = ( 1 / 2r ) dg dr

so a prior flat in a and b is not flat in the physical variable and the gauge variable: it carries the factor 1/2r. The integral over r is the orbit volume, and it is finite only because the prior cuts it off. The choice of gauge does not change the physics, but forgetting the Jacobian does.

Soft modes. When a continuous symmetry is exact, moving along it costs no energy: a zero mode. When it is slightly broken the mode acquires a small stiffness, as a Goldstone boson acquires a small mass. Approximate degeneracies are these soft modes. They dominate the posterior's shape, they are what make samplers slow (samplers chapter), and the small Fisher eigenvalue is the squared mass.

Building the symmetry into the network. If the physics is unchanged by a transformation of the data, such as a rotation, a translation in time, or a relabelling of identical detectors, the estimator should be too. Equivariant networks enforce this by construction, exactly as a field theory is built from invariants so that its predictions respect the symmetry. The gain is in data: the network does not have to learn the symmetry from examples, and it cannot violate it.

← 31. Phase transitions33. Hierarchical models →

Chapter 33 of 39

Hierarchical models: many objects, one population

A hundred galaxies, a thousand patients, eight detectors. Each has its own parameter, and all are drawn from a common population whose properties you also want. The objects then inform one another, by a mechanism physicists know as mean field.

physics

The connection. In a hierarchical model each object feels the average of all the others through the population. That is a mean field.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Population parametersCouplings shared by every site
Each object's own parameterA local degree of freedom
Shrinkage towards the population meanEach spin aligning with the mean field
Averaging over objects before or after taking logsAnnealed and quenched averages

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: borrowing strength

In 1956 Stein proved a result that statisticians at first found hard to accept. To estimate three or more unrelated averages, it is better, in total squared error, to pull every estimate towards a common value than to use each sample mean by itself. James and Stein gave an explicit rule in 1961. Efron and Morris made it famous in the 1970s with baseball: early-season batting averages predict the remainder of the season better after being shrunk towards the league average.

Lindley and Smith in 1972 gave the Bayesian reading. If the individual parameters are drawn from a population, and the population's mean and spread are themselves unknown, the posterior for each individual is automatically pulled towards the population mean, by an amount that depends on how noisy that individual's data are. A much-used teaching example, analysed by Rubin in 1981, concerns coaching programmes in eight schools.

Physics knows the structure as mean-field theory. Each spin in a magnet feels the average of all the others, and that average is in turn determined by the spins. The self-consistency between individual and collective is the same as between each object and the population it belongs to. Astronomy uses it for populations of exoplanets, supernovae and merging black holes, where the properties of the population are the scientific goal.

The structure

There are two levels. Population parameters φ (a mean and a spread) generate a parameter θj for each object, and each θj generates that object's data yj:

φ  →  θ1, θ2, …, θJ  →  y1, y2, …, yJ

In the language of disordered systems the θj are quenched random variables, fixed for each object but drawn from a distribution, and the goal is to learn that distribution from one sample of the disorder.

Shrinkage

Worked example: eight noisy measurements

Let each θj ~ N(μ, τ²) with τ known and μ unknown, and let each be measured once, yj ~ N(θj, σ²). The population mean is estimated by the average ȳ. The posterior for one object is a compromise between its own measurement and that average:

⟨θj⟩ = yj − λ (yj − ȳ),     λ = σ² / (σ² + τ²)
Var(θj) = (1 − λ) σ² + λ σ² / J

The weight λ is the fraction of the scatter among the measurements that is noise. Numbers: J = 8, σ = 1, τ = 0.5, so λ = 0.8. Suppose the average is ȳ = 2.0 and one object reads y = 3.5.

AnalysisEstimate for that objectError bar
Object alone3.501.00
Hierarchical2.300.55
All objects assumed identical2.000.35
Result. The estimate is pulled 80% of the way to the population average and its error bar nearly halves. An outlying reading is most likely an average object with an unlucky noise draw, and the model says so. The population mean itself is known to √[(σ² + τ²)/J] = 0.40.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
J, sigma, tau, ybar, y = 8, 1.0, 0.5, 2.0, 3.5
lam = sigma**2 / (sigma**2 + tau**2)
print("lambda = %.1f" % lam)
print("object alone:  %.2f +/- %.2f" % (y, sigma))
print("hierarchical:  %.2f +/- %.2f" % (y - lam * (y - ybar), np.sqrt((1 - lam) * sigma**2 + lam * sigma**2 / J)))
print("all identical: %.2f +/- %.2f" % (ybar, sigma / np.sqrt(J)))
print("population mean known to %.2f" % np.sqrt((sigma**2 + tau**2) / J))

Output

lambda = 0.8
object alone:  3.50 +/- 1.00
hierarchical:  2.30 +/- 0.55
all identical: 2.00 +/- 0.35
population mean known to 0.40

The two limits are instructive. If the population is very broad (τ large, λ → 0) the objects tell each other nothing and each is analysed alone. If it is very narrow (λ → 1) they are copies and the data are simply averaged. A hierarchical model finds the right point between, and when τ is unknown it learns that point from the data. Quantities like μ and τ, which describe the prior and not any one object, are called hyperparameters, and the next chapter is about them.

The mean-field picture

Each object feels the others only through the population average, and the population average is set by the objects. That is the self-consistency of mean-field theory, where a spin feels the mean magnetisation that the spins jointly produce. The weight λ plays the part of the coupling strength: zero for free spins, one for a rigid block.

Why this is awkward for SBI, and what is done

A simulation of the whole population has thousands of parameters and its data set has no fixed size. Three approaches are in use.

ApproachIdeaPhysics counterpart
Add up per-object termsLearn a likelihood or ratio for a single object (NLE or NRE). For the population, add the log terms and sample φ and the θj together.Energies of independent subsystems add.
Set networksFeed all objects through the same small network and average the outputs before estimating φ, so the answer cannot depend on the order of the objects.Identical particles: observables are symmetric under exchange.
Combine scoresLearn the posterior score for one object and add scores across objects (scores chapter).Forces from independent sources add.

A mistake to avoid

It is tempting to run NPE on each object separately and multiply the J posteriors for the shared parameter. Each of those posteriors already contains the prior, so the product contains the prior J times. With eight objects the prior is counted eight times over and the result is far too confident. Likelihoods and ratios may be multiplied freely. Posteriors may not, unless the surplus J − 1 copies of the prior are divided out.

More worked examples

Worked example: two stars, measured well and badly

A population of stars has a mean brightness of 100 and a true spread of 10. Two stars are both measured at 130. The first measurement has an error of 20 and the second an error of 5. The weight given to a star's own measurement is the population variance divided by the sum of that variance and the measurement variance.

first star: weight 100/(100 + 400) = 0.2   ⇒   100 + 0.2 × 30 = 106 ± 8.9
second star: weight 100/(100 + 25) = 0.8   ⇒   100 + 0.8 × 30 = 124 ± 4.5
Result. The same reading of 130 becomes 106 for the poorly measured star and 124 for the well measured one. A reading three population standard deviations out with a large error is more likely a noisy ordinary star than a remarkable one. Both error bars are smaller than the measurement errors, because the population supplied information. In the mean-field picture, each star feels a pull towards the population mean with a strength set by how weakly its own data hold it.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
pop_mean, pop_sd, reading = 100.0, 10.0, 130.0
for err in (20.0, 5.0):
    w = pop_sd**2 / (pop_sd**2 + err**2)
    print("error %2.0f: weight %.1f, estimate %.0f +/- %.1f" % (err, w, pop_mean + w * (reading - pop_mean), (1 / pop_sd**2 + 1 / err**2)**-0.5))

Output

error 20: weight 0.2, estimate 106 +/- 8.9
error  5: weight 0.8, estimate 124 +/- 4.5

Questions people ask

Is shrinkage not just bias? It is a bias for each individual and an improvement overall. Extreme measurements are extreme partly because of noise, and pulling them in corrects for that on average. The individual estimates are no longer unbiased, and their total error is smaller.

How much should each estimate shrink? In proportion to how noisy it is, compared with the true spread of the population. A precisely measured object keeps its own value. A poorly measured one is replaced almost entirely by the population mean. The population spread is learned from the data in the same analysis.

Why are hierarchical models hard for SBI? The parameter count grows with the number of objects, and a network trained for a hundred objects does not apply to a hundred and one. The usual remedy is to exploit the structure: learn the posterior for one object given the population parameters, and combine.

What are quenched and annealed averages? Two orders of doing things. Averaging the evidence over objects and then taking the log treats the individual parameters as free to adjust. That is the annealed average. Taking the log for each object and then averaging treats them as fixed. That is the quenched average, and it is the correct one for a population of real objects.

constructionIntegrating out the population field couples everything to everything

The mean-field picture in this chapter can be made exact. Hold the objects' parameters fixed and integrate over the unknown population mean μ with a flat prior:

∫ dμ Πj exp[ −(θj − μ)² / 2τ² ] ∝ exp[ −(1 / 2Jτ²) Σj<k (θj − θk)² ]

Before the integration the objects were independent, each interacting only with μ. Afterwards every pair is coupled by a spring of strength 1/Jτ². This is an infinite-range ferromagnet, the Curie–Weiss model: each element interacts equally with every other, with a coupling that falls as one over their number, which is the condition for mean-field theory to be exact.

Read from right to left, the same identity is the Hubbard–Stratonovich transformation. It takes a system with all-to-all interactions and decouples it by introducing an auxiliary field, here μ, that every element feels independently. Field theorists use it to turn an interacting problem into free particles in a fluctuating background. A hierarchical model is that construction used as a modelling device: the population parameter is the auxiliary field, and shrinkage is the interaction it mediates.

Quenched and annealed. The objects' parameters are drawn once and then fixed while the data fluctuate, which is quenched disorder. The quantity that predicts typical behaviour is then the average of the log-evidence over the disorder, not the log of the average evidence. Computing an average of a logarithm is the problem the replica trick was invented for,

⟨ ln Z ⟩ = limn→0 ( ⟨Zn⟩ − 1 ) / n

and it is how the statistical mechanics of spin glasses was turned into a theory of how much can be learned from a given amount of data.

Exchangeability is a symmetry. The posterior for the population is unchanged if the objects are relabelled. Set networks build in that permutation symmetry, as a many-body wavefunction is built to be symmetric under exchange of identical particles.

← 32. Symmetry and degeneracy34. Hyperparameters →

Chapter 34 of 39

Hyperparameters: parameters about your assumptions
