How wide is the population? How much should each data set be trusted? These are not the physics you set out to measure, but the answer depends on them. A hyperparameter is a knob on the prior or the noise model, and the Bayesian treatment is to let the data set it and then integrate it away.

physics

The connection. A hyperparameter is a temperature or a coupling constant: a knob on the ensemble, not a degree of freedom in it.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
HyperparameterTemperature, or coupling constant
Evidence as a function of the hyperparameterFree energy as a function of temperature
Choosing it by maximising the evidenceMinimising the free energy
A weight on each data setA separate temperature for each subsystem

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: parameters of the analysis itself

A hyperparameter is a number that shapes the prior or the noise model and is not itself the quantity of scientific interest: the width of a prior, the smoothness length of a Gaussian process, the weight given to a data set. Robbins in 1956 proposed estimating such quantities from the data, an approach called empirical Bayes. MacKay in 1992 put it on a systematic footing for neural networks: choose hyperparameters by maximising the evidence, or better, integrate over them.

In cosmology the idea was applied to a recurring problem. Different experiments measuring the same quantity sometimes disagree by more than their stated errors allow. Lahav and colleagues in 2000, and Hobson, Bridle and Lahav in 2002, proposed giving each data set a weight that multiplies its log-likelihood and letting the data determine the weights. An experiment whose errors were underestimated is automatically down-weighted, and the evidence indicates whether the weights were needed at all.

A weight multiplying a log-likelihood is an inverse temperature. The scheme therefore amounts to letting each data set have its own temperature and finding the temperatures at which the combined system is most probable.

Two kinds

in the prior

Population hyperparameters

The mean μ and spread τ of the population in the hierarchical chapter. They control how strongly objects are pulled together.

in the likelihood

Weight hyperparameters

A factor on each data set's quoted errors. They control how much each data set counts when several are combined. This is the formalism of Lahav et al. (2000) and Hobson, Bridle and Lahav (2002).

There are three things you can do with a hyperparameter: fix it by hand, choose the single best value, or marginalise over it. The rest of this chapter works through the weight kind, where all three can be done on paper.

A weight on a data set is an inverse temperature

A data set of n points with Gaussian errors has likelihood e−χ²/2. Suppose the quoted errors might be wrong by a common factor. Multiply every inverse variance by a weight α:

p(data | θ, α) ∝ αn/2 exp( −α χ²(θ) / 2 ) α = 1: errors as quoted. α < 1: errors were underestimated.

Compare with a Boltzmann factor e−βE. The weight α is an inverse temperature and χ²/2 is the energy. A data set with small α is a hot one: its likelihood is flattened and it constrains little. The factor αn/2 is the normalisation, the partition function of the noise, and it is what stops the data from simply voting for α = 0.

Worked example 1: the best single value

Maximise over α. The log-likelihood is (n/2) ln α − α χ²/2, and its derivative vanishes at

α̂ = n / χ²
Result. The best weight is the one that makes the reduced chi-squared equal to 1. This is the familiar practice of rescaling error bars until χ²/n = 1, now with a derivation. Choosing a hyperparameter this way is called empirical Bayes, or maximising the evidence.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import minimize_scalar
n, chi2 = 20, 45.0                               # twenty points that fit badly
best = minimize_scalar(lambda a: -(n / 2 * np.log(a) - a * chi2 / 2), bounds=(1e-3, 10), method="bounded").x
print("numerical maximum at alpha = %.4f, formula n/chi2 = %.4f, rescaled chi2 per point = %.2f" % (best, n / chi2, best * chi2 / n))

Output

numerical maximum at alpha = 0.4444, formula n/chi2 = 0.4444, rescaled chi2 per point = 1.00

Worked example 2: integrating the weight away

Better to admit that α is uncertain and marginalise. With a prior uniform in ln α (no preferred scale), the integral is a gamma function:

∫ αn/2 e−αχ²/2 dα/α ∝ (χ²)−n/2   ⇒   −2 ln p(data | θ) = n ln χ²(θ) + const

With a proper prior instead, an exponential of unit mean, e−α:

∫ αn/2 e−α(χ²/2 + 1) dα ∝ (χ²/2 + 1)−(n/2 + 1)   ⇒   −2 ln p = (n + 2) ln(χ² + 2) + const
Result. After marginalising, χ² is replaced by its logarithm. A Gaussian likelihood falls as e−χ²/2. The marginalised one falls only as a power of χ², so a badly fitting data set is penalised far less. It has become a Student-t distribution, with heavy tails.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import integrate
n = 10
flat_log = lambda chi2: integrate.quad(lambda a: a**(n/2 - 1) * np.exp(-a * chi2 / 2), 0, np.inf)[0]
unit_exp = lambda chi2: integrate.quad(lambda a: a**(n/2) * np.exp(-a * (chi2 / 2 + 1)), 0, np.inf)[0]
print("prior flat in ln(alpha): ratio of marginal likelihoods at chi2 = 10 and 30: %.2f, (30/10)^(n/2) = %.2f" % (flat_log(10) / flat_log(30), 3.0**(n / 2)))
print("exponential prior:       ratio %.2f, (32/12)^(n/2+1) = %.2f" % (unit_exp(10) / unit_exp(30), (32 / 12)**(n / 2 + 1)))
print("a Gaussian likelihood would give exp(10) = %.0f" % np.exp(10))

Output

prior flat in ln(alpha): ratio of marginal likelihoods at chi2 = 10 and 30: 243.00, (30/10)^(n/2) = 243.00
exponential prior:       ratio 359.59, (32/12)^(n/2+1) = 359.59
a Gaussian likelihood would give exp(10) = 22026

Combining data sets that disagree

The standard way to combine independent data sets is to add their chi-squareds. That trusts every quoted error bar equally. With one weight per data set, each marginalised as above, the rule becomes:

minimise   Σk χk²   →   minimise   Σk nk ln χk²

Worked example 3: two experiments, one of them overconfident

Two experiments measure the same quantity θ, ten points each, every point quoted with error 1. Experiment A averages 1.0 and its points scatter as they should (sum of squared deviations 9). Experiment B averages 3.0 but its points scatter three times more than quoted (sum of squares 81). So

χA² = 9 + 10 (θ − 1)²,     χB² = 81 + 10 (θ − 3)²
AnalysisEstimate of θError barComment
Add the chi-squareds2.000.22Midway between two results it fits badly: χ² = 110 for 19 degrees of freedom
Marginalised weights1.200.37Follows the trustworthy experiment, with a wider and skewed error bar
Experiment A alone1.000.32For comparison

The effective weights at the best fit are n/χ²: 1.08 for A and 0.09 for B. The method has worked out, from B's own internal scatter, that its error bars were too small by a factor of about 1/√0.09 = 3.4, and discounted it accordingly.

Result. Nobody told the analysis which experiment to distrust. Each data set's weight was set by its own statistical consistency. The price is honesty about the error bar: 0.37 instead of a falsely precise 0.22.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
theta = np.linspace(-2, 6, 800001); n = 10
chiA = 9 + 10 * (theta - 1)**2
chiB = 81 + 10 * (theta - 3)**2
def summary(logp):
    p = np.exp(logp - logp.max()); p /= np.trapezoid(p, theta)
    m = np.trapezoid(theta * p, theta); return m, np.sqrt(np.trapezoid((theta - m)**2 * p, theta)), theta[p.argmax()]
m, sd, _ = summary(-(chiA + chiB) / 2)
print("add the chi-squareds: %.2f +/- %.2f, chi2 at the best fit %.0f" % (m, sd, (chiA + chiB).min()))
m, sd, peak = summary(-n / 2 * np.log(chiA) - n / 2 * np.log(chiB))
i = np.abs(theta - peak).argmin()
print("marginalised weights: %.2f +/- %.2f (peak %.2f); weights n/chi2: A %.2f, B %.2f" % (m, sd, peak, n / chiA[i], n / chiB[i]))
m, sd, _ = summary(-chiA / 2)
print("experiment A alone:   %.2f +/- %.2f" % (m, sd))

Output

add the chi-squareds: 2.00 +/- 0.22, chi2 at the best fit 110
marginalised weights: 1.20 +/- 0.37 (peak 1.15); weights n/chi2: A 1.08, B 0.09
experiment A alone:   1.00 +/- 0.32

Are the weights needed at all?

Extra hyperparameters are extra freedom, and the model comparison chapter says freedom must be paid for. Compare the evidence with the weight (exponential prior) against the evidence with α fixed at 1, for one data set at a given χ²:

ln B = ln Γ(n/2 + 1) − (n/2 + 1) ln(χ²/2 + 1) + χ²/2
χ² for n = 10 pointsReduced χ²ln BVerdict
101.0−0.96Weight not needed; mild Occam penalty for adding it
181.80.0Even
303.0+3.2Strong case for a weight
818.1+22.9Decisive: the quoted errors cannot be right

So the same machinery that estimates the weights also says whether to introduce them. A data set that fits well is left alone. Using the evidence in this way to decide whether the data warrant weights is the second half of the Hobson, Bridle and Lahav method.

The physics: a mixture of temperatures

Marginalising over α is averaging Boltzmann factors over a distribution of inverse temperatures. Statistical physics has a name for this, superstatistics: a system whose temperature fluctuates from place to place or time to time. If the inverse temperature follows a gamma distribution, the average of e−βE is no longer exponential in the energy but a power law,

⟨ e−βE ⟩β ∝ (1 + E/E0)−ν

which is the form found in worked example 2, with E = χ²/2. Heavy-tailed distributions in turbulence and in cosmic-ray spectra are modelled the same way. An uncertain noise level and a fluctuating temperature are one calculation.

Fix, optimise or marginalise?

TreatmentWhat it doesRisk
Fix by handAssume the quoted errors and the prior width are rightOverconfident whenever they are not (misspecification chapter)
Optimise (empirical Bayes)Pick the value that maximises the evidence, then proceed as if it were knownIgnores the uncertainty in the hyperparameter. Fine with much data, too narrow with little.
Marginalise (full Bayes)Give it a prior and integrate over itNeeds a prior on the hyperparameter, and the answer can depend on it when data are few

In free-energy language, optimising a hyperparameter is minimising the free energy with respect to it, a saddle-point approximation. Marginalising keeps the fluctuations about that minimum.

Hyperparameters in SBI

  • They are just more inputs to the simulator. For each training simulation draw the hyperparameter from its prior, then the parameters, then the data. Nothing else changes.
  • Marginalisation is free with NPE. Train the network to output the posterior of the physics parameters only. Because the hyperparameters varied across the training set and were not asked for, the network has integrated over them. No extra integral is ever done.
  • With NLE or NRE they must be sampled. The hyperparameters are arguments of the learned likelihood, so the sampler explores them along with everything else and they are dropped at the end.
  • Make the noise level one of them. A simulator with an adjustable noise amplitude protects against the wrong-noise failure of the misspecification chapter at almost no cost.

A different use of the word: in machine learning, "hyperparameters" also means the settings of the training itself, such as the learning rate, the network size and the number of training simulations. Those are chosen by validation, not inferred, and should be reported so that a result can be reproduced.

Questions people ask

Is it legitimate to tune the prior using the data? With one or two hyperparameters and many data points, yes: the evidence is sharply peaked and the result is close to a full Bayesian treatment. With many hyperparameters and few data it overfits, and they should be integrated over.

What does a weight below one mean? That the data set is being treated as less informative than its quoted errors claim. For a Gaussian likelihood a weight of one quarter is exactly the same as doubling every error bar.

Does introducing weights always weaken the result? Only when the data sets conflict. If they agree, the evidence favours weights near one and little is lost. The cost is a small Occam penalty for the extra freedom.

Why is this in a course on SBI? Because neural methods have hyperparameters of their own: the architecture, the learning rate and the number of simulations. And because tempering the likelihood is one of the main defences against a misspecified simulator.

constructionCanonical and microcanonical, saddle point and fluctuation

Marginalising a weight is a Laplace transform. The integral ∫ dα p(α) αn/2 e−αE has the form that connects the two basic ensembles of statistical mechanics: the canonical partition function is the Laplace transform of the density of states, with inverse temperature as the transform variable. Fixing α is working at a definite temperature. Integrating over it gives a mixed ensemble whose energy distribution has the power-law tail found above.

Optimising is the saddle point of that integral. For many data points the integrand is sharply peaked at α̂ = n/χ², the value at which the ensemble's mean energy matches the observed one. This is how temperature is defined for a large isolated system: the value of β at which the canonical energy equals the actual energy. Empirical Bayes is that definition applied to a noise level. It is accurate when n is large, because the fluctuations of α about the saddle are of relative size √(2/n), and it fails for small n exactly as the notion of temperature fails for a system of a few particles.

A weight is a Lagrange multiplier. The Boltzmann factor arises from maximising entropy subject to a constraint on the mean energy, with β the multiplier that enforces it. In the same way α enforces the constraint that the mean squared residual of a data set has its observed value. A set of hyperparameters is a set of conjugate variables, one for each feature of the data that the model is required to reproduce on average.

← 33. Hierarchical models35. Bias →

Chapter 35 of 39

Bias: three different things with one name

"The result is biased" can mean the method is approximate, the prior is doing its job, or the model is wrong. They have different cures, and an error bar reveals none of them.

physics

The connection. The harmless kinds of bias have physical counterparts: an external pull, a regulator, and a finite-size correction.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Pull of the priorExternal field on the system
RegularisationMass term, or cutoff
Bias falling as 1/nFinite-size correction falling as 1/N
Error of an approximate posteriorTruncating a perturbation series

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: one word, several meanings

In classical statistics an estimator is biased if its average over repeated experiments differs from the true value. For much of the twentieth century unbiasedness was treated as a requirement. Stein's result of 1956 showed that insisting on it can make total error larger, and the modern view is that bias and variance are to be traded against each other. Ridge regression, from 1970, and Tikhonov's regularisation, developed for ill-posed inverse problems in physics, both accept a small bias in exchange for a large gain in stability.

In physics the word usually means something else: a systematic error, an effect that shifts every measurement the same way and does not average out. Calibration errors, unmodelled backgrounds and selection effects are of this kind. They are the dominant uncertainty in most precision experiments.

The two meanings are often confused in discussions of machine-learning methods. A neural posterior that is slightly too narrow, a prior that pulls an estimate towards zero, and a simulator that omits an effect are all described as bias. They have different causes, different tests and different cures, which is why this chapter separates them.

Why an error bar cannot show a bias

Add a constant force f to a quadratic well. The energy becomes κ θ²/2 − f θ. Completing the square:

κ θ² / 2 − f θ = κ (θ − f/κ)² / 2 − f² / 2κ

The minimum moves to f/κ and the stiffness is unchanged. A tilted potential has a displaced ground state and the same thermal width. This is the general pattern: a systematic effect moves the centre of a posterior and leaves its width alone, so the width carries no warning. Systematic error has to be found by tests, not read from the output.

Kind 1: bias from approximation

Every practical method trades some bias for less noise. ABC shows the trade in closed form.

Worked example: the best tolerance for a fixed budget

From the ABC chapter, a tolerance ε shifts the posterior mean. For small ε the shift grows as ε²:

bias ≈ −x̄ τ² ε² / (τ² + s²)²

With n simulations the number kept is proportional to n ε, so the Monte Carlo scatter of the estimated mean has variance proportional to 1/(n ε). The total squared error is the sum:

error² = A ε⁴ + B / (n ε)

A tight tolerance wastes simulations and is noisy. A loose one is biased. Setting the derivative to zero gives the best compromise, ε ∝ n−1/5. For τ = 2, s² = 0.25, x̄ = 1.2:

Simulations nBest toleranceBias thereTotal error
1,0000.27−0.0200.055
10,0000.18−0.0080.021
100,0000.11−0.0030.008
1,000,0000.07−0.0010.003
Result. The error falls as n−2/5, slower than the n−1/2 of ordinary Monte Carlo. Ten times the simulations buys a factor of 2.6, not 3.2. With d summaries the rate worsens to n−2/(4+d).
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import minimize_scalar
tau2, s2, xbar = 4.0, 0.25, 1.2
mean = lambda e: xbar * tau2 / (tau2 + s2 + e**2)
var = lambda e: 1 / (1 / tau2 + 1 / (s2 + e**2))
kept = lambda e: np.sqrt(e**2 / (e**2 + tau2 + s2)) * np.exp(-xbar**2 / (2 * (e**2 + tau2 + s2)))
for n in (1e3, 1e4, 1e5, 1e6):
    err2 = lambda e: (mean(e) - mean(0))**2 + var(e) / (n * kept(e))         # bias squared + Monte Carlo variance
    e = minimize_scalar(err2, bounds=(1e-3, 2), method="bounded").x
    print("n = %7d: best tolerance %.2f, bias %.3f, total error %.3f" % (n, e, mean(e) - mean(0), np.sqrt(err2(e))))

Output

n =    1000: best tolerance 0.27, bias -0.020, total error 0.055
n =   10000: best tolerance 0.18, bias -0.008, total error 0.021
n =  100000: best tolerance 0.11, bias -0.003, total error 0.008
n = 1000000: best tolerance 0.07, bias -0.001, total error 0.003

Neural methods have the same kind of bias from a network that is too small or trained too briefly. It shrinks with more simulations and more training, and a coverage test detects it.

Kind 2: the pull of the prior

The posterior mean for a Gaussian measurement is x τ²/(τ² + s²). It is always closer to the prior centre than the measurement is. With τ = 2 and s² = 0.25 the pull is 6%.

A statistician who repeats the experiment at a fixed true value will call this a biased estimator, and it is. It is also the correct use of what was known beforehand, and averaged over the prior it has a smaller error than the unshrunk measurement. The hierarchical chapter is this effect put to work. It becomes a real problem only when the prior is wrong or was chosen carelessly: check by widening the prior and seeing whether the conclusion moves.

Kind 3: bias from a wrong model

If the simulator omits something real, the fit absorbs it by shifting the parameters. This kind does not shrink with more simulations or more training, and it gets worse, relative to the error bar, with more data. It has its own chapter, next.

Telling them apart

KindShrinks with more simulations?Shrinks with more data?Test
ApproximationYesNoCoverage test; compare with an exact method on a simple case
Prior pullNoYesWiden the prior and repeat
Wrong modelNoNo; grows relative to the error barResiduals; simulate from the fit and compare with the data

A practical way to measure any bias when the truth is known, as in a simulation: repeat the analysis over many independent noise realisations. The average offset is the bias. The scatter about that average is the true statistical error, and it should match the quoted error bar.

More worked examples

Worked example: the pull of a prior, and how it fades

A quantity is measured as 5.0 ± 1.0. The prior is a Gaussian centred on zero with width 3. The posterior mean is the measurement multiplied by 3²/(3² + 1²) = 0.9.

Result. The estimate is 4.5 ± 0.95, pulled half a standard deviation towards zero. With a hundred such measurements the combined error is 0.1, and the estimate is 4.994: the pull has shrunk to one twentieth of a standard deviation. A prior is a force of fixed strength, and the data are a spring that stiffens as they accumulate.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
prior_var = 9.0
for err in (1.0, 0.1):                           # one measurement, then the mean of a hundred
    w = prior_var / (prior_var + err**2)
    est, sd = 5.0 * w, np.sqrt(w) * err
    print("error %.1f: estimate %.3f +/- %.3f, pulled by %.2f sigma" % (err, est, sd, (5.0 - est) / sd))

Output

error 1.0: estimate 4.500 +/- 0.949, pulled by 0.53 sigma
error 0.1: estimate 4.994 +/- 0.100, pulled by 0.06 sigma

Worked example: a biased estimate that is more accurate

A quantity whose true value is 1 is estimated from data with a standard error of 1. Use the estimate c times the measurement. Its mean squared error is the variance c² plus the squared bias (1 − c)².

Result. At c = 1, the unbiased choice, the error is 1. At c = 0.5 it is 0.25 + 0.25 = 0.5, which is half. Shrinking the estimate introduces a bias and removes more variance than it adds. This is why regularised and Bayesian estimates beat unbiased ones when the data are weak, and it is the trade made by every prior.
Show the code 
for c in (1.0, 0.5):
    variance, bias2 = c**2 * 1.0, ((1 - c) * 1.0)**2
    print("c = %.1f: variance %.2f + bias^2 %.2f = mean squared error %.2f" % (c, variance, bias2, variance + bias2))

Output

c = 1.0: variance 1.00 + bias^2 0.00 = mean squared error 1.00
c = 0.5: variance 0.25 + bias^2 0.25 = mean squared error 0.50

Questions people ask

Should a good method be unbiased? Not as a requirement. A Bayesian posterior mean with an informative prior is biased towards the prior by design, and it is more accurate on average for that. What a good method must have is honest uncertainty: intervals that contain the truth as often as they claim.

How can I tell which kind of bias I have? Bias from approximation shrinks when the method is given more simulations or a larger network. The pull of the prior shrinks as data are added and is unaffected by the method. Bias from a wrong model does neither, and tends to become more significant as the error bars shrink.

Why can an error bar not show a bias? Because the error bar is computed inside the model. It answers the question of how much the estimate would scatter if the model were true. A fault in the model moves the whole distribution without widening it.

What is the physical picture? A bias is a force. A prior, or a term left out of the model, adds a constant push to the energy, and the minimum moves by the push divided by the stiffness. Well-constrained parameters barely move, and soft ones move a long way.

constructionA bias is a source term; a tolerance is a regulator

Linear response. The tilted well at the top of this chapter is the simplest case of a general statement. Add a small perturbation to the action and the mean of any quantity shifts by the perturbation times a susceptibility, which by the fluctuation–response relation is a covariance in the unperturbed system:

δ⟨θ⟩ = f · Var(θ) for an added term −fθ in the energy

For the quadratic well Var(θ) = 1/κ, giving the shift f/κ found there. In field-theory language a term linear in the field is a tadpole. It displaces the vacuum and leaves the spectrum of fluctuations unchanged, which is the precise sense in which a systematic error moves the answer without altering the error bar. It also gives a rule of thumb: a posterior is most easily biased along its widest direction, because that is where the susceptibility is largest.

Regulator error and statistical error. A lattice calculation has two errors that pull against each other. A finer lattice reduces the discretisation error but makes each configuration more expensive, so fewer are generated and the statistical error grows. The optimum balances the two, and the discretisation error is then removed by extrapolating in the lattice spacing. The ABC tolerance is the same kind of object, and the worked example in this chapter is that optimisation: bias proportional to ε², statistical error rising as ε falls, and a best ε that shrinks slowly with the budget. The extrapolation in ε² is carried out in the ABC chapter.

← 34. Hyperparameters36. Misspecification →

Chapter 36 of 39

Misspecification: when the simulator is wrong

Every method in this course answers the question "which parameters make the simulator produce data like mine?" If the simulator cannot produce data like yours, the question still gets a confident answer.

physics

The connection. A wrong simulator is a wrong Hamiltonian. Fitting it gives the best effective theory in a restricted family, and that can fail without warning outside its range.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
The simulatorThe assumed Hamiltonian
Best-fitting wrong modelBest effective theory within a restricted family
Overconfidence growing with dataLow temperature freezing the system into the wrong ground state
Down-weighting the likelihoodRaising the temperature

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: all models are wrong

The statistician George Box wrote in 1976 that all models are wrong and some are useful. The mathematical study of what happens when a wrong model is fitted began earlier. Berk in 1966 and Huber in 1967 showed that, with enough data, a fit converges to the member of the model family that is closest to the truth in relative entropy. White in 1982 worked out the consequences for error bars: they are no longer given by the Fisher matrix alone.

The Bayesian version is more troubling. As data accumulate the posterior concentrates around the best wrong answer, and its width shrinks as if the model were right. Confidence grows while the error stays fixed. Remedies proposed since about 2010 include raising the likelihood to a power below one, which widens the posterior, and replacing the likelihood by a loss function chosen for robustness.

For simulation-based inference the problem has an extra edge, examined from 2022 by Cannon and colleagues, by Schmitt and colleagues, and by Ward and colleagues. A neural network trained only on simulations is asked to process real data that may lie outside anything it has seen. Its output there is not constrained by training at all, and it can be confidently wrong in ways that have nothing to do with the best-fitting model.

What the posterior converges to

With a correct model and plenty of data the posterior narrows onto the true parameter. With a wrong model it still narrows, onto the parameter value that makes the model least different from reality. Statisticians call it the pseudo-true value. The posterior reports ever-growing certainty about a number that describes the model's best imitation of the world.

The physics version: fit data with the wrong Hamiltonian and you obtain excellent thermodynamics of the wrong system. An effective theory used outside its range of validity fails the same way, quietly.

Two cases that can be solved exactly

Worked example 1: the wrong noise level

You measure θ with true noise strue but the model assumes smodel. With a broad prior the posterior is centred on the measurement, which is right, with width smodel, which is not. The actual error of the centre is strue.

coverage of the "68%" interval = P( |z| < smodel / strue )
Result. If the model assumes half the true noise, the stated 68% interval contains the truth 38% of the time. The centre is unbiased and every error bar is wrong by the same factor. The remedy is to make the noise level a parameter and fit it.
Show the code 
from scipy import stats
for ratio in (0.5, 1.0, 2.0):                    # assumed noise / true noise
    print("model noise = %.1f x true: the '68%%' interval covers %.0f%%" % (ratio, 100 * (2 * stats.norm.cdf(ratio * stats.norm.ppf(0.84134)) - 1)))

Output

model noise = 0.5 x true: the '68%' interval covers 38%
model noise = 1.0 x true: the '68%' interval covers 68%
model noise = 2.0 x true: the '68%' interval covers 95%

Worked example 2: a missing component

The data contain an offset c that the model lacks: each of n measurements is θ + c + noise of size s. The model attributes everything to θ, so the estimate is too high by c. Its error bar is s/√n, exactly as if nothing were wrong.

bias / error bar = c √n / s

For an offset one fifth the size of the noise, c = 0.2 s:

Measurements nError barBiasBias in error bars
11.00 s0.2 s0.2σ
250.20 s0.2 s1σ
1000.10 s0.2 s2σ
4000.05 s0.2 s4σ
Result. An omission that was harmless with one measurement is a 4σ error with 400. Better data do not cure a wrong model. They expose it, and a larger instrument or a longer observation makes the same omission more serious.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
c = 0.2                                          # the missing offset, in units of the noise s
for n in (1, 25, 100, 400):
    print("n = %3d: error bar %.2f s, bias %.1f s, bias = %.1f error bars" % (n, 1 / np.sqrt(n), c, c * np.sqrt(n)))

Output

n =   1: error bar 1.00 s, bias 0.2 s, bias = 0.2 error bars
n =  25: error bar 0.20 s, bias 0.2 s, bias = 1.0 error bars
n = 100: error bar 0.10 s, bias 0.2 s, bias = 2.0 error bars
n = 400: error bar 0.05 s, bias 0.2 s, bias = 4.0 error bars

What is particular to neural SBI

A sampler with a wrong likelihood at least computes that wrong likelihood correctly. A network adds a second hazard: real data that the simulator could never have produced lie outside everything the network was trained on, and its output there is an extrapolation with no guarantee of any kind. The posterior may be narrow, broad or in the wrong place, and it will look as smooth as ever.

How to detect it

  • Posterior predictive check. Draw parameters from the posterior, simulate data, and compare with the real data in as many ways as you can think of. If the simulations never look like the observation, the model is wrong.
  • Residuals. Subtract the best-fit model from the data. What remains should look like noise. Structure in it is the missing physics.
  • Is the observation typical of the training set? Compare the real data's summaries with the cloud of simulated ones. An observation far outside the cloud is a warning before any inference is run.
  • Fit parts of the data separately. Split by time, frequency or detector. A correct model gives consistent parameters from each part.

What to do about it

  • Add the missing effect to the simulator. This is the real cure.
  • Add nuisance parameters for what you cannot model in detail (an offset, a gain, a noise level) and marginalise over them. The error bars grow to an honest size. The hyperparameters chapter works this out for a noise level.
  • Use summaries that ignore the faulty part. If the model fails on small scales, compress to large scales only. You lose information and gain validity, the same choice an effective theory makes with its cutoff.
  • Train with deliberately varied simulators, so the network learns not to rely on features that differ between them.

How this shows up in practice

A typical case: a model evaluates a signal at the centre of each time sample, while the instrument averages over the sample. On most of the data the difference is negligible. Where the signal changes quickly it is not, and the fit shifts its parameters to absorb the mismatch. The quoted error bars stay small while the answer moves by several of them from one noise realisation to the next. A comparison of the scatter over noise realisations with the quoted error bar finds it, and the residuals show where.

Questions people ask

If every model is wrong, is every result wrong? Every result is conditional on its model. What matters is whether the model's faults affect the question being asked. A simulator can be poor at small scales and still give sound answers from large-scale data, if the small scales are left out of the analysis.

Why do more data make things worse? They do not change the systematic shift, and they shrink the statistical error. A discrepancy of half a standard deviation becomes five standard deviations when the error bar is ten times smaller. The model is equally wrong, and more visibly so.

How do I test for misspecification? Simulate data from the fitted posterior and compare them with the real data in ways the fit did not use. Check whether the real data lie inside the cloud of training simulations in the space of summaries. Compare results from independent subsets of the data.

What can be done if the test fails? Improve the simulator, which is the real answer. Failing that, remove the parts of the data the simulator describes badly, add nuisance parameters that can absorb the discrepancy, or widen the posterior deliberately by tempering.

What is the effective-theory picture? An effective theory is a model known to be incomplete, used inside a stated range of validity, with an estimate of the size of what it omits. A simulator should be treated in the same way: as a description valid over a certain range of scales, with an error budget for what lies outside.

constructionA wrong simulator is an effective theory used outside its range

No simulator contains all the physics. Each is an effective theory: a description valid up to some scale, with the effects of everything beyond that scale either absorbed into its parameters or neglected. The pseudo-true value is the physicist's matching condition, the values the effective couplings must take for the truncated theory to reproduce the full one as well as it can.

Three results in this chapter are standard effective-theory behaviour.

  • Absorbed effects shift the parameters. The missing offset c did not disappear. It was absorbed into θ, as integrating out a heavy particle renormalises the couplings of the light ones. A fitted parameter is always a renormalised quantity: the "bare" value plus everything the model left out that looks like it.
  • Precision reveals the truncation. An effective theory's error is a power of (scale of the experiment)/(scale of the neglected physics). It is invisible at low precision and dominant at high precision. The growth of bias/error bar as √n is the same statement with the number of data as the measure of precision.
  • The remedy is to add the next operator. A nuisance parameter for an offset, a gain or a noise level is the next term in the expansion, with a coefficient to be fitted. Restricting the summaries to the scales where the model is trusted is imposing a cutoff.

Information geometry gives the picture a shape. The set of distributions the simulator can produce is a surface in the space of all distributions. The truth lies off it. The posterior concentrates at the point of the surface nearest the truth, with distance measured by the Kullback–Leibler divergence, and the residuals are the component of the truth perpendicular to the surface: the part no setting of the parameters can reach.

← 35. Bias37. Checks →

Chapter 37 of 39

Checks: how to tell when the answer is wrong

A neural posterior always returns something smooth and plausible. These tests find out whether it deserves to be believed.

physics

The connection. The tests of a posterior are the tests a simulation in physics must pass: that it has reached equilibrium, and that it satisfies the exact relations any equilibrium state obeys.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Coverage testChecking a fluctuation relation that must hold exactly
Agreement between independent chainsThermalisation: independent runs agree
Posterior predictive checkComparing predicted observables with experiment

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: how to trust an answer you cannot verify

A Bayesian calculation can be tested even when the right answer for the real data is unknown, because it must satisfy certain identities exactly. Cook, Gelman and Rubin pointed out in 2006 that if parameters are drawn from the prior, data are simulated from them, and the posterior is then computed, the true parameter must fall at a uniformly distributed rank within the posterior samples. Talts and colleagues developed this into simulation-based calibration in 2018. The test needs only the ability to simulate and to compute posteriors many times.

For neural methods the need became clear in 2021, when Hermans and colleagues tested the standard algorithms on standard problems and found that they often produced posteriors that were too narrow. Their paper asked whether a crisis of trust was coming. Its effect was to make coverage tests an expected part of any SBI analysis. Lemos and colleagues in 2023 introduced a test that works from samples alone.

Simulations in physics are held to the same discipline. A Monte Carlo run of a lattice model is checked against exact sum rules and symmetry relations, and for agreement between runs started in different states. An unchecked simulation is not accepted as a measurement, and an unchecked posterior should not be either.

Coverage: are the error bars honest?

If a method says "68% interval", the truth should fall inside it 68% of the time. With a simulator this can be tested directly, because you can manufacture cases where the truth is known:

  1. Draw a true θ from the prior and simulate data from it.
  2. Run the method on those data to get a posterior.
  3. Record whether the truth lies inside the 68% interval.
  4. Repeat a few hundred times and count.

Worked example: what overconfidence looks like

Suppose the true posterior is a Gaussian of width σ, and the method reports the right centre with a width of σ/2. Its 68% interval is ±σ/2. The truth is scattered about the centre with the real width σ, so the chance it lands inside is the Gaussian probability of being within half a standard deviation:

P( |z| < 0.5 ) = 38%
Result. Error bars too small by a factor of 2 turn a nominal 68% interval into a 38% one. Too small by a factor of 3 gives 26%. Too wide by a factor of 2 gives 95%. Counting how often the truth is covered reads the error straight off.
Show the code 
from scipy import stats
for factor, label in ((0.5, "too small by 2"), (1 / 3, "too small by 3"), (2.0, "too wide by 2")):
    print("%-15s: a nominal 68%% interval covers %.0f%%" % (label, 100 * (2 * stats.norm.cdf(factor) - 1)))

Output

too small by 2 : a nominal 68% interval covers 38%
too small by 3 : a nominal 68% interval covers 26%
too wide by 2  : a nominal 68% interval covers 95%

A finer version is simulation-based calibration: for each test case, rank the true value among samples from the posterior. If the posterior is right the ranks are uniformly distributed. A histogram of ranks piled up at both ends means too narrow, piled in the middle means too wide, and tilted to one side means biased.

The physics counterpart is checking that a simulation has equilibrated, for example by confirming that measured fluctuations match the response function.

Is the simulator right?

SBI learns the simulator's world. If the simulation omits an effect that is present in the real data, every method in this course returns a confident answer that is wrong, and a coverage test made with the same simulator will pass. Coverage tests check the inference, not the model. Testing the model needs a comparison with the real data, and that is the subject of the misspecification chapter.

A checklist

CheckWhat it catches
Recover known parameters from simulated dataGross errors in the pipeline
Coverage or rank test over a few hundred simulated casesError bars too narrow or too wide; bias
Compare with an exact method on a simplified problemWhether the network has enough capacity and training
Simulate from the posterior and compare with the real dataA simulator that cannot produce what was observed
Repeat training with different random seedsAn answer that depends on the training run
Check that the real data resemble the training dataExtrapolation outside what the network has seen

More worked examples

Worked example: what a rank test shows for an overconfident posterior

Run 200 test simulations. For each, find the rank of the true parameter among the posterior samples, and sort the ranks into 10 bins. If the posterior is calibrated every bin expects 20 cases, give or take 4.2. Suppose instead that the posterior is too narrow, with 0.8 of the correct width. The truth then falls in the lowest tenth of the posterior with probability Φ(0.8 × −1.28) = 0.153, and the same for the highest tenth.

Result. The two end bins each hold about 31 cases where 20 are expected, an excess of 2.5 standard deviations at each end. The histogram is U-shaped. A posterior that is too wide gives the opposite, a hump in the middle, and one that is shifted gives a slope. The shape of the rank histogram tells you what kind of fault there is. 200 simulations are enough to see a 20 per cent error in the width.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
p_end = stats.norm.cdf(0.8 * stats.norm.ppf(0.1))            # truth in the lowest tenth of a posterior 0.8 as wide
expected, sd = 200 * 0.1, np.sqrt(200 * 0.1 * 0.9)
print("P(end bin) = %.3f: %.1f cases where %.0f +/- %.1f are expected, an excess of %.1f sd" % (p_end, 200 * p_end, expected, sd, (200 * p_end - expected) / sd))

Output

P(end bin) = 0.153: 30.5 cases where 20 +/- 4.2 are expected, an excess of 2.5 sd

Worked example: two chains that disagree

Two MCMC chains are run from different starting points. Each has a standard deviation of 1.0 within the chain. Their means are 0.0 and 0.6. The Gelman-Rubin statistic compares the variance between the chain means, here 0.18, with the variance within a chain: R̂ = √((1 + 0.18)/1).

Result. R̂ = 1.086. The usual requirement is below 1.01, so these chains have not converged, though each looks stable when plotted alone. In physics terms the two runs have not reached the same equilibrium. Either both need longer, or the distribution has two regions that the sampler cannot cross between.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
means, within_var = np.array([0.0, 0.6]), 1.0
between = means.var(ddof=1)
print("variance between chain means %.2f, R-hat = %.3f" % (between, np.sqrt((within_var + between) / within_var)))

Output

variance between chain means 0.18, R-hat = 1.086

Questions people ask

If the coverage test passes, is the posterior right? It is right on average over the prior. It could still be wrong for your particular data set while being right on average, and the test is blind to faults in the simulator, since it uses the simulator as its truth.

How many test simulations are needed? Enough to resolve the miscalibration you care about. With 200 test cases, 68 per cent coverage is measured to about plus or minus 3 per cent, which distinguishes a calibrated method from one whose intervals are a fifth too narrow.

What if my method is not amortised? Then each test case costs a full analysis, and a complete coverage test may be out of reach. The usual compromise is to run a few dozen cases near the observed data. That is weaker, and the report should say so.

Is it better for intervals to be too wide or too narrow? Too wide. A conservative interval loses some precision, and an overconfident one produces false discoveries. Several methods now deliberately bias the training towards conservative posteriors.

constructionTests are identities that any correct calculation must satisfy

Field theories are checked with exact identities, relations that follow from the structure of the theory alone and hold whatever the dynamics: sum rules, Ward identities, the Schwinger–Dyson equations. A numerical result that violates one is wrong, with no need to know the right answer. The tests in this chapter are identities of that kind for Bayesian inference.

  • The data-averaged posterior is the prior. Draw θ from the prior, simulate data, compute the posterior, and average over many repetitions: ∫ p(θ | x) p(x) dx = p(θ). This is a sum rule. Simulation-based calibration tests it rank by rank, and a coverage test checks one of its consequences.
  • The average score vanishes. ⟨∂ ln p(x | θ)/∂θ⟩ = 0 when the average is over data simulated at that θ. It follows from integrating a total derivative, the same step that gives the Schwinger–Dyson equations, and it can be used to test a learned likelihood without knowing the true one.
  • Fluctuation equals response. The variance of the score equals minus the mean of its derivative. Both sides can be estimated from simulations, and their disagreement measures the error of a learned likelihood.
  • Scatter equals error bar. The spread of the posterior mean over independent noise realisations must match the quoted width. This is a fluctuation–dissipation check on the whole analysis, including the simulator.

The first three test the inference machinery against the simulator and will pass even if the simulator is wrong. Only a comparison with real data tests the simulator itself.

The whole course on one screen

Ideas
  • The posterior is a Boltzmann distribution with energy −ln(likelihood × prior). The evidence is its partition function.
  • A likelihood is intractable for the reason a partition function is: a sum over too many hidden states.
  • SBI replaces that sum with samples from a simulator, as Metropolis did in 1953.
Results derived here
  • ABC with tolerance ε is exact inference with measurement variance s² + ε². The kept fraction falls as εd.
  • In a Boltzmann-form model the conjugate variable is a sufficient statistic, and Fisher information is a response function.
  • The best possible temperature error from a small system is √(kT²/C).
  • A linear-Gaussian NPE trained on simulations reproduces Bayes' rule exactly.
  • The odds of a perfect classifier equal the likelihood-to-evidence ratio.
  • Under an invertible map the density changes by the Jacobian, p(θ) = p(u)|du/dθ|. Exponentiating a Gaussian gives the lognormal, with its 1/θ.
  • F[q] ≥ −ln Z, with the gap equal to the mismatch between q and the posterior.
  • Mean field on correlated parameters gives a variance too small by 1 − ρ².
  • Error bars too small by 2 turn 68% coverage into 38%.
  • The log-evidence as a function of the data generates the posterior cumulants: ⟨θ | x⟩ = x + s² ∂ ln p(x)/∂x.
  • The Laplace approximation is a one-loop calculation. For n! it gives Stirling's formula, with corrections in powers of 1/n.
  • Summing over the hidden steps of a random walk gives a Gaussian of variance 2Dt: a path integral done exactly.
  • Integrating out a population mean couples all objects with strength 1/Jτ², an infinite-range ferromagnet.
  • Extrapolating ABC results in ε² to zero removes the leading bias, as a lattice calculation is extrapolated to the continuum.
  • Rejection costs (√V/ε)d simulations per sample for d summaries.
  • Metropolis satisfies detailed balance using energy differences only. A Hamiltonian trajectory of a quarter period gives an independent sample.
  • An amortised network's error grows with distance from the bulk of its training data. Amortisation pays after Ct/Cs data sets.
  • The score is a force and needs no partition function. Denoising moves a sample by σ² times the score.
  • For a Gaussian target, running a diffusion to the end reproduces the shift-and-stretch flow θ = μ + σ u exactly.
  • The Bayes factor contains an Occam penalty of ½ ln(1 + τ²/s²). A 1.7σ excess is break-even in the example.
  • In a hierarchical model each estimate shrinks toward the population mean by σ²/(σ² + τ²).
  • A marginalised weight on a data set replaces χ² by n ln χ², and the best single weight is n/χ².
  • The best ABC tolerance scales as n−1/5 and the error as n−2/5.
  • A missing component biases the answer by c√n/s error bars: more data make it worse.

  • Song, Sohl-Dickstein, Kingma, Kumar, Ermon and Poole, "Score-based generative modeling through stochastic differential equations", ICLR (2021).
  • Lahav, Bridle, Hobson, Lasenby and Sodré, "Bayesian hyper-parameters approach to joint estimation: the Hubble constant from CMB measurements", MNRAS (2000).
  • Hobson, Bridle and Lahav, "Combining cosmological data sets: hyperparameters and Bayesian evidence", MNRAS 335, 377 (2002).
  • Gelman, Carlin, Stern, Dunson, Vehtari and Rubin, Bayesian Data Analysis, 3rd edn (2013). Hierarchical models and model checking.

← 36. Misspecification38. Applications →

Chapter 38 of 39

Applications: where SBI is used in physics

The same recipe, a simulator in place of a likelihood, has been taken up wherever forward modelling is easier than writing down probabilities.

physics

The connection. In every field the simulator plays the Hamiltonian, and the inference problem is the inverse problem of statistical mechanics: from observed behaviour back to the couplings.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Simulator of the experimentHamiltonian of the system
Inferring parameters from dataInverse statistical mechanics
Summary statistics of a surveyCorrelation functions

The section marked construction at the end of this chapter derives it.

Background: where simulators outran likelihoods

Every quantitative science reaches a point where its best models are computer programs and not formulae. Particle physics reached it early: the chain from a proton collision to the signals in a detector involves the showering of quarks, the formation of hadrons and the response of millions of detector channels, and has been simulated in detail since the 1980s. For decades the analyses compared histograms of one or two variables, chosen by hand, between simulation and data. Work by Cranmer, Brehmer, Louppe and others from about 2015 showed that neural networks could use the full information in each event.

Cosmology followed the same course. The standard analyses of galaxy surveys use the two-point correlation function and a Gaussian likelihood, which was adequate for early data and discards information that later surveys contain. From about 2018, analyses by Alsing, Charnock, Feeney, Wandelt and others replaced the assumed likelihood by one learned from simulations. In gravitational-wave astronomy the motivation is speed. In neuroscience, where Gonçalves and colleagues published an influential study in 2020, it is that models of neurons have no likelihood at all.

The common pattern is that the simulator encodes decades of domain knowledge, and SBI is the first general way to use all of it for inference.

FieldSimulatorParameters wantedWhy the likelihood is hardUsual method
CosmologyN-body and hydrodynamic simulations of structure formation, then a mock surveyMatter density, clustering amplitude, dark-energy propertiesThe density field is not Gaussian on small scales; survey masks and selection are complicatedNPE or NLE on compressed statistics or on maps
Particle physicsEvent generators followed by detector simulationCouplings, masses, coefficients of new interactionsEach collision passes through a long chain of unobserved random stepsClassifier-based ratio estimation
Gravitational wavesWaveform models plus detector noiseMasses, spins, distance, sky positionThe likelihood is known but slow: a sampler takes hours to days per eventAmortised NPE, giving a posterior in seconds
Radio astronomyForward model of an interferometer: sky, antenna beams, interference sourcesSource positions and fluxes, satellite orbits, instrument gainsMany nuisance signals and instrument effects to marginaliseSamplers where the noise is Gaussian; neural methods tested against them
Condensed matter and statistical physicsMonte Carlo of lattice models, molecular dynamicsCouplings, temperatures, interaction potentialsThe partition functionABC and neural likelihoods
NeuroscienceBiophysical models of neurons and circuitsChannel conductances, connection strengthsStochastic, nonlinear dynamics with no closed formNPE; much of the open software grew up here
Population genetics and epidemiologyFamily-tree and outbreak simulatorsMutation and migration rates, transmission ratesA sum over all possible ancestries or infection chainsABC, which was invented for this

Three patterns across the fields

  • Replacing a Gaussian assumption. In cosmology the traditional analysis compresses a map to a power spectrum and assumes a Gaussian likelihood for it. SBI is used to extract what that assumption throws away.
  • Speed. In gravitational-wave astronomy the likelihood is perfectly well known. SBI is used because the amortised answer arrives fast enough to point telescopes.
  • A testbed with a known answer. Where an exact sampler is available, as with Gaussian noise in interferometry, a neural method can be validated against it before being trusted on problems where no exact answer exists. This is the most persuasive kind of check and the easiest to skip.

A sensible order of work for a new problem

  1. Write the simulator and confirm it reproduces real data in a posterior predictive sense (misspecification).
  2. Choose summaries from the physics, or let a network learn them (summaries).
  3. Start with the simplest version of the problem in which an exact answer exists, and make the method reproduce it.
  4. Train, then run a coverage test before looking at the real data (checks).
  5. Apply it to the observation, and check that the observation resembles the training set.
  6. Report the prior, the number of simulations, the coverage result and what was left out of the simulator.

Software to start with: the open-source sbi package (Python, built on PyTorch) implements NPE, NLE, NRE and their sequential versions behind one interface; dynesty and NumPyro provide nested sampling and NUTS for the comparison runs.

More worked examples

Worked example: a counting experiment in particle physics

A search expects 15 background events and observes 25. Is there a signal, and how large? With a flat prior on the signal rate s ≥ 0, the posterior is proportional to (s + 15)25 e−(s + 15).

most probable s = 10     mean 11.1 ± 5.0     68% interval 6.1 to 16.1     95% upper limit 19.9

The chance that background alone gives 25 or more is 1.1 per cent, or 2.3 sigma.

Result. A signal of about 11 ± 5 events, not yet a discovery. Here the likelihood is known and the calculation takes one line. A real search has hundreds of such bins, backgrounds that are themselves uncertain, and a detector whose response is known only through simulation. Each of those turns this one line into an integral over hidden variables, and that is where SBI is used.
Show the code 
import numpy as np
from scipy import stats
obs, bkg = 25, 15.0
s = np.linspace(0, 60, 60001)
post = np.exp(obs * np.log(s + bkg) - (s + bkg)); post /= np.trapezoid(post, s)   # flat prior on s >= 0
cdf = np.cumsum(post) * (s[1] - s[0])
mean = np.trapezoid(s * post, s); sd = np.sqrt(np.trapezoid(s**2 * post, s) - mean**2)
q = lambda f: s[np.searchsorted(cdf, f)]
print("mode %.0f, mean %.1f +/- %.1f, 68%% interval %.1f to %.1f, 95%% upper limit %.1f" % (s[post.argmax()], mean, sd, q(0.16), q(0.84), q(0.95)))
p = stats.poisson(bkg).sf(obs - 1)
print("P(25 or more from background) = %.1f%% = %.1f sigma" % (100 * p, stats.norm.isf(p)))

Output

mode 10, mean 11.1 +/- 5.0, 68% interval 6.1 to 16.1, 95% upper limit 19.9
P(25 or more from background) = 1.1% = 2.3 sigma

Questions people ask

Has SBI produced results that older methods could not? Yes, where the information lies beyond simple summaries: non-Gaussian features of the matter distribution in cosmology, the full kinematics of collider events, and fast parameter estimation for gravitational-wave events.

What is the main obstacle to wider use? Trust in the simulator. A conventional likelihood analysis has the same problem, but hides it inside a covariance matrix. SBI makes every prediction of the simulator matter, including the ones nobody checked.

Does SBI replace conventional methods? It extends them. Where a likelihood is known and trusted, using it is simpler and safer. SBI earns its place where the likelihood is unknown, or where the usual approximation to it is throwing information away.

What should a newcomer do first? Take a problem with a known answer, such as the curve fit in this course, and run ABC and one neural method on it. Then apply the same code to the real problem, and keep the known-answer case as a permanent test.

constructionWhere the two subjects are literally one

In three areas the identification made in this course is not a way of seeing things but the working method.

  • Lattice field theory. Expectation values in QCD are posterior averages under the weight e−S. Hamiltonian Monte Carlo was invented there, and flows and diffusion models are now trained with the lattice action as the target, to sample gauge fields without critical slowing down.
  • Field reconstruction in cosmology and astronomy. Inferring a density field or a sky image from noisy, incomplete data is a field theory with the data as source. The free theory gives the Wiener filter, and interaction terms encode non-Gaussian priors.
  • Molecular simulation. Free-energy differences between chemical states are evidence ratios. The estimators developed for them, including free-energy perturbation, the acceptance-ratio method, thermodynamic integration and umbrella sampling, reappear in this course as NRE, tempering and sequential training.

The traffic runs both ways. Methods that statistics took from physics half a century ago are returning as neural estimators, and physics is taking them back to attack the sampling problems it could not solve with Markov chains.

← 37. Checks39. Summary and open problems →

Chapter 39 of 39

Summary and open problems

The course has made one claim thirty-seven times. This last chapter states it once more, tests it on a single small problem, and says where it stops being true.

physics

The connection. The whole course in one line: inference is the statistical mechanics of a model and a data set, and every estimator is a way of evaluating a partition function that cannot be computed exactly.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
Posterior, evidence, −ln(evidence)Boltzmann distribution, partition function, free energy
Likelihood of a simulatorEuclidean path integral
EstimatorsSaddle point, variational bound, Monte Carlo, change of variables, stochastic dynamics
Summaries and marginalisationCoarse-graining and effective actions

Summary: the argument on one page

Write the posterior as a Boltzmann weight. Then minus the log of likelihood times prior is an energy, the evidence is a partition function, and minus its log is a free energy. Nothing has been approximated. It is a change of notation.

What the change buys is a century of methods. The partition function of anything interesting cannot be computed, and physics is largely the craft of living with that. Each method of statistical inference turns out to be one of the physicist's answers.

  • Keep the dominant configuration and its small fluctuations. Saddle points and loops in physics. The Laplace approximation, Fisher forecasts and information criteria in statistics.
  • Bound the free energy with a simpler system. The variational method and mean-field theory. Variational inference and neural posterior estimation.
  • Sample the configurations. Metropolis and hybrid Monte Carlo. MCMC, Hamiltonian Monte Carlo and nested sampling.
  • Change variables until the problem is trivial. Canonical transformations and trivialising maps. Normalising flows.
  • Reach equilibrium by a stochastic process. Langevin dynamics and stochastic quantisation. Diffusion models and training by gradient descent.
  • Integrate out what is not of interest. Effective actions and the renormalisation group. Marginalisation, summary statistics and eigenmode compression.

Simulation-based inference is the case in which even the energy is not available as a formula, only a machine that draws configurations from the Boltzmann weight. The likelihood is then a path integral over the simulator's hidden choices. ABC evaluates it by counting, and the neural methods learn it, or its ratio, or the posterior it implies.

One small problem, every tool

To see that these are one calculation, take the simplest problem in the course and put it through each chapter. A quantity θ is measured 16 times with noise of standard deviation 2. The average of the readings is 1.0, so its standard error is 0.5. The prior is a Gaussian centred on zero with standard deviation 2.

QuestionToolChapterAnswer
What is the energy?E(θ) = (θ − 1)²/(2 × 0.25) + θ²/(2 × 4)The dictionaryA spring of stiffness 4.25
Best value and error bar?Minimum and inverse stiffnessError bars0.941 ± 0.485
How far did the prior pull it?Shrinkage weight τ²/(τ² + σ²/n)The Wiener filter0.941 of the data value: a pull of 0.12σ
What is the evidence?Gaussian integralModel comparisonZ = 0.172, ln Z = −1.760
Is F = E − TS?Mean energy 2.456, entropy 0.695The dictionary2.456 − 0.695 = 1.760 = −ln Z
Evidence by another route?Thermodynamic integration from β = 0 to 1Computing the evidence−1.760
How much was learned?Relative entropy, prior to posteriorInformation1.06 nats = 1.5 bits
How many parameters constrained?2 Var(ln L), the heat capacityComputing the evidence0.91 of one
Is θ different from zero?Savage-Dickey ratio of densities at 0Model comparisonOdds of only 1.6 to 1, though it is "2σ"
What would ABC give?Keep simulated averages within 0.25 of 1.0ABC8.6% kept; error bar 4% too wide
What would a sampler do?A particle jiggling in the spring at T = 1SamplersVisits θ with spread 0.485
What must a neural method learn?A Gaussian with mean 0.941 × (average) and fixed widthNeural methodsTwo numbers, from simulations alone

Twelve questions, one Gaussian integral. The entries agree with each other to every digit, because they are the same object read in different ways: the free energy from the energy and the entropy equals the free energy by integration over temperature equals minus the log of the evidence. In a real problem the integral cannot be done and the entries come from different approximations. Their agreement is then the test that the analysis is right, which is the point of the checks chapter.

Where the correspondence stops

An identity of mathematics is not an identity of meaning, and five differences are worth keeping in view.

  • The randomness means different things. Thermal fluctuations happen. Posterior uncertainty is a statement about knowledge. A sampler's particle does not exist.
  • The direction of the problem is reversed. Physics usually knows the energy and wants the averages. Inference observes some averages and wants the parameters of the energy. Every step of the second contains an instance of the first.
  • There is no thermodynamic limit to lean on. A gas has 1023 particles and its fluctuations are negligible. A data set may be small, and the small-system corrections that physics can ignore are often the whole answer.
  • The model can be wrong. A Hamiltonian is a hypothesis about nature that experiments test. A simulator is a hypothesis too, and nothing in the formalism of this course notices if it is false. The partition function of a wrong model is computed just as confidently.
  • Some analogies in this course are loose. Stochastic gradient descent is only approximately thermal. The link between deep networks and the renormalisation group is suggestive, not proved. Where a chapter says "picture" and not "identity", that is the reason.

Open problems

The dictionary is most useful where it is incomplete, because an entry missing on one side is a research problem that the other side may already have studied.

Open question in inferenceWhat physics suggests
Why do very large networks generalise, when counting parameters says they should not?Singular models pay a smaller Occam cost (the pole structure of the phase-transitions chapter). Wide networks have a 1/width expansion like a large-N field theory.
When is a posterior impossible to learn with a feasible number of simulations?Thresholds between easy, hard and impossible phases, known for a few idealised problems and not for real simulators.
How should a misspecified simulator be handled?Effective field theory: state the range of validity and carry an error budget for what was left out. A systematic version for simulators does not exist yet.
How can a learned posterior be trusted for one particular data set, not only on average?Coverage tests are averages over the prior, like thermodynamic averages. A test for a single realisation is the harder, sample-specific question.
What are the right summary statistics for a non-Gaussian field?The relevant operators of a renormalisation-group flow. Finding them for the cosmic web is unsolved, and networks currently find them without telling us what they are.
Can training be understood well enough to predict its error?A theory of the non-equilibrium dynamics of a glassy system with anisotropic noise. Partial results only.

How to use this course

If you are a physicist starting to use these methods, the practical advice of the whole course fits in a few lines.

  1. Start with a problem whose answer you know, and keep it as a permanent test.
  2. Try ABC first. It has the fewest ways to go wrong.
  3. Use neural posterior estimation for the main result, with well-chosen summaries.
  4. Run a coverage test and train more than one network. Report both.
  5. Ask what your simulator leaves out before you believe a small error bar.
  6. When a method fails, ask what the corresponding physical system would be doing: trapped in a metastable state, treated in mean field where correlations matter, or expanded about the wrong saddle.

The last item is the reason for writing the course in this language. A physicist already has intuition for how many-body systems behave and misbehave. That intuition transfers to estimators without change, once the two vocabularies are seen to be one.

The course in five sentences

  • A posterior is a Boltzmann distribution, and the evidence is its partition function.
  • A simulator's likelihood is a path integral over its hidden variables.
  • Each estimator, classical or neural, is a known strategy for evaluating such an integral.
  • Each estimator fails the way the corresponding physical approximation fails.
  • The mathematics is shared and the meaning is not: check the model, not only the calculation.
← 38. Applications40. Appendix: history →

Appendix

Appendix: a shared history

The two subjects in this course have been lending to each other for a hundred and fifty years. This appendix sets the loans out in order, and marks which way each one went.

physics

The connection. The two subjects built the same mathematics, then lent it back and forth. Each algorithm in this course has a date in physics and a later date in statistics, or the reverse.

In inferenceIn statistical physics and field theory
MCMC, 1970 to 1990Metropolis Monte Carlo, 1953
Hamiltonian Monte Carlo, 1990sHybrid Monte Carlo for lattice QCD, 1987
Boltzmann machines, 1985Ising model and Hopfield network
Normalising flows, 2015Flow-based lattice sampling, 2019 (the loan returned)
Diffusion models, 2015Non-equilibrium thermodynamics; Langevin dynamics

How to read the timeline

The tables that follow list the developments that the chapters refer to, in order of date. The third column says where the work was done: in physics (brick red), in statistics and machine learning (blue), or in both. The last column says what it has to do with this course. The account is selective. It follows the ideas used in the chapters and leaves out a great deal of both subjects. Dates are those of first publication as usually cited.

Three periods stand out. Up to about 1950 the two subjects built the same mathematics independently. From 1950 to about 2000 the traffic ran mostly from physics to statistics: the algorithms for sampling and approximating were invented to simulate matter and adopted for inference a decade or two later. Since about 2010 it has run both ways, and quickly: machine learning now returns methods to physics within a few years of taking the idea from it.

1763 to 1948: the same mathematics, built twice

Probability as a logic of inference and probability as a description of matter grew up separately. By the end of this period each had its central object, the posterior and the Boltzmann distribution, and each had met the integral it could not do.

YearWhat happenedWhereWhy it matters here
1763, 1812Bayes, and then Laplace, formulate inverse probability: from observed data to the probability of causes.StatisticsThe posterior distribution.
1805, 1809Legendre and Gauss introduce least squares, and Gauss derives it from Gaussian errors.BothGauss was fitting orbits. The first likelihood, and the quadratic energy of the curve-fitting chapter.
1877Boltzmann relates entropy to the number of microscopic arrangements.PhysicsEntropy as a count of states: maximum entropy, large deviations.
1902Gibbs publishes the theory of ensembles and the partition function.PhysicsThe central object of the course: the dictionary, three ensembles.
1905, 1908Einstein explains Brownian motion, and Langevin writes its equation of motion.PhysicsFluctuation and response; the ancestor of diffusion models and of SGD as physics.
1907Weiss introduces the mean field to explain ferromagnetism.PhysicsThe first variational approximation: free energy, hierarchical models.
1922, 1925Fisher defines likelihood, sufficiency and information.StatisticsThe Fisher matrix, which is a stiffness: error bars, summaries.
1925Ising solves his model of a magnet in one dimension.PhysicsThe model that later becomes the Boltzmann machine: energy-based models.
1938Cramér proves the first large-deviation theorem, for insurance risk.StatisticsEntropy is a rate function: large deviations.
1939Jeffreys publishes his Theory of Probability, with Bayes factors.StatisticsJeffreys was a geophysicist. Model comparison.
1942Wiener derives the optimal filter, for wartime prediction.BothThe Wiener filter.
1945, 1946Rao and Cramér prove the bound on estimator variance. Rao notes that the Fisher matrix is a metric.StatisticsError bars, information geometry.
1948Shannon defines information. Feynman publishes the path integral.BothIn the same year: information and path integrals.

1946 to 1990: physics invents the algorithms

Electronic computers arrived, and physicists were among their first users. Faced with partition functions they could not compute, they invented ways of sampling them. Each method in this table is now a standard tool of statistics.

YearWhat happenedWhereWhy it matters here
1946Ulam and von Neumann devise the Monte Carlo method at Los Alamos, for neutron transport.PhysicsEstimating integrals by random sampling. Importance sampling follows around 1950.
1953Metropolis, the Rosenbluths and the Tellers simulate a fluid of hard discs.PhysicsMarkov chain Monte Carlo: samplers.
1954Zwanzig derives free-energy perturbation.PhysicsThe physics of an importance weight: importance sampling.
1957Jaynes shows that statistical mechanics follows from maximum-entropy inference.BothThe first explicit statement that the two subjects are one: maximum entropy.
1960Kalman publishes his filter, soon used for spacecraft navigation.StatisticsEngineering, on the mathematics of Brownian motion: inference in time.
1970Hastings generalises the Metropolis rule, in a statistics journal.StatisticsThe first crossing of the algorithm into statistics. Little noticed for fourteen years.
1971Wilson develops the renormalisation group.PhysicsCoarse-graining as a systematic procedure: summaries, eigenmodes, nuisance parameters.
1975 to 1979Edwards and Anderson, Sherrington and Kirkpatrick, and Parisi found the theory of spin glasses.PhysicsThe replica method, later used to analyse learning. Parisi shared the 2021 Nobel Prize for it.
1981Parisi and Wu propose stochastic quantisation.PhysicsSampling a field theory by Langevin dynamics: diffusion.
1982Hopfield models memory as the energy minima of a spin system.PhysicsPhysics enters neural networks: energy-based models.
1983Kirkpatrick, Gelatt and Vecchi introduce simulated annealing.PhysicsOptimisation by cooling: training as physics.
1984Geman and Geman use Gibbs sampling to restore images. Rubin describes inference by simulate-and-reject.StatisticsStatistics adopts MCMC, with physical vocabulary in the title. Rubin's thought experiment is ABC before its name.
1985Ackley, Hinton and Sejnowski publish the Boltzmann machine.StatisticsAn Ising model with learned couplings. Machine learning adopts the Boltzmann distribution by name.
1986Swendsen and Wang introduce replica Monte Carlo for spin glasses.PhysicsParallel tempering: samplers, computing the evidence.
1987Duane, Kennedy, Pendleton and Roweth invent hybrid Monte Carlo for lattice QCD.PhysicsHamiltonian Monte Carlo: samplers.
1985 to 1990Amit, Gutfreund and Sompolinsky, and Gardner, compute the capacity of neural networks with spin-glass methods.PhysicsPhysicists analyse learning itself. The start of the statistical physics of inference: phase transitions.
1990Gelfand and Smith show that sampling solves a broad class of Bayesian problems.StatisticsBayesian statistics becomes practical, thirty-seven years after Metropolis.

1990 to 2010: statistics makes the methods its own

With the algorithms in hand, statisticians and computer scientists extended them, gave them new uses and began to add ideas of their own. ABC, the first method of simulation-based inference, came from biology and not from physics.

YearWhat happenedWhereWhy it matters here
1993Hinton and van Camp apply the variational bound to neural networks. Gordon, Salmond and Smith introduce the particle filter.StatisticsFree energy; inference in time. Both have earlier counterparts in physics.
1995Green introduces reversible-jump MCMC.StatisticsSampling across models of different size: the grand canonical ensemble.
1996Neal brings hybrid Monte Carlo to Bayesian neural networks, and shows that wide networks are Gaussian processes.StatisticsThe transfer of Hamiltonian Monte Carlo to statistics: samplers, Gaussian processes.
1997Jarzynski proves his equality for non-equilibrium work.PhysicsFree energies from fast switching. Neal's annealed importance sampling of 2001 is the same identity: importance sampling.
1997 to 2002Tavaré, Pritchard, Beaumont and colleagues develop approximate Bayesian computation in population genetics.StatisticsThe first likelihood-free method: ABC.
1998Amari proposes the natural gradient. Gelman and Meng bring thermodynamic integration to statistics as path sampling.StatisticsInformation geometry; computing the evidence.
2002Hinton introduces contrastive divergence.StatisticsBoltzmann machines become trainable, and in 2006 start the revival of deep networks.
2004Skilling proposes nested sampling.BothA physicist's method for the evidence, via the density of states: samplers.
2005Hyvärinen introduces score matching. Baik, Ben Arous and Péché prove the detection threshold in random matrices.BothLearning a force without a partition function: diffusion. Phase transitions in inference made rigorous.
2009Lüscher proposes trivialising maps for lattice field theory. Enßlin and colleagues formulate information field theory. Watanabe publishes singular learning theory.BothFlows before the name; the Wiener filter as a field theory; the Occam penalty as a pole.

2010 to today: neural methods, and the return to physics

Neural networks became able to represent complicated probability distributions, and the methods of this course took their present form. Several of them were explicitly modelled on physics, and several went back into physics within a few years.

YearWhat happenedWhereWhy it matters here
2010, 2015Tabak and Vanden-Eijnden, then Rezende and Mohamed, introduce normalising flows.StatisticsFlows. Used from 2019 by Albergo, Kanwar and Shanahan to sample lattice field theories, closing the loop with Lüscher.
2011Hoffman and Gelman automate Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (NUTS). Welling and Teh turn SGD into a sampler.StatisticsSamplers; training as physics.
2013Kingma and Welling introduce the variational autoencoder.StatisticsAmortised variational inference: free energy, amortisation.
2015Sohl-Dickstein and colleagues introduce diffusion models, citing non-equilibrium thermodynamics in the title.BothDiffusion. The clearest case of a method designed from physics.
2016Papamakarios and Murray train a network to output a posterior from simulations.StatisticsNeural posterior estimation: neural methods.
2019Papamakarios, Sterratt and Murray introduce neural likelihood estimation, and Greenberg and colleagues the standard sequential NPE.StatisticsNeural methods.
2019, 2020Song and Ermon, then Ho, Jain and Abbeel, make score-based and denoising diffusion work at scale.StatisticsDiffusion.
2020Hermans and colleagues, and Durkan and colleagues, introduce neural ratio estimation. Cranmer, Brehmer and Louppe name the field.BothNeural methods. Two of the three authors of the review are particle physicists.
2021Dax and colleagues analyse gravitational-wave events in seconds. Hermans and colleagues show that standard methods are often overconfident.BothAmortisation; checks.
2022, 2023Score-based and flow-matching posterior estimators are introduced.StatisticsDiffusion comes to SBI: the curve-fitting chapter tests both.
2021, 2024Nobel Prizes in Physics to Parisi (2021) for disordered systems, and to Hopfield and Hinton (2024) for neural networks.BothRecognition that the two subjects overlap.

Who borrowed what

Set side by side, the loans form a clear pattern. The table lists the main ones, with the delay between the original and its adoption.

Taken into statistics and machine learningFrom physicsDelay
MCMC (Hastings 1970; Geman and Geman 1984)Metropolis Monte Carlo, 195317 to 31 years

Annealed importance sampling (2001)Jarzynski equality, 1997 (found independently)4 years
Thermodynamic integration, path sampling (1998)Kirkwood, 193563 years

Flow-based sampling of lattice field theories (2019)Normalising flows, 20154 years
Neural-network quantum states (Carleo and Troyer, 2017)Restricted Boltzmann machinesabout 10 years
SBI analyses in particle physics, cosmology and gravitational waves (2019 onwards)Neural posterior, likelihood and ratio estimation, 2016 to 20201 to 3 years
Diffusion models as samplers and emulators of physical fieldsDiffusion models, 2015 to 20202 to 5 years

What the history shows

  • The delays are shrinking. Metropolis sampling took three decades to reach statistics. Normalising flows took four years to reach lattice field theory. The two communities now read each other's preprints.
  • The direction has become two-way. Until about 2010 physics was mostly the lender. Since then machine learning has returned flows, diffusion models and neural estimators to physics, usually built on ideas it had borrowed earlier.
  • The same discovery is often made twice. Jarzynski's equality and annealed importance sampling, diffusion Monte Carlo and the particle filter, Wiener's filter and Kolmogorov's: when two fields face the same integral they tend to find the same answer. A missing entry on one side of the dictionary is a good place to look for the next one.
  • Simulation-based inference began outside physics. ABC came from population genetics. Physics supplied the samplers before it and much of the neural machinery after it, and is now among its heaviest users.
  • The people overlap. Jeffreys was a geophysicist, Jaynes a physicist, Skilling a physicist, Hopfield a physicist, and many of the authors of the neural methods trained in physics or astronomy. The shared history is partly a shared population.

The history in four lines

  • Before 1950: the posterior and the Boltzmann distribution, developed apart.
  • 1950 to 1990: physics invents Monte Carlo, annealing, hybrid Monte Carlo and the theory of disordered systems.
  • 1984 to 2010: statistics adopts and extends them, and adds ABC.
  • Since 2010: neural methods built on physical ideas, returned to physics within years.
